# Maqāṣid al-kalām fī ʿaqāʾid al-Islām

## Saʿd al-Dīn al-Taftāzānī — complete English translation based on the published critical Arabic edition

**Editorial scope, clarified 30 October 2026:** a complete English translation based on the published YEK 2019 critical Arabic edition, with commentary-supported interpretation, technical glossary, and selective textual notes. A new manuscript-based Arabic edition is outside the requested scope. All six divisions are translated; comprehensive sentence-by-sentence verification and independent bilingual review have not yet been completed. The 25 critical dossiers record the checks actually made.

The translation concerns the *Maqāṣid* itself. Material from the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid* is used to interpret or check the text and is identified in editorial notes; it is not silently incorporated into the author’s concise text. The five divisions following Division I are Divisions II–VI.

**Revision C1: 30 October 2026.** The introduction, all six divisions, and the author’s colophon are included. “We,” “our teachers,” and judgements of correctness within the translation belong to the author or the speaker he reports. Bracketed expansions and the separately headed editorial notes belong to the translator. A translated objection is not necessarily the author’s adopted position.

Current English translation length: approximately 87,404 words, excluding this introduction, glossary, and editorial notes.

### Source and editorial practice

Base edition: *el-Makâsıd: Kelâm İlminin Maksatları*, Istanbul: Türkiye Yazma Eserler Kurumu Başkanlığı, 2019, first edition, ISBN 9789751743428. Editor: Ahmet Kaylı; Turkish translation: İrfan Eyibil. The Arabic text is credited to Ahmet Kaylı, Ziya Çetintaş, Selahaddin Cimitoğlu, and Ahmet Çınar. The page numbers below refer to the printed Arabic pages of this edition, not manuscript folios. Modern Turkish translation, introduction, and apparatus are not translated wholesale here.

Source access: [YEK PDF](https://ekitap.yek.gov.tr/Uploads/ProductsFiles/_129.%20MAKASID.pdf); [copy of the 2019 Arabic–Turkish volume used for page checking](https://can-ada.net/wp-content/uploads/2023/04/EL-MAKASID-KELAM-ILMININ-MAKSATLARI.pdf).

Arabic extraction is a reading aid, not an established critical text. Page images are consulted where recorded; the entire extracted Arabic text has not been proofread. This English reading file therefore does not reproduce the faulty extraction as a facing Arabic edition. Source-page boundaries are retained even where a sentence continues over the boundary. Short square-bracketed expressions in the translation supply terminology or necessary context. Editorial notes are separated below.

Reference corpus acquired: the OpenITI JK001157 and ShamAY0033884 transcriptions of the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*; al-Jurjānī’s *al-Taʿrīfāt* (JK000768); and al-Tahānawī’s *Kashshāf iṣṭilāḥāt al-funūn* (Shamela0002573). The two *Sharḥ* transcriptions derive from the same 1981 print base and are not independent manuscript witnesses. The author’s local usage and explanation take priority; later technical dictionaries serve as comparative lexical evidence. Acquisition of a reference does not imply that every passage has been exhaustively collated against it.

The critical introduction distinguishes the manuscript leaves reproduced in YEK that have now been inspected from readings known only through its apparatus. The complete Istanbul 1305 print scan has also been acquired, and selected passages have been checked directly. The Turkish translation has been acquired with the base volume but has not been systematically collated. No complete independent English translation has been established as a collation source.

Historical physical, astronomical, medical, and ethnogeographical claims are translated as claims within the work. The translation does not adopt them as present scientific conclusions. Reported positions, objections, replies, and the author’s judgements are kept distinct.

<a id="critical-introduction"></a>

# Critical introduction to revision C1

This edition presents the complete English translation of the standalone *Maqāṣid*, with a selective apparatus of textual decisions and interpretive checks. Its purpose is to make each intervention accountable: what the Arabic reading is, where the evidence comes from, what has been adopted, and why. **It is not yet a definitive establishment of the Arabic text.** Full translation coverage does not establish that every sentence has been independently verified or every witness collated.

## Text, commentary, and editorial voice

The *matn* is the object of the translation. The author’s *Sharḥ* is a privileged source for understanding its compressed arguments, but it remains a distinct text. A commentary can explain an ellipsis, identify a speaker, or support a disputed reading; its agreement does not, by itself, prove what stood in every copy of the *matn*. In the older prints, the concise text often runs around a central block of commentary. These two areas must be read separately.

The English preserves the author’s sequence of positions, objections, and replies. “They say,” “we reply,” and “this is denied” are argumentative markers. A proposition stated inside an objection must not be indexed as the author’s conclusion. Square brackets identify supplied context. The original page divisions are preserved for verification, including sentences divided between pages.

The project follows an existing published critical Arabic text rather than undertaking a new manuscript edition. Completion of the English edition therefore depends on verification of the translation, references, terminology, and disclosed departures from that published text; acquiring complete manuscripts is not a prerequisite. C1’s documented corrections and facsimile observations remain part of the evidence record. The present scope clarification makes no additional claim of completed textual or bilingual review.

## Witnesses and access

The following sigla distinguish physical textual witnesses from the modern edition and digital reading aids. Folio identifications for reproduced manuscript leaves follow their captions in YEK. Only the stated image extent has been inspected here.

| Siglum | Source | Access used in this revision | Evidential limit |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Y | YEK, *el-Makâsıd: Kelâm İlminin Maksatları*, Istanbul, 2019 | Complete volume acquired; Arabic text pp. 89–777, selected page images and apparatus checked; endnotes pp. 794–796 examined | The working base, not an independent medieval witness |
| J | Süleymaniye, Carullah 1264 | Reproductions of ff. 1b, 184b, 38b, 159a in Y, pp. 778–781 | Four reproduced leaves; other J readings are reported from Y’s apparatus |
| S | Süleymaniye, Serez 1402 | Reproductions of ff. 1b and 190b in Y, pp. 782–783 | Two reproduced leaves; the ending of the work is missing from this copy |
| L | Süleymaniye, Laleli 2442 | Reproductions of ff. 1b and 145b in Y, pp. 784–785 | Opening and ending viewed; dense marginalia have not been comprehensively deciphered |
| Gh | Ragıp Paşa 796 | Reproductions of ff. 2a and 200b in Y, pp. 788 and 790 | Matn and commentary layout inspected; no complete folio collation |
| M | Istanbul print, 1277 AH | Reproductions of I:4 and II:226 in Y, pp. 789 and 791 | Opening and closing matn areas inspected |
| N | Istanbul print, 1305 AH, Columbia copy | Complete 719-image PDF acquired from Arabic Collections Online; selected passages checked directly | A printed witness; its relation to earlier prints is not established by counting it as another independent vote |
| JK | OpenITI, JK001157, *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid* | Searchable Arabic transcription, used at explicitly cited passages | Reading aid based on a 1981 print; page markers and sequence need checking against images |
| AY | OpenITI, ShamAY0033884, *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid* | Acquired as a parallel digital resource | Shares JK’s print base; not an independent manuscript witness |

Y’s editor reports J as dated 789 AH and L as dated 813 AH, and discusses possible authorial writing in small portions of J and S. Those attributions are not independently authenticated here. The reproduced leaves do not justify calling either entire manuscript an autograph. Y also proposes a close relationship between M and Gh; their agreement must therefore not automatically be treated as independent testimony. These historical descriptions are attributed to Y’s introduction, especially pp. 33–36 and 64–67.

## Evidence labels

| Label | Meaning | What it establishes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| D | Direct image observation | The specified reading or layout is visible in the identified reproduction or scan |
| R | Reported reading | Y’s apparatus attributes the reading to the named witness; that particular manuscript location has not been independently inspected |
| C | Commentary evidence | The author’s explanation supports a construction or sense; it is not automatically a variant of the matn |
| T | Translation revision | The English has been corrected or clarified without changing the Arabic reading |
| U | Unresolved | Available evidence is insufficient for a final decision |

A combined label, such as R/C, means that a reported variant is supported by commentary. “D” never means that the whole witness has been collated. An omission due to a lost leaf is not evidence that the omitted passage was absent from that witness’s exemplar. A marginal correction is distinguished from the body text wherever the available evidence allows it. No unrecorded witness is inferred from an appealing reconstruction.

## Principles of selection

Y supplies the running text and page references. Its reading is retained unless a specific reason for departure is recorded. The departures at pp. 415 and 729 are particularly consequential: a small negative or restrictive particle reverses the argument. The alternatives and the commentary evidence are therefore stated explicitly in the apparatus. Where the wording remains doubtful, as at p. 247, the uncertainty is preserved rather than hidden by a smooth translation.

Manuscript age, number of witnesses, and apparent doctrinal plausibility are not used as automatic rules. Two related prints can reproduce one error. An author can report a view he rejects. A difficult reading can be authentic; an unintelligible extraction can also be merely an extraction error. Changes to extraction, changes to translation, and changes to the underlying text are recorded separately.

Technical dictionaries assist interpretation but do not decide the author’s position. In particular, a later definition of *taṣdīq* cannot override Taftāzānī’s express criticism of that definition. The glossary records contextual equivalents: *imkān* is not translated mechanically by a single word, and epistemic certainty is distinguished from metaphysical necessity. Historical terminology in astronomy, medicine, and mineralogy is retained where identification with a modern category would introduce unsupported precision.

## Reproducible source locations

- **Y:** [publisher PDF](https://ekitap.yek.gov.tr/Uploads/ProductsFiles/_129.%20MAKASID.pdf); [copy used for checking](https://can-ada.net/wp-content/uploads/2023/04/EL-MAKASID-KELAM-ILMININ-MAKSATLARI.pdf). Printed page numbers coincide with PDF page numbers in the acquired copy.
- **N:** [Columbia / Arabic Collections Online catalogue and viewer](https://sites.dlib.nyu.edu/viewer/books/columbia_aco000419/display?lang=ar); [complete scan](https://mc.dlib.nyu.edu/files/books/columbia_aco000419/columbia_aco000419_lo.pdf); [persistent identifier](https://hdl.handle.net/2333.1/vq83bpsz).
- **Lexical corpus:** al-Jurjānī, *al-Taʿrīfāt*, OpenITI JK000768; al-Tahānawī, *Kashshāf iṣṭilāḥāt al-funūn*, OpenITI Shamela0002573. Specific comparisons appear in the glossary, not as a claim of exhaustive checking.

**Later-print check, 30 October 2026:** the publisher lists a [2024 second printing](https://esatis.yek.gov.tr/urun/el-makasid--ikinci-baski-_3847.aspx) under the same ISBN. That printing has not been acquired or collated here. Its catalogue record does not establish whether any Arabic readings or editorial notes were changed. C1’s pagination and decisions refer specifically to the acquired 2019 volume.

For N, cited PDF pages are one-based image positions in the downloaded file. The directly checked correspondences include I:56 = PDF 72; I:117 = PDF 133; I:122–123 = PDF 138–139; I:153–154 = PDF 169–170; I:336 = PDF 352; II:262 = PDF 660; II:304 = PDF 702; and II:308 = PDF 706. Inserted material prevents assuming a single offset throughout the volume. The neighbouring manuscript-leaf reproductions in Y are identified separately in the table above.

## What the revision establishes

All six divisions and the authorial closing date are translated. Local checks now distinguish an error in the scope of a modal claim, an obscure formulation about negative predicates, the respective examples of complete causes, a disputed restrictive particle, and a consequential double negation. The terminal witness observations support retaining the author’s date while excluding the copyist’s colophon from the translated work.

The published Arabic text supplies the edition’s base. Remaining translation work includes complete verification of names and quotations, sentence-by-sentence Arabic–English checking, and independent bilingual review. The machine-extracted Arabic is not published as a facing text because extraction can drop ligatures and negative particles. The apparatus below is **selective and auditable**, not exhaustive; a new exhaustive manuscript apparatus is outside scope.

<a id="division-i"></a>

# Author’s introduction and Division I

### M-P01 — Praise

*Source: p. 89.*

In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Ever-Merciful. Praise to Him: the fragrances of contingency breathe forth the necessity of His existence, and upon the pages of created beings shine the traces of His generosity and bounty. Amid the darkness of temporal origination gleam the flashes of His beginningless majesty; the comprehensive expressions of His attributes and names proclaim the wisdoms of divinity.

### M-P02 — Blessings

*Source: p. 89.*

I invoke blessings upon him whom He sent with radiant light to clarify the path and disclose the clear proofs, and whom He commissioned with a decisive command to establish arguments and remove specious objections: the bearer of the triumphant religion and dazzling wisdom, Muḥammad, the seal of His messengers and prophets; and upon his pure progeny and the shining stars among his family, Companions, successors and allies. And I invoke peace upon them.

### M-P03 — Exhortation to the reader

*Source: p. 89.*

You discerning ones among my brothers in religion and my helpers in attaining certainty: hold fast to God’s firm cord, and you will ascend to the horizon of manifest truth. Remain upright—as you have been commanded—upon the direct, well-trodden way, and you will reach ample shade. Do not follow the footsteps of partisan desires, lest you stray from the straight path.

### M-P04 — The aim of the compendium

*Source: p. 89–91.*

Here, in this compendium, I present to you, from the principal aims of kalām, the finest results of what intellects have refined and understandings distilled. In laying the foundations of Islamic doctrines, I dictate to you what will raise you to the finest dwelling and station among their lofty chambers. I expend the last reserves of effort to attain the stations of reasoned verification in divine unity; shake the dust of objection from the hems of the proofs of holiness and glory; scatter the gems of true texts understood only by the learned; and raise the banners of truthful signs denied only by wrongdoing people. Perhaps, when you have acquired flashes of secrets from the distillation of my discourse, and the rising lights from its horizons have illuminated your inward sight, you will no longer follow the sheets of doubts that people circulate when opinions clash, nor stand at the stations of probabilistic judgements and imaginings when desires collide. Rather, from your destinations you will look out for the crescents of success and the signs of felicity, calling to one another: “Extinguish the lamp: dawn has broken.”

### M-P05 — Prayer and arrangement of the work

*Source: p. 91.*

I implore God to guide me upon the straight path. In Him I place my trust: He suffices me, and how excellent a guardian He is. I have arranged the work in six principal divisions (مقاصد).

### M-I-01 — Definition and scope of kalām

*Source: p. 93.*

The first principal division: principles. It contains chapters. Chapter one: preliminaries. Kalām is knowledge of religious doctrines derived from certain proofs. Its subject matter (موضوع) is the known, insofar as establishing those doctrines is related to it. Its questions are the theoretical propositions of revealed religion concerning belief. Its end is to adorn faith with firm grounding; its benefit is to secure an ordered life in this world and salvation in the return. It is therefore the noblest of the sciences.

### M-I-02 — The earlier account of its subject

*Source: p. 93.*

The earlier scholars hold that its subject is the existent insofar as it is existent. It is distinguished from philosophical metaphysics by conducting its inquiries according to the governing principles of Islam (قانون الإسلام): that is, what is known with certainty from religion, such as the proceeding of multiplicity from the One, an angel’s descent from heaven, and the world’s being encompassed by nonexistence and passing away, together with other matters affirmed decisively by revealed religion rather than philosophy. What is meant is not simply what is true—even by claim—for philosophy, like the kalām of an opponent, shares in making that claim.

### M-I-03 — Mental existence and supplementary inquiries

*Source: p. 93.*

If it is objected that, despite denying mental existence, kalām investigates the states of things whose existence is not taken into account, such as rational investigation and proof, and things that have no existence, such as the nonexistent and a state (حال), we reply: these are principles and supplementary discussions. Even if the objection is granted, the denial of mental existence is only some scholars’ position.

### M-I-04 — God and contingent beings as subject matter

*Source: p. 95.*

It is also said that its subject is God Most High’s essence alone, or together with the essences of contingent beings insofar as they depend upon Him, on the ground that these are what it investigates. Thus it is defined as the science investigating the Maker’s states—His affirmative and negative attributes, and His acts relating to this world and the hereafter—or the states of the Necessary Existent and of contingent beings in their beginning and return, according to the governing principles of Islam.

### M-I-05 — Preliminary discussions and their purpose

*Source: p. 95.*

If it is objected that the discussions of general matters, substances and accidents investigate contingent beings without considering their dependence, we reply: this occurs by way of a digression for completeness, a report for refutation, or as a preliminary principle for establishing the point at issue. Otherwise it is among kalām’s superfluous discussions.

### M-I-06 — Establishing a science’s principles

*Source: p. 95.*

If it is objected that kalām’s principles must be self-evident, since no religious science stands above it, we reply: a science’s principles may be established within that science, or in a lower science, provided this does not produce circularity. The principles of a religious science may also be established in a nonreligious one, as with the principles of jurisprudence in Arabic linguistic science.

### M-I-07 — The objection from proving the Maker

*Source: p. 95.*

It has been objected that establishing the Maker is among kalām’s highest inquiries, whereas a science’s subject is not established within it, but within a higher science, until one reaches a science whose subject evidently exists, such as the existent insofar as it is existent.

### M-I-08 — Is the conception of knowledge necessary

*Source: p. 97.*

Chapter two: knowledge. It contains investigations. First investigation. It is said that the conception of knowledge is necessary (ضروري): it occurs, and other things are known only through knowledge, so if knowledge were known through something else, circularity would follow. Moreover, everyone’s knowledge of their own existence is self-evident, and this is preceded by knowledge without qualification; the latter therefore has a stronger claim to self-evidence.

### M-I-09 — Conceiving knowledge and its occurrence

*Source: p. 97.*

The reply distinguishes conceiving knowledge from its occurring. The conception of knowledge depends upon conceiving something else, whereas the conception of that other thing depends upon knowledge’s occurring; hence there is no circularity. What is self-evident is the occurrence of knowledge of one’s existence. This does not require conceiving knowledge, much less the self-evidence of that conception.

### M-I-10 — Representation and possession of a quality

*Source: p. 97.*

If it is objected that occurrence in the soul is knowledge, we reply: not occurrence without qualification, but occurrence through a non-original mode of existence. Its test is whether the soul is qualified by the thing or not: an unbeliever may possess unbelief without conceiving it, and conceive faith without possessing it.

### M-I-11 — Reflexive knowledge and knowledge under an aspect

*Source: p. 97–99.*

If it is objected that knowing another thing entails the possibility of knowing that one knows it, leading to knowledge of the qualified instance before knowledge of the unqualified, and that knowing oneself to know one’s existence is self-evident and requires no investigation at all—thus establishing the point at issue—we reply: even if this is granted, only a conception under some aspect follows.

### M-I-12 — Definitions and senses of knowledge

*Source: p. 99.*

Most definitions of knowledge are defective. Some say this is because knowledge is obscure; the exacting scholars say it is because it is clear. There is no dispute that the term has multiple senses. It may mean intellectual apprehension without qualification, and is then explained as the occurrence of a form in the intellect or the soul’s reaching a meaning. It may mean one of the kinds of assent, and is then explained as a firm judgement corresponding to reality on an adequate ground. It may also include both conception and certain assent, and is then explained as a quality through which what is referred to becomes manifest to the one in whom it inheres—for there is no such manifestation in what is not certain—or as a quality that produces a discrimination among meanings admitting no contradictory alternative. Matters of customary occurrence admit a contradictory alternative only in the sense that supposing it entails nothing intrinsically impossible, not in the sense that the knower allows it, either actually, as in probabilistic judgement, or by implication, as in an uncritical follower’s belief.

### M-I-13 — Conception and assent

*Source: p. 99.*

Second investigation. If knowledge is a judgement—that is, acknowledgement and acceptance of a relation—it is assent (تصديق); otherwise it is conception (تصور). They differ in their realities, not merely in a relation. It is necessarily evident that each divides into the theoretical, which requires rational investigation, and the necessary, which does not.

### M-I-14 — Necessary knowledge and power to disengage

*Source: p. 101.*

Necessary knowledge is sometimes explained as what attaches to a creature’s soul in such a way that it finds no means of disengaging from it: that is, it has no power at all to disengage from it. The disappearance of necessary knowledge when a contrary intervenes, or its failure to occur because a condition is absent, therefore presents no objection. Nor does the inescapability of theoretical knowledge after it has been acquired, when there is then no power to disengage from it, because such power existed beforehand.

### M-I-15 — Can conceptions be acquired

*Source: p. 101.*

The Imām’s preferred position is that whatever conceptions occur are necessary, leaving no scope for acquisition. From the side of what is sought, the argument is that it is either known altogether, and so is not sought; unknown altogether, so that attention cannot be directed toward it; or known under one aspect but not another, so that neither aspect can be sought. Assent differs: it can be sought because its constituent conceptions are already present. The reply is that knowledge of some aspects of an unknown thing suffices for directing attention toward it.

### M-I-16 — The means of acquiring a conception

*Source: p. 101.*

From the side of the means of acquisition, the argument is that it is either all the parts—and this is the thing itself—or some of them, which involves defining by something extrinsic; or something extrinsic, whose use depends upon knowing that it is peculiar to the thing, a knowledge that depends upon conceiving it and conceiving everything else in detail.

### M-I-17 — Conceptions of parts and conception of the whole

*Source: p. 103.*

The reply is that the defining account is the aggregate of the conceptions of the parts, whereas acquisition is the conception of their aggregate. Acquisition has its effect in bringing them to mind together in an ordered arrangement. Insofar as separate conceptions relate to the parts, they form a definition; insofar as a single conception relates to them, they are what is defined. Because the two considerations are closely connected, their identity may be imagined. But what is identical is the aggregate of the parts and the quiddity, not the conceptions of those parts and the conception of the quiddity. Furthermore, defining a quiddity through one of its parts entails defining one of its parts only if merely distinguishing that quiddity from everything else does not count as defining it, and if knowing it is itself knowing the parts, just as it is itself those parts. Finally, definition through something extrinsic depends upon that thing’s being peculiar to the quiddity, not upon knowing that it is peculiar to it. Even if this is granted, conceiving it under some aspect and conceiving everything else in a general way suffices.

### M-I-18 — Six classes of necessary cognition

*Source: p. 103.*

Third investigation. Necessary cognitions fall into six classes: (1) self-evident judgements (بديهيات), which the intellect judges merely upon conceiving the two terms; these are called primary judgements (أوليات); (2) observations (مشاهدات), judged through an external sense, when they are called sensory judgements (حسيات), or an internal sense, when they are called inward awarenesses (وجدانيات); (3) innate judgements (فطريات), judged through an intermediary that does not leave the mind, and called propositions whose syllogisms accompany them; (4) experiential judgements (مجربات), judged through repeated observation; (5) multiply transmitted judgements (متواترات), judged merely upon the report of a group whose collusion in falsehood is impossible; and (6) intuitive judgements (حدسيات), judged through an intuition of the soul, which you will come to understand later.

### M-I-19 — Alternative classifications

*Source: p. 105.*

They are sometimes restricted to self-evident judgements and observations, either because those two include all the classes, or because the necessity of the other classes—and even the certainty of experiential and intuitive judgements—is open to question. Yet the exacting scholars among those who take this position do not classify them as theoretical, but as intermediate. The dispute is verbal.

### M-I-20 — Hearing a report and knowing its content

*Source: p. 105.*

If it is objected: how can multiply transmitted knowledge be disputed when it is a kind of sense perception? We reply: the issue concerns the content of the reports heard—for example, the existence of Mecca. The narrators’ statement that he, blessings and peace be upon him, said, “The proof is incumbent upon the claimant,” is heard. Knowledge that this is a saying of the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, arises through multiple transmission, necessarily or otherwise. Knowledge that proof is indeed incumbent upon the claimant arises inferentially through the report of one who speaks truth.

### M-I-21 — Objections to sensory cognition

*Source: p. 105.*

As for those who deny these cognitions, some impugn sensory judgements on the ground that the senses often err. The answer is that this does not exclude firm, true judgement where no error occurs.

### M-I-22 — Objections to self-evidence

*Source: p. 105.*

Others impugn self-evident judgements on the ground that the clearest and highest of them is “A thing either is or is not,” and this depends upon conceiving existence and nonexistence, establishing the meaning of subjecthood and predication, and answering the objections concerning them—matters requiring subtle investigations. The answer is that these objections do not produce doubt. We may disregard them, or, if we wish, direct attention to what makes the matter evident.

### M-I-23 — Radical scepticism and the author’s polemic

*Source: p. 107.*

Others impugn both together. The most plausible among them are the agnostics who say, “I doubt, and I doubt that I doubt,” and so on. They invoke the objections of both groups to produce doubt. The right course is to torment them, even with fire, so that they acknowledge [these truths] and their doctrine disappears, or they burn and their flame is extinguished.

### M-I-24 — The two movements of rational investigation

*Source: p. 107.*

Chapter three: rational investigation (نظر). It contains investigations. First investigation. When we seek to obtain something, the soul moves from it among its intelligibles, seeking and identifying its principles, and then returns from those principles by arranging them and proceeding to what is sought. Here there are two movements, acts of consideration, an arrangement, the removal of obstacles, orientation toward what is sought, and an end of the movement. The reality of rational investigation is the combination of the two movements. Yet some of its parts or concomitants may suffice for describing it. Thus it is explained as the first or second movement; arranging known things to reach an unknown; considering an intelligible to obtain an unknown; clearing the mind of inattentiveness; directing the intellect’s gaze toward intelligibles; or thought through which knowledge or probabilistic judgement is sought. ‘Thought’ means the soul’s movement among meanings. This excludes what does not seek knowledge or probabilistic judgement, such as most inner talk, and includes seeking a conception or a firm or preponderant assent, without regard to whether it corresponds to reality.

### M-I-25 — Sound investigation and the production of knowledge

*Source: p. 109.*

Second investigation. Rational investigation is sound if its matter and form are sound; otherwise it is unsound. Sound investigation accompanied by its conditions yields knowledge: in our account, knowledge occurs after it through God Most High’s creation, either customarily—with acquisition or without it—or by rational entailment. For the Muʿtazila it occurs through generation; for the philosophers, necessarily, because preparation is complete and emanation is perfect.

### M-I-26 — The objection from circularity

*Source: p. 109.*

If it is objected: if the judgement that rational investigation yields knowledge were necessary, rational people would not disagree about it, and it would be as clear as the judgement that one is half of two. If it were theoretical, it would depend upon that which depends upon it, producing circularity; and it would be known before it was known, producing contradiction.

### M-I-27 — Replies based on particular investigations

*Source: p. 109.*

We reply: it is necessary, and disagreement and differences in clarity may occur in necessary cognitions because of differences in familiarity or obscurity in the conceptions. Alternatively, it is theoretical and is acquired through another investigation whose premises are necessary, without contradiction. Thus, concerning our statement, “The world changes, and everything that changes is temporally originated,” one may say: this is an investigation, and it has necessarily yielded knowledge of the world’s temporal origination; therefore investigation yields knowledge. It is then known that this is due not to its particularity but to its soundness and satisfaction of its conditions. Every investigation of that kind therefore yields knowledge.

### M-I-28 — Singular and universal judgements about investigation

*Source: p. 109–111.*

The unknown proposition that depends on something else is the unquantified or universal proposition whose subject is described by the concept of investigation. What it depends upon, already known, is the singular proposition whose subject is this particular investigation itself. The precise point is that what follows from one judgement may differ according to how its subject is expressed: for example, judging that the world is temporally originated while expressing its subject as ‘what exists after nonexistence’, ‘what accompanies a temporally originated thing’, or ‘what changes’. If it is objected that the yield of knowledge is plainly necessary in the first syllogistic figure but theoretical in the others, so that neither description can be applied without qualification, we reply: the discussion concerns taking ‘investigation’ as the description of the subject; the detailed distinctions concern particular cases. Moreover, what yields knowledge is not merely arranging the two premises, but doing so while attending to how they produce the conclusion and to the manner of subsumption. With that qualification, the figures are alike.

### M-I-29 — The objection from knowing that knowledge is knowledge

*Source: p. 111.*

The opponent argues in several ways. First: if knowing that what occurs after investigation is knowledge were necessary, its contrary would not become apparent; if it were theoretical, an infinite regress would follow. We reply: we deny that the contrary becomes apparent after sound investigation. We also deny that knowing it to be knowledge depends upon another investigation: this knowledge arises through the very same investigation, as does knowledge that no countervailing argument exists.

### M-I-30 — Conditions and the obligation to know

*Source: p. 111.*

Second: investigation’s yielding knowledge conflicts with requiring the absence of knowledge as a condition. We reply: this is denied; what is meant is that knowledge follows it. Third: if investigation invariably yielded knowledge, imposing an obligation to know would be improper. We reply: the obligation is to obtain knowledge, and this lies within one’s power.

### M-I-31 — Disagreement and the conception of divine realities

*Source: p. 111.*

Fourth: the thing nearest to a human being is that person’s own identity, yet disagreement about it is beyond counting; how much more so with what is increasingly remote? We reply: this shows difficulty, which is undisputed, not impossibility. Fifth: the condition of assent—conception—is absent in divine realities. We reply: this is denied.

### M-I-32 — A proof and what it indicates

*Source: p. 113.*

Sixth: if inference to the Maker through a proof were sound, what the proof necessitates would be either the Maker’s existence—in which case His nonexistence would follow upon supposing the proof absent—or knowledge of Him, in which case it would not be a proof when no one investigates it. We reply: by its yielding a result and indicating something, we mean only that it is such that whenever it exists, what it indicates exists, and whenever it is investigated, what it indicates is known. The absence of the proof therefore does not entail the absence of what it indicates; nor does the absence of investigation remove its being such as to indicate it.

### M-I-33 — The self-defeat of denying rational investigation

*Source: p. 113.*

Against all these arguments it is objected that, if knowledge that investigation does not yield knowledge is theoretical and acquired through them, contradiction follows; whereas, if it is necessary and they merely direct attention to it, most rational people would not disagree about it. If it is said, “We opposed an unsound argument with an unsound argument,” we reply: if your arguments establish that ours are unsound, the point at issue has been established; otherwise they are idle.

### M-I-34 — Unsound investigation and false belief

*Source: p. 113.*

As for unsound investigation, the correct position is that it does not necessarily entail false belief. Where the form is unsound, this is evident. Where only the matter is unsound, it is because a false proposition may entail a true one: as when someone believes that the world is the effect of a necessitating cause, and that every effect of a necessitating cause is temporally originated. It may, however, produce false belief: as when someone believes that the world is independent and that everything independent is beginningless.

### M-I-35 — Conditions of investigation and multiple proofs

*Source: p. 113.*

Third investigation. Beyond the conditions of knowledge, rational investigation as such requires the absence of firm conviction in what is sought or its contradictory, since with such conviction there is no seeking. Multiple proofs serve to increase assurance, to produce in a learner a readiness to accept through their combination, or to reach different learners through different proofs. The Imām says that what is sought through a second proof is its being a proof, which is not yet known.

### M-I-36 — Proof and ground of indication

*Source: p. 115.*

Sound investigation must concern a proof rather than a specious argument, and must consider it under the ground of its indication, not another aspect. That ground is what enables the mind to move from the proof to what it indicates: for example, the world’s contingency or temporal origination in establishing the Maker. The world is the proof; the Maker’s existence is what it indicates; its being such that investigating it yields knowledge of the Maker’s existence is its indication; and its contingency or temporal origination is the ground of that indication. These are different things, so the cognitions relating to them differ. But the ground of indication is closely connected with what is indicated, which has led to the mistaken supposition that knowing the former is itself knowing the latter.

### M-I-37 — Does knowing God require a teacher

*Source: p. 115.*

Investigation directed toward knowing God Most High does not require the existence of a teacher. Sound investigation has been established to yield knowledge without qualification; the teacher too would need another teacher, producing an infinite regress—unless the ruling is restricted to the investigation of someone other than the teacher, or the series terminates in revelation. Furthermore, knowledge of the teacher’s truthfulness comes either through investigation, which suffices for the point; through the teacher’s own statement, which is circular; or through another teacher, which produces a regress. It may be answered that it comes through investigation accompanied by guidance from the teacher.

### M-I-38 — Teachers and the difficulty of learning

*Source: p. 115.*

The heretics argue from the great disagreement of opinions in divine matters and from the acknowledged need for a teacher even in the easiest sciences and crafts. The answer is that disagreement results from the abundance of unsound investigations. If ‘need’ means that acquisition is impossible without a teacher, this is not conceded; if it means that acquisition is difficult without one, this is undisputed. For guidance toward premises and the resolution of difficulties plainly provide excellent help in acquiring perfections.

### M-I-39 — The obligation to investigate

*Source: p. 117.*

Fourth investigation. There is no dispute about the obligation to investigate in order to know God Most High, since investigation is an attainable prerequisite for knowledge that is obligatory without qualification. In our account the obligation comes through revelation, on the grounds of scriptural text and consensus, since reason’s authority to impose the ruling is set aside, as will be explained. For the Muʿtazila the obligation is rational, because knowledge removes the harm arising from fear of punishment and other things. The reply denies that such fear usually exists, because there is no awareness of it. Even if fear is granted, it remains because error is possible. Nor is the knower’s being better situated true without qualification: lack of understanding may be nearer to deliverance, as with a child or a person bereft of reason.

### M-I-40 — Objections to the obligation and its prerequisites

*Source: p. 117.*

Dispute may arise over: (1) the possibility of imposing an obligation to know, since it would require obtaining what is already obtained or obligating someone who is unaware; (2) consensus on its obligation, since perhaps the scholars accepted uncritical following and reliance on others; (3) investigation’s being its prerequisite, since knowledge might arise through teaching or inspiration; (4) the unconditional character of its obligation, since it is conditioned on doubt or the absence of knowledge; and (5) the obligation of the prerequisite, since the principal end might be made obligatory without attention to its prerequisite.

### M-I-41 — Replies and the meaning of unconditional obligation

*Source: p. 117–119.*

The answer is that there is no unawareness once the address is understood; consensus on the obligation to know is multiply transmitted; and what was accepted was reliance on general proofs. Moreover, some people’s being allowed to omit it does not exclude its being obligatory in general. It is necessarily known that the means of obtaining nonnecessary knowledge require some investigation, the ruling being restricted to the usual case. Unconditional obligation means that it is not conditioned on that prerequisite: compare the obligation of fasting in relation to intention and residence, and of pilgrimage in relation to entering consecration and having the means. Knowledge is a quality; to make it obligatory can mean only making its acquisition obligatory, which certainly includes its means—as cutting the neck is included in imposing an obligation to kill. Had we relied simply on consensus concerning the obligation to investigate, we would have been spared these burdens.

### M-I-42 — Revelation and the binding force of obligation

*Source: p. 119.*

They say: if investigation were obligatory only through revelation, the morally responsible person could say, “I will not investigate until it is obligatory, and it is not obligatory until I investigate,” since the obligation comes through revelation, while establishing revelation requires investigation. The prophet could not compel that person by argument and would thus be silenced. The reply is that the objection applies equally to both sides, since rational obligation too is theoretically known: the person may decline to investigate or listen to the premises offered. Furthermore, the validity of the binding argument depends only upon the actuality of the obligation, not upon knowing it. What depends upon investigation is knowledge of the obligation, not its actuality.

### M-I-43 — The first obligation

*Source: p. 119–121.*

Fifth investigation. They disagree about the first obligation. Some say it is knowledge of God Most High, because that is the principal end; others say it is investigation directed toward that knowledge, or the intention to investigate, because knowledge depends upon it. The correct position is that, if ‘obligation’ is restricted to what is sought for its own sake, the first answer holds; otherwise the second holds. As for the absence of knowledge, it is not within one’s power, or else the obligation is conditional upon it; maintaining that absence is not a prerequisite. Some say the first obligation is doubt, because investigation comes after it. The reply is that doubt is not within one’s power, since, like knowledge, it belongs to the category of quality; nor is it a prerequisite, since investigation can occur with probabilistic judgement or weaker supposition. If ‘doubt’ is intended broadly enough to include both, and is considered within one’s power in the sense that it can be brought about, the obligation to investigate is conditional upon it, since there is no investigation when firm conviction is present. What is obligatory for an uncritical follower, or for someone in compound ignorance, is to investigate the ground of indication so that it leads them to knowledge.

### M-I-44 — Definitions and kinds of inference

*Source: p. 121.*

Sixth investigation. The completion of rational investigation consists in obtaining a method that leads of itself to what is sought. If this is a conception, the method is a defining account (معرّف): an essential definition or a description, each either complete or incomplete, since there must be an essential or accidental distinguishing feature, with or without the proximate genus. If what is sought is an assent, the method is a proof (دليل): either a syllogism—exceptive, whether conditional or disjunctive, or conjunctive, whether categorical or conditional—or induction, complete or incomplete, or analogy, certain or probabilistic. For what is sought must fall under the proof, or conversely the proof under what is sought, or both under a third thing.

### M-I-45 — Proof and indicator

*Source: p. 121.*

‘Proof’ may also mean that from which a judgement can be reached through sound investigation, as the world serves as a proof of the Maker. Frequently the term is restricted to what yields firm conviction, contrasted with an indicator (أمارة).

### M-I-46 — Rational and transmitted proofs

*Source: p. 123.*

If a proof depends upon no transmission at all, it is rational; otherwise it is transmitted, whether all its immediate premises depend upon transmission or not. ‘Transmitted’ may be restricted to the first case, with the second called composite. A purely transmitted proof, however, is untenable, since the reporter’s truthfulness must be established by reason. If reason treats the two alternatives concerning what is sought as equal, it is established through transmission. Otherwise, if establishing the authority of transmission depends upon it, it is established by reason; if not, it may be established through either.

### M-I-47 — When transmitted proof yields certainty

*Source: p. 123.*

That transmitted proof yields probabilistic judgement is evident. Its yielding certainty depends upon knowledge of linguistic convention and intended meaning. This requires that the transmitters of Arabic be free from error, and that such possibilities as a transfer of meaning, equivocation, figurative usage, ellipsis and a rational counterargument be absent. Where a rational counterargument exists, transmission must be interpreted, because it depends upon reason: to discredit reason would be to discredit transmission itself. Yet contextual indications may accompany the transmitted proof, exclude the alternatives, yield certainty about what is sought, and exclude a counterargument, just as occurs in rational matters—for example, “Say: He is God, One,” and “There is no god but God.”

---

<a id="division-ii"></a>

# Division II: General matters

<a id="m-ii-p125"></a>

**Source p. 125**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.01](#c1-01).*

SECOND PRINCIPAL DIVISION: GENERAL MATTERS

These are what extend to most existents: the Necessary Existent, substance and accident. Investigation of nonexistence and impossibility is therefore incidental; necessity is investigated because it is one of the divisions of necessity without qualification, which is comprehensive. These matters are explained in chapters.

CHAPTER ONE: EXISTENCE AND NONEXISTENCE
It contains investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
The conception of existence is necessarily self-evident. Defining it through such expressions as being, establishedness, actuality and thinghood is a verbal explanation. Defining it as what has an established identity, what can be reported and known, what divides into agent and patient, or what divides into beginningless and temporally originated, defines it through something less evident; moreover, these descriptions apply to the existent.

Some have maintained that this judgement [that the conception of existence is self-evident] is acquired, arguing in several ways. First: self-evident assent to the incompatibility of existence and nonexistence depends upon conceiving existence. The reply is that, if self-evidence without qualification is intended, meaning complete independence from acquisition, this is denied—

<a id="m-ii-p127"></a>

**Source p. 127**

indeed, it begs the question. If only the self-evidence of the judgement is intended, it establishes nothing, since that does not entail conceiving the reality itself and does not exclude acquiring its conception.

It cannot be said: although the whole's self-evidence depends upon that of its parts, knowing the whole to be self-evident does not depend upon knowing its parts to be so, but entails that knowledge; hence there is no begging the question. For we reply: the dependence of knowledge of the whole's self-evidence upon knowledge of the part's self-evidence is necessary, just as the whole depends upon its part. The part's self-evidence is part of the whole's self-evidence; knowledge of the whole is either identical with knowledge of the parts or obtained through it.

Second: existence is known and cannot be acquired. It cannot be acquired by essential definition because it is simple. If it were composite, it would consist either of existences, entailing a thing's priority to itself and the part's equality with the whole in quiddity, or of other things. In the latter case, their conjunction must yield something additional that is existence, lest existence consist entirely of what is not existence. But what is additional to a thing is accidental to it, and composition would therefore not be in existence itself. Acquisition through description is excluded by what preceded.

This is countered by the other composites. The resolution is that what results is additional to each part, not to the whole: it is the whole itself. Composition therefore belongs to it, and existence consists entirely of things none of whose parts is existence, as with other composites. Description has already been discussed.

Third: existence is a part of my existence, which is self-evident. The reply is that, if conception is intended, this is denied; if assent is intended, it establishes nothing, as already explained.

<a id="m-ii-p129"></a>

**Source p. 129**

It has also been said that existence cannot be conceived at all.

If it is objected: (1) existence is either identical with quiddity, and so acquired as quiddity is, or an accident, and so intelligible only in dependence upon its subject. Saying that the discussion concerns existence without qualification, or that its subject is quiddity without qualification, does not remove this dependence but increases it. (2) Moreover, were existence self-evident, rational people would not occupy themselves with defining it, (3) disagree about its self-evidence, or argue for that self-evidence.

We reply: (1) an accident may be understood without its subject; even if dependence is conceded, a self-evident quiddity suffices. (2) A self-evident thing may receive a verbal explanation to convey what a word means, not to produce conception of the reality. (3) Assent to the self-evidence of something self-evident may itself be acquired or obscure; it may therefore be disputed and require proof or an indication that brings it to attention.

SECOND INVESTIGATION
Existence is one concept shared by existences, and these are additional to quiddities.

The first point is indicated by certainty of existence alongside hesitation about its specific character, and by the soundness of dividing existence into necessary and other existence independently of linguistic designation and language. If quiddity and individuation are offered as counterexamples, we reply that their unqualified concepts are also shared. Further indications are the exhaustiveness of the division into existent and nonexistent,

<a id="m-ii-p131"></a>

**Source p. 131**

and certainty that the concept of nonexistence is one, even if taken as the removal of a reality, since differentiation arises only through a relation.

The second point is indicated by the soundness of denying existence of quiddities, the informativeness of predicating it of them, the acquired character of establishing it for them, the unity of its concept as against their diversity, and the separability of conceiving it from conceiving them.

The philosophers deny that existence is additional in the Necessary Existent. If it inhered in His quiddity, that quiddity would be both recipient and agent, and would precede existence in existence, because a cause necessarily precedes its effect. Moreover, existence could cease, considering its intrinsic dependence.

The first argument is answered by denying that the consequence is impossible; the last two by denying the entailment. Priority need not be priority in existence, as with three in relation to oddness and a contingent quiddity in relation to its existence. What depends on something may be unable to cease, because it is necessarily required by the quiddity.

If it is objected: what bestows existence necessarily precedes in existence, since unless the intellect considers a thing as existent it cannot conceive it as bestowing existence, unlike the recipient, which must be considered devoid of existence, we reply: this is denied. Bestowing existence here means only requiring existence through one's own essence; necessarily, it does not precede itself in existence.

If it is objected that His existence would then be an effect and hence contingent, we reply: an effect of His own essence, and hence necessary. Necessary existence means precisely that existence is required by the essence.

The philosophers' position is opposed in several ways.

<a id="m-ii-p133"></a>

**Source p. 133**

First: if the Necessary Existent's existence is not conjoined with His quiddity, its separation is either intrinsic, in which case it extends to every existence, or due to something else, in which case the Necessary Existent is dependent.

Second: the principle of contingent beings would then be either existence alone, making a thing the principle of itself and its causes, or existence together with separation. If separation is a constituent, the Necessary Existent is composite; if it is a condition, existence is the principle of everything, with an effect failing to arise because a condition is absent, rather than because of existence itself.

Third: the Necessary Existent shares existence with contingent beings but differs from them in reality; hence reality and existence differ.

Fourth: if the Necessary Existent is mere being, He is multiple; if being together with separation, He is composite; if being conditional upon separation, He is dependent. If He is something other than being, then without being this is impossible; with being, being is additional, since it necessarily cannot be a constituent.

Fifth: existence is necessarily known, unlike the Necessary Existent.

The answer is that there is no dispute about existence without qualification being additional. The dispute concerns particular existence, and these arguments do not establish the claim about it.

If it is objected that existence is a specific nature whose concomitants cannot differ,

<a id="m-ii-p135"></a>

**Source p. 135**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.02](#c1-02).*

we reply: this is denied. Existences differ in reality: what is necessary for some is impossible for others, as with lights. Existence without qualification applies to them as a nonessential concomitant, either univocally or with ordered variation. The latter is correct, because existence belongs to the Necessary Existent more primarily, more intensely and more antecedently. Thus the unqualified concept is additional to particular existences; this does not entail that those existences are additional to their quiddities.

If it is objected that existences would then be disparate, we reply: if this means that they are not predicable of one another, that is not impossible; if it means that they share no concept of being, it does not follow, as with the individuals falling under 'walking'.

The Shaykh maintains that each thing's existence is identical with it and that the sharing is only verbal. For (1) if existence were additional, it would inhere either in what is nonexistent, producing contradiction; in what exists through that very existence, producing circularity; or in what exists through another existence, producing an infinite regress. (2) Moreover, existence would be either nonexistent, and thus qualified by its contradictory, with something having no actuality becoming actual in a subject; or existent, producing an infinite regress.

The first argument is answered by saying that existence inheres in quiddity considered as such. If it is objected that it would then inhere in nonexistence, an even more evident contradiction, we reply: rather, in what is considered without taking either existence or nonexistence into account, although it is inseparable from one of them.

<a id="m-ii-p137"></a>

**Source p. 137**

If it is objected that quiddity will therefore accompany one of the two and the difficulty returns, we reply: inherence here is intellectual. Quiddity's occurrence in the intellect without considering existence suffices; if existence is considered, a regress among intellectual considerations is not impossible.

The second argument is answered by saying that the existence of existence is identical with existence itself; the dispute concerns other things. The precise point is that things become actual through existence, whereas existence is actual through itself, as time is in relation to priority and posteriority. Moreover, there is no impossibility in its being nonexistent.

If you say: clearly the concept of existence is not the concept of human being, for example. Nor is 'existence', with its synonyms in all languages, equivocally assigned to meanings that are virtually innumerable. Both sides' arguments show that the dispute concerns existence as being, and does not arise because 'existence' can mean an essence as well as being. Besides, the concept of essence is itself shared. How, then, does this disagreement arise?

I reply: the majority's arguments establish that the concept of existence is not the concept of the quiddity qualified by it. The Shaykh's arguments establish that they are not two distinct identities, one inhering in the other, like a body and whiteness. There is therefore no disagreement that existence is additional mentally, in the sense that the intellect can consider quiddity without existence and conversely; but not concretely, as though quiddity had one actuality and its accident called existence another, the two coming together as recipient and received, like body and whiteness. Once the issue is precisely formulated, no dispute remains, and treating the sharing as merely verbal proves obstinate.

<a id="m-ii-p139"></a>

**Source p. 139**

The only consequence for mental existence is that its proponent may say 'additional in the intellect', whereas its denier must say 'intellectually' or 'in intellection'. The latter cannot deny intellectual difference and conceptual sharing, any more than with other universal concepts, especially negated ones. For those scholars do not take intellection to entail establishedness. Hence most of them commonly say that existence is conceptually shared and mentally additional.

This concerns contingent beings. As for the Necessary Existent, we hold that He has a reality to which His particular existence is mentally additional, as in contingent beings. The philosophers hold that His reality is particular existence subsisting through itself, different in reality from other existences, described as pure existence and existence on condition of the exclusion of anything else. For quiddity together with existence bears a trace of composition and dependence. Particular existence together with existence without qualification does not: it is a particular being actual through itself, subsistent through itself, and independent for its actuality of existence without qualification or anything else. The unqualified concept applies to it only as an extrinsic, nonconstitutive concomitant. This cannot be conceived of anything other than existence, since everything else necessarily depends upon existence for actuality.

This account rests upon existences being specified and multiplied through themselves, while sharing an accident: the concept of being. Compare sunlight and lamplight, or the whiteness of snow and ivory. Because these existences have no particular names, it was imagined that their specification and multiplicity arise merely through relation to their subjects, as with the whitenesses of different pieces of snow.

If you say: were existence without qualification accidental to them, each would contain a share of the concept of being, as with general accidents. That share would then be additional to what constitutes the Necessary Existent's reality, just as in contingent beings,

<a id="m-ii-p141"></a>

**Source p. 141**

entailing two existences in Him, accident and subject, and two existences plus a quiddity in a contingent being. On this account snow would contain two whitenesses, which both intellect and sense contradict.

I reply: they do not dispute that the share of the concept of being is additional to the particular existence that is the Necessary Existent's reality. For the concept of being differs from its shares only through consideration of a relation. Their dispute concerns His having a quiddity to which particular existence is additional in the intellect.

Once ordered variation is affirmed, existences necessarily differ from shares of the concept of being—but intellectually, not externally. For subject and predicate are identical in concrete identity, as established. Moreover, shares of a general concept are intellectual forms having no actuality in external things. Hence neither two existences in a contingent being nor two whitenesses in a white thing follow.

Some philosophisers and Sufis imagined that particular existence together with existence without qualification also bears a trace of composition and dependence. They therefore maintained that the Necessary Existent's reality is existence without qualification, which is not a universal meaning multiplied into particulars, but one individual existing through an existence identical with itself. Multiplicity among existents arises only through relations. 'The Necessary Existent exists' means that He is existence; 'the contingent exists' means that it possesses existence, in the sense that it has a relation to the Necessary Existent. They claimed that the philosophers' statement 'He is pure existence, on condition of the exclusion of anything else' alludes to this, as does their statement 'Existence is pure good; no contrary, counterpart, genus or differentia can be conceived for it.'

<a id="m-ii-p143"></a>

**Source p. 143**

You know that this conflicts with their explicit statements that existence is an intellectual predicate, because it cannot be independent of a subject or occur in it externally; that it is a second intelligible, because among external things there is nothing that is existence itself, but rather a human being or blackness, for example; that it divides into necessary and contingent, beginningless and temporally originated; that it multiplies with individual, specific and generic subjects; and that it is predicated of existences with ordered variation. The ways in which this opinion is false, in both principles and consequences, are too evident to be hidden and too numerous to count.

How astonishing is the case of existence! They unanimously declared it self-evident, with nothing better known, then disagreed whether it is particular or universal; necessary or contingent; an accident or neither accident nor substance; existent, merely considered without external actuality, or intermediate; whether its individuals are identical with quiddities or additional to them; and whether its term is equivocal, univocal, or predicated with ordered variation. God is the guide.

THIRD INVESTIGATION
Existence descends through the concrete, mental, spoken and written levels. The first is original: what exists through it is the thing's reality. The second is non-original, like a body's shadow: what exists through it is the thing's form. The last two are figurative: what exists through them is the thing's name and the form of its name. Each subsequent level indicates the preceding one. The first indication is intellectual, and its two terms do not vary. The last two are conventional: in the former only the sign varies; in the latter both terms vary.

<a id="m-ii-p145"></a>

**Source p. 145**

Mental existence is established by our making affirmative judgements about things with no external establishedness, such as impossibilities, while affirmation of what has no establishedness is impossible; by our finding concepts that are universal and therefore, precisely because of their universality, impossible externally; and by real propositions whose judgement is not restricted to what exists externally.

It is objected that affirmative judgement requires only the subject's being distinguishable to the intellect, which is what intellection means. Everything therefore returns to the claim that understanding and intellection require establishedness in the intellect; and that is disputed.

The answer is that intellection and distinguishability necessarily require a relation between intellect and intelligible. A relation to sheer negation cannot be conceived: some establishedness is required. Since it is not external, it is in the intellect.

If it is objected that the intelligible may subsist through itself, like Plato's separate Forms or others' suspended forms, or in an immaterial being, like the forms of generated beings in the active intellect according to the philosophers, we reply: it is necessarily known that an impossible thing—indeed, a nonexistent thing, especially one not belonging to the class of essences—does not subsist through itself or in an immaterial being in its own identity, but through its form. This establishes the claim because it entails that intellection occurs through the form's presence. It does not establish it merely by entailing a kind of distinguishability other than external individual distinction, whether the intellect invents it or observes it in another subject; for distinguishability's requiring establishedness in the intellect is precisely the point at issue.

Those who deny mental existence argue that the mind's being qualified by heat and cold, and the heavens' occurring within it, are self-evidently impossible. They also argue that, if something having no external actuality existed in the mind, it would exist externally, because what exists in something that exists in a thing itself exists in that thing.

<a id="m-ii-p147"></a>

**Source p. 147**

The reply is that these consequences concern original existence. What is hot is that in which heat itself inheres, not its form. What is impossible is the occurrence in the mind of the heavens themselves, not their forms. What occurs in the mind is the nonexistent thing's form; what occurs externally is the mind itself. This differs from water's presence in a jug and the jug's presence in a house.

FOURTH INVESTIGATION
Existence is synonymous with establishedness and coextensive with thinghood; nonexistence is synonymous with negation. Thus the nonexistent is not established, and there is no intermediary between it and the existent. Both claims have been disputed, separately and together.

Some say that the known either has no establishedness, and is nonexistent; has establishedness in respect of itself, and is existent; or has establishedness dependent upon another, and is a state. A state is therefore an attribute of an existent that is neither existent nor nonexistent; an intermediary is thus affirmed.

Most Muʿtazilites say: what has being in external things is existent; otherwise it is nonexistent. What has actuality in itself is established; otherwise it is negated. The existent is narrower than the established, and the negated narrower than the nonexistent. A nonexistent may therefore be established, but there is no intermediary between it and the existent.

Some of them say: what has external being either has it independently and is existent, or dependently and is a state. Otherwise it is nonexistent: either actual in itself and thus established, or not and thus negated. On this account a nonexistent is established and there is an intermediary between it and the existent.

<a id="m-ii-p149"></a>

**Source p. 149**

Our argument in both matters is necessary awareness. Nothing is understood by establishedness except existence, mentally or externally, or by nonexistence except the negation of that; no intermediary between them is conceivable. Arguments have also been offered:

First: the establishedness of the nonexistent conflicts with its being an object of power, since the essence is beginningless and existence is a state to which power does not relate. Second: nonexistence is an attribute of negation, so what is qualified by it is negated. Third: according to you the establishedness of essences does not derive from another, entailing a plurality of necessary beings. Fourth: those essences are infinite, whereas the existent ones among them are finite. The total therefore exceeds those remaining nonexistent by a finite quantity, and must itself be finite. Fifth: if the nonexistent is coextensive with the negated or narrower, it cannot be established. If broader, it cannot be sheer negation, since otherwise general and specific would not differ; it must therefore be established. But it applies to the negated, entailing the latter's establishedness, which is impossible.

The first is answered by allowing that the essence's qualification by existence arises temporally through power. If it is objected that this qualification is negated, since otherwise an infinite regress and the qualification of a nonexistent by existence follow, the answer denies the impossibility of a regress in established things and of the established being qualified by existence.

<a id="m-ii-p151"></a>

**Source p. 151**

The second is answered by denying the first premise if 'an attribute that is negation' is meant, and the second if 'a negated attribute' is meant. The third: the necessary is what is independent of another for its existence, not its establishedness. The fourth: exceeding another by a finite quantity does not entail finitude unless the other is finite. Establishing that by correspondence between it and the total is weak. The fifth: not being sheer negation does not entail being sheer affirmation; it may be one in some cases and the other in others. Only 'some nonexistent is established' follows, not the establishedness of the negated.

If it is said that the intended argument is that what is broader must be distinguished from what is narrower and hence, by the opponent's admission, established, we reply: most of the premises then become redundant.

The opponent argues as follows. First: the nonexistent is distinguishable, since it is known, willed and subject to power. Everything distinguishable is established, because distinguishability involves intellectual pointing, and pointing to sheer negation is impossible. Second: the nonexistent is contingent; everything contingent is established, since contingency is positive. We reply: distinguishability and contingency are intellectual; the distinguishable and contingent thing's establishedness in the mind suffices. Were external establishedness required, impossible things would be established because distinguishable, as would imaginary composites because distinguishable and contingent.

Third: the establishedness of the nonexistent means that nonexistent blackness, for example, is blackness in itself. If this depended upon another, it would disappear when that other disappeared, so existent blackness would then no longer remain blackness.

<a id="m-ii-p153"></a>

**Source p. 153**

We reply: this is denied, since its existence disappears just as its being blackness does. If it is objected that blackness would then not be blackness, which is impossible, we reply: if this is a negative judgement, its impossibility is denied; if it is an affirmative judgement with a negative predicate, so that blackness's quiddity is established while being non-blackness, the entailment is denied.

If it is objected that blackness is blackness even if the other does not exist, since every thing necessarily has a quiddity through which it is itself, independently of considering anything else, we reply: disregarding something does not entail its absence.

This concerns thinghood as external establishedness. Whether 'thing' names the existent, the known, what is not impossible, the beginningless, the temporally originated, or something else is a linguistic question, determined by transmitted linguistic evidence and usage.

Those who affirm states argue in several ways. First: existence is not existent, otherwise a regress follows; nor nonexistent, otherwise it is qualified by its contradictory. Saying that the division does not apply to it concedes an intermediary. We reply: existence is existent and its existence is itself; alternatively, it is nonexistent. Qualification by the contradictory would follow only if existence were nonexistence or the existent nonexistent.

Second: a universal is not existent, since then it would be individuated; nor nonexistent, since then it could not be part of an existent. The same applies to every genus or differentia in relation to its species. Moreover, were it existent, an accident would inhere in an accident in the case of accidents.

<a id="m-ii-p155"></a>

**Source p. 155**

We reply: there is no such external composition. There is not one thing that is humanity and another that is Zayd's particularity. Nor is there in blackness one thing that is colour, another that contracts sight, and a third compounded from both, with one inhering in another. Moreover, this sort of inherence has nothing to do with an accident's inherence in a subject. Distinction is mental, and universal and genus are established there. Error would arise only if they were taken mentally as forms of externally distinct things.

Both arguments are countered by states themselves. States resemble one another in establishedness but differ in particular features, making their establishedness additional and producing a regress. They are predicated of their particulars: if established they are individuated; otherwise negated. They are also predicated of states that are accidents, making an accident inhere in an accident.

If it is said that states admit neither resemblance nor difference, so that their establishedness is not additional and no regress follows, and one cannot be universal and a state while another is particular and its subject—and furthermore that regress is impossible only among existents, not established things—we reply: two concepts' admitting resemblance or difference is necessary. The impossibility of regress will be discussed later.

Third: bringing into existence is not existent, otherwise it requires another bringing into existence and a regress follows; nor nonexistent, otherwise the agent would not bring anything into existence. We reply: the absence of the principle underlying a predicate does not entail the failure of predication. Zayd is blind although blindness is nonexistent; the conjunction of contraries is impossible although impossibility is nonexistent.

They derive further theses from these two principles. They agree, for example, that essences actual in nonexistence are infinite, that no agent affects them, and that there is no spatial separation among them; and that one may be certain that the world has a maker

<a id="m-ii-p157"></a>

**Source p. 157**

qualified by power, knowledge and life, while doubting His existence. They disagree whether nonexistent essences bear their generic attributes, such as being blackness, and what accompanies these in existence, such as inherence in a subject; whether occupying space differs from substantiality; whether a nonexistent has an attribute by being nonexistent; and whether it can be qualified by corporeality.

They also divide states into those explained by an existent attribute, such as being a knower explained by knowledge, and those without such an explanation, such as blackness's being a colour; they explain the difference between essences through states, and so forth.

If you ask: the falsity of the nonexistent's establishedness and of an intermediary is perfectly evident; how did so many rational people miss it? I reply: the first thesis rested upon the claim that nonexistent blackness, for example, is externally blackness, with its being blackness independent of the causes of existence. The second rested upon the claim that some attributes are proved not to be existent, yet cannot be denied, since an existent is qualified by them whether or not the intellect makes a supposition: existence, bringing into existence, being a knower and being a colour. They therefore judged them neither existent nor nonexistent.

Explaining this by saying that they treated the opposition of nonexistence and existence as privation and possession rather than negation and affirmation—nonexistence being the absence of what is capable of existence—and thus called merely considered concepts incapable of existence neither existent nor nonexistent, is sound only if the impossible is not classified as nonexistent.

FIFTH INVESTIGATION
Nonexistences are distinguished intellectually: the nonexistence of an effect is specifically dependent upon the nonexistence of its cause; the nonexistence of a condition excludes the conditioned thing's existence; and the absence of a contrary permits the other's existence.

<a id="m-ii-p159"></a>

**Source p. 159**

Nonexistence may occur to itself: it is conceived, then overlooked. It is thus a kind of nonexistence in one respect and opposed to it in another. Similarly, the absolutely nonexistent is established in one respect, making judgement about it possible, and is a coordinate division opposed to the established in another, making such judgement impossible.
