If it is objected: insofar as it is not established, judgement about it is impossible—but this itself is a judgement—we reply: yes, but made insofar as it is established. There is no contradiction because the considerations differ. The same applies to judging that judgement about the absolutely unknown or inconceivable is impossible.

Generally, there is no restriction on the intellect's operations. It may consider contradictories and judge their contradiction; consider the nonexistence of anything, even itself; divide the existent into what is and is not established in the mind, or into conceivable and inconceivable; and judge their difference. Thus what is unestablished and what is inconceivable each lacks an identity in itself while having an identity through mental establishedness, as with identity and nonidentity.

SIXTH INVESTIGATION
Existence and nonexistence may each function as predicate or copula. Affirmative predication requires identity of the two terms in concrete identity for its correctness and conceptual difference for its informativeness. Predicating existence or nonexistence of a quiddity does not require taking either into account within that quiddity, thereby making the predication redundant or contradictory,

<a id="m-ii-p161"></a>

**Source p. 161**

just as predicating 'black' of a body does not involve considering blackness or its absence within the body; that consideration comes from the predicate. The same applies to mental establishedness, even though it is inseparable.

Correct judgement does not require correspondence to what is in external things, since its two terms may not occur there. Correspondence to what is in minds is insufficient, since falsehoods may be represented there. What matters is correspondence to the matter as it is in itself (nafs al-amr): what is understood when we say 'this matter is thus in itself', that is, intrinsically, independently of the judge's judgement. Explaining it as what is in the active intellect is very remote if the aim is to explain the concept, since assent can occur in someone who does not know of the active intellect, or even denies it. The objections that this account excludes knowledge of the active intellect itself, knowledge prior to it, and knowledge of particulars can, however, be answered.

CHAPTER TWO: QUIDDITY
It contains investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
A thing's quiddity is what answers the question 'What is it?' It is also explained as that through which the thing is itself. The agent is not a counterexample, since the agent accounts for the thing's existence, not for what it is. Considered in respect of actuality, quiddity is called essence and reality; considered in respect of individuation, identity.

<a id="m-ii-p163"></a>

**Source p. 163**

Quiddity differs from its inseparable and separable accidents and is opposed to them in accordance with their opposition. Thus, when one says 'four, as such, is even' or 'is not odd', one means that this follows from the quiddity. Otherwise, quiddity considered as such is nothing but itself. If asked 'Is four, as such, even or not even?' or 'Is it even or odd?', we reply: it is not, as such, even or odd, meaning that neither is identical with it or included within it. It is not correct to say 'as such it is even', 'as such it is not odd', or 'as such it is neither'.

SECOND INVESTIGATION
Quiddity may be taken on condition of something, and is called mixed; its existence is evident. It may be taken on condition of nothing, and is called abstracted. This does not exist in minds, much less externally, even if the excluded concomitants are restricted to external ones. For being in the mind is something that accrues to it in itself, even if the intellect does not conceive that being or treat it as an attribute. The intellect's considering quiddity alone or considering it abstracted from everything else, even mental being, does not make it abstracted. If mere intellectual consideration sufficed, it could also exist externally by considering quiddity conjoined with individuators in that way.

The Forms attributed to Plato do not affirm the existence of abstracted quiddity, but the existence of species in God's knowledge, or a separate substance for each species that governs its affairs as a soul governs a body.

<a id="m-ii-p165"></a>

**Source p. 165**

Quiddity may also be taken without condition of anything. It is broader than mixed quiddity and exists because externally it is identical with mixed quiddity, not a part of it. There is no external distinction here, much less a part–whole relation; that belongs only to the mind.

If it is objected that what is taken without condition is the natural universal and cannot exist externally, since external existence necessarily entails individuation, incompatible with universality, we reply: no. The natural universal is what is taken on condition of being the subject of universality. Saying that it exists means that its subject—what is taken without condition—exists when individuation accrues to it. The upshot is that what it is truly predicated of exists.

Ibn Sīnā says that quiddity may be taken on condition of nothing in the sense that everything accompanying it is additional to it. It then forms matter for the aggregate, precedes it in both modes of existence, and cannot be predicated of it, since subject and predicate must share existence. It may instead be taken without this condition, allowing something else either to accompany it or not. If it is then indeterminate, capable of predication of things differing in reality, and not determinate through itself but through an addition that makes it one of those different things, it is a genus and the addition a differentia.

<a id="m-ii-p167"></a>

**Source p. 167**

If it is determinate through itself or through what has been added, it is a species. Thus animal, on condition that rational is not included in it, is matter for human being and a part not predicable of it. On condition that rational is included, it is a species, identical with human being itself. Without either condition it is the genus of human being and predicable of it, and therefore not a part of it. It is called a part because it forms part of its definition: the intellect must consider it in obtaining the form of human being. Externally, however, it is posterior, since until human being exists, something embracing both it and others cannot be conceived for it.

THIRD INVESTIGATION
Necessary awareness affirms the existence of composite quiddities, and these must terminate in simple ones. Composition is indicated by sharing an essential constituent while differing in another essential constituent or in a concomitant of quiddity, not merely by sharing or differing in an essential constituent.

Composition and simplicity may also be considered relatively. The two senses of 'simple' then overlap without either including the other. The two senses of 'composite' are coextensive if actually considering the relation is not required in the relative sense; if it is required, one includes the other. The claim that absolute simplicity is strictly narrower than relative simplicity, and composition the reverse, is false.

<a id="m-ii-p169"></a>

**Source p. 169**

A part must be prior mentally and externally. Consequently, assent concerning it requires no middle term, and its establishedness no intermediary. The first characteristic is intrinsic; the latter two are relative.

Composition may be real, entailing some parts' dependence upon others: the form of a composite constituted through its material parts; a genus, which is indeterminate and becomes an actual species only through conjunction with a differentia—this is the meaning of the differentia's causality; otherwise genus and differentia, indeed species and individual, are not externally distinct, since Zayd is human, animal and rational, while distinction is intellectual, multiple forms of one thing arising under different considerations; and matter and form, each dependent upon the other in a particular respect.

Composition may instead be a matter of consideration, as with an army, and such dependence does not follow. Parts may overlap, being predicable of the same things coextensively, by strict inclusion or by partial overlap. They may also be disparate: alike or different; positive, negative or mixed; intrinsic, relational or a mixture of both.

<a id="m-ii-p171"></a>

**Source p. 171**

FOURTH INVESTIGATION
Quiddities are made, contrary to most philosophers and Muʿtazilites, who deny this without qualification, and to some who deny it for simple quiddities. Our arguments are as follows.

First: the ground of dependence is contingency, an attribute of quiddity, whether composite or simple, relative to its existence. Second: causal influence can be understood only as establishing the quiddity, meaning its becoming that quiddity externally; the establishment of being follows. For if the effect were already established in its entirety when it acquired existence, the agent would have no influence. Externally, no difference between the aggregate of existences and the existence of the aggregate is intelligible. Third: the quiddity's establishedness is not through itself, and must therefore be through the agent. All three are answered by saying that they amount to existence's being made. Fourth: what is made is either quiddity, existence, quiddity's qualification by existence, or the conjunction of parts; all are quiddities. This is answered by saying that what is made is particular existence, not the quiddity of existence.

They say: if a human being's humanity came from the agent, it would not be human when the agent was absent. We reply: what follows is negation; what is impossible is an affirmative negative predication.

If it is asked: clearly there is not one influence upon the contingent quiddity and another upon its existence, and it has no external establishedness without its agent; what, then, explains this disagreement?

<a id="m-ii-p173"></a>

**Source p. 173**

The answer is that 'being made' may mean dependence upon an agent, which accompanies existence, like a body's finitude, rather than quiddity, like four's evenness. It may instead mean dependence upon another, in which case it accompanies the quiddity specifically of a composite. Whoever affirms made-ness without qualification means its accruing to quiddity in some respect; whoever denies it means that dependence upon an agent is not an accident of quiddity. Whoever distinguishes composites from simples means that dependence upon another accompanies composite quiddity but not simple quiddity, although both depend upon an agent for existence.

CHAPTER THREE: CONCOMITANTS OF EXISTENCE AND QUIDDITY
Let us arrange this in approaches.

FIRST APPROACH: INDIVIDUATION
It contains investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
Individuation differs from quiddity, existence and unity, since these apply to a universal whereas individuation does not. It need not involve considering something shared, unlike differentiation. The two coincide when a shared feature of two individuals is considered, and diverge when such sharing is not considered or the differentiated thing is universal. Their extensions therefore overlap without either encompassing the other.

<a id="m-ii-p175"></a>

**Source p. 175**

SECOND INVESTIGATION
Individuation is a matter of intellectual consideration, for two reasons. First: if it existed, it would have an individuation, producing a regress. If it is objected that what requires differentiation through individuation is sharing a quiddity, whereas individuations share only a name or an accident, we reply: every individuation has a quiddity for the intellect, whether or not that quiddity has multiple instances. If it exists externally, individuation necessarily follows. If it is objected that its individuation is itself, we reply: it is then a matter of consideration, since subject and attribute necessarily differ in external realities.

Second: if individuation existed, its conjunction with an individual's share of the species would depend upon that share's being distinguished, producing circularity or regress. If it is objected that, when quiddity exists, it exists as particular and as the subject of individuation—not that each first becomes actual and they then are conjoined, requiring a prior distinction—we reply: the subject's priority in existence, accompanied by distinction, is necessary. This argument is open to question.

The opponent argues in several ways. First: individuation is part of the individuated, which exists.

<a id="m-ii-p177"></a>

**Source p. 177**

We reply: what exists is the subject of individuation, not the composite of subject and attribute; that composite is a matter of consideration. If it is objected that the individuated is the individual, Zayd for example, whose existence is evident, and whose concept is not mere human being but human being with something else called individuation, making individuation a part of existent Zayd and hence existent, the answer is that Zayd is the human being qualified by individuating accidents, not the aggregate. Even if the aggregate is conceded, the additional things are individuators—particular quantity, quality, place and the like—whose existence is necessary. The question concerns individuation itself.

Second: one specific nature does not multiply through itself but through something added to it; that is what individuation means. Third: if individuation were negative, it would not be individuated in itself and could not individuate another. We reply: this is not the disputed point.

Fourth: if negative, individuation would be the absence either of non-individuation without qualification or of another individuation, itself negative or positive. It would thus be positive, since removing a negative is positive and similar cases share a ruling. We reply, even conceding that the negative is the absence of something and its contradictory positive: if the concepts of non-individuation and individuation are meant, the division is not exhaustive; if their instances are meant, what falls under non-individuation need not itself be negative.

<a id="m-ii-p179"></a>

**Source p. 179**

Fifth: if individuation were negative, it would be the absence of what conflicts with it. If the absence of unrestrictedness, or of something coextensive with unrestrictedness, it would be shared by individuals like absence of unrestrictedness, and so would not distinguish them. Otherwise, it could separate from absence of unrestrictedness: either that absence occurs without individuation, making a thing neither unrestricted nor individuated, or the reverse occurs, making it both unrestricted and individuated.

We reply: if individuation without qualification is meant, its being shared by individuals is not impossible, their distinction arising through particular individuations. If a particular individuation is meant, a thing's being neither unrestricted nor individuated by that individuation is not impossible: it may have another individuation.

CONCLUSION
Individuals of a species differ only through particular accidents, which may terminate in what yields thisness. 'Negative' may mean nonexistent, the absence of something, or what includes absence in its concept; 'positive' is its counterpart. 'Real' means established in the matter itself without an admixture of supposition or estimation; 'considerational' is its counterpart. Once the intended meanings of positive and negative are specified, and it is determined whether individuation is those accidents, the thisness arising with them, the individual's being incapable of sharing, or its not admitting sharing, the truth is evident.

THIRD INVESTIGATION
Individuation requires that sharing be intellectually impossible. It is not achieved by adding universal to universal, even if the combination excludes external sharing. According to us, it derives from the will of an agent possessing power and choice;

<a id="m-ii-p181"></a>

**Source p. 181**

according to some, from external existence, since individuation certainly occurs with it and individuals multiply with existences. The reply is that concomitant variation does not establish causality; even if it did, the question concerns the particular individuations.

According to the philosophers, individuation derives either from quiddity itself, which is then confined to one individual, or from matter individuated through accidents accruing according to successive dispositions. Individuals multiply with pieces of matter, which are intrinsically capable of multiplicity. It is objected that accidents are individuated through matter's individuation, so individuating matter through them is circular. The answer is that matter is individuated through the accidents, not their individuations. We reply: then let quiddity be individuated through its distinctive attributes and individuals multiply through their multiplicity.

SECOND APPROACH: NECESSITY, IMPOSSIBILITY AND CONTINGENCY
It contains investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
These are intelligibles arising from relating a concept to its simple or composite 'whether it is': predicating existence, or connecting terms through it, may be necessary, impossible or contingent. Their conceptions are necessary. Defining them as 'necessity of existence', 'necessity of nonexistence', and 'the non-necessity of both' is a verbal explanation.

<a id="m-ii-p183"></a>

**Source p. 183**

SECOND INVESTIGATION
Necessity and impossibility are each intrinsic when considered with respect to a thing's essence; otherwise they are extrinsic: causal, conditional upon an attribute, temporal, or otherwise. If existence is the predicate, what is qualified by intrinsic necessity or impossibility is necessary or impossible in existence through itself. If existence is the copula, the predicate is necessarily or impossibly existent for the subject in view of that subject's essence. Thus a quiddity's concomitant, such as four's evenness, necessarily belongs to it through its essence; it is not itself a necessary existent through itself.

Contingency is intrinsic only. It may be taken as the negation of necessity of existence or of nonexistence, encompassing strict contingency and the necessity of the opposite alternative. The impossible may thus be called possible in nonexistence, and the necessary possible in existence. It may mistakenly be understood as negating necessity of one or the other alternative indeterminately, which would encompass everything.

Possibility may be considered with reference to the future. Someone who requires present nonexistence apparently intends the possibility of existence supervening in the future. For the possibility of nonexistence, present existence is then required; no conjunction of contradictories follows.

<a id="m-ii-p185"></a>

**Source p. 185**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.03](#c1-03).*

Possibility may also mean matter's readiness for a thing to occur, in view of the fulfilment of conditions. It varies in strength and weakness and is called dispositional possibility.

Contingency accrues to a concept considered as such in relation to existence. When existence or nonexistence is taken into account, extrinsic necessity or impossibility accrues. Contingency is thus separable from them in conception, not in actuality.

Extrinsic necessity and impossibility share the name 'necessity.' When their related terms are opposed, they coincide in application. When their related term is the same, they exclude each other: they cannot coincide, though transition between them is possible. Intrinsic necessity and impossibility likewise cannot coincide, but transition is impossible. The same incompatibility holds between intrinsic and extrinsic necessity and impossibility, since extrinsic modality entails contingency, incompatible with intrinsic necessity or impossibility. The argument that an intrinsic modality, were it due to another, would cease when the other ceased, has a denied entailment. Between contingency and intrinsic necessity or impossibility there is a genuine exclusive disjunction, and transition is impossible.

If it is objected that a temporally originated thing is impossible in beginninglessness and then becomes possible, and that being subject to power is possible before existence but impossible afterward,

<a id="m-ii-p187"></a>

**Source p. 187**

we reply: beginninglessness of possibility differs from possibility of beginninglessness. A temporally originated thing is possible beginninglessly and endlessly, whereas a thing temporally originated in beginninglessness is always impossible. Its inability to be made by power after already existing is extrinsic, not intrinsic.

THIRD INVESTIGATION
When existence is the copula, the three modalities are in themselves the matters of propositions; insofar as conceived or spoken, they are their expressed modalities. If the predicate is itself one of the three, or existence or nonexistence—as in 'the Creator Most High is necessary' or 'exists', 'the conjunction of contradictories is impossible' or 'nonexistent', and 'human being is contingent' or 'existent'—the considerations multiply. The three intrinsic modalities are necessarily related to their subjects, while the two extrinsic modalities are contingently related to theirs. Everything whose existence belongs contingently to another is contingent in existence in itself; the converse does not follow.

FOURTH INVESTIGATION
Every concept by which any supposed instance of it is itself qualified—necessity, beginninglessness, unity and their opposites, individuation, persistence and being qualified—is considerational. If it existed, a regress would follow. An existent attribute predicated derivatively of a thing cannot be identical with that thing; such identity occurs only among intellectual considerations. A thing's being necessary externally thus means that, when the intellect considers it in relation to existence, an intelligible called necessity follows in the intellect. The same applies to the rest.

<a id="m-ii-p189"></a>

**Source p. 189**

It is also argued that, if necessity and contingency existed, impossibilities would follow. First: they would not apply to nonexistence. Second: the necessary would be contingent. The attribute, depending upon its subject, is contingent; what is contingent can cease in itself. Moreover, if that through which a thing is necessary is contingent, the thing itself is all the more so. Third: a thing would precede itself or a regress would follow, because what necessitates must precede in necessity. Fourth: a contingent thing's existence would precede its contingency, because a subject precedes its accident. Fifth: an existent attribute would inhere in a nonexistent or in something other than what it qualifies. Intrinsic contingency precedes the thing's existence and requires a subject. Sixth: modality would change. Contingency is a relation between a contingent thing and its existence; if existent, it would be posterior to both, so before it the thing would be necessary or impossible. Most of these arguments admit dispute.

The opponent argues that, if necessity and contingency were negative, impossibilities would follow. First: nonexistence would confirm existence and require its stability, since necessity does so.

<a id="m-ii-p191"></a>

**Source p. 191**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.04](#c1-04).*

We reply: it is an intellectual consideration, not sheer nonexistence. Second: both contradictories would be absent, since non-necessity and non-contingency are negative, applying as they do to the impossible. We reply: what applies to a nonexistent can be positive in some instances. Even if this is conceded, contradictories may both be negative, as with impossibility and non-impossibility, blindness and non-blindness. For simple terms, the absence of both contradictories means that neither applies to a thing, not that both lack existence and establishedness, as in propositions. Similarly, equality, inclusion, specificity and disparity concern applicability to things in simple terms, but intrinsic establishedness in propositions.

Third: contingency would be denied of the contingent and necessity of the necessary when there is no intellectual supposition—indeed, absolutely, since 'its contingency is nonexistent' means 'it has no contingency', and likewise with necessity. We reply: this is denied. A predicate may be negative while its predication is necessary, as with the nonexistent and impossible. Contingency is negative, while Zayd is necessarily contingent, meaning that relating him intellectually to existence yields the intelligible contingency. 'Its contingency is nonexistent' means that the applicable attribute is negative; 'it has no contingency' means that the attribute does not apply. Likewise with necessity.

If it is objected that something's being established for another presupposes its own establishedness, we reply: this is denied when establishedness means true predication, since many attributes are negative.

<a id="m-ii-p193"></a>

**Source p. 193**

FIFTH INVESTIGATION
Necessary awareness judges that a contingent thing requires an agent and that neither alternative can prevail without a determining ground. A judgement's obscurity owing to obscurity in its conception does not impugn this.

Arguments that one alternative's occurring without a cause would imply its preponderance, contradicting their equality, and that preponderance must precede existence and be a positive feature inhering in the agent because the effect is posterior, are weak.

Among the strongest objections of the deniers are: (1) causal influence during existence produces what already exists, while influence during nonexistence conjoins contradictories; (2) necessary awareness affirms preponderance without a determining ground, as when someone fleeing a beast takes one of two equal roads or a thirsty person drinks one of two equal waters; (3) nonexistence is sheer negation and cannot be an effect.

The answer to the first is that the impossible case is producing something whose existence has already been obtained through another act of production. That does not follow. At most, existence and its production coincide temporally, which does not exclude essential posteriority.

The second: what follows, if the example is conceded, is a choosing agent's preferring one of two equals without a further specifying ground, not an alternative's prevailing without a cause. If it is said that this choice and preference occurred without a cause, we reply: no; it occurred through will, whose function is preference and specification.

<a id="m-ii-p195"></a>

**Source p. 195**

The third objection is answered by distinguishing a relative nonexistence dependent upon the absence of a cause: the intellect judges that the thing is absent because its cause is absent.

As for arguments that causality, being the contradictory of non-causality, must be positive, as must its subject; that influence must concern quiddity, existence or qualification, all allegedly impossible for reasons already given; and that existent efficacy or dependence would produce a regress—their weakness is evident.

SIXTH INVESTIGATION
The intellect judges dependence merely by considering that an essence requires neither existence nor nonexistence. What makes it dependent is therefore contingency, not temporal origination, whether independently, as a constituent or as a condition. Indeed, temporal origination is an attribute of existence, which follows being affected, which follows dependence.

Many theologians reverse the claim, argument and refutation. They say that the intellect judges dependence merely by considering that a thing was not and then was. Dependence therefore arises from temporal origination, not contingency, which is a mode of the quiddity's relation to existence, itself posterior to dependence. Answering that contingency does not first become actual and then necessitate dependence, but that the intellect considers contingency and judges dependence—as one says that spatial occupancy is the reason for dependence upon a place—is precisely the answer available to them.

<a id="m-ii-p197"></a>

**Source p. 197**

The objection that there is no dependence during persistence—for influence upon existence then obtains what has already been obtained, while influence upon persistence or something renewed concerns something other than the persisting thing—also applies to contingency, with the additional difficulty of the state before existence, which is sheer negation.

The answer is that dependence upon an agent means that existence, nonexistence or their continuation depends upon something.

SEVENTH INVESTIGATION
Neither alternative of a contingent thing has priority in view of its essence. Some give nonexistence priority without qualification; others do so for flowing accidents. Evidently, if priority means that occurrence needs no cause, it is necessarily false. If it means proximity to occurrence because of fewer conditions and obstacles and more frequent concurrence of causes, it derives from another. If it means some tendency toward existence or nonexistence short of necessity, it is conceivable.

Arguments for its impossibility are: (1) if the opposite alternative is impossible despite that priority,

<a id="m-ii-p199"></a>

**Source p. 199**

this alternative is necessary; if the opposite is possible, it occurs either without a cause, making the less favoured prevail, or through a cause, in which case the favoured alternative depends upon that cause's absence and is not intrinsically prior; (2) requiring equality conflicts with requiring priority of one alternative; (3) if the priority can be removed by a cause, it is not intrinsic but depends upon that cause's absence, while if it cannot, the quiddity is necessary or impossible. These arguments are weak. (1) What depends upon the cause's absence is occurrence, not priority. (2) Not requiring either alternative differs from requiring equality. (3) The impossibility of removing one alternative's priority does not entail its occurrence, much less its necessity: the other may occur through an extrinsic priority reaching necessity. Such necessity is required, because occurrence on one occasion and nonoccurrence on another under identical circumstances would be preponderance without a determining ground.

Thus a contingent thing does not exist until its proceeding becomes necessary, and while it exists its nonexistence is impossible. Its existence is enclosed by two necessities, prior and subsequent. Priority here does not mean dependence for actuality or conception, but order in the intellect's consideration of these meanings: it judges that the thing did not exist until it became necessary. This necessity does not conflict with choice, because it arises through choice, which belongs to the complete cause.

THIRD APPROACH: BEGINNINGLESSNESS AND TEMPORAL ORIGINATION
It contains two investigations.

<a id="m-ii-p201"></a>

**Source p. 201**

FIRST INVESTIGATION
These may mean not being preceded by another and being preceded by another. 'Another' may be restricted to nonexistence, which is the familiar usage. They may also be used according to differences, greater or smaller, in elapsed duration of existence. Essential beginninglessness is narrower than temporal beginninglessness, which is narrower than relative antiquity; with origination the order is reversed.

Nothing is beginningless essentially except God Most High. As for temporal beginninglessness, the philosophers add many contingent beings, and the theologians add God's attributes. The Muʿtazilites are also committed to this when they treat being knowledgeable, powerful, living and existent as states established beginninglessly with the essence. By existence we mean nothing other than what they mean by establishedness.

What is temporally beginningless cannot depend upon a choosing agent, because an intention to bring something into existence necessarily accompanies its nonexistence; disputing this is obstinate. It could, however, depend upon a necessitating cause, if such a cause were possible, because an effect's failure to follow its complete cause would be preponderance without a determining ground. The claim that influence during persistence produces what already exists was answered above.

The beginningless cannot cease to exist. It is either necessary or depends, through necessitation, upon the Necessary Existent, immediately or ultimately, since infinite regress is impossible, and necessarily without depending upon a temporally originated condition. If it ceased, the Necessary Existent would cease. If it is objected that it must therefore itself be necessary, we reply: inability to cease because its cause is complete does not entail intrinsic necessity.

<a id="m-ii-p203"></a>

**Source p. 203**

SECOND INVESTIGATION
The philosophers maintain that everything temporally originated is preceded by matter and duration. Regarding matter: before existence the thing is possible; its possibility is existent and requires a subject. That subject cannot be the originated thing, since a thing cannot precede itself, unlike the possibility of something beginningless. The answer is that, if intrinsic possibility is meant, its being existent is not conceded. If dispositional possibility is meant—which differs in requiring preponderance, admitting degrees and possessing actuality—it is not conceded that everything originated is possible in that sense.

If it is objected that an effect necessarily persists with its complete cause, so that an originated thing cannot depend upon a beginningless cause, but must be preceded by successive events that confer varying dispositions requiring a subject related to it, we reply: the beginningless cause chooses and produces the originated thing whenever He wills.

Regarding duration: succession of events and the priority of nonexistence to existence are inconceivable without time. The answer is that the first point rests upon the foregoing claim; the second upon their claim that priority and its counterparts occur through causality, nature, time, nobility, or sensory or intellectual rank, naturally or conventionally.

<a id="m-ii-p205"></a>

**Source p. 205**

According to us, priority may be intrinsic, as among parts of time, without needing another time. Calling it temporal does not harm our position. Some say that temporal priority has two kinds; others that its genuine instance is only among parts of time, accruing to other things through them, so that a father's priority to his son means the priority of his time to his son's time.

Priority of rank and nobility may also be reduced to temporal priority, and temporal priority to natural priority, leaving only intrinsic and natural priority as genuine kinds. Hence their hesitation whether priority and its counterparts are predicated equivocally or with ordered variation.

FOURTH APPROACH: UNITY AND MULTIPLICITY
It contains investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
These are intellectual considerations needing no definition. Unity is better known to the intellect, multiplicity to imagination; each therefore appears in explanations of the other. Certainty that they can be separated in conception, and that combining or separating is not annihilation, indicates their difference from quiddity and existence, although unity is coextensive with existence.

<a id="m-ii-p207"></a>

**Source p. 207**

Their positive character is sometimes argued as follows. If unity were negative, it would be absence of multiplicity. Multiplicity would then be either negative, making unity positive as the negation of negation, contrary to the supposition, or positive, making a collection of nonexistences positive, which is impossible, and making unity positive as its part, again contrary to the supposition. Since multiplicity is nothing but a composition of units, it too must be positive. Their negative character is argued from nothing being understood by unity except nondivision, and by multiplicity except what is composed of units. Both arguments are weak.

SECOND INVESTIGATION
The subject of unity may not be the subject of multiplicity. If it then has no concept beyond nondivision, it is unity without qualification. Otherwise, if incapable of division, it is either positioned and thus a point, or unpositioned and thus an immaterial separate being. If divisible, it is either intrinsically so, and thus quantity, or incidentally so into similar parts, and thus one by continuity, or into dissimilar parts, and thus one by aggregation, naturally, artificially or conventionally, as with a tree, house or dirham.

The subject of unity may also be the subject of multiplicity, but the respect must differ. If the respect of unity constitutes that of multiplicity, unity is generic, specific or differential. Otherwise, if it accrues to it, unity is accidental; if not, relational.

<a id="m-ii-p209"></a>

**Source p. 209**

Unity in genus is called common genus; in species, likeness; in quantity, equality; in quality, similarity; in relation, correspondence; in a distinctive property, conformity; in extremities, coincidence; and in the disposition of parts, parallelism. Some of these kinds have a stronger claim to unity, so unity is predicated with ordered variation. Likewise multiplicity: its first degree is twoness, obtained by adding one to one. It then increases by successive units without limit into species with different concomitants, realised intellectually by considering the conjunction of units. Considering one thing in the east together with one in the west yields twoness without something becoming actual in both. If such a thing became actual, it would inhere in the aggregate and something of it would be in each individual; that is nothing but considerational unity.

THIRD INVESTIGATION
Two things cannot become one: (1) the difference of their quiddities or identities is intrinsic and cannot cease; (2) if both remain, they are two; otherwise one perishes and the other remains, or both perish and a third arises. The first argument is answered by saying that it is no clearer than the claim; the second by denying that two-ness follows if both remain—it would follow only if they did not unite. The argument is reformulated: if both are existent, they are two; otherwise the preceding alternatives apply. It is answered that both may exist through one existence. The reply is that this existence is either one of the two existences or a third. The answer is that it is the two existences themselves become one. It is then asserted that the judgement is necessary and the argument merely directs attention to it.

<a id="m-ii-p211"></a>

**Source p. 211**

FOURTH INVESTIGATION
Among multiplicity's properties is otherness. According to our teachers, two others are two existents capable of separation. A part is therefore neither identical with nor other than the whole; likewise a qualified thing and its attribute. Thus one may correctly say 'there is nothing in the house other than Zayd' or 'I hold nothing other than ten', although parts and attributes are present.

If it is objected that separation on only one side allows a part without the whole and a subject without the attribute, whereas separation on both sides is countered by the Maker and the world, the answer is that the intended separation is intellectual and on both sides. Hence the formulation: two things one of which can be known while the other is unknown. Conceiving the world without the Maker is not impossible; considering it as an effect introduces a relation.

Correlatives have been raised as an objection. The answer is that they are not existent. If it is objected that the otherness of father and son, cause and effect, is necessary, we reply: yes, in their concrete identities. The majority hold that otherness is the contradictory of identity: it is inconceivable that one thing is neither identical with nor other than another. Two others are simply two insofar as neither is the other.

<a id="m-ii-p213"></a>

**Source p. 213**

The defence is either that this is a technical convention or that the intended meaning is 'not identical conceptually and not other in concrete identity', as in predication. The first defence is refuted by their unsound argument that, if one were other than ten, it would be other than itself because it belongs to ten. The second is refuted by the fact that the discussion concerns parts and attributes not predicated of their subjects, such as one in relation to ten, and power and knowledge in relation to the essence.

Some require, for two things not to be other, the impossibility of either without the other, as with the Beginningless One's attributes, unlike whiteness and a body. They claim that the one within ten cannot exist without the ten. The objection from other relations is evident.

NOTICE: denying otherness between essence and attribute removes the beginninglessness of something other than the essence, not the plurality of beginningless things.

Another property is likeness: sharing intrinsic attributes. This entails sharing what is necessary, possible and impossible, and each thing's being able to take the other's place. Some respect of difference is required for distinction; whoever excludes difference means in the respect constituting their likeness. Whether otherness is required is disputed. Two like accidents cannot coexist, because distinct individuals of one accidental species cannot be conceived except through distinct subjects.

<a id="m-ii-p215"></a>

**Source p. 215**

This is answered by denial. A body's intense blackness does not result from two or more blacknesses coming together: blacknesses differing in intensity are different species succeeding one another in the body.

Another property is contrariety: two meanings are such that their conjunction in one subject in one respect is intrinsically impossible. Contrariety therefore occurs only between accidents, not between blackness and sweetness, between knowledge of motion and knowledge of rest together, or between smallness and largeness unless their relation is to something specified.

According to the philosophers, any two are others. If they share their entire quiddity, they are alike; otherwise different. They are opposed if they cannot coexist in one subject in one respect. If both are positive, they are correlatives when each is conceived relative to the other, and contraries otherwise. If one is negative, they are possession and privation when the subject must be capable of the positive feature according to its individual nature, species, proximate genus or remote genus; otherwise affirmation and negation.

<a id="m-ii-p217"></a>

**Source p. 217**

Since a negative's contradictory may itself be negative—as with impossibility and non-impossibility, or blindness and non-blindness, where the latter means its removal and includes both sight and incapacity for sight—it is more appropriate to say: if one opposite removes the other, they are possession and privation or affirmation and negation; otherwise correlation or contrariety, as explained.

It is sometimes said that two negatives cannot be opposed. This is evident for absolute and relative nonexistence. Two relative nonexistences coexist outside what they are related to, as non-blackness and non-whiteness coexist in red. Moreover, opposition requires identity of subject; this excludes humanity and horseness, or an implicant and the absence of its implication. This account is open to question.

If it is objected that propositions can be contradictory or contrary without a subject of inherence being conceived, we reply: the terminology is equivocal. Like other relations between simple terms, these relations concern propositions' truth in themselves, rather than their being true of something. Alternatively, the proposition's subject is the locus of affirmation and negation.

Contrariety may require maximal difference, and is then called genuine contrariety, the earlier kind being called commonly recognised contrariety. Possession and privation may require the subject's capacity for the positive feature at that—

<a id="m-ii-p219"></a>

**Source p. 219**

time, and this is specifically called the commonly recognised sense, the earlier kind the genuine sense. In each classification the first is broader; on that basis opposition includes whiteness against yellowness and sight against its absence in a tree. Yet they explicitly state that one contrary in the commonly recognised sense may be the other's absence: rest against motion, darkness against light, sickness against health, speechlessness against speech, femaleness against maleness, and oddness against evenness; and that maximal difference is required in commonly recognised contrariety too.

Opposition is broader than correlation in respect of the subjects qualified, but narrower in respect of the attribute itself. Likewise contrariety is a coordinate division alongside correlation in the former respect and a subdivision of it in the latter. Opposition is predicated of its kinds with ordered variation. Its strongest kind is affirmation and negation, because the others' incompatibility is grounded in it, not contrariety on account of maximal difference: nothing exceeds intrinsic incompatibility.

Affirmation and negation ultimately concern expression and judgement. Negations are intellectual considerations with verbal expressions, not essences; otherwise, by negating everything else of human being, it would possess infinitely many meanings. When converted into judgements, affirmation and negation divide truth and falsehood between them. Other oppositions may both be false because the subject does not exist or possesses neither opposite.

<a id="m-ii-p221"></a>

**Source p. 221**

A subject may be unable to lack a particular contrary, as fire cannot lack heat, or unable to lack both indeterminately, as a body cannot lack both motion and rest. It may lack both because it has an intermediate state expressed by a positive name, such as lukewarm; by negation of both extremes, such as neither just nor unjust; or without such an intermediate, as a transparent body lacks blackness and whiteness.

Genuine contrariety occurs only between two ultimate species of one genus, such as blackness and whiteness: not between two genera, such as virtue and vice or good and evil; two species of different genera, such as chastity and profligacy; or several species of one genus, such as blackness, whiteness and redness. Their reliance here is upon induction, which is open to question.

They explicitly allow commonly recognised contrariety between two genera, such as good and evil; two species of different genera, such as chastity and profligacy; several species of one genus, such as blackness, whiteness and redness; or species of two genera, such as courage, rashness and cowardice.

CONCLUSION
They say that unity and multiplicity are not intrinsically opposed but are so through the accidental relations of causality and measuring: their subject is not numerically one, and neither of two opposites is constituted through the other. The first argument is answered by saying that the subject need not be numerically one: it may be one in species,

<a id="m-ii-p223"></a>

**Source p. 223**

genus or a more general accident, and even this through mere supposition. The second is answered by denying that multiplicity is constituted through unity: only its subject is constituted through unity's subject. There is no dispute that opposites taken together with their subjects—horse and non-horse, sighted and blind, father and son, black and white—are not intrinsically opposed, much less the subjects alone.

If it is objected that constitution between the subjects entails possible coexistence of the attributes, we reply: if conceded, this concerns coexistence in existence and does not exclude opposition of affirmation and negation. A white sweet thing contains whiteness and non-whiteness, namely sweetness. Hence it has been said that their concepts are opposed as affirmation and negation.

The truth is that the philosophers intend to deny opposition between multiplicity as number and unity as that from which number is constituted. Their concepts, explained as division and nondivision, are evidently opposed as affirmation and negation.

FIFTH APPROACH: CAUSALITY AND BEING CAUSED
These are correlative intellectual considerations, which cannot coincide in one thing except relative to two things. Their explanation comprises investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
A cause is what a thing needs, although the term without qualification usually means that from which a thing proceeds.

<a id="m-ii-p225"></a>

**Source p. 225**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.05](#c1-05).*

If the cause is internal to the thing, it is formal when the thing is necessarily actual with it, and material when necessarily potential with it. Parts of form and matter fall under these classifications. Mentioning necessity avoids the objection that a thing may exist actually, rather than potentially, together with matter. However, a nonfinal part of form creates difficulties for both inclusion and exclusion.

If external, the cause is efficient when the thing is through it, and final when the thing is for its sake. The first two are called causes of quiddity, the last two causes of existence. Conditions and instruments are referred to the agent. Some conditions are negative, such as the removal of an obstacle. Their inclusion in the cause of existence is not impossible, meaning that when the intellect considers the effect's existence it does not find it occurring without them. It is sometimes said that such a negative condition actually reveals a positive one: removing moisture for wood to burn indicates dryness, which is the condition. The nonexistence of an originated thing is one of its principles only incidentally.

Everything a thing needs collectively is called its complete cause: the agent alone or together with the end—as with a simple agent producing a simple effect necessarily or by choice, respectively—or together with the remaining causes, as in composites. In the latter case the cause's priority and the thing's dependence upon it cannot be conceived, since it includes all the parts that are the thing itself.

Each of the four causes divides into simple and composite, universal and particular,

<a id="m-ii-p227"></a>

**Source p. 227**

intrinsic and incidental, proximate and remote, general and specific, shared and exclusive, potential and actual.

SECOND INVESTIGATION
The effect must exist when the agent is complete, since preponderance without a determining ground is impossible. Conversely, the effect requires its complete agent, since dependence accompanies contingency. The agent's priority is therefore only essential. An originated effect can depend upon a beginningless agent only through a concomitant originated condition, such as the will's relation to it. An effect's nonexistence, even for something nonpersistent such as motion, requires the absence of its cause, even if only of some condition. A condition's lack of positive establishedness does not exclude its being a condition in this sense.

The agent governing the contingent thing's two alternatives is one: its presence necessitates the thing's presence, and its absence the thing's absence, prior if prior and subsequent if subsequent. An effect's persistence after its cause disappears is conceivable only with preparatory causes, as a son after his father, a building after its builder, or heated water after the fire, not with efficacious causes.

The cause of existence may also cause persistence, as the sun causes light; or it may differ, as contact with fire causes ignition, while that contact's continuation with other assisting causes maintains it.

THIRD INVESTIGATION
The effect's numerical unity entails the agent's unity, contrary to some Muʿtazilites. The converse does not hold, contrary to the philosophers, who prohibit many effects proceeding from what is truly one.

<a id="m-ii-p229"></a>

**Source p. 229**

The first point holds because, if one individual were caused by two independently sufficient causes, it would need each because each is its cause, and be independent of each because the other is its cause. Moreover, it either needs both, making each only part of the cause, or only one, making that one the cause. A species differs: the individual needing one cause differs from the individual needing the other, as with instances of heat produced by different fires. A particular individual needs a particular cause; some individual needs some cause, without the causes coinciding upon that individual; the species needs some cause, with coincidence possible through its multiple individuals. Whether a particular individual may derive alternatively from either cause without its individuation changing is uncertain.

The opponent argues that the motion of an atom pushed by Zayd while pulled by ʿAmr depends upon each. We reply: it depends upon both collectively, or upon God Most High.

The second point holds because possibility is the default unless proof excludes it, and because we shall show that everything depends immediately upon the Necessary Existent.

The philosophers argue: first, being the source of this differs from being the source of that. If either enters into the agent, it becomes composite; otherwise a regress follows, since an accident is an effect and has a proceeding of its own.

<a id="m-ii-p231"></a>

**Source p. 231**

The reply is that sourcehood is considerational. Were it actual, the agent's unity would not be pure, and multiple—indeed infinite—effects would follow whenever anything proceeded from the Necessary Existent, since the accident's being an effect is conceded there. Moreover, if this argument were sound, nothing at all could proceed from the one, because its proceeding would be different; only one thing could be denied of it, only one attribute ascribed to it, and only one thing received by it.

If it is objected: negations are considerations without external actuality or distinction, as are qualification and receptivity. Proceeding differs: besides a consideration accruing to cause and effect together, it names a real feature—the cause's being such that the effect proceeds from it, a particularity through which the effect is necessary. It is multiple if effects are multiple and one otherwise. If the cause causes through itself, this feature is the cause's essence; otherwise it is an accruing state. Thus multiple respects follow only from multiple effects, not one.

We reply: these are arbitrary assertions warranted by no argument. If it is said that multiple effects require multiple agency, even merely in respect, because being an agent of this differs in consideration from being an agent of that, so that complete absence of multiplicity in the agent entails one effect, we reply: this is empty and undermines their principles founded upon the impossibility of multiple effects from the simple. Multiple intellectual respects do not impair genuine unity. Otherwise even one effect could not proceed, since being its source necessarily differs intellectually from the agent itself.

<a id="m-ii-p233"></a>

**Source p. 233**

Second: if A proceeds from it, then B, which is not A, also proceeding would conjoin contradictories. This differs from multiple respects, each effect deriving from a different respect. The reply is that A's proceeding does not contradict the proceeding of what is not A, but A's not proceeding. Although the latter can be truly predicated of a proceeding of what is not A, a thing and something to which its contradictory applies may coexist in existence without coinciding in predication. What is impossible is that it be true of the same thing that A proceeds from it and does not proceed from it.

Third: inferring different agents from different effects is rooted in intellects. The reply is that this rests upon the impossibility of an effect failing to accompany its cause, or an implicant occurring without its implication.

Counterarguments are then offered. First: corporeality requires spatial occupation and receiving accidents, or at least receptivity to both. The reply denies corporeality's unity and the positive existence of both features. Second: everything that proceeds has quiddity and existence, both effects. After conceding their external distinctness, the reply is that the effect is existence or qualification by it. Third: the centre is the principle of its alignments with points on the circumference. The reply is that these are considerations. Fourth: if only one proceeded from one, there would be a single chain of existents, and of any two things one would cause the other, even through intermediaries.

<a id="m-ii-p235"></a>

**Source p. 235**

The reply is that unity of essence does not exclude multiple considerations. Many effects may proceed from the single first effect according to its intelligible existence, quiddity, contingency, intellection of itself and intellection of its principle. A second effect may proceed from the true One together with His first effect, a third through the second, a fourth through both, and so on without limit; the chains would then be unrestricted in number.

It may be said that, if such considerations suffice, the true One also has many negations and relations and can therefore be the principle of multiplicity without intermediate effects. The reply is that these depend upon another's establishedness; making that other's establishedness depend upon them is circular.

FOURTH INVESTIGATION
The philosophers maintain that one thing cannot be both recipient and agent, since these are two effects, as discussed, and because the agent's relation is necessity whereas the recipient's is possibility. The answer—even conceding strict possibility, incompatible with necessity, or broad possibility capable of occurring without necessity—is that necessity and non-necessity in two different respects are not impossible.

<a id="m-ii-p237"></a>

**Source p. 237**

FIFTH INVESTIGATION
According to us bodily powers have no causal efficacy. Their acts' appearance therefore does not require position, and their everlasting continuation through God's creation is not impossible.

According to the philosophers their efficacy requires position, since fire plainly heats only what has a particular position relative to it. Their action must be finite in intensity, which is evident, and in duration and number. Forced motion varies with the recipient, natural motion with the agent: small and large bodies differ in resistance but are equal in receptivity. Resistance comes from nature, stronger in the larger body, whereas receptivity comes from corporeality, equal in both. Supposing their motions have the same starting point, the other extremity differs, entailing finitude. Celestial motions are no counterexample, since they derive from volitions of their immaterial souls. Even conceding efficacy, the answer denies that power is proportional to volume.

SIXTH INVESTIGATION
The ascending succession of causality and being caused cannot proceed without limit, whether in finitely many subjects, called circularity, or infinitely many, called infinite regress.

<a id="m-ii-p239"></a>

**Source p. 239**

The first is impossible because a thing cannot precede itself in the sense that makes 'it existed, and therefore this existed' correct, as causality requires. Thus the hand's motion existed and therefore the ring's motion existed, not conversely.

If it is objected that self-priority does not follow, because what depends upon something dependent upon a thing need not depend upon that thing—the proximate cause suffices, or else an effect could fail to follow—and because a thing's quiddity may cause what causes its existence, we reply: without the remote cause the proximate does not exist; without the proximate the effect does not exist. This is dependence. A thing's quiddity causing what causes its existence is impossible and, in any case, not our present subject.

The second is impossible for several arguments. First: unless the series of effects terminates in a pure cause, the totality identical with the collection of contingent existents, each dependent upon another, is an existent and contingent. Its independently sufficient agent is neither itself nor a part, since a thing cannot cause itself or its causes, but an external necessary cause. This cause produces some member, terminating the series and excluding that member's dependence upon another member, since two efficacious causes cannot coincide upon it.

On this formulation, the following objection does not apply: 'If a complete cause is meant, it need not be distinct from the totality, since a complete cause need not precede, as with a composite. If an efficient cause is meant,

<a id="m-ii-p241"></a>

**Source p. 241**

it may be a part of the totality, since it need not cause every part, as a carpenter does not cause every part of a bed. Even if conceded, the chains may be infinite, with what is external to this chain internal to another, never reaching the necessary. Even if conceded, this proves a Necessary Existent, not the impossibility of regress. It is also countered by the totality of contingent beings together with the Necessary Existent.'

But another objection applies. If an independently sufficient cause of a composite of contingent parts must itself cause every part, then where the parts are temporally ordered, an effect must precede or fail to accompany what independently suffices to produce it. If it must cause every part either itself or through one of its own parts—so that no part's cause lies outside the composite's cause, and a composite with temporally ordered parts also has a cause with ordered parts—then something within the series can be a cause in that sense: everything preceding the pure effect without limit. Each part of that aggregate produces a part of the series, and likewise every prior aggregate. Its dependence for existence upon its causes, or the series' dependence upon the pure effect too, does not impair its independent sufficiency for production. This also defeats the arguments that no part has priority in causality, or that each part's cause has a greater claim to be the cause. All this concedes that the series needs something beyond its parts' causes, although it has no existence beyond its parts' existences.

Second: remove one member from the series' extremity, then align the complete and diminished series. If each member of the complete corresponds to one of the diminished, whole and part are equal;

<a id="m-ii-p243"></a>

**Source p. 243**

otherwise the diminished ends, and the complete, exceeding it by only one, is finite. The argument's basis is countered by the series of numbers according to everyone, God's objects of knowledge according to us, and celestial motions according to the philosophers. The inference that the diminished must terminate is countered by repeatedly doubling one and two without limit, by God's objects of power alongside His objects of knowledge, and by Saturn's revolutions alongside the moon's. The point is that each member may correspond to a member because both series are infinite, not because they are equal. If such correspondence is called equality, its impossibility is denied.

The escape is to claim necessary awareness while restricting the ruling: according to us, to what has entered existence, because an imagined series ends when imagination ends; according to them, to actual existents ordered positionally or naturally, because correspondence is impossible otherwise. In truth, treating them as two series and aligning them is intellectual. If a general intellectual supposition suffices, the argument applies to all cases; if detailed consideration is required, it never succeeds.

Third: because the series includes a pure effect, it must include a pure cause to realise the equivalence of correlatives, and therefore terminates. This gives rise to several formulations: an infinite causal regress makes effects more numerous than causes, since every cause is an effect but not conversely, defeating equivalence; or align the causal and caused relations in the series—if unequal, equivalence fails; if equal, there must be causality without being caused, since at the finite extremity there is being caused without causality.

<a id="m-ii-p245"></a>

**Source p. 245**

Fourth: set aside the pure effect, count each remaining member twice under causality and being caused, and align the series of causes and effects. Because causes necessarily precede, causal relations exceed caused relations and both series are finite.

Fifth: the series is even if divisible into two equals, odd otherwise. Every even number is one less than the following odd, and conversely; thus it is finite. The reply is that failure of such division may result from infinity.

Sixth: what lies between this effect and any remote cause is finite because bounded by two limits. The whole series is therefore finite, exceeding that finite interval by only one. Necessarily, if the interval between the start of a distance and each part never exceeds a parasang, the whole exceeds a parasang by at most one part, as intuition judges. This is open to question. The claim that a collection of finite numbers must itself be finite is weaker still.

Seventh: the number of thousands in the series is either equal to the number of units or greater, both evidently impossible, or smaller. The units then comprise a group equal in number to the thousands and another equal to the excess. If the first group lies at the finite end, actually or hypothetically, the number of thousands is finite, since a cut marks the beginning of the excess. The series is then composed of finitely many groups with finitely many members and is finite. If the first group lies on the infinite side, the second lies on the finite side between the extremity and the beginning of the counted thousands. It is therefore finite, being the excess of units over thousands. The number of thousands and the series must then be finite. Against this and some preceding arguments, one may deny that equality or inequality must apply to the infinite, or that the smaller infinite must terminate.

<a id="m-ii-p247"></a>

**Source p. 247**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.06](#c1-06).*

SEVENTH INVESTIGATION
Matter is the locus, recipient and bearer of form; form is matter's (agent or) part of its agent. Matter cannot be constituted through two forms at one level. Their independent constitution of it is evidently impossible; if together, what constitutes it is the aggregate, which is one. Two forms at different levels are possible, such as corporeal and specific form.

'Form' may mean any configuration in a recipient one intrinsically or by consideration, and 'matter' its subject, as with whiteness and body. Sword and bed appear to belong here: the artisan produces a configuration, not a substance. The objection that the sword's configuration does not necessarily make a sword actual—as when present in stone—then does not apply. The Imām's answer, that the species of form does not necessitate the composite but the individual sword-form necessitates that sword, unlike its individual matter, suggests that form is being used here in the earlier sense. Moreover, 'sword', for example, names the composite of a subject that is substance and an attribute that is configuration.

A thing's end causes it insofar as the thing needs a cause whose causality depends upon conceiving the end. Hence they say: through its quiddity the end causes the agent's agency, whereas through its concrete existence it is the agent's effect—indeed, the effect of the agent's effect. It is a cause in mental existence and an effect in concrete existence. 'End' may also mean what an act terminates in, even when neither an effect nor intended, indeed even when the agent lacks intention and choice. In this sense ends are affirmed for natural and accidental events. An accidental end is one to which its cause leads neither always nor for the most part.

NOTICE: since according to us God Most High alone brings things into existence, a cause among contingent things means that after which the thing is customarily created.

---

<a id="division-iii"></a>

# Division III: Accidents

<a id="m-iii-p249"></a>

**Source p. 249**

THIRD PRINCIPAL DIVISION: ACCIDENTS
It contains chapters.

CHAPTER ONE: GENERAL INVESTIGATIONS
There are five.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
According to our teachers, an existent not preceded by nonexistence is beginningless: the Necessary Existent and His attributes. Otherwise it is temporally originated, either occupying space intrinsically, and thus a substance, or inhering in such a thing, and thus an accident. The existence of immaterial substances has not been established, although the proof of their impossibility is incomplete. An accident is either peculiar to living things—life and its consequent apprehensions and other attributes—or not peculiar to them: modes of spatial being and sensible qualities.

According to the philosophers, an external existent whose existence belongs to it through itself is the Necessary Existent; otherwise it is contingent. A contingent independent of a subject—that is, a locus constituting it—is a substance; otherwise an accident. Substantial form requires a locus but not a subject. An accident's existence in a locus means that its existence in itself is its existence in that locus, unlike a body's presence in a place.

<a id="m-iii-p251"></a>

**Source p. 251**

The genera of accidents are nine, according to induction. Ibn Sīnā acknowledges that it is impossible to demonstrate that they are neither fewer nor more, and that every explanation offered for that enumeration is contrived.

They maintain that these are highest genera, substance being the tenth. This assumes that each is a genus and that what falls beneath it comprises genera; that existent is not a genus of substance and accident, accident not a genus of the accidents, and relation not a genus of relational categories.

Some say that the genera of accidents are three: quantity, quality and relation; some add motion. The majority place locomotion in the category of where; others in undergoing action, as with nonlocal motion. As for unity and the point, some call them negative, like ignorance and blindness; others place them under quality; others exclude them from the categories but say their generic status is unestablished, the enumeration concerning only highest genera. The same applies to existence, necessity, contingency and similar notions.

<a id="m-iii-p253"></a>

**Source p. 253**

The philosophers do not affirm the Creator's attributes; we restrict neither all contingent things nor those attributes to substance and accident, but only the temporally originated.

SECOND INVESTIGATION
Necessary awareness judges (1) that an accident cannot subsist through itself—Abū al-Hudhayl's admission of an accidental will without a locus is obstinate—and (2) that it cannot inhere in more than one locus. Arguments that inherence in two loci would permit two causes to coincide, a body to exist in two places, and uncertainty about the distinctness of two blacknesses explain why the judgement holds and draw attention to its necessity. Some earlier thinkers admitted such inherence, supposing similar relations, such as proximity and adjacency, to inhere in both terms. The reply is that there are two accidents, each inhering in one term, as with the dissimilar relations of fatherhood and sonship.

Abū Hāshim also admitted it, supposing the composition of a body's parts to cause difficulty in separating them. It must therefore be a positive attribute inhering in two parts, necessarily not in one and not in more than two: otherwise it would not remain when one part disappeared and two remained. The reply denies both its causality and the persistence of the composition originally between three.

<a id="m-iii-p255"></a>

**Source p. 255**

The unity of ten, a triangle's being triangular, a living structure's life and Zayd's standing are not the disputed cases. Perhaps Abū Hāshim intended something of this kind.

THIRD INVESTIGATION
They agree that an accident cannot transfer, because its existence in itself is its existence in its locus. Apparent transfer of qualities, such as scents, is the origination of a similar quality in a neighbouring thing. They offer arguments.

First: transfer means occupying one spatial region after another and is inconceivable in something not spatially extended. The reply is that this concerns substance; transfer in an accident means occurring in one locus after another.

Second: its individuation cannot come from its quiddity, since it would then have only one individual; from something inhering in it, since this is circular; from something separate, whose relation to all individuals is equal; or from its identity, which does not precede individuation. It must therefore come from its locus and cannot remain without it. The reply denies equality of relation, especially for a choosing agent.

Third: the required locus is either determinate, in which case the accident cannot leave it, or indeterminate, in which case it cannot exist.

<a id="m-iii-p257"></a>

**Source p. 257**

The reply is that the locus is determinate by some determination, like a body's spatial region. Fourth: during transfer the accident is either without a locus, which is impossible; in the locus left or entered, which is settlement; or in a third, renewing the argument. This is countered by a body's transfer. The resolution is that it occupies part of the first and part of the second.

FOURTH INVESTIGATION
An accident cannot inhere in an accident, because inherence means dependence in spatial occupation and is inconceivable in what does not intrinsically occupy space. Moreover, a substance must ultimately be reached, and some accidents' inhering in others has no greater claim than all inhering in that substance.

It is objected that inherence means a qualifying attachment. It may therefore occur in something not spatially extended, and an accident may qualify another accident rather than its substance: a motion's speed, a surface's smoothness or a line's straightness. Accordingly the philosophers admit it and make the point inhere in the line and the line in the surface. Some go further, treating accidents' unity and existence this way. The theologians hold that some of these are intellectual considerations, some inhere in substances, and some are substances. Treating existence as an accident is a gross error.

FIFTH INVESTIGATION
Many theologians hold that an accident cannot persist. Those adhering to the apparent wording argue that inability to persist belongs to the meaning of its name, as with 'something that supervenes' and similar expressions,

<a id="m-iii-p259"></a>

**Source p. 259**

and that, if it persisted, it would do so either through its locus's persistence, lasting as long as the locus and possessing its other attributes, or through another persistence, making its persistence possible after the locus perished. Both arguments are evidently weak.

The exacting scholars give two arguments. First: persistence would make an accident inhere in an accident, which is impossible. Both premises are denied. Second: if an accident persisted, it could not cease. If it could, this would occur through itself, making its existence impossible; through a condition's disappearance, producing regress; through a contrary's supervention, producing circularity, since a locus's qualification by either contrary requires the other's absence; or through an agent, requiring an effect, whereas sheer negation cannot be an effect. Moreover, a persisting accident's removal by a supervening one has no greater claim than the reverse—indeed less, since prevention is easier than removal.

First reply: a body is a counterexample. It may be answered that God creates in it an accident of annihilation, or ceases to create an accident required for its persistence, whereas an accident cannot be the locus of another accident. Second reply: reverse the argument. If an accident cannot persist, its annihilation is either through itself or through another. Third reply: resolve the alternatives. Its essence may require nonexistence in some circumstances; it may depend upon accidents renewed in alternation until one with no replacement is reached, at which point it ceases; a contrary's supervention and the other's disappearance may coincide, as each part of a ring enters another's spatial region while the other leaves it,

<a id="m-iii-p261"></a>

**Source p. 261**

without excluding intellectual priority in respect of causality; and originated nonexistence may be an agent's effect. Even if that is denied, let the meaning be that the agent does not produce the accident, not that the agent produces its nonexistence.

The truth is that an accident's persistence in some cases is like a body's persistence, especially accidents inhering in the soul. Reliance is not upon observation alone: successive similar things may appear continuous, like water poured from a pipe.

CHAPTER TWO: QUANTITY
It contains investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION: ITS GENERAL CHARACTERISTICS
Quantity's properties include intrinsic divisibility, either mentally, by supposing one thing in it distinct from another—the majority's definition—or actually, through separation. Another is admitting equality and inequality, consequent upon the first, though the Imām reverses their order. Another is containing a measuring unit. The Imām considers this appropriate for defining quantity, since equality means agreement in quantity and would be circular, while divisibility is peculiar to the continuous and would not cover all quantity. Apparently he takes 'admitting' as incompatible with actual occurrence; hence his qualification, 'unless admission is taken equivocally'. Interpreting him as meaning actual separability is mistaken, since he explicitly denies such separation in magnitude.

<a id="m-iii-p263"></a>

**Source p. 263**

Discrete quantity is that whose parts have no common limit: number alone, since speech's divisibility is incidental. Continuous quantity is the converse. If nonpersistent, it is time; otherwise magnitude: a line if divisible in one direction only, a surface in two only, and a mathematical body in all directions. The last uniquely can be taken on condition of nothing, although all can be taken without condition of anything.

Quantity may be intrinsic, admitting neither contrariety nor intensification, or incidental: the subject of intrinsic quantity, what inheres in quantity or in its subject, or what is related to it, as powers are finite or infinite through their effects. Intrinsic and incidental quantity do not exclude each other: time is intrinsically nonpersistent and measures motion corresponding to distance. Nor do the kinds of incidental quantity exclude one another: motion is divisible because it inheres in the divisible, differs in amount through correspondence to distance, and in speed through correspondence to time.

Discrete quantity may accrue to continuous quantity, as with hours of a day and handbreadths of a cubit. Magnitude considered with a relation is called length, breadth or depth.

<a id="m-iii-p265"></a>
