**Source p. 265**

The theologians deny number's existence for reasons already given. They take magnitudes as substances assembled in different ways, or as negative things because they are limits and terminations. The first view is opposed by their changing while the same body remains, by the surface's dependence upon finitude, which requires proof, and by a line in a sphere depending upon motion or cutting. The second is opposed by their having positions. The answer is that what changes is the disposition of substances, what depends upon another is their being in a particular state, and what is pointed to is the substances themselves.

SECOND INVESTIGATION: TIME
The theologians deny it for several reasons. First: if time existed, some parts would necessarily precede others, which could happen only through time, producing regress. The reply is that priority is intrinsic: yesterday's priority to today needs no additional attribute.

Second: time is either past or future, neither of which exists, or present. If the present existed, it would be indivisible, since parts of time cannot coexist. A succession of instants would follow, entailing indivisible parts. Motion differs: its existent aspect is being between the termini, continuously from beginning to end. This cannot apply to time, since the Flood's time certainly does not exist now. The reply denies their absolute nonexistence: they do not exist in the present, but exist successively.

<a id="m-iii-p267"></a>

**Source p. 267**

If it is objected that the past does not exist in the past or the future in the future, since the same division returns, the answer is that 'existent in one of the times' is narrower than 'existent' without qualification; falsity of the narrower does not entail falsity of the broader. If it is objected that a general class exhausted by nonexistent divisions is necessarily nonexistent—as all past motions from beginninglessness cannot jointly exist in past, future or present—the answer denies the exhaustion. Some existents are not in any time, such as time itself; the division applies to temporal things like motion. Time is indeed exhausted by the three, even by past and future, but their existence in themselves need not be existence in a time.

Third: if time existed, it could not subsequently cease, since subsequent nonexistence would require time. It would thus be necessary despite composition and passing away. The reply is that nonexistence's posteriority can consist in its being at the instant bounding the past at which time ends. Even if conceded, inability to cease after existence entails permanence, not intrinsic necessity.

The philosophers establish time in two ways. First: suppose two motions over a distance end together. If also equal in speed and beginning, they traverse the distance together; if the second starts later or is slower, it traverses less. Between the first motion's endpoints there is the possibility of traversing a definite distance at a definite speed,

<a id="m-iii-p269"></a>

**Source p. 269**

and a smaller distance at a definite slower speed. Between the second's endpoints there is less possibility at that same speed. There is therefore a quantitative feature irreducible to speed, distance's extension or the moving thing: this is time. If it is objected that judging simultaneity, lateness and speed presupposes time, making the argument circular, we reply: this is denied; those denying time are certain of these meanings.

Second: a father's priority to his son is necessary. It is evidently not the father's existence, nor that existence together with the son's absence, since that conjunction can occur subsequently without priority. One must ultimately reach what intrinsically bears priority and posteriority, whose before never becomes after or after before. This is time.

The answer is that these possibilities and priorities are intellectual considerations applicable to nonexistences: the intervals between today and the year's or month's beginning differ, and an originated thing's nonexistence precedes it. They maintain that the arguments only draw attention to the matter: an extension qualified by pastness and futurity is necessarily acknowledged by ordinary people, who divide it into years, months, days and hours. Only its reality is obscure.

Some take time as a known renewal used to measure an uncertain renewal. These may exchange roles according to the addressee's knowledge, as 'when ʿAmr sat' answers 'when did Zayd stand?', or conversely. Clearly this does not supply a conception. Aristotle and his followers take time as the measure of the outermost sphere's motion, because its variation makes it a quantity—

<a id="m-iii-p271"></a>

**Source p. 271**

and continuous, since a succession of instants would entail indivisible parts. Being nonpersistent, it measures a nonpersistent state, namely motion. Being incapable of ceasing, since subsequent nonexistence requires time, it measures circular motion, for rectilinear motion must end, as will be explained. Since all motions are measured by it, it measures the fastest, namely daily motion: larger is measured by smaller and more by less, like a parasang by cubits and a hundred by tens, not conversely.

The reply is that, besides resting on false principles, this succeeds only if time admits variation intrinsically. Counterarguments follow. First: unchanging things—a body, its rest, indeed the Necessary Existent and all immaterial beings—are qualified by being yesterday, today and tomorrow exactly as motion is. This also shows that time is not the measure of existence, since the changing does not coincide with the unchanging, or conversely. Second: motion as being between the termini is stable, so its measure cannot vary; motion as extended from beginning to end is imaginary, so its measure cannot exist. Third: an accident's establishedness without its subject is self-evidently impossible, unlike what we call time without celestial motion. This also shows that time is neither the sphere itself nor its motion.

The first is answered by saying that the unchanging is related to time by occurring with it, not in it. The changing's relation to the changing is time; the unchanging's relation to the changing is dahr, and to the unchanging sarmad.

<a id="m-iii-p273"></a>

**Source p. 273**

The second: just as two parts of something nonpersistent need not—and cannot—coexist, the same applies to its measure. The third: its basis is imagination's judgement. All these answers are weak.

Earlier thinkers made time an independently subsisting substance. Some called it necessary because it cannot be absent before or afterward. The reply is that this does not entail impossibility of nonexistence without qualification. Others called it contingent, including Plato and his followers, relying chiefly on certainty of its existence even without body or motion.

THIRD INVESTIGATION: PLACE
The principal positions make place the containing body's inner surface or the extension penetrated by a body's extension. Extension may be material, inhering in a body and excluding a similar extension; or separate, with a body inhering in it and coinciding with it throughout, so that it matches and unites with the body's extension. Plato makes this existent and incapable of being empty; the theologians make it supposed and capable of emptiness: imagined space that would be empty if nothing occupied it. There are two questions.

FIRST QUESTION: WHETHER PLACE IS SURFACE OR EXTENSION
Arguments for surface follow. First: place exists, admits differences of magnitude and pointing, and permits transfer from and to it. If existent extension admits motion, it has a place, producing regress. Moreover, all places together need a place,

<a id="m-iii-p275"></a>

**Source p. 275**

which would be inside them as one of them and outside them as their container. If extension cannot move, neither can the body, because its extension is inseparable from it. Second: a body's emplacement would make its extension penetrate spatial extension, yielding two extensions and coincident like qualities. Certainty that this cubit is one, and that occupant and place are originally equal, would disappear. Third: extension either needs a locus and cannot be separate, or is independent and cannot inhere. The answer is that all these arguments assume the two extensions are alike, which is denied.

The argument for extension is that a surface would not equal its occupant—as when a round body is made into a thin sheet, or conversely—and would not embrace all bodies, since the outermost body has no container. Judgements would also change: a bird stationary in blowing air exchanges surrounding surfaces and would therefore move; the moving moon does not exchange them and would therefore rest; Zayd's place remains when air fills it, though it would have to disappear. Such indications may strengthen probability without amounting to demonstration.

<a id="m-iii-p277"></a>

**Source p. 277**

SECOND QUESTION: WHETHER VOID IS POSSIBLE
Arguments for possibility follow. First: lift one smooth plate from another all at once. At the first stage the middle must be empty, since on your account air fills it only after passing the edges. The reply, even granting the possibility of lifting, denies that it can occur all at once in an instant: motion requires time. If simultaneity only means that the plate's parts rise together without breaking apart, nothing follows, since air may reach the middle during the lifting. The opponent can thus deny either the entailment or the antecedent's possibility.

Second: without void a body could not transfer between places. If what occupies the second is annihilated and another body arises in the first, this contradicts your doctrine. If it remains, interpenetration follows, or its condensation and the rarefaction of what surrounds the first place, which require matter, whose existence we shall refute; or void occurs, contrary to the supposition. If it leaves for the first place, each transfer depends upon the other, producing circularity; if for another, collisions continue without limit. Once matter has been refuted, the reply is that dependence as mutual inseparability is not impossible: both transfers may occur together, as with the spokes of a wheel. If antecedent dependence is intended, the entailment is denied.

<a id="m-iii-p279"></a>

**Source p. 279**

Third: without void every surface would meet another without limit. The reply is that the series terminates in sheer nonexistence, not an extension or space capable of occupation—the disputed void. Fourth: observation, as with a flask from which air is strongly sucked so that water rises into it, or a sealed leather bag whose sides are drawn apart without admitting air. The reply allows rarefaction of the small amount of air remaining.

The argument for impossibility is that void entails absurdities. First: resistance's presence and absence would be equivalent. Suppose a body crosses a parasang of void in an hour and a corresponding filled space in two hours. In a filled space with half the first medium's density, it would also take one hour, since variation in time follows resistance. It is objected that motion itself requires time: in the thinner medium one hour corresponds to motion itself, as in void, plus half an hour for half the original resistance.

If it is said that motion must have speed or slowness because it occurs in infinitely divisible time, then motion abstracted from both does not exist and requires nothing, whereas motion common to particulars requires no particular time—otherwise every motion would require it, even a motion occurring in part of it, which is impossible—

<a id="m-iii-p281"></a>

**Source p. 281**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.07](#c1-07).*

we reply: time may be divisible only in imagination, making motion in a part impossible. Even if divisibility is conceded, the point is that a particular motion requires a certain duration according to the mover and moved. Resistance may increase it, or it may remain unincreased, as in void.

Second: bodies could not occupy void, since no direction has priority. The reply allows specification by external causes. Third: a thrown stone would reach the heavens without resistance. The reply is that void does not entail absence of every obstacle. Fourth: observed effects would disappear: flesh rising in a cupping glass, water rising in a tube, water not descending through a hole in a vessel sealed above, and a flask containing a rod breaking outward when the rod is pushed in and inward when pulled out. The reply allows other causes.

CHAPTER THREE: QUALITY
Quality is an accident that does not intrinsically require division or relation. Some add 'or nondivision' to exclude unity and the point. Induction restricts qualities to four classes.

<a id="m-iii-p283"></a>

**Source p. 283**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.08](#c1-08).*

FIRST CLASS: SENSIBLE QUALITIES
These comprise kinds.

FIRST KIND: TANGIBLES
There are two investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
They agree that the basic tangibles are heat, cold, wetness and dryness. Their existence and nature need no explanation, although properties may be indicated: heat gathers similar things and separates dissimilar ones; cold does the reverse. Wetness enables easy adhesion and separation, or easy reception of shapes; dryness does the reverse.

Precisely, heat elevates, and the subtler more readily accepts elevation. In a composite whose simple constituents are loosely joined, heat separates dissimilar parts, incidentally gathering similar ones. In firmly joined composites it produces circular motion, as in gold; complete sublimation, as in sal ammoniac; liquefaction, as in lead; softening, as in iron; or mere warming, as in talc, according to the recipient.

'Hot' may describe what produces heat, either on contact with the body, as foods and medicines, or without that condition, as celestial things. The innate heat sustaining life is said to be fiery, celestial, or different in reality from both because its effects differ, even repelling adventitious heat.

<a id="m-iii-p285"></a>

**Source p. 285**

The reply is that this difference may come from accidents. Including adhesion in wetness's definition is challenged because honey would then be wetter than water. The answer is that easy adhesion together with easy separation is intended. Including easy shaping is challenged because fire would be wet and softness identical with wetness. The answer denies easy shaping of simple fire and distinguishes softness as admitting inward pressure while parts are difficult to separate. Whether softness and hardness are tangibles or dispositions is uncertain. Moistness, desiccation, viscosity, brittleness, subtlety and density relate to the four basic qualities. Whether smoothness and roughness are qualities is disputed.

SECOND INVESTIGATION
Iʿtimād belongs to tangibles for those identifying it with the felt pressure against what prevents motion in a direction, rather than its intellectually apprehended principle. Its kinds are sometimes numbered six according to directions. Natural pressure, however, is only upward or downward, these being genuine directions; the others are relative and change, and so are not distinct species. Restricting directions to six is customary, not exact: directions are very numerous like a body's parts, or unbounded like its divisions.

Natural pressure comprises heaviness, a quality requiring motion until a body's centre coincides with the world's centre, or toward the centre through most of the distance from the circumference without reaching it; and lightness, the converse.

<a id="m-iii-p287"></a>

**Source p. 287**

These do not reduce to wetness and dryness, or to greater and smaller numbers of bodily parts, as some say. A leather bag holds several times as much mercury by weight as water, although mercury is wetter and their parts equal; otherwise water would contain voids whose ratio to its parts matched mercury's weight relative to water's.

The Qāḍī denies multiple pressures, making one bodily quality heaviness relative to below and lightness relative to above. Some deny their contrariety because they coexist, as in a stone pulled upward and downward or a rope pulled right and left. The truth is that the two natural pressures are contraries, but a natural and a nonnatural pressure are not, as in a lifted stone. Different nonnatural pressures are said to be contraries because pressure immediately initiates motion, and two opposed pressures would entail intrinsic motions in two directions. The reply is that pressure is not the complete cause: the two coexist in a rope pulled both ways. It may be said that there is no actual pressure here, only something like a stationary thing resisting movement.

The Muʿtazilites call natural pressure inherent and other pressure acquired. They disagree whether air's upward pressure is inherent or acquired; whether inherent pressure persists, unlike acquired pressure; and whether pressure generates motion and rest. Some deny generation; others attribute to it motions, rests and other effects, some intrinsically, with or without conditions, others not intrinsically.

<a id="m-iii-p289"></a>

**Source p. 289**

The philosophers call it inclination and divide it into natural, forced and psychic. If its principle is external it is forced; otherwise psychic when accompanied by awareness and natural when not. Some restrict awareness to voluntary inclination and make psychic inclination broader, including plants' inclination to emerge and grow.

Their hesitation whether inclination is pressure itself or its principle appears in their claims: natural inclination is absent when a body is in its own region, otherwise it would incline away rather than toward it; it cannot coexist with forced inclination in another direction, because pressure toward a direction cannot coexist with withdrawal from it; but it coexists when the direction agrees, as a stone pushed downward moves faster. They say a stone's upward motion strengthens initially and weakens near its endpoint, and its downward motion the reverse: collisions progressively weaken forced inclination and strengthen natural inclination until it prevails and the projectile returns. They explain the different motions of small and large stones thrown with equal force solely through greater opposing natural inclination in the larger.

SECOND KIND: VISIBLES
These include colours and lights. Through them other qualities, indeed nonqualities such as positions, magnitudes and related things, may also be seen. There are investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
Colour has two contrary extremes, white and black, with intermediates: distinct species, indeed contraries unless maximal difference is required.

<a id="m-iii-p291"></a>

**Source p. 291**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.08](#c1-08).*

Strictly, the species is not whiteness without qualification but particular whitenesses, such as snow's and ivory's. The same applies to colours and everything predicated with ordered variation: the species among tangibles is particular heat, and among visibles particular light, not heat or light without qualification. Groups of species may share a special accident with a special name, as colours do, or lack such names, as lights do.

This rests upon denying degrees within quiddity and its essential constituents: if what accounts for the difference does not enter the quiddity, the point is established; if it does, there is no shared quiddity. An accident is offered as a counterexample. The answer is that, though the differentiating feature does not enter the accident, it enters the more intense subject's quiddity. This is questionable. Generally, either the differentiating feature's exclusion from what varies prevents variation, making the counterexample valid, or it does not, defeating the argument. Hence some deny ordered variation and intensification, while others affirm them in quiddity and its essentials, making linear quiddity more perfect in a longer line and less perfect in a shorter.

SECOND INVESTIGATION
Some deny colour's reality: whiteness is imagined from light mixing with transparent bodies, as in foam, snow and powdered crystal or glass;

<a id="m-iii-p293"></a>

**Source p. 293**

blackness from light's failing to penetrate a body, hence its attribution to water when air is expelled and light's penetration incomplete, as observed in wet cloth; other colours from variations of these two conditions. In truth these are only some causes of colour. As Ibn Sīnā says, whiteness certainly arises in the stated way, but also otherwise, as in a boiled egg, a virgin's milk and gypsum. Some deny whiteness alone because it disappears and its subject receives colours, unlike blackness; the weakness is evident. Some make white and black primary, others add red, yellow and green, deriving the rest by composition because this is observed in some cases. Particular observations plainly do not establish the universal claim.

THIRD INVESTIGATION
Light is intrinsic when derived from its subject itself, as in the sun, and is called radiance; otherwise incidental, as in the moon, and called illumination. Incidental light directly from something intrinsically luminous is first-order, like sunlight on an opposing surface; otherwise second-, third-order and so forth, like light on the earth before sunrise and light passing from a courtyard into a room, until it ceases. That absence is darkness: a privation of light, not a positive quality. Otherwise it would prevent someone in a cave from seeing outside just as it prevents the converse, since an intervening barrier makes no difference whether surrounding viewer or viewed. Darkness is not sheer negation incompatible with being made, as 'and made the darknesses and the light' indicates [Qurʾān 6:1].

<a id="m-iii-p295"></a>

**Source p. 295**

They hesitate whether what appears luminous in air is pure air or particles mixed with it. Shade is what results from air facing something luminous intrinsically, such as sun or fire, or derivatively, such as the moon. Defining it as what is received from a derivatively luminous thing is not exclusive, because it includes direct moonlight. When light shimmers upon a body as though flowing from it and almost veiling it, intrinsic light is called a ray, as in the sun, and incidental light a gleam, as in a mirror.

FOURTH INVESTIGATION
Because illumination may arise from a luminous thing above, moving, or intermediate between the illuminated thing and an intrinsically luminous source, some imagined that light itself moves downward, follows the source, or is reflected. They make it small bodies separating from source and reaching recipient. This is refuted by the unintelligibility of natural motion in different directions, motion in a moment from the solar sphere to earth while piercing the spheres, and things behind a perceptible body being clearer to healthy sight.

FIFTH INVESTIGATION
Light differs in reality from colour and is required for colour's visibility. The first is established by sensation, colours' contrariety unlike lights, and their separability in existence: nonluminous black and crystal whose light but not colour is seen at night.

<a id="m-iii-p297"></a>

**Source p. 297**

The second: colour is not seen in darkness despite other conditions being met and certainty of its existence. Some identify light with colour's manifestation: absolute manifestation is light, absolute concealment darkness, and the intermediate shade, varying with proximity to either extreme. They have no significant argument.

Ibn Sīnā makes light a condition of colour's existence: colour is unseen in darkness because absent, not because dark air obstructs sight, since someone in a cave sees things outside around a fire. The reply is that a condition of sight is absent, namely light surrounding the object. Imām al-Rāzī says that receiving light requires colour, so requiring light for colour is circular. This is weak: it is only concomitant dependence.

THIRD KIND: AUDIBLES
There are two investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
According to us, sound arises solely through God's creation, like other originated things. According to the philosophers it is a quality arising in air through undulation caused by striking or pulling apart—not the undulation, striking or separation itself, as some thought, since these are not heard. Sound's external existence and sensation's relation to it there are indicated by perceiving its direction and distinguishing near from far. This does not result merely from air arriving from that direction or from stronger near and weaker distant impacts; otherwise a sound from the side opposite the hearing ear could not be located, nor a powerful distant sound distinguished from a weak near one.

<a id="m-iii-p299"></a>

**Source p. 299**

That hearing occurs through sound-bearing air reaching the auditory passage is indicated by sound's deflection with winds, one person's exclusively hearing speech through a tube terminating at that person's ear, and hearing lagging behind seeing the cause, as with distant axe blows. Such signs may yield intuitive certainty without serving as proof for another. Yet it would not be unreasonable to doubt whether qualities of this kind inhere in all the air's parts and retain their arrangements through winds and passages in solid bodies. Likewise their return after striking a smooth body, as proposed for echo, whether the returning air itself reaches the ear or other air takes on its quality, as seems apparent.

SECOND INVESTIGATION
A sound may bear a quality distinguishing it audibly from sounds equal in pitch: a letter or speech sound. These divide into consonants and vowels, short vowels being vowel-movements and long vowels prolongations. 'Movement' here is the quality produced by inclining a consonant's articulation toward one of the long vowels: toward wāw gives ḍamma, alif fatḥa, and yāʾ kasra. Beginning with a vowel is intrinsically impossible, not because it is unvowelled. Beginning with an unvowelled consonant is certainly possible, though some languages disallow it, just as they disallow stopping on a vowelled consonant or combining two unvowelled consonants, except where the organ is deficient.

<a id="m-iii-p301"></a>

**Source p. 301**

Speech sounds also divide into instantaneous, such as ṭāʾ, and temporal, such as fāʾ; alike, as two unvowelled bāʾ sounds; and different intrinsically, as bāʾ and tāʾ, or incidentally, as vowelled and unvowelled bāʾ or bāʾ with ḍamma and fatḥa.

A consonant with a short vowel is a short syllable, such as la; with a long vowel, a long syllable, such as lā. 'Long syllable' may also name a short syllable followed by an unvowelled consonant, such as hal: two consonants with a short vowel between. By contrast lā consists only of consonant and long vowel, without an intervening short vowel as Arabic analysis supposes: the long vowel is merely prolongation of the short, which is included as part of it.

Letters form discourse, divided into simple and compound with their subdivisions, also called utterance. 'Discourse' may be restricted to what conveys meaning, even one syllable, such as qi and qī; 'utterance' to what comprises syllables. In that usage it contrasts with letter and syllable: a compound's parts are utterances, letters or syllables. Al-Fārābī places utterance under quantity, whose whole can be measured by part of it, since every utterance is measured by short or long syllables or their combinations. The reply is that this belongs to it incidentally, as with body.

FOURTH KIND: TASTABLES
These are tastes, with nine roots. Heat produces pungency in the subtle, bitterness in the dense and saltiness in the intermediate; cold produces sourness in the subtle, astringency in the dense and constriction in the intermediate; the temperate produces oiliness in the subtle, sweetness in the dense and insipidity in the intermediate. Innumerable kinds arise through their combinations.

<a id="m-iii-p303"></a>

**Source p. 303**

FIFTH KIND: SMELLABLES
These are odours. Their kinds have names only through pleasantness or offensiveness, such as fragrant and fetid; association, such as sweet-smelling; or attribution, such as musk's odour.

SECOND CLASS: QUALITIES OF THE SOUL
These are peculiar to beings possessing animal souls. When firmly established they are called dispositions; otherwise states.

LIFE
In observable beings life is a power requiring sensation and motion, meaning a principle of their powers. Hence the definition: a power following a species' balanced constitution from which other powers flow—that is, animal powers. It differs from the powers of sensation and motion because principle and what derives from it differ; and from nutritive power because plants possess nutrition without life. Thus a paralysed or withered organ retains life and its effect without sensation and motion or nourishment.

According to us neither balanced temperament, bodily structure nor animal spirit is required: God can certainly create life in an indivisible part. The philosophers and Muʿtazilites disagree, relying on life's observed disappearance when structure breaks down or balance and spirit are lost. Some of us argue that, if structure were required, either one life would inhere in two parts, making an accident inhere in multiple loci, or each part would have a life,

<a id="m-iii-p305"></a>

**Source p. 305**

in which case mutual dependence would be circular and one-sided dependence preference without a determining ground. The reply is that life inheres in the aggregate, as explained; even if separate lives are conceded, the circle is concomitant dependence; even if that is denied, absence of a determining ground is unproved—at most it is unknown.

Death is life's cessation: its absence from what possessed it, or a quality contrary to it. The latter apparently explains calling it an act of God or an angel requiring bodily life to cease without a wound, excluding killing. 'Act' means effect, since the causal influence is causing death. On the first account, creating death means determining it or creating its causes.

APPREHENSION
This is explained through investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
When we apprehend something, it becomes distinct and manifest to the intellect. This is not through external existence: often it is apprehended without existing or exists without being apprehended, but through intellectual existence, what is meant by a form. Apprehension's reality is therefore the thing's presence to the intellect, either through its reality, as the soul apprehends itself and its attributes—considerational difference suffices, as one physician treats himself; knowledge of knowledge is similar, so no infinite existents follow—or through its form, abstracted as with material things, or not abstracted as with immaterial and nonexistent things.

<a id="m-iii-p307"></a>

**Source p. 307**

Because a thing's form differs from its identity, an apprehender need not possess the blackness, heat, roundness or other qualities apprehended. Moreover, a form's presence to the soul is not an accident's presence in a substance. Even apprehending intellectual meanings—faith, unbelief, generosity and miserliness—does not require the soul to possess them. The discussion concerns the converse; how much less should cognition resemble blackness or whiteness inhering in a body.

This is not invalidated by the fact that what is seen is this blackness, not its image, or what is understood human being, not its form; by assigning apprehension to soul or common sense although the form occurs in the crystalline humour; or by the form's occurring there without apprehension when the soul does not attend.

Although the cognitive form is an accident through its occurrence in the intellect, the quiddity apprehended may be substance, meaning that if externally existent it would not be in a subject. Likewise the form is particular through inherence in a particular soul and universal through relation to individuals. Occurrence relates to the form in the intellect as existence relates to quiddity externally: it is additional only intellectually. Hence knowledge may be identified with the form. Insofar as occurrence is identical with it, it is knowledge and an externally existent accident like other psychic qualities; insofar as occurrence is additional, it is a concept actual only in minds. The known is that whose form it is, not the form itself, unless a new act of intellection takes the form as object. It then acquires determinations called second intelligibles; in this respect universality genuinely qualifies the known.

Theologians denying mental existence make apprehension a relation between apprehender and apprehended, or an attribute bearing a relation to the apprehended.

<a id="m-iii-p309"></a>

**Source p. 309**

They face knowledge of nonexistents and impossibilities, since relation to what has no actuality is inconceivable. Forms must then be affirmed throughout, apprehension being one meaning. If it is objected that sheer nonexistence can no more have a form than a relation, and making it a form of something mental introduces two mental items, form and bearer, which is plainly false, we reply: the nonexistent has only a form, meaning non-original existence. Through inherence in mind it is knowledge; intrinsically it is known. Existent things differ: knowledge is mental and the known external.

Ibn Sīnā says that the impossible has no intellectual form. Its conception occurs by analogy—conceiving conjunction between blackness and sweetness and judging that nothing similar is possible between blackness and whiteness—or by negation, judging that the concept conjunction does not obtain between them. Perhaps this is what Abū Hāshim intended by affirming knowledge without a known object.

SECOND INVESTIGATION
Apprehension has four kinds: sensation, imagination, estimation and intellection. Sensation requires present matter, accompanying configurations and a particular object. Imagination dispenses with the first, estimation with the first two, intellection with all three.

According to the Shaykh, sensation is knowledge of its object. If this means it differs from other knowledge only in object and route, it is refuted by the difference we experience between complete knowledge and sensation of a thing. If sensations are meant as species of knowledge, the issue is verbal, resting on calling all apprehension knowledge;

<a id="m-iii-p311"></a>

**Source p. 311**

whereas 'knowledge' is used for apprehension other than sensation. It may be restricted to intellection, or to apprehending a composite, with apprehending a particular or simple thing called recognition; or to firm, true and stable assent. Assent lacking firmness is probable judgement, lacking truth compound ignorance, and lacking stability belief. Correspondence to reality may also be disregarded in the last. Doubt, being hesitation in judgement, and weaker supposition, consideration of the less probable alternative, are wrongly counted as assent. If doubt means judging the alternatives equal, it belongs to one of the preceding divisions.

Inattention that removes the cognitive form so that acquisition is needed is forgetting; otherwise heedlessness. Simple ignorance is privation of knowledge, compound ignorance contrary to it because it satisfies the definition. The Muʿtazilites call compound ignorance similar to knowledge, differing only through the accidental features of truth and falsity. Knowledge becomes ignorance while remaining itself, as when one believes Zayd standing throughout the day although he sits during part. The answer is that an accident may be inseparable, so different accidents indicate different realities. The cognition's remaining identical is itself disputed.

THIRD INVESTIGATION
Knowledge divides into beginningless and originated. Originated knowledge has three levels: (1) pure potentiality or preparedness, arising through the senses for necessary knowledge and through necessary knowledge for theoretical knowledge;

<a id="m-iii-p313"></a>

**Source p. 313**

(2) actuality in a comprehensive way, with one simple thing serving as principle of the details; (3) detailed actuality, considering the parts separately, as when viewing a page at once and then letter by letter. Comprehensive knowledge involves one form matching the whole, not each individual; detailed knowledge involves multiple forms. This answers the Imām's objection that one form cannot match different things, while multiple forms would already be detailed—unless 'detailed' means their ordered occurrence and 'comprehensive' their simultaneous occurrence.

FOURTH INVESTIGATION
It is said that no one disputes theoretical knowledge's becoming necessary through God's creation. The Qāḍī permits the converse because cognitions are homogeneous: relations and individuations are accidents not required by their essence, so whatever is possible for one is possible for another, as with humanity in Zayd and in ʿAmr. Whoever concedes homogeneity but insists on specific difference has overlooked what homogeneity means. The majority prohibit the change absolutely, since necessary knowledge cannot be absent while attention is directed to it; some prohibit it only for necessary cognitions conditioning investigation, to avoid circularity. The dispute about necessary knowledge depending on theoretical knowledge appears verbal.

FIFTH INVESTIGATION
Does originated knowledge multiply with its objects? The Shaykh and many Muʿtazilites say yes, because relation enters its constitution.

<a id="m-iii-p315"></a>

**Source p. 315**

Others deny this, making relation extrinsic, as with beginningless knowledge. The issue thus depends on the definition of knowledge. Some require multiplicity when both objects are theoretical, lest two investigations meet in one cognition. The reply allows both objects to be obtained through one investigation, just as known through one cognition; this reply is weak. The Qāḍī and Imām require multiplicity when knowing either object can occur independently of knowing the other; otherwise a thing could separate from itself. The reply is that objects may sometimes be known through one cognition and sometimes two.

Where multiple cognitions occur, those of two objects differ even if the objects are alike; those of one object are alike. Some qualify this by sameness of time: otherwise the cognitions differ because the object necessarily differs with time.

SIXTH INVESTIGATION
Revealed evidence makes the heart the locus of knowledge, although God may create it in any substance He wills. Apparently 'heart' does not mean the bodily organ. The philosophers assign knowledge to the rational soul, with instruments mediating knowledge of particulars. Further explanation follows later.

SEVENTH INVESTIGATION
The intellect on which moral responsibility rests is, according to the Shaykh, knowledge of certain necessities. Others make it the power arising then through which theoretical knowledge can be acquired: the innate faculty followed by necessary knowledge when the instruments are sound, and the power discriminating good and bad things.

<a id="m-iii-p317"></a>

**Source p. 317**

WILL
There are two investigations.

FIRST INVESTIGATION
Its meaning appears clear to the intellect. Will differs from appetite: a person may will what is not desired, or desire what is not willed. Most Muʿtazilites identify it with belief in benefit or an inclination following that belief. According to us that is not even a condition of will, much less its identity: someone fleeing a beast takes one of two roads without believing one beneficial or having a consequent inclination.

Our scholars' definition of will as an attribute through which an agent prefers one of two possible acts, doing or refraining, establishes neither difference from belief or inclination nor that its object must be within the agent's power, which would invalidate the claim that will and aversion may each have a will or aversion as object.

The philosophers, making the Necessary Existent necessitating and recognising the objectionableness of denying Him will, identify will with a kind of knowledge: knowledge of what is perfection and good for the knower, or the agent's knowing its action when that knowledge causes the action without being overcome or compelled. They also give a broader meaning: an inclinational state occurring in humans and others.

SECOND INVESTIGATION
According to the Shaykh, willing a thing is identical with aversion to its contrary. Otherwise will would be contrary or similar to that aversion and could not coexist with it, or merely different and could coexist with its opposite, namely willing the contrary.

<a id="m-iii-p319"></a>

**Source p. 319**

The reply, even if one grants that one of these alternatives must follow, is that two different things may be inseparable concomitants, or may both be contraries of a single thing. If it followed that each could coexist with the contrary of the other, the coexistence of mutually incompatible things would consequently be possible.

Against the argument it is urged that something may be willed without awareness of its contrary. Even supposing awareness of the contrary, there is no proof of an implication between the two—although the Qāḍī affirms one—much less of their identity.

### Power

Its exposition comprises several investigations.

#### First investigation

A force is an attribute that is a principle of change in another thing, insofar as that thing is other. It either accompanies intention or does not; in either case its effects are either diverse or not. The first is animal force, the second celestial force, the third vegetative force, and the fourth elemental force. The discussion does not concern specific forms and souls, since these belong to the category of substances.

What is taken into account in a force's being a power is either its accompanying intention or the diversity of its effects. Thus power has been described as an attribute that acts in accordance with will, or that is a principle of diverse actions. A force possessing both characteristics, such as animal force, is unanimously recognised as power. One lacking both, such as the elemental forces, is unanimously recognised as not being power. A force possessing only one of them, such as celestial or vegetative force, is disputed. What is meant is a preparedness to produce an effect, so that, on our position, temporally originated power is included. Hence power has also been described as an attribute by which one is enabled to act and to refrain.

<a id="m-iii-p321"></a>

**Source p. 321**

Immediate awareness attests both to power and to its being an attribute distinct from temperament and sound bodily constitution, present in some beings but not others, and extending to some acts but not others.

#### Second investigation

Temporally originated power for an act does not exist before that act, contrary to the Muʿtazila. Our argument is that it is an accident, and therefore does not persist until the time of the act, unlike beginningless power. Moreover, before its existence the act is not possible, because existence together with nonexistence is impossible, and because supposing its occurrence entails a contradiction.

The first argument is answered—even if the impossibility of an accident's persistence is granted—by explaining that what is meant is a preceding power that continues through the successive renewal of its likes, as do knowledge, inclination, wishing, and similar things which, by agreement, precede the act.

The second is countered by beginningless power. Its resolution is that what is impossible and entails a contradiction is the act's existence on the condition of its nonexistence, not its existence at the time of its nonexistence, with existence being supposed in place of nonexistence.

The Muʿtazila argue that, if power related to the act only when the act occurred, this would entail bringing the already existent into existence, the impossibility of imposing obligation, and the beginninglessness of the effects of beginningless power.

The first objection is answered by what has already been said. To the second, we reply that we do not require the act imposed as an obligation to be actually an object of power, but only possibly so—as with an unbeliever's belief, in contrast to the creation of bodies. To the third, we deny that the two powers are alike.

<a id="m-iii-p323"></a>

**Source p. 323**

Consequences of power's accompanying the act are that someone prevented from performing an act has no power for it, like a person with a disabling infirmity, and that a single power does not relate to two objects of power, even when they are not contraries.

The Muʿtazila say that the difference between a person in restraints and one with a disabling infirmity is immediately evident. How could it be otherwise, when restraint involves no replacement of an essence or attribute, nor the advent of a contrary to power? They agree that power relates to similar acts, though at different times. Some permit its relation to contrary acts alternatively. Abū Hāshim vacillated: sometimes he allowed each of the heart's power and the bodily organs' power to relate to its own objects but not those of the other; sometimes he allowed each to relate to the objects of both, without producing an effect on the other's objects; and sometimes he restricted these two judgements to the heart's power.

The truth is that, if by power one means the force that is the principle of acts either by bringing them into existence—in which case it is called effecting power—or through the customary course of events—in which case it is called acquisitive power—it is present before, during, and after the act. It relates to two objects of power, and its relation to the two contraries is equal. If, however, one means the force possessing all the conditions for producing the effect in either of these two ways, it accompanies the act and does not relate to two objects of power, since the conditions differ with the objects.

#### Third investigation

Incapacity is the contrary of power, rather than its privation, as Abū Hāshim holds. For we find a difference between someone with a disabling infirmity and someone prevented from acting, although both lack power. He can, however, deny that the person prevented from acting lacks power; or he can locate the difference in the latter's being such as to possess power, unlike the person with a disabling infirmity.

<a id="m-iii-p325"></a>

**Source p. 325**

A consequence of their being contraries is the view reported from the Shaykh—although contrary to what is apparent—that the object of incapacity is what exists. Thus a person with a disabling infirmity is incapable of sitting, in the sense that there is in him an attribute followed by sitting without power.

This is refuted by our certainty that the incapacity of those challenged concerns only their producing something like the Qurʾān. To maintain that the word is equivocal between that attribute and the absence of power is contrary to linguistic usage.

Whether sleep is contrary to power is uncertain: some acts may issue from the sleeper, while most cannot. Character-disposition is contrary to it insofar as it is a settled disposition through which acts issue from the soul easily and without deliberation, and insofar as its relation to the two alternatives is unequal.

### Pleasure and pain

Both are immediately evident. They may be explained as the apprehension of what is agreeable or disagreeable, insofar as it is such. On this account they are two kinds of apprehension in which account is taken of a relation that varies with the apprehender, and of actual contact with the agreeable or disagreeable thing itself, rather than its form.

The truth is that we find a state which is pleasure, and know that there is an apprehension of what is agreeable. Whether pleasure is this apprehension itself or something produced by it, and whether it can arise without it, remain uncertain.

Pleasure is sometimes explained as departure from an unnatural state. This is refuted by the pleasure of acquiring wealth or contemplating beauty without prior seeking or longing.

Pleasure and pain are each divided into sensory and intellectual, according to the apprehension involved. Intellectual pleasure and pain are stronger, because intelligibles are more numerous and the intellect's apprehension more perfect. Both belong to psychic qualities,

<a id="m-iii-p327"></a>

**Source p. 327**

since in sensory pleasure or pain what is sensed is that in which one takes pleasure or by which one suffers, not pleasure or pain itself. Sensory pain, especially tactile pain, is called aching.

Ibn Sīnā confines its causes to a disruption of continuity and to a disordered temperament that is dissimilar [to the organ's established temperament] and is hot or cold, since moisture and dryness are passive qualities. Dryness may indeed cause pain accidentally, because its severe constriction entails a disruption of continuity. Moisture does not: the stretching that follows from it is due to the matter. Nor does a conformable disorder cause pain—meaning one that has become established in the organ's substance, eliminated its resistance, and acquired the status of its original temperament. For a condition of a sense's being affected by a sensible object is a difference in quality.

The Imām al-Rāzī objects that disruption is privative; that the continuous processes of nourishment and dissolution involve much disruption in the organs without pain; and that pain may follow disruption only after a delay, as in cutting with an extremely sharp instrument. The reply is that disruption is the movement of some parts away from others. Furthermore, what is impossible is causation by the nonexistent, not by something privative, especially in a preparatory cause. What is meant is that a perceptible degree of disruption in a sensitive organ, accompanied by awareness and the soul's attention, without habituation and continuity, and apprehended insofar as it is disagreeable, is painful—even if through its entailing the loss of the organ's configuration and its appropriate perfection. On this understanding no difficulty remains.

### Health and disease

Ibn Sīnā defines health as a settled disposition or a state from which the acts of its subject issue soundly. He means that its genus is psychic quality, whether firmly established or less than firmly established, unlike those who restrict health to the firmly established kind.

<a id="m-iii-p329"></a>

**Source p. 329**

Thus in the *Shifāʾ* he says: a settled disposition in the animal body by reason of which its acts issue without impairment. He places settled disposition first because it is nobler, more prevalent, and unanimously recognised as health. The Imām's claim that the definition includes the health of plants overlooks the meaning of settled disposition and state. As for Ibn Sīnā's restriction to human beings in saying that health is a configuration by which the human body, in its temperament and composition, is such that all its acts issue correctly and soundly, this is because human health is what medicine investigates. Correctness and soundness have their ordinary linguistic meanings, as is indicated by their predication of acts; there is therefore no circularity. The indication, by the two prepositions “from” [ʿan] and “from” [min], that both the state and its subject are principles rests on the first being efficient and the second material, or on the first being instrumental and the second efficient.

As for disease, he sometimes makes it a settled disposition or state contrary to health, and sometimes a privation of health. This is because the word may designate the loss of health, or may designate the principle of impairment in acts that arises upon that loss. If disease and health are contraries, both belong to the genus of psychic quality. In enumerating their kinds, however, things may be mentioned suggesting that both, or disease alone, belong to sensible qualities or to something other than qualities. This is a loose manner of speaking.

If what is considered in disease is the absence of soundness in all the acts, no intermediate state is established; if it is impairment of every act, an intermediate state is established.

Other psychic qualities include joy, distress, anger, fear, sorrow, anxiety, and the like. There is no discussion of these here.

<a id="m-iii-p331"></a>

**Source p. 331**

### Third class: qualities peculiar to quantities

These are qualities whose inherence in something is inconceivable except through its continuous quantity—such as straightness, curvature, concavity, convexity, and angle—or through its discrete quantity, such as evenness and oddness.

Physical appearance, meaning the combination of shape and colour, is sometimes counted among them. Shape is peculiar to quantity because it is the configuration of a body's enclosure by a boundary or boundaries; colour likewise, according to those who restrict it to a surface. This particular composite is accorded recognition because it possesses a unity by virtue of which a person is described as beautiful or ugly. Some hold that shape belongs to position, and angle to quantity because it admits division. Angle is accordingly explained as a surface enclosed by two lines meeting at a point without becoming one line. What is intended is the portion of the surface adjacent to that point, as is made explicit by those who call it the convex portion of that surface.

The reply is that its admitting division need not belong to it essentially. Indeed, it lacks a necessary property of quantity, namely that multiplication does not abolish it. For this reason angle has been explained as the configuration of the two lines' enclosure of the surface at their meeting point.

### Fourth class: dispositional qualities

These are either a strong preparedness to be affected, as in susceptibility to illness and softness, called lack of strength; or a strong preparedness to resist and not be affected, as in resistance to illness and hardness, called strength. Their common feature is a quality by which a recipient is disposed more strongly toward one of the two alternatives in what it can receive.

<a id="m-iii-p333"></a>

**Source p. 333**

It is said: or a preparedness to act, as in wrestling; the common feature would then be a fully developed bodily preparedness for something external.

This is answered in two ways. First, wrestling, for example, involves knowledge of a craft and power to perform acts, both psychic qualities, and hardness in the limbs, which returns to the first account. Second, heat is a strong power to burn, although it belongs to sensible qualities. Both replies rest on the four classes of quality being essentially distinct.

## Chapter Four: Place—the category “where”

This is being situated in a spatial locus. It is approached by two routes.

### The first route: that of the theologians

It comprises two investigations.

#### First investigation

The existence of spatial situatedness [kawn] is immediately evident. Its kinds are four. For a substance's occupancy of a spatial locus, if considered in relation to another substance, is separation when a third can intervene between them—with its kinds differing in nearness and distance—and otherwise is conjunction. Its names include adjacency and, on the more plausible account, contact. It inheres in one substance, since each of the two substances has a conjunction inhering in it. If occupancy is not considered in relation to another substance, it is rest when preceded by occupancy of that same locus, and motion when preceded by occupancy of another. Rest is thus a second occupancy of a first locus, and motion a first occupancy of a second locus.

<a id="m-iii-p335"></a>

**Source p. 335**

Occupancy at the first moment of origination, however, is neither motion nor rest, so the division is not exhaustive.

The Qāḍī and Abū Hāshim say that it is rest, since it is like the second occupancy. This entails that motion is a collection of rests, since the first situatedness in the second locus is also rest. This consequence has been accepted, to the point of saying that every motion is rest, though not conversely. The contrariety would be between rest in a locus and motion away from it, not motion toward it, since the latter is identical with that rest.

The objection is that, if this were correct, the second occupancy of the second locus would have to be motion just as the first is. Saying that not being preceded by occupancy of that locus is included in the concept of motion does not avert the consequence.

The precise account is that a moving thing has, between the two ends of a distance, occupancies that continue without settling. If motion means what is actually realised of these, it is occupancy after occupancy of another locus. If it means the imagined extension from beginning to end, it is the successive occupancies of successive loci. If “rest” is then made a name for occupancy without a requirement of remaining, motion is rest or a collection of rests; if remaining is required, it is not. In the latter case there is uncertainty whether rest is the second occupancy or the combination of the two occupancies.

The truth, then, is that the reality of spatial situatedness is one in all four kinds; their distinction is only through aspects of consideration. A single individual instance may even be conjunction, separation, motion, and rest under different considerations. Those who state without qualification that spatial states are contraries mean spatial states distinct in existence. These cannot coexist, because two such states either determine the substance to a single locus,

<a id="m-iii-p337"></a>

**Source p. 337**

in which case they are alike, or to two loci, in which case they are contraries, since a substance's being in two loci at one instant is necessarily impossible. In either case the two do not coexist. This rests on contact not being among spatial states. Otherwise their coexistence is obvious, as with a substance surrounded by six substances. To deny this, as is reported of some, is obstinate denial.

#### Second investigation

The truth is that the internal parts of a moving body are moving, and that what rests on the ground or remains stationary in the atmosphere is at rest when water or air is replaced around it. Reason and ordinary usage agree on this. The dispute in the first case returns to the dispute over the spatial locus of an internal part. In the second it returns to whether a spatial locus is an independently extended dimension or the inner boundary of the containing body, and whether motion occurs through the locus's departing from what occupies it—in which case a single motion could have different directions at one time—or must occur through the occupant's departing from its locus, which would exclude this.

The opponent does not mean, “I make the word spatial locus, or motion, a name for this meaning.” Rather, he means that the reality for which the name was originally instituted is this. The dispute is therefore not merely over naming.

### The second route: that of the philosophers

It comprises several investigations.

#### First investigation

“Where” is proper when the spatial locus does not exceed the thing, as with water's being in a jug; otherwise it is improper, as with Zayd's being in a house, a town, or the world. It may be generic, specific, or individual, as with something's being in a place, in air, or in this house. It admits contrariety, as with above and below, and intensification, as with being more completely above.

<a id="m-iii-p339"></a>

**Source p. 339**

#### Second investigation

Motion has been described as emergence from potentiality into actuality gradually, little by little, or not all at once. This rests on the concepts of these expressions—whose content is nonstable continuity—being immediately evident, without depending on the conception of time, which itself depends on the conception of motion; otherwise circularity would result.

It has also been described as the first perfection of what is potential insofar as it is potential. “Perfection” means the attainment of what was not previously attained. “First” excludes arrival, since arrival is attained second and proceeding toward it first. The qualification of potentiality indicates that what undergoes motion must have a sought end toward which it proceeds, and that something of this must remain potential. The qualification “insofar as” indicates that motion is a perfection of the moving thing specifically with respect to an arrival that it possesses potentially; this excludes its perfections that are not of this kind, such as squareness.

The purpose is to elucidate and establish the meaning called motion without qualification, rather than to distinguish it and make it conceivable to the intellect. It is therefore no objection that the defining expression is less evident, or that in circular motion the two perfections—proceeding and arriving—depend merely on supposition and consideration. For the body's state in relation to each point, insofar as it seeks that point, is proceeding, and insofar as it is present at it, is arrival.

The content of this meaning is a quality through which the body has a continuous intermediacy between beginning and end, whose earlier part does not coexist with its later part, and through which the body occupies a locus after having occupied another. Its reality is one thing, continuous in itself, and divisible by supposition in correspondence with distance and time. “Motion” may also be used for the imagined continuous whole extending between beginning and end. This has no existence among external things: before arrival it is incomplete, and at arrival it has already passed away.

<a id="m-iii-p341"></a>

**Source p. 341**

The existence of motion in the first sense, however, is immediately evident. The absence of what has passed and what is yet to come, together with the absence of the present—on the argument that, if the present were indivisible, an indivisible part would follow, while if divisible the argument would recur—does not entail absolute nonexistence. How could it, when what has passed is what has ceased after existing, and what is to come is what is about to exist?

#### Third investigation

Motion must have: (1) that from which it proceeds, its beginning; (2) that toward which it proceeds, its end; (3) that in which it occurs, the category; (4) that through which it occurs, the mover; (5) that to which it belongs, the moving thing; and (6) time. This relation to time differs from the relation of the motion from which time derives: there motion is like what is followed, whereas here it is like what follows.

(1–2) As for beginning and end, each stands in correlation to the motion and in contrariety to the other. Their bearers are consequently contraries, even if identical in themselves, as in circular motion; or they may also be contrary in themselves, as in motion from whiteness to blackness; or contrary through some other incidental consideration, as in motion from the centre to the circumference.

(3) As for the category, there are four. The first is “where,” which is evident. The second is position, as in a sphere's motion about itself: its positions change without its leaving its place.

If it is said, “Each part has local motion, since its places necessarily change; therefore the whole does too,”

<a id="m-iii-p343"></a>

**Source p. 343**

we reply: even if actual parts are granted, the judgement applying to every part need not apply to the whole.

If it is said, “On this basis we do not grant the whole's motion and change of position either; these belong only to the parts,” we reply: this is immediately evident.

The third is quantity. Transition in it is either from decrease to increase through the addition of matter, which is growth, or without such addition, which is rarefaction; or conversely, through the separation of matter, which is withering, or without it, which is condensation.

Rarefaction and condensation may also designate expansion and compacting: the infiltration of air as parts move apart, and its opposite. An increase in magnitude through the addition of matter may occur without a natural proportion, which is swelling; or in a natural proportion but not in all dimensions, which is fattening, opposed to emaciation.

The fourth is quality, as with grapes' becoming black and water's becoming hot, with certainty that the quality was neither latent within the thing nor came into it from elsewhere.

The truth is that, finding a body to have passed from one quantity or quality to another without doing so all at once, they imagined a motion. In reality there is no motion here, since the quantities and qualities between the two extremes are actually distinct, unlike parts of a distance, and transition to each occurs all at once—like earth becoming water, then air, then fire.

<a id="m-iii-p345"></a>

**Source p. 345**

To establish this precisely: if the intermediate is one, there is no motion; if many, it is finite, since it is necessarily bounded by two limits. Motion would then consist of indivisible parts, which is impossible, since it entails the existence of the indivisible substance and that slowness consists in interspersed rests. Local motion is different: its intermediate is actually one, admitting infinitely many divisions by supposition.

There is no established proof of motion in the remaining categories.

As for the moving thing, if motion belongs to it in reality, it moves essentially, as with a ship's motion; otherwise it moves accidentally, as with the motion of its passenger.

As for the mover, if it is external to the moving thing's essence, the motion is forced. Otherwise, if accompanied by intention and awareness, it is voluntary; if not, it is natural.

Breathing is voluntary insofar as its particular instances can be shifted from their times, although natural insofar as there is a need for breathing in general. Growth is natural, as is the pulse. It is not implausible for their directions to differ when their ends differ. The statement that natural motion proceeds only in one manner, ascending or descending, concerns the simple elemental bodies. Some make such motion as the pulse a further class, calling it subordinated motion.

The cause of natural motion is neither the shared corporeality nor the particular nature without qualification, but the particular nature when an agreeable state has been lost. The thing then moves in pursuit of that state. Such states differ, and therefore the directions of motion differ. Pursuit here means natural orientation toward the state, and does not entail will.

<a id="m-iii-p347"></a>

**Source p. 347**

#### Fourth investigation

Motion's relation to that in which, that from which, and that toward which it occurs is nearly essential: a difference in these entails a difference in quiddity. Its relation to the remaining things is accidental: a difference in these entails only a difference in individual identity. The mover is an exception, since a difference in mover does not impair motion's continuous identity, which is essentially one, although a plurality may be imagined in it through its relations to movers. Hence its specific unity depends on the unity of the first three things, and its individual unity on the unity of everything except the mover.

The claim that motion's generic unity depends on the unity of that in which it occurs—so that motion in each category constitutes a highest genus, descending thereafter in the order of the category's genera—rests on motion in general not being a genus of what occurs in each category, but being predicated of these only by gradation or equivocation.

Motion's contrariety results from the contrariety of that from which and that toward which it proceeds, either essentially, as in a black thing's becoming white and a white thing's becoming black, or accidentally, as in ascent and descent, according to the above-ness and below-ness attaching to two points. It is also said to result from their being beginning and end; this would entail contrariety between every motion and its reverse, even in circular motion.

They have stated that positional motions are not contraries, even eastward and westward motions, because each performs the same thing as the other, but alternately in the two halves. Perhaps contrariety will be accepted if a difference in quiddity and the utmost opposition are established.

Motion divides through the division of time, which is evident; through the division of that in which it occurs, since motion to half the distance,

<a id="m-iii-p349"></a>

**Source p. 349**

or half the quantity attained by growth, or one of two intermediate qualities in a white thing's becoming black, is half the motion to the whole; and through the division of that to which it belongs, since the motion inhering in each part of the moving thing differs from that inhering in another. In local motion this becomes actual only if separation occurs in the part, since the parts, especially the internal ones, do not leave their respective “wheres.”

#### Fifth investigation

Among motion's concomitants is a quality admitting intensification and diminution, differing according to comparison, called speed in respect of its intensity and slowness in respect of its weakness.

The cause of slowness is internal resistance in motion other than natural motion, and external resistance in every motion, rather than interspersed rests. There are several arguments.

First, interspersed rests would make it impossible for two motions to accompany one another while their distances differ. For this would entail differing rests, incompatible with the motions' accompanying one another. The consequence is false, as in the motion of the sun and the apparent motion of a shadow, or the motion of the different extremities of a millstone, and similar cases.

Second, the absence of motion when its determinant is present and its impediment absent is necessarily false.

Third, the excess of the rests of a swiftly running horse over its motions would then equal the excess of the motions of the outermost sphere over the horse's motions. It would follow that the horse appeared continuously at rest, its motions being submerged, or at least appeared at rest for a period many thousands of times the period of motion. For a body, when at rest, is certainly seen as being at rest, even if rest is privative.

The first is answered by saying that the two motions' accompanying one another is customary, not rationally necessary, so their separation is not impossible.

<a id="m-iii-p351"></a>

**Source p. 351**

The second is answered by saying that motions arise solely through God's creation, without any efficacy belonging to forces. The third is answered by saying that motions and rests are intermingled so that sense cannot distinguish their times; the motions, being positive and continually renewed, overwhelm the rests, however numerous.

Speed and slowness each admit intensification and diminution. Whether this terminates at a limit is uncertain. The Imām inclines to the first alternative, although the second accords better with their principles.

#### Sixth investigation

Some maintain that between every two rectilinear motions there is a rest, since the instant of arrival necessarily differs from the instant of return. If no time intervened, two instants would be immediately successive, entailing the existence of an indivisible part. Since there is no motion between arrival and return, rest must occur.

The answer, even granting the impossibility of an indivisible part, is that there is no actual instant unless time is interrupted—unless by an instant one means a time divisible only in imagination. In that case we grant neither that the instant of arrival differs from the instant of return nor that the immediate succession of two instants is impossible.

One may counter with every rectilinear motion—especially when a sphere is rolled along a surface—since the instant of arrival at each point differs from the instant of departure from it, entailing either successive instants or an intervening time of rest. This may be answered by saying that here the division of the distance is purely imagined.

<a id="m-iii-p353"></a>

**Source p. 353**

Al-Jubbāʾī maintains that a stone rises because its acquired inclination predominates over its inherent inclination, and falls when the reverse occurs. Between them there must be an equilibrium requiring rest, since predominance without a determinant is impossible.

The answer is that, if equilibrium is granted, it occurs at the instant of arrival. The necessity of rest in the sense of an absence of motion, even for an instant, is not disputed.

The opponent argues that, if this rest were necessary, impossibilities would follow. First, it would persist because the equilibrium persisted, on the assumption that the forced inclination weakened only through the resistance of the air traversed. Second, rest would occur without a cause, since it is not natural, and force and will are assumed absent. Third, it would occur in no determinate duration, since a duration shorter than any supposed duration would suffice. Fourth, a descending mountain would stop upon encountering an ascending mustard seed.

The first is answered by saying that the natural inclination progressively strengthens and the forced inclination weakens through their own natures; hence a stone's fall is stronger near the earth. The second is answered by saying that the equilibrium of the two forces itself acts as a constraint. The third is answered by saying that rest occurs in a time admitting no actual division. The fourth is answered by saying that the mustard seed reverses its course on colliding with the mountain's air before the mountain meets it; moreover, the mountain's stopping is improbable, not impossible.

<a id="m-iii-p355"></a>

**Source p. 355**

#### Seventh investigation

A body may have two motions in one direction, and then recede from its starting point by their combined amount; or in two opposite directions, and then recede by the excess of one over the other, if there is an excess, otherwise remaining at rest; or in two directions that are not opposite, and then recede in both according to the amount of the two motions. It may have motions in several directions, taking an intermediate course in proportion to those motions.

#### Eighth investigation

Rest in “where” is the preservation of spatial relations, and in the other categories the preservation of the species; it is therefore contrary to motion. It is also said to be the absence of motion in what is such as to move, and thus a privation. It is opposed both to motion away from it and to motion toward it, unless “opposite” is restricted to that which supervenes upon rest or upon which rest supervenes. Rests are contrary because that in which they occur is contrary, as with rest in an upper and a lower place, or in heat and cold. Rest may be natural, forced, or voluntary. The ground of natural rest is nature without qualification. Composition is inconceivable in rest. A human being's rest in a place is natural; the effect of will is to refrain from removing it.

## Chapter Five: The remaining relational accidents

One is relation [iḍāfa]: a repeated relation, that is, one conceivable only with reference to a relation itself conceived with reference to the first. This is called the proper relative; the composite of it and its bearer is called the commonly recognised relative. Both are included in the description “that whose quiddity is conceived only with reference to another,”

<a id="m-iii-p357"></a>

**Source p. 357**

provided that “another” means something conceived with reference to the first. This is the meaning of the necessity of reciprocity. Reciprocity may require no preposition, as with greater and smaller; or it may require one, either alike on both sides, as with “a slave of a master” and “a master of a slave,” or differently, as with “a knower of an object known” and “an object known to a knower.”

The two relations may agree, as in brotherhood, or differ, as in fatherhood and sonship. Each may be expressed by a noun, as with father and son; or one may require a connecting expression, as with head and possessor of a head. Their occurrence may require an attribute in both terms, as with lover and beloved; in one, as with knower and known; or in neither, as with right and left.

Relation occurs in every existent: for example, first, father, less, hotter, higher, earlier, nearer, more upright, more clothed, more cutting, and more intensely heated. Its determination occurs through reference to its bearer, although the category consists solely in the attribute occurring in the bearer.

The two terms correspond in determination and unrestrictedness, existence and nonexistence, mentally and externally, potentially and actually. In the case of prior and posterior, the correlatives are the two concepts, which coexist in the mind; it is only their bearers that can be separate.

The majority hold that relation has no realisation externally. Otherwise an infinite regress would follow, since inherence in a subject is itself a relation having an inherence; circularity would also follow, since being qualified by existence is a relation whose existence depends on relation in general being something that has existence. Moreover, every number would have infinitely many attributes through its relations to all other numbers.

<a id="m-iii-p359"></a>

**Source p. 359**

It may be answered that this proves at most the impossibility of every relation's existing. Denial of all collectively does not entail universal denial.

An argument for relations is that we are certain of the sky's being above and the earth's being below, and of Zayd's fatherhood and ʿAmr's sonship, even without the intellect's consideration. Something like this has already been discussed.

The well-known position is that relations follow their bearers in genus, species, subclass, individuality, and contrariety. Agreement in quality is a genus, and agreement in quantity another; agreement in whiteness is a species, and agreement in blackness another; the fatherhood of a just man is a subclass, and that of an unjust man another. Zayd's brotherhood to ʿAmr is an individual relation through the individuation of both relatives, not merely through reference to an individual—as in “Zayd's brotherhood” or “Zayd's brother.” ʿAmr's brotherhood to him is another individual relation.

The established statement that the diversity of the bearers' species does not require diversity in the species of the relations occurring in them means that, for example, two human beings' agreement in whiteness is not a different species from two horses' agreement in it.

Another is “when”: a thing's relation either to time, because it occurs gradually, as with motion and its concomitants, or all at once but through a succession of instants, as with spatial situatedness and intermediacy; or to an instant, because it is realised only at a boundary of time, as with arrival at the midpoint or the endpoint.
