This explicitly affirms an existent instant, although an instant is inconceivable except through time's interruption. Moreover, if an instant existed, the origination of its nonexistence could occur only at an instant, entailing the immediate succession of two instants. It does not help to say that its nonexistence occurs in time, but not gradually.

Like “where,” “when” is proper or improper, except that a proper “when” does not prevent many things from sharing it.

<a id="m-iii-p361"></a>

**Source p. 361**

Another is position: a body's being such that its parts have a relation among themselves and to things outside them, whether surrounding, surrounded, or otherwise. Position may be potential or actual, natural or imposed. It admits contrariety, as with standing upright and being inverted, and intensification, as with being more upright.

Another is “having,” also called possession and equipment: a body's relation to something enclosing it or a part of it and moving when it moves. This may be essential, as with an animal in its skin, or accidental, as with a human being in his clothes. The word is used equivocally for such relations as that of faculties to the soul or a horse to Zayd. Ibn Sīnā hesitated over whether this category is a genus in its own right.

Others are acting and being acted upon: one thing's affecting another and a thing's being affected by another while the process proceeds continuously, as with what belongs to the heating agent and the thing being heated so long as the one heats and the other is heated. They are not the state attained after the process has settled, such as a tree's height, water's heat, or a person's standing. Contrariety and intensification occur in them.

The claim that their establishedness is mental, since otherwise an infinite regress would follow, is answered by explaining that they do not mean affecting and being affected without qualification, so as to include origination without a material intermediary and instantaneous coming-to-be. Rather, they mean the state belonging to agent and patient. Thus al-Fārābī explained acting as causing motion, and being acted upon as undergoing motion.

---

<a id="division-iv"></a>

# Division IV: Substances

<a id="m-iv-p363"></a>

**Source p. 363**

It comprises a preliminary discussion and two treatises.

## Preliminary discussion

For us, a substance, if divisible, is a body; otherwise it is an indivisible substance. For the majority of philosophers, if it inheres in a substance, it is form; if it is a substrate for form, it is prime matter; if compounded of the two, it is body. Otherwise, if it is connected to a body by governance and administration, it is soul; if not, intellect. Alternatively: if separate in both essence and action, it is intellect; if in essence alone, soul. If conjoined, it is either an inherent, a substrate, or a compound. Alternatively: if it has the three dimensions, it is body; otherwise it is either a part through which body is actual, or a part through which it is not actual; or something outside body, either connected with it or not.

This division rests on their established doctrines denying the indivisible substance, affirming an actual substance inhering in body, and other principles. The final formulation is preferable, since it includes what ensures the distinction between body and prime matter; thus a body that is the substrate of a specific form is not included under prime matter.

For the ancients, a substance, if spatially located, is corporeal, consisting solely in body; otherwise it is spiritual, consisting in soul and intellect.

Notice: “substrate” is more general than “subject,” and “inherent” more general than “accident.” Subject and accident are mutually exclusive, whereas substrate is more general than accident in one respect. An accident's dependence on a subject may occur through an intermediary, in which case the intermediary is a substrate but not a subject.

<a id="m-iv-p365"></a>

**Source p. 365**

It has been imagined that the universals of substances require a subject, since they are essential predicates of an individual and forms subsisting in the soul.

The reply is that “subject” here does not mean the subject of a proposition. The substantiality of the forms means that, if they existed among external things, they would not be in a subject. Insofar as they inhere in an individual soul, however, they are individual accidents, not universal substances.

## First treatise: Matters concerning bodies

It comprises two chapters.

### Chapter One: Bodies considered generally

It comprises several investigations.

#### First investigation

For us, body is a substance admitting division. This includes what is composed of two parts or more, but not each of the two parts individually, as the Qāḍī maintains. He argues that each is that in which composition subsists, and therefore is composite, and every composite is a body. Yet there is an evident difference between being composed of something and being compounded with something.

For the Muʿtazila, body is that which is long, broad, and deep. This is a definition by a necessary and universally applicable property, on the understanding that what is meant is the capacity for these dimensions without qualification. It is therefore no objection that they are absent in actuality, as in a sphere, or that they change while corporeality remains, as in a piece of wax.

<a id="m-iv-p367"></a>

**Source p. 367**

Moreover, for them this returns to the arrangement of the parts, without affirming magnitudes additional to corporeality. Hence they make it a property without needing to mention substance. The qualifications of breadth and depth exclude the composite intermediate between body and the indivisible substance: one whose parts are arranged in only one or two directions, or whose parts number fewer than the minimum constituting a body—eight, six, or four, according to their disagreement.

For the philosophers, body is the substance in which three dimensions can be supposed. Sometimes they add “intersecting at right angles.” This clarifies the account and removes a misconception, rather than excluding something from the definition.

Dimensions here mean lines imagined within the body, not actually realised extensions—whether necessarily determinate, as in celestial bodies, or not necessarily determinate in their particular forms, as in elemental bodies—nor boundaries, which an infinite body lacks.

What is meant is the capacity to receive the dimensions themselves, not their intellectual or imaginative forms. Thus neither soul nor imagination is an objection; moreover, substance does not include the latter. It is evident that what is qualified by the dimensions is body, not prime matter.

Their statements vary over whether this is a definition by essence or by description. The Imām rejects its being an essential definition: substance is not body's genus, since it is explained as the existent not in a subject, whereas existence is additional and indeed a second intelligible, and “not in a subject” is negative. Furthermore, if substance were a genus, substances would be distinguished by differentiae, which would either be substances, generating an infinite regress, or accidents, making substance constituted by accident. Nor is receptivity, or its equivalent, a differentia: it belongs to conceptual considerations without establishedness among external things. Otherwise it would subsist in a receptive substrate, generating a regress among things possessing order and actual existence, which is unanimously held false.

<a id="m-iv-p369"></a>

**Source p. 369**

The answer is that “the existent not in a subject” describes substance rather than defining its essence. A genus's predication of a differentia is accidental and does not require another differentia. Moreover, the differentia is not receptivity, but the receptive—that is, the thing whose nature it is to receive. Its being, in existence, the very essence of body is no objection, as with other differentiae.

#### Second investigation

A simple body admits division. Either its divisions are actual and finite, the theologians' position; actual and infinite, toward which al-Naẓẓām inclines; merely potential and finite, a position attributed to al-Shahrastānī; merely potential and infinite, the majority of philosophers' position; or partly actual and partly potential. The last is Democritus's view that the parts of a simple body are small, solid bodies admitting imaginative division but not division by separation.

The philosophers then disagree. The Peripatetics hold that a body's capacity for division requires matter, and that body is composed of this matter and the form inhering in it. Others hold that body admits division through itself, so that it is simple in itself as it appears to sense. The view attributed to some that body is composed solely of accidents is necessarily false.

Three positions deserve consideration: first, that body is composed of finitely many indivisible parts; second, that it is composed of prime matter and form; third, that it is entirely simple.

<a id="m-iv-p371"></a>

**Source p. 371**

There appears to be agreement that there is a matter upon which forms and accidents successively occur. The dispute concerns whether it is body itself, the matter in which form inheres, or the indivisible substances in which composition subsists. On close examination, the claim that body consists of indivisible substances and composition is near to the claim that it consists of prime matter and form.

#### Third investigation: The arguments of the two parties

The theologians have two routes.

The first establishes that admitting division entails the division's actuality. There are several arguments. First, if what admits division were one, unity would admit division, since an inherent necessarily divides with its substrate. Second, if it were one, separation would be annihilation, since the single identity necessarily disappears when two identities arise. Dividing the sea with a needle would therefore annihilate it and bring two seas into existence. Third, the sections marking parts—half, third, quarter, and so forth—are necessarily distinct. If the parts were not distinct, their properties would not differ.

The first may be answered by saying that unity is an intellectual consideration that does not divide with its substrate. The second: if “the sea” means that water together with its continuity, it plainly ceases to exist when separation occurs; if it means the water itself, nothing comes into existence or ceases to exist. The third: the difference in properties follows only after division has been supposed.

<a id="m-iv-p373"></a>

**Source p. 373**

Their second route establishes within body a substance that admits no division at all. There are several arguments.

Some rest on the capacity for division entailing the actuality of the parts. For example: God can create separation in the body's parts in place of conjunction, establishing the indivisible part; for if further divisibility remained, conjunction would remain.

Another: without indivisible parts a mountain would not be greater than a mustard seed, since their parts would be equal, both being infinite. The objection is that the equality concerns the number of parts, not their magnitudes. The answer is that differences of magnitude certainly depend on differences in parts. It may also be claimed that equality even in possible parts is impossible.

Another: if division of a body did not terminate in what has no extension at all, the body's extension would be infinite, since it would be composed of infinitely many extensions.

Some arguments rest on motion's being successive occupancies and time's being successive instants. For example: what exists of motion and time is the present, since the past existed only when present and the future exists only when it becomes present. The present in something whose essence is nonstable is indivisible; so too is the portion of distance corresponding to it.

Some rest on the substrate of a point being an indivisible substance. For example: the point exists, since it is the extremity of an existent line, and lines touch through it. If the point is a substance, the conclusion is obtained. If an accident, it inheres directly or through an intermediary in an indivisible substance, lest the point itself divide.

<a id="m-iv-p375"></a>

**Source p. 375**

Another argument: when a true sphere is placed on a flat surface, or a line stands upon a line, contact occurs through something indivisible. If the sphere is then rolled through its entirety along the surface, and the line passes along the other line to its end, the indivisibility of all the parts becomes evident and the conclusion is established.

Another: it has been established that the angle produced by a straight line's contact with a circle's circumference is the smallest angle. It therefore admits no division, establishing the indivisible part.

They argue for the finitude of parts: (1) the parts are enclosed between two extremities; (2) their infinity would make it impossible for a moving thing to reach any endpoint, or for a faster thing to overtake a slower one in finite time; (3) a body composed, for example, of eight parts provides a counterinstance; (4) when this is compared with bodies of finite magnitudes, their parts are shown finite, since the ratio of volume to volume is the ratio of parts to parts, volume being determined by them. Interpenetration is impossible, the leap is imaginary, and traversing the infinite in the finite is error.

The philosophers have several routes for denying the indivisible substance.

(1) Some rest on differences of direction and extremities entailing division in the thing itself. First: what belongs to it toward one direction differs from what belongs to it toward another, so it divides. Second: when one part is joined to another, either it meets the other through its entirety, in which case there is no volume and therefore no magnitude, or not through its entirety, entailing division.

<a id="m-iv-p377"></a>

**Source p. 377**

Third: when three parts are packed together, if the middle part prevents the two extremities from meeting, it is divisible; otherwise it has no volume. Fourth: when the sun shines on a sheet of parts, the illuminated face confronting it differs from the other face. Fifth: when one part falls on the meeting point of two parts, all three divide. This may be supposed directly; or a part moves from one part to another, its being in motion occurring at their meeting point; or one supposes a line of four parts, with a part above the first and another below the fourth, moving equally together, so that their alignment occurs at a meeting point; or one supposes a line of five parts with a part above each extremity, the two moving until they meet, so that the third part lies beneath their meeting point.

(2) Other arguments rest on slowness not consisting in interspersed rests, either because this is impossible in itself or because it entails things evidently absent, such as the disintegration of connected things and the separation of inseparable concomitants. This is illustrated in several configurations: first, the motion of the two concentric rings of a millstone; second, the motion of a three-branched compass; third, the motion of a person's heel and extremities while he turns about himself.

<a id="m-iv-p379"></a>

**Source p. 379**

Fourth: the motion of the equator and of circles near the pole. Fifth: the motion of the sun and a tree's shadow. Sixth: the motion of a bucket attached to one end of a rope whose other end is fixed halfway down a well, with a hook inserted to pull the rope; the bucket traverses the well's distance while the hook traverses half of it.

(3) Other arguments concern geometrical principles founded on the absence of indivisible parts. First: every line can be bisected; in a line composed of an odd number of parts, the middle part must therefore divide. Second: an equilateral triangle can be constructed on every line; on a line composed of two parts, this is inconceivable except by a part's falling upon the meeting point of those two parts. Third: every rectilinear angle can be divided without end.

Fourth: if one end of a straight line is fixed and the line rotated until it returns to its initial position, a circle is produced; if a semicircle is then rotated about its fixed diameter, a sphere is produced. Indivisible parts exclude both. For, if a circle's circumference is supposed to consist of indivisible parts, either the outer faces of the parts equal their inner faces, making the outer circumference equal to the inner; or they are greater, entailing division; or there are empty gaps between the outer faces. If each gap cannot contain a part, division follows; if it can, the outer circumference is twice the inner. Moreover, in the circle immediately adjacent to the equator, either there is a part corresponding to every part of the equator, making them equal, or there are fewer, entailing division.

<a id="m-iv-p381"></a>

**Source p. 381**

Fifth: the square on a right angle's hypotenuse equals the sum of the squares on its two enclosing sides. If each side is ten parts, the hypotenuse is greater than fourteen and less than fifteen, being the square root of two hundred.

Sixth: take a line of two parts, with a part above one of them. There is then a right angle whose hypotenuse exceeds two and falls short of three; otherwise a right angle's hypotenuse would equal either each of its two sides or their sum.

Seventh: form a square by joining four lines, each consisting of four parts. If the diagonal's parts are contiguous, it consists of four parts, equalling the side, which is impossible. If it contains empty gaps each equal to a part, it consists of seven parts, equalling the two sides, which is also impossible. If the gaps are smaller, division follows.

(4) Other arguments rest on premises that cannot be established. First: if body consisted of indivisible parts, the part would be essential to it, and its belonging to it would therefore be self-evident. The reply is that this applies to intellectual constituents, and only after the quiddity has been conceived.

Second: the part is finite and must therefore have a shape. If polygonal, it divides; if spherical, joining parts leaves gaps smaller than a part. The reply is that this applies to spherical bodies.

Third: when a body's shadow is twice its size, half the shadow is the shadow of half the body. In a body composed of an odd number of parts, division therefore follows. The reply is that this applies to what has a half.

<a id="m-iv-p383"></a>

**Source p. 383**

They then reject body's being composed of parts divisible in imagination but not in actuality. Since, on that account, the parts are equal in nature, everything possible for the whole is intrinsically possible for each part, even if prevented by an accidental feature of individuation or something else.

The Peripatetics then argue for prime matter. Since a body's continuity does not consist in the conjunction of parts, nor its separation in their dispersal, but it is intrinsically continuous while admitting separation, it has a substantial extension upon which accidental extensions successively occur, as with wax. This is called form. It cannot itself be what receives separation, since it does not remain with separation. There must therefore be, together with it, a recipient of continuity and separation that remains with both, upon which continuous identities differing individually successively occur. This is called prime matter.

The precise account is that what is first apprehended of body's substantiality is an extended identity, which does not disappear when magnitudes change and without which body is inconceivable. They call it continuity—or rather something continuous—meaning the substance whose nature is to be continuous in the sense that dimensions can be supposed in it. Clearly this very identity does not remain upon separation, but gives way to two continuous identities, while something remains in both states that essentially receives continuity and separation. For there is a necessary difference between a body's being wholly annihilated and two bodies arising, or the reverse, and its separating into two bodies, or the reverse—as when the water of a jar is distributed among cups and conversely. Opposites can successively occur in this recipient because in itself it is pure preparedness, becoming one through the form's unity and multiple through its multiplicity, while remaining in both states. This resolves the following difficulties.

<a id="m-iv-p385"></a>

**Source p. 385**

First: continuity's being a substance and a constituent of body is necessarily false. Rather, continuity and separation are accidents alternating in body; precisely speaking, they express its unity and multiplicity.

Second: separation means nothing but the disappearance of one continuous identity into two identities, so no remaining recipient is needed.

Third: if receptivity to separation required matter, an infinite regress of matters would follow.

Fourth: if what disappears upon separation is the essential, substantial continuity, the body has ceased to exist and is therefore not a recipient; if accidental continuity, the intended conclusion has not been established.

Fifth: when a body separates into two, if the matter of this one is the matter of that one, a single individual exists in two spatial loci and is qualified by two corporealities. If the matters differ, then, at the time of continuity, either both existed, in which case the body was not intrinsically continuous but consisted of actual parts; or they did not, in which case separation annihilates the body entirely, not merely its continuous form.

The Illuminationists hold that body is one in itself, without any composition at all. Prime matter is simply its name insofar as configurations successively occur in it and species are realised from it. They maintain that continuity in the sense opposed to separation and abolished by its occurrence is an accident. In the sense of something whose nature is to receive dimensions and extend in directions, it subsists in itself, occupies space through itself, and is nothing other than body. What is imagined as an extension remaining when the dimensions of wax change is simply the magnitude preserved through successive particular determinations. How could the nature of extension differ through substantiality and accidentality? Moreover, if there were two extensions, substantial and accidental, either they would differ in extent, leaving a portion of one extension without matter, or they would be equal, eliminating the distinction.

<a id="m-iv-p387"></a>

**Source p. 387**

Prime matter is also denied by arguing that, if it does not occupy space, it cannot be a substrate for what is assigned to a spatial locus. If it does occupy space, it does so either independently—in which case it is like corporeality, cannot coexist with it, has no better claim to be its substrate, and entails either corporeality's independence of matter or an infinite regress of matters—or dependently, in which case it is an attribute of corporeality inhering in it.

The answer is that dependence need not arise through its inhering in something; it may arise through something's inhering in it. Moreover, sharing a concomitant does not entail likeness in essence.

#### Fourth investigation: Consequences of the doctrines

Those affirming the indivisible part disagree over whether it can receive life and the accidents conditioned by life; whether a part can fall on the junction of two parts; whether a line composed of parts can be made into a circle; and whether the part has a shape. Those affirming a shape disagree: it resembles a sphere, a triangle, or a square—meaning a cube, so that it can be surrounded by six substances. They agree that it has no share of length or breadth, except for what is attributed to al-Ṣāliḥī and Ibn al-Rāwandī.

The reported agreement that it has a share of spatial magnitude rests on that expression meaning what occupies space and the bulk that produces greater density through the addition of its likes. What is reported from al-Jubbāʾī is in any case contrary to this.

Those affirming parts divisible imaginatively but not actually disagree over their shapes: spheres, cubes, triangles, squares, or various shapes.

<a id="m-iv-p389"></a>

**Source p. 389**

The well-known position of both groups is that the parts have a single nature in all bodies, so bodies differ through accidents. For us, differences of accidents depend on the power of the freely choosing agent; for them, on differences of shape. There is therefore no need to make some accidents internal to body's reality.

Those affirming prime matter and form agree on several consequences.

First: prime matter belongs to every body, even one that cannot be separated, such as the celestial bodies. For corporeality is a specific nature and does not differ in its concomitants. The precise account is that matter has been established as a necessary concomitant of corporeality, disregarding its individuations and causes separate from it. Corporeality is not an accidental or generic nature applying to bearers or quiddities with different concomitants, as existence or animality does. Rather, it is specific, since it is something determined in itself. It differs only through what attaches to it, such as heat and cold, and what accompanies it, such as celestial or elemental nature, all external to it and distinct in existence. Hence the answer concerning either the whole or a part is simply “a substance continuous in itself.” This does not conflict with body's being a genus when it is considered indeterminately, becoming determinate only through the addition of differentiae. The argument may also be presented by saying that every body admits separation intrinsically, even if an incidental feature prevents it, or that imaginative separation suffices to establish matter.

Second: prime matter cannot exist without form. If it can be pointed to, it is body, since an indivisible substance is impossible. Otherwise, when form arises, it must be in some particular spatial locus, which would be determination without a determinant.

<a id="m-iv-p391"></a>

**Source p. 391**

The reply denies that the determinant must be confined to form.

Third: form cannot exist without prime matter. If it subsisted through itself, it would be independent of a substrate and would not inhere in one. The reply is that neither separateness nor inherence need belong to it essentially.

Fourth: their inseparability does not result from either one's being the cause of the other. Rather, prime matter requires some form for its persistence, while form requires a particular prime matter for its individuation.

Fifth: bodies' differing effects do not arise from their shared corporeality, but from something peculiar to each, which cannot be incidental, lest an infinite regress follow. This is specific form. The difference of the forms is a counterinstance. If it is maintained that forms succeed one another without end, or that their difference depends on differing preparednesses, we maintain the same of incidental attributes.

It may be said that the principles of bodies' effects are things through which the bodies acquire their species and become determinate; they can therefore only be constitutive substances. The content of this is that we are certain that the realities of water and fire, for example, differ, although they share matter and corporeal form. They must therefore differ through a substantial constituent, which we call specific form. This rests on the impossibility of body's being constituted by an accident subsisting in its two constituents, or by a substance that does not inhere in its matter.

#### Fifth investigation: The judgements concerning bodies

(1) One is that bodies are alike, differing only in incidental attributes, and that what is possible for one is possible for another. Upon this depend the attribution of differing accidents to the freely choosing powerful agent, the possibility of the heavens' being rent, and many events that break the customary order.

<a id="m-iv-p393"></a>

**Source p. 393**

This is because bodies consist solely of alike indivisible substances, share spatial occupation and the reception of accidents, and are all included in the division of body. It may be imagined that their likeness means unity in a concept common to different species, like animality. One then argues that the definition of body, despite their differing expressions, is one and contains no differentiation into species, while differences of properties arise only from differences of species.

(2) Another is that bodies persist, by immediate necessity, not merely because they remain before sense; and that they admit annihilation, since they are temporally originated, as will follow, and because God says, “Everything is perishing except His face” [Qurʾān 28:88]. The perishing of a simple thing is conceivable only through its annihilation. Yet temporal origination plainly entails only the intrinsic possibility of nonexistence, which does not conflict with nonexistence being impossible through another—precisely what is disputed. If one appeals to the rule that possibility holds until something rendering it impossible is established, mention of temporal origination is superfluous.

When the specious argument against accidents' persistence implied an incompatibility between persistence and the possibility of annihilation, and a similar argument arose for bodies, al-Naẓẓām gave priority to the proof of their admitting annihilation and accepted their nonpersistence. The Karrāmiyya gave priority to the necessary knowledge of their persistence and accepted the impossibility of annihilation. You already know the reply. A distinction is also possible: accidents depend on substances that depend on those accidents, producing circularity; substances are different, since God may preserve them through successive accidents that they require, and annihilate them directly, by not creating those accidents or the accident called persistence, or by creating the accident called annihilation, one or many according to the differing doctrines.

<a id="m-iv-p395"></a>

**Source p. 395**

The philosophers support the impossibility of bodies' annihilation with their unsound principles: that bodies depend by necessitation on what is beginningless, and require matter that cannot receive nonexistence because an infinite regress of matters is impossible, and that cannot be without form, for the reasons given earlier.

(3) Another is that a body cannot lack shape, because it is finite, or spatial locus, by necessary knowledge. For us, however, the particular determinations of these arise solely through God's creation. The philosophers maintain that every body has a natural shape and a natural spatial locus: if left to itself and its nature, it would necessarily have a shape and occupy a locus, whether a place or otherwise. This must be determinate, since occupancy of the indeterminate is impossible, and must be only one, since it is the consequence of one nature.

(4) Another is that a body cannot be devoid of both an accident and its contrary. Some philosophers allow this in beginninglessness; some Muʿtazilites allow it without qualification in subsequent existence; some allow it except with spatial states; others except with colours.

Those denying this possibility argue that bodies cannot lack both motion and rest or both conjunction and separation; and that, being alike, they are distinguished and individuated only through accidents, while the existence of what is not individuated is impossible. The answer is that this does not establish the disputed universal claim unless some accidents are inferred from others, which is invalid.

Those allowing it argue that the first bodies were devoid of conjunction and separation, and that air is devoid of colour. For failure to apprehend a sensible object without an impediment proves its absence, while asserting an impediment without demonstrating it leads to sophistry.

<a id="m-iv-p397"></a>

**Source p. 397**

(5) Another is that bodies are finite in dimension. There are several arguments.

First: if an infinite dimension existed, a sphere could necessarily move toward it, so that its diameter, initially parallel to it, inclined into alignment with it. A point would then have to be assigned in imagination as the first point of alignment, since alignment comes into existence. Yet this is impossible in an infinite line: for any point supposed, alignment with a point beyond it precedes alignment with it. Alignment necessarily occurs through an angle and a motion, each of which, by a truthful judgement of imagination, admits division without end; alignment through half of each precedes alignment through the whole. This defeats the denial of the implication based on the explanation of the consequent's impossibility; the denial that the conclusion is established on the ground that impossibility follows only from an infinite dimension together with the stated supposition; and the denial of the consequent's impossibility on the ground that the endless divisibility of angle and motion is merely imaginary.

The Imām objects that the longest straight line that can be supposed is the world's axis. Alignment with it occurs only after alignment with a point beyond it outside the world, and so without end, entailing infinite dimensions. The answer is that this belongs to purely imaginary constructions.

Second: from a point we suppose two lines whose separation increases in proportion to the increase of their extension, each increase being present together with what it augments in a single intervening distance. If the lines extended infinitely, preserving the proportion would necessarily entail a distance containing infinitely many increases and exceeding the first distance by their amount, although enclosed between two boundaries.

<a id="m-iv-p399"></a>

**Source p. 399**

More clearly: suppose the intervening distance always equals either line, making the angle two-thirds of a right angle and the triangle equilateral. The lines' infinity then necessarily entails an infinite distance between them.

If it is said, “This depends on the two extensions having an endpoint, so that there is a distance which is the last of the distances, and therefore begs the question,” we reply: no; rather, it entails such an endpoint, which is a contradiction.

Third: we subtract a cubit from the infinite dimension and then superimpose the two. Either a cubit corresponds to every cubit, making the two equal, or not, in which case they terminate. Something similar has already been discussed.

These three are the foundations of the demonstrations of finitude. The first rests on denying the indivisible part; the second on infinity extending in several directions; the third on weak premises. Many further arguments have arisen by modifying these three.

The opponent offers several arguments. First: what faces south differs from what faces north, and what lies opposite a quarter of the world is less than what lies opposite half, so it cannot be sheer nonexistence. We reply: this is mere imagination.

Second: someone standing at the edge of the world can either extend his hand, in which case there is dimension beyond it, or cannot, in which case there is an obstruction. We reply: he cannot, because a condition is absent, not because an obstruction exists.

Third: body is a universal, not confined to one individual. Its possible individuals therefore have no limit, like other universals; indeed they exist, because emanation is universal.

<a id="m-iv-p401"></a>

**Source p. 401**

We reply: universality entails only the possibility of a multiplicity of supposed individuals considered with reference to its concept. It does not conflict with all or some being impossible for a determining reason, such as the aforementioned impossibilities. Moreover, the possibility of its infinitely many individuals does not mean their coexistence, which is what is required; it means that they do not reach a limit beyond which another individual cannot exist.

Conclusion: the extremity of an extension is a boundary in relation to the extension itself, and a direction insofar as it is the terminus of pointing and the destination of a moving thing that comes to occupy it. Direction is thus existent and positionally situated, admitting no division; otherwise motion would occur within it and the direction would be the endpoint of that motion.

Directions are not restricted in number. However, if extensions are considered as standing upon one another, or account is taken of the human being's head, feet, back, belly, and the two hands—the usually stronger and weaker—directions are confined to six. The natural ones are above and below; the others depend on position and change, as when someone facing east turns west, unlike the case of someone inverted. Above need not be relative to a below: the earth has no below except in imagination, since all the extremities of its actual extensions face the sky.

(6) Another is that bodies are temporally originated in both their essences and their attributes, as the adherents of revealed religions hold. The later philosophers oppose this in both respects. They maintain that celestial bodies are beginningless except for their particular motions and positions; and that elemental bodies are beginningless in their matters, in their corporeal forms as a species, and in their specific forms as a genus.

<a id="m-iv-p403"></a>

**Source p. 403**

The earlier philosophers oppose it with respect to essences alone, maintaining that there is beginningless matter. They disagree over whether this is body—the four elements, or earth, fire, water, or air, the others deriving through rarefaction and condensation and the sky from rising vapour; or another substance; or small solid bodies, spherical or variously shaped—or not body, but light and darkness; soul and prime matter; or units that acquired spatial location and became points, points combining into lines, lines into surfaces, and surfaces into body.

We have several arguments.

First: bodies are never devoid of temporally originated things. (1) They certainly are not devoid of accidents, and accidents are originated, given the established impossibility of their persistence without qualification. (2) They are not devoid of motion or rest: a body necessarily has occupancy of a spatial locus. If this is preceded by occupancy of that locus, it is rest; otherwise motion. Each is originated. Motion entails being preceded by another in a precedence in which the earlier does not coexist with the later, which is the meaning of temporal origination. Motion is also certainly liable to cease, which conflicts with beginninglessness. Rest is positive, being a spatial state, and can cease, since every body admits motion: (1) by agreement; (2) because bodies are alike and what is possible for one is possible for another; and (3) because bodies are either simple, whose similar parts each admit the loci admitted by the others, or composite, in which each simple constituent can touch another, which occurs only through motion.

<a id="m-iv-p405"></a>

**Source p. 405**

The argument is countered generally by a body at the moment of its origination, and in detail by denying that occupancy not preceded by occupancy of the same locus is motion. It would be motion only if preceded by occupancy of another locus; this does not apply in beginninglessness, since beginninglessness excludes being preceded.

The answer is that the discussion concerns occupancy preceded by another occupancy. Beginninglessness is not a state in which there is an occupancy with no occupancy before it; rather, it means continuation through infinitely many supposed past times.

If it is said: “The impossibility of particular motions' being beginningless does not entail the impossibility of their universal quiddity's being beginningless. Every motion may therefore be preceded by a motion without a beginning, and body may be moving beginninglessly and endlessly, meaning that no time can be supposed without some particular motion in it. This also undermines the claim that whatever is never devoid of originated things is originated, since that holds only if those originated things are finite. The firm foundation in this matter is therefore to show the impossibility of an infinite succession of originated things.”

The answers are: first, motion's reality is change from state to state, so being preceded, which conflicts with beginninglessness, is a concomitant of its quiddity. Second, the universal exists only within the particular, so beginningless motion together with the temporal origination of every particular is unintelligible. Third, a beginningless succession of originated things, whether motions or otherwise, is false: (1) by intellectual superimposition of a series extending from now and a series extending from the Flood, as already discussed; (2) because, if we consider a series from this preceded originated thing, it must contain something prior but not preceded, to secure the correspondence of the correlative properties of being prior and being preceded contained in each originated thing.

<a id="m-iv-p407"></a>

**Source p. 407**

Other arguments are sometimes mentioned: the origination of each entails the origination of all; admission of increase and decrease entails finitude; infinitely many past motions could not have elapsed; every part of motion, because it ceases, must be the effect of a freely choosing agent rather than a necessitating cause, and is therefore originated; if motion is preceded by another, it cannot be beginningless, and if not, its having a first beginning is established; and every particular motion is preceded by beginningless nonexistence, so their nonexistences coincide in beginninglessness, since the lateness of any would conflict with its beginninglessness—thus, if a motion existed beginninglessly, contradictories would coexist. All these arguments are weak.

Were it not intended to refute both those who hold that celestial bodies are beginningless and motions perpetual, and those who hold that small bodies not actually divisible are beginningless and were at rest beginninglessly before motion occurred in them, or conversely, the following would suffice: spatial situatedness necessarily belongs to body; body's beginninglessness therefore entails either beginningless situatedness or a beginningless succession of spatial states, both impossible for the reasons given.

Second: body is a substrate of originated things, as is evident, and is therefore originated, since it will be shown that what is beginningless cannot be qualified by what is originated.

Third: body is, immediately or ultimately, the effect of the freely choosing agent, since the Necessary Existent's power will be established. It is therefore originated, as previously explained.

Fourth: body is contingent because of its composition and multiplicity, and requires a giver of existence. Bringing it into existence while it persists would produce what is already attained; doing so while it is nonexistent or coming into existence entails the conclusion. You already know the difficulty in this argument.

<a id="m-iv-p409"></a>

**Source p. 409**

Fifth: body cannot lack a particular magnitude, which is originated because it depends on a freely choosing agent, since a necessitating cause's relation to all magnitudes is necessarily equal. Apart from its weakness, this returns to the claim that whatever cannot be devoid of an originated thing is originated. The same applies to the argument from body's not lacking a particular spatial locus.

Sixth: if body were beginningless, its beginninglessness would either be beginningless, generating a regress, or originated, entailing body's origination. Its weakness is evident.

The argument to rely on is the first. Yet it establishes only the origination of bodies and the accidents subsisting in them. To establish the world's origination, one must therefore deny immaterial entities or establish their origination.

Those affirming the world's beginninglessness invoke several arguments.

First: if everything indispensable to the world's existence was present beginninglessly, the world must have existed, since an effect cannot lag behind its complete cause. Otherwise the discussion is transferred to the newly arisen factor, generating a regress.

The answer is: (1) daily events provide a counterinstance. Saying that they differ because they depend on an endless succession of celestial events does not remove it. Moreover, the discussion concerns the corporeal world: why could its origination not be conditioned by conceptions or volitions—or, generally, successive events—in something immaterial? The weakness of the claim that every originated thing requires matter has already been explained. (2) We deny the premise: why could will not be among the indispensable factors, its nature being to determine at whatever time it chooses, without another determinant? The will's attachment may itself occur solely through will. The world's necessity through this attachment does not conflict with the Maker's choice, but establishes it.

<a id="m-iv-p411"></a>

**Source p. 411**

Second: both the world's possibility and the Necessary Existent's capacity to produce an effect in it are beginningless; otherwise an alteration of intrinsic status would follow. If its existence were not beginningless, beneficence would have been withheld for an infinite duration.

The answer is that, besides being rhetorical, this rests on failing to distinguish the beginninglessness of possibility from the possibility of beginninglessness. Something similar has already been discussed.

Third: body is composed of matter, which is beginningless because an infinite regress of matters is impossible, and form, which necessarily accompanies matter for the reasons given. Body is therefore beginningless. The answer is to deny the premises.

Fourth: time is beginningless, since nonexistence's precedence over it is conceivable only through time, entailing time's existence during its nonexistence. Time's beginninglessness entails that of motion and body, as previously explained.

The answer is: even if time's existence as the measure of motion is granted, why could nonexistence's precedence over it not resemble the precedence of some of its parts over others? The proposed distinction—that priority and posteriority enter the concept of time's parts but not the nonexistence and existence of an originated thing—is denied. Even if granted, the purpose is to deny that the kinds of precedence have been exhaustively enumerated.

The precise account is that the world's being preceded by nonexistence is only according to an imagined extension by which things are measured, which we call time. If the existence of a time measuring motion is established, its origination in this sense is not impossible. This also answers the following argument: if the Maker's existence did not precede the world's by an infinite measure, either the Maker would be originated or the world beginningless; if it did, time would be beginningless, since an infinite measure means beginningless successive before-nesses and after-nesses, entailing beginningless motion and body.

<a id="m-iv-p413"></a>

**Source p. 413**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.09](#c1-09).*

### Chapter Two: Bodies considered in detail

The discussion is arranged in four classes. A body composed of bodies of different natures is composite; otherwise it is simple. A simple body is celestial or elemental; a composite body is blended or unblended. A simple body is also described as that whose quantitative part shares the whole's name and definition, and a composite body as its opposite.

What is included in each of the two explanations of each kind may be considered perceptually or in reality. Under both considerations, an animal is composite and water simple without qualification; a celestial sphere is simple only under the first explanation. Gold, under both explanations, is composite in reality and simple to sense.

It should be understood that most of this chapter's investigations report philosophical doctrines founded on principles that are unsound or unestablished.

### First class: Simple celestial bodies

It comprises several investigations.

#### First investigation: Establishing the body that determines directions

It has already been explained that some directions are real, toward which certain bodies naturally move: above and below. Determining them therefore requires a single spherical body encompassing everything,

<a id="m-iv-p415"></a>

**Source p. 415**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.10](#c1-10).*

whose circumference determines nearness and whose centre determines remoteness. It must be one: if several, and some encompass others, the encompassing one is assigned the determining role; without enclosure, each would stand in a direction relative to another, so direction would not arise through it but would precede or accompany it. Moreover, what each determined would be nearness to itself, not remoteness.

It must be spherical, because composition or departure from roundness is impossible for it. Either would entail the possibility of rectilinear motion, which occurs only from one direction to another, contradicting direction's arising through this body. This is not because, as has been said, (1) a nonspherical body determines nearness but not remoteness, which is denied; or (2) its motion, especially around a spherical body, entails a void. For if the direction-determiner's concave surface were spherical and its convex surface egg-shaped, rotating on its longer axis, or lentil-shaped, rotating on its shorter axis, no void would follow. It must encompass everything because a body that does not encompass determines only nearness, which is evident.

Its determining directions means that their positions are fixed through it. Otherwise the efficient cause need not be a body, and the recipient is not one: above is a point of the celestial sphere, below a point of the earth, but only insofar as the earth is the encompassing body's centre and determined by it. For the encompassing body determines its centre, not conversely; hence the earth plays no role in determination. The body encompassing everything is selected because pointing may extend beyond an encompassed body, which would therefore not be the terminus. On this account, what truly determines direction is the convex surface of the encompassing body, while its concave surface is below, as with the other spheres relative to their supposed parts. Some hold that the determiner is the encompassing body itself, so that all of it is intrinsically above.

<a id="m-iv-p417"></a>

**Source p. 417**

Notice: since for us a void is possible, bodies are alike, and motions depend on the freely choosing agent's power, their argument is incomplete. Rectilinear motion is not impossible for the heavens, nor are the consequences they derive established: that the heavens do not admit rending and rejoining, generation and corruption, active and passive qualities, and so forth.

Some ancients maintain that the heavens possess extreme hardness, dryness, and smoothness, and that their mutual contact produces melodies heard by practitioners of spiritual exercises.

#### Second investigation

They maintain that the direction-determiner is the ninth sphere, meaning that proof establishes nine spheres, although some allow their reduction to eight or even seven; that it bears no star; and that it moves from east to west around a great circle called the celestial equator and two poles called the poles of the world. It completes a revolution in approximately a day and night and moves all the others, since they are like parts of it.

Beneath it is the sphere of the fixed stars, moving from west to east around a great circle and poles different from those of the ninth sphere. It completes a revolution in thirty-six thousand years, or twenty-three thousand seven hundred and sixty years, or twenty-five thousand two hundred years, according to differing views. Beneath it are, successively, the spheres of Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, the sun, Venus, Mercury, and the moon. The positioning of the sun relative to the two inferior planets is based on preference; the rest on evidence from occultation.

<a id="m-iv-p419"></a>

**Source p. 419**

The seven stars are called the wandering stars; the sun and moon, the two luminaries; and the others, the errant planets. Their comprehensive spheres are called homocentric spheres [mumaththalāt], because their equatorial circles correspond to the ecliptic.

Within the moon's homocentric sphere is another sphere whose centre is the earth's centre, called the inclined sphere. Within the thickness of the inclined sphere and of each homocentric sphere other than the moon's is a sphere encompassing the earth but with a centre displaced from the earth's centre. In Mercury this is called the director, and in the other errant planets and the moon the deferent. Its convex surface touches the convex surface of the inclined or homocentric sphere at a point called the apogee, and its concave surface touches the other's concave surface at a point called the perigee.

The remaining excess forms two rounded bodies about the world's centre, called the complementary bodies. Each gradually changes from a thickness equal to the distance between the two centres to a thinness terminating at the two points of contact, alternately: the enclosing complementary body is thin at apogee and thick at perigee, and the enclosed one conversely. Within the director's thickness is another eccentric sphere called the deferent, separated from the director as the director is from the homocentric sphere. Mercury therefore has two apogees, two perigees, and four complementary bodies. For the errant planets, “inclined sphere” is a name for the deferent.

Within each deferent's thickness is a sphere called the epicycle. One end of its diameter touches the deferent's convex surface and the other its concave surface. The planet is embedded in it, its surface touching the epicycle's surface. The sun is situated in its eccentric as an epicycle is situated in its deferent.

<a id="m-iv-p421"></a>

**Source p. 421**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.11](#c1-11).*

#### Third investigation

They imagined six circles intersecting at the ecliptic poles and cutting its equator at equal distances, one passing through the two intersections of the celestial equator and ecliptic. They then supposed these circles to cut the world, thereby dividing the outermost sphere and the other homocentric spheres into twelve portions. Each portion they called a zodiacal sign; each sign they divided into thirty degrees, each degree into sixty minutes, each minute into sixty seconds, and so on.

They called the intersection beyond which the sun passes north of the celestial equator the vernal equinox, and that beyond which it passes south the autumnal equinox. The midpoint between the intersections in the north they called the summer solstice, and that in the south the winter solstice. The sun's passage through the northern signs Aries, Taurus, and Gemini is spring; through Cancer, Leo, and Virgo, summer; through the southern signs Libra, Scorpio, and Sagittarius, autumn; and through Capricorn, Aquarius, and Pisces, winter.

Each wandering star traverses these signs in their sequence, meaning by motion from west to east. In general: the sun does so in three hundred and sixty-five and a quarter days, the solar year; the moon in three hundred and fifty-four days, a sixth of a day, and a fifth; Saturn in thirty years; Jupiter in twelve; Mars in two years less a month and a half; Venus in approximately a year, and Mercury likewise. All these are approximations.

In detail, daily motion against the sequence—that is, east to west—is: for Mercury's director, on a circle and poles different from those of the world and the ecliptic, fifty-nine minutes, eight seconds, and twenty thirds; for the moon's homocentric sphere, around the ecliptic and its poles, three minutes and a fraction; for its inclined sphere, around a circle and poles different from both, eleven degrees and nine minutes.

<a id="m-iv-p423"></a>

**Source p. 423**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.11](#c1-11).*

Motion in the sequence is: for the homocentric spheres other than the moon's, in agreement with the eighth sphere, as though through its motion; for the sun's eccentric, around the ecliptic but not its poles, fifty-nine minutes, eight seconds, and twenty thirds; for Saturn, two minutes; for Mars, thirty-one minutes; for Venus, as for the sun; for Mercury, a degree and a half; for the moon, twenty-four degrees and twenty-three minutes. All these latter motions occur around circles and poles different from the two previously mentioned.

For the epicycles of the superior planets, the motion is the excess of the sun's motion over that of their deferents; for Venus's epicycle, thirty-seven minutes; Mercury's, three degrees and six minutes; and the moon's, thirteen degrees and four minutes. In the errant planets' epicycles, these motions concern the upper half and occur around circles different from the deferents' equators. In the moon's epicycle, they concern the lower half and occur around the equator of the deferent and inclined sphere. The other half must consequently move against the sequence.

The moon's deviation from the ecliptic occurs only through the inclined sphere. The errant planets' deviation occurs through the inclined sphere—meaning the deferent—from the ecliptic, and through the epicycle from the inclined sphere. Both deviations are called latitude. The two intersections of the inclined and homocentric spheres are called the two nodes; the one beyond which the planets pass north is the head, the other the tail.

It is thus evident that the individual spheres number twenty-four, as do the simple motions. This rests on a belief in regularity among celestial things despite observing many variations, such as speed and slowness after intermediate motion, and retrogression and station after direct motion.

<a id="m-iv-p425"></a>

**Source p. 425**

There is no doubt that, in an eccentric sphere moving the sun, for example, uniformly about its own centre, the arc lying in the apogeal half of the homocentric sphere is greater than that lying in its perigeal half. The sun traverses either half only by traversing the eccentric arc within it. The time taken through the apogeal half is therefore greater, and the motion appears slower. Hence spring and summer last longer than autumn and winter.

When a planet is in the half of its epicycle whose motion agrees with the deferent's motion, it appears swift. In the other half, if its motion is less than the deferent's, it appears slow; if equal—which occurs only for the errant planets—it appears stationary; if greater, it appears retrograde. Such explanations are not, as might appear, inferences from the existence of a consequent to its antecedent, but intuitive conjectures. Likewise, from the moon's changing illuminated shapes according to its changing positions relative to the sun, they intuited that it is intrinsically dark and receives light from the sun. At conjunction, its dark face is toward us: this is the dark moon. When enough of its illuminated face turns toward us to be visible, it is the crescent. The visible illumination then increases until opposition, when its entire illuminated face is toward us: this is the full moon. It then turns, and the light diminishes until the dark moon.

If at conjunction the moon lies on the ascending or descending node, or near enough that its latitude is less than the sum of the two luminaries' semidiameters, all or part of its dark body is seen upon the sun's face: this is a solar eclipse. If at opposition it lies on a node or near enough that its latitude is less than the sum of its own semidiameter and the diameter of the earth's shadow-cone, the earth blocks the sunlight from it, and all or part is seen in its original darkness: this is a lunar eclipse.

<a id="m-iv-p427"></a>

**Source p. 427**

They nevertheless found further variations requiring additional spheres, whose numbers, configurations, positions, and motions perplexed them. For the epicycle's centre being moved by the deferent's motion requires that the uniformity of its motion, the equality of its distances, and the alignment of the diameter passing through its highest and lowest points all be relative to the deferent's centre. Yet in the moon they found uniform motion about the world's centre, while the diameter aligned with a point toward perigee whose distance from the world's centre equals the distance between the two centres. In the errant planets they found uniform motion about a point halfway between the world's centre and the centre of Mercury's director, or of the deferent in the others. Furthermore, the inclined sphere's inclination from the homocentric sphere is northward in one half and southward in the other. The epicycle's centre should therefore behave likewise; yet they found it always northward for Venus and southward for Mercury.

#### Fourth investigation

For every place on earth they imagined a circle on the celestial sphere separating its visible and hidden portions, calling it the horizon circle. Its poles are the zenith and nadir. If these are the world's poles, the horizon coincides with the celestial equator; otherwise it intersects it, either at right angles, in which case it is called the equatorial horizon, or at other angles, in which case it is called the inclined horizon.

They imagined another circle passing through zenith, nadir, and the world's poles, calling it the meridian circle. Its poles are the east and west points. In the plane of each of the celestial equator, the equatorial horizon, and the meridian, they imagined a circle on earth. The first, called the equator, divides the earth into southern and northern portions; the second into visible and hidden portions—the exposed portion being one of the two northern quarters, called the inhabited world; and the third into eastern and western portions.

<a id="m-iv-p429"></a>

**Source p. 429**

They called the arc of the meridian between a locality's zenith and the celestial equator its latitude; and the arc of the celestial equator between the locality's meridian and the meridian of the western extremity of habitation its longitude.

They divided the inhabited world into seven bands parallel to the celestial equator, extending east to west, calling them the climes.

At the equator the sphere's rotation is like a waterwheel, and night and day are always equal. The sun passes overhead at the equinoxes, producing summer, and recedes at the solstices, producing winter; the seasons thus number eight. At latitude ninety the rotation is like a millstone: half the sphere is always visible and half hidden, and the year is one day and one night. At inclined horizons the rotation is oblique. In northern latitudes, the visible arcs of the northern circles are greater, so day is longer when the sun is in the northern signs; the reverse holds in the southern signs. Differences of latitude determine the differences of arcs and of night and day.

Conclusion: there is no doubt that the creation of the heavens is greater, and that the wonders they contain indicate all-surpassing power and manifest wisdom more clearly. Yet founding this account on denial of the freely choosing powerful agent, and attributing events to successive motions and positions, strips the Maker—exalted and holy is He—of agency.

<a id="m-iv-p431"></a>

**Source p. 431**

Although, concerning observed differences of colour, they hold that blueness is imagined in the atmosphere, the moon's darkness is absence of illumination, and Saturn's dimness, Jupiter's whiteness, and Mars's redness are differences of light; and although, concerning alleged differences in the natures of planets and zodiacal signs, they refer these to effects according to motions and positions—nevertheless, in accounting for the differentiation of parts into equator, pole, seat of a planet or epicycle, and the like, they are compelled to refer it to efficient causes. This is impossible on the doctrine of a necessitating cause, whose relation to all is equal. They must therefore return to the freely choosing powerful agent. It is astonishing that, while believing these motions to follow this order necessarily, beginninglessly and endlessly, they make them voluntary, following successive particular volitions of celestial souls, as will be explained.

### Second class: Simple elemental bodies

It comprises several investigations.

#### First investigation

Finding elemental bodies never devoid of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness, without restriction to just one quality or the combination of three, they held that there are four elements according to the possible pairings: hot and dry, fire; hot and moist, air; cold and moist, water; cold and dry, earth. They based their methods of exhaustive division on these four qualities or their concomitants, such as absolute or relative lightness and heaviness, and ease or difficulty of conjunction and separation. Their reliance is on induction.

<a id="m-iv-p433"></a>

**Source p. 433**

Serious doubt concerns only the existence of the sphere of fire, then its dryness, and the heat of air. Argument from effects is weak, since other causes are possible. Weaker still is the argument that, if fire were hot and moist, it would be air, or if air were cold and moist, it would be water, since sharing concomitants is possible, especially concomitants differing in intensity and weakness.

They then made these four the constituents of generated things, from which composition occurs and into which dissolution returns. They observed that, when earth and water combine with air interspersed and heat bestowed from the sky, plants arise and become material for animals' generation; and that, when a compound is placed in a cucurbit and alembic, an airy part rises, a watery part condenses into drops, and an earthy part remains. A fiery part must also be present to assist in producing the form that preserves the composition brought about by its determining causes. The weakness of this is evident.

#### Second investigation

Each of the four transforms into its neighbour by shedding one form and assuming another. This is called generation and corruption. Between fire and air this is evident; between air and water, it occurs as in boiling water and the drops forming on a vessel inverted over ice; between water and earth, as in the artificial liquefaction of stones and the solidification into stone of certain spring waters. These are six kinds. If transformations between nonadjacent elements are considered, four occur through one intermediary and two through two intermediaries.

<a id="m-iv-p435"></a>

**Source p. 435**

#### Third investigation

Fire is a single layer, strongly capable of transforming other things, with a truly spherical surface—except according to those who regard it as air heated by the celestial sphere's motion. It moves dependently, as is observed in the motion of meteors and comets following the sphere's motion. Air does not behave this way relative to fire, because its moisture makes it readily separate and its parts do not remain in their positions.

It is sometimes argued that each part of fire has a corresponding part of the sphere functioning as its natural place. The objection is that this is unintelligible given the likeness of fire's parts and likewise of the sphere's parts.

Air is truly spherical at its convex surface, not its concave surface. It has four layers: the smoky; beneath it the pure; then the intensely cold layer, chilled by proximity to vapours; then the layer heated by reflected rays.

Water is a single layer. Earth has three: the layer exposed to rays, then the clayey, then the pure. Earth together with water is like one sphere. Earth is not truly spherical because of its uneven surface. This results from constraint, and it has not returned to sphericity because dryness belongs to its nature. The statement that this does not impair its sphericity means its sphericity to sense; otherwise sphericity admits no intensification or diminution.

Their principles require water to encompass all the earth. For the exposure of part of it they mention no reliable cause except divine providence. If they mean this in its apparent sense, they have found the right path; but they explain it as knowledge of the order in its most perfect form.

<a id="m-iv-p437"></a>

**Source p. 437**

The principal argument for the sphericity of all these bodies is their simplicity; for the earth's centrality, its absolute heaviness; and for its rest, its being in its own locus, together with the absence of circular inclination because rectilinear inclination exists. Water's sphericity is sometimes inferred from seafarers' seeing mountain summits before their bases; the earth's centrality from stars' not differing in apparent size according to locality; and its sphericity from stellar rising and setting and the elevation and depression of the pole varying in a uniform proportion according to locality. This appears to be intuitive conjecture; otherwise, even granting the absence of another cause, it does not establish exact sphericity and centrality.

### Third class: Compounds without temperament

They are of several kinds.

#### First kind: What occurs above the earth

Ascending vapour may become rarefied and turn to air; or it may reach the intensely cold layer, condense, collect into cloud, and fall in drops as rain. If cold strikes it before it takes the form of drops, it falls as snow; if afterward, as hail. If it does not reach that layer, when abundant it becomes fog; when scanty and condensed by the night's cold, it descends as frost or dew. Abundant vapour may also collect into rain-bearing cloud through condensation by cold without ascending to the intensely cold layer, owing to an impediment.

Smoke may ascend with vapour and become confined in a cloud, violently tearing it upward if it retains its heat, or downward if struck by cold. Its tearing and striking the cloud produce a sound,

<a id="m-iv-p439"></a>

**Source p. 439**

which is thunder, and a subtle fiery phenomenon, which is lightning, or a dense one, which is a thunderbolt. Thick smoke may ignite upon reaching the sphere of fire and appear like a falling star: this is a meteor. Its burning may persist in the shape of a forelock, tail, serpent, or horned animal, rotating with the celestial sphere; its burning may descend to earth, producing conflagration.

Ascending smoky exhalations may condense through cold and descend by their nature, or be driven back by the sphere's motion upon reaching the sphere of fire. Air then undulates: this is wind. Two winds may meet from different directions and rotate, producing whirlwinds. As for winds' varying conditions and terrifying violence, uprooting trees and snatching ships from the seas—although the smoke's return ought to be straight, like vapour's—they have offered no reliable account, nor received from the Sender of winds a breath drawing them back to Him.

A moist, thin, white cloud with small, uniformly positioned particles may intervene between sight and the moon. The visual ray is reflected from the cloud's particles toward the moon's body, each particle conveying the moon's light. A complete or incomplete white circle then appears: the halo. It may be multiple. Something similar may occur around the sun, called the solar halo. When similar particles lie opposite the sun, reflection of the visual ray produces an arc whose upper side is bright red, lower side purple, and middle leek-green: the rainbow. I have observed something similar from the moon on a night of drizzling atmosphere, except that it was smaller, with dense colours, and nearly a complete circle.

<a id="m-iv-p441"></a>

**Source p. 441**

#### Second kind: What occurs on the earth

Intense heat may encounter abundant sticky clay and harden it into stone. Winds and waters excavate its softer parts, leaving the harder parts elevated as mountains. Mountains may also arise from accumulated ruined buildings and other causes. Their hardness retains vapours within them; their limited heating by reflected rays allows snow and moisture to remain upon them. Minerals, clouds, and springs consequently form.

#### Third kind: What occurs within the earth

Vapour, smoke, or something similar may become confined within the earth while its surface is compact. It moves, moves the earth, and may split it, producing earthquakes, sometimes accompanied by burning fire and terrifying sounds.

Vapour may turn into water, and the earth break open into springs, flowing if replenished and otherwise stagnant. Sometimes the overlying weight of earth must be removed, revealing wells or flowing channels. Snow and rain have an evident effect in this.

They acknowledge that their accounts of upper and lower phenomena are conjectures based on intuition and experience, which may become certain for some minds with the assistance of indications; that these phenomena may arise through other causes; and that some of the causes they mention are incomplete and require an influence from spiritual forces. The properties of plants and stones observed in the lands of the Turks concerning winds and rain attest that there is no effecting agent but God and no creator besides Him.

### Fourth class: Compounds possessing temperament

<a id="m-iv-p443"></a>

**Source p. 443**

It comprises a preliminary discussion and several investigations.

## Preliminary discussion: Temperament

When elements with extremely small parts combine and interact through their powers, breaking the intensity of each of the four qualities, an intermediate quality arises, uniform throughout the whole: temperament [mizāj]. The smallness of parts is specified because, although a body can act without contact, in blending it acts through contact, which increases with the surfaces' multiplication following the multiplication of parts.

“Elements” means all four, since according to the majority no temperament arises from only some. Generation and corruption must therefore occur for fire to be obtained. “Powers” means qualities for the physicians, but specific forms for the philosophers, who affirm these and make qualities intermediaries in their action rather than agents. For, if two qualities acted on one another simultaneously, a thing would be overcome by something it was overcoming; if successively, what had been overcome by something would overcome it, and conversely.

The same objection is raised against the quality's serving as intermediary. If it is answered (1) that these are preparatory factors and the effecting cause is something separate, either necessitating—in which case preparation is for necessary consequence—or freely choosing—in which case it is for the customary course of events; or (2) that what is broken is the quality's intensity, which occurs through the contrary quality itself, as when intensely hot water mixes with cold, lukewarm, or even merely less hot water,

<a id="m-iv-p445"></a>

**Source p. 445**

the same answer can be made on the other side. Moreover, the intermediate quality certainly arises where there is no form requiring the breaking of cold's intensity, as when hot and cold water mix.

If it is maintained that this occurs not through acting and being acted upon but through the disappearance of the two qualities and the arising of the intermediate one through a separate cause, the same can be maintained of temperament. If the fiery form that produced the water's heat is made the breaker, it becomes evident that the agents need not be the forms of the simple bodies being blended.

More consistent with their principles is that the agent is the watery form through the incidental heat. For each element's form acts essentially in its own matter and in other matter through a quality, whether essential or incidental, active or passive; its matter is affected correspondingly. They thus avoid what follows for those who make the qualities themselves interact: the active quality's being acted upon and the passive quality's acting.

If it is said, “We know certainly that what is broken in blending is, for example, fire's heat and water's cold,” we reply: yes, in the sense that these disappear and the intermediate quality arises. What is affected and changes from quality to quality, however, is matter alone. Just as matter's being affected with respect to an active quality is not impossible, a form's acting through a passive quality is not impossible. For water's form, for example, certainly breaks fire's dryness through its moisture, not its cold.

<a id="m-iv-p447"></a>

**Source p. 447**

The quality's uniformity means its likeness in every part, composite or simple, so that instances differ only numerically. If the original elemental qualities remained unchanged and uniformity were merely perceptual, owing to thorough blending and inability to discriminate them, there would be no interaction and no unitary quality preparing the compound to receive the emanation of a form or soul. All the elements must therefore undergo alteration in their qualities.

The definition also includes a second temperament and higher orders—as in gold formed from mercury and sulphur—if their agents are made the preserved forms of the simple constituents, as disclosed by the cucurbit and alembic, rather than forms supervening upon compounds through the preparation supplied by a temperamental quality.

Temperament is thus another species of tactile quality, present in every part, including the simple constituents, which retain their forms but not their qualities. Some say they retain their qualities too, and that blending merely makes us sense the intermediate quality; others that their forms have disappeared into a form intermediate between them; others that they have disappeared into another specific form.

### The divisions of temperament

If temperament arises from powers equal in amount, it is balanced. Its impossibility is sometimes claimed on the ground that no constraint produces combination except the predominance of one power, which is denied. Otherwise it is unbalanced, departing from equality through one quality or two noncontrary qualities; its kinds are therefore confined to eight.

<a id="m-iv-p449"></a>

**Source p. 449**

“Balanced” may also designate a blend that receives the appropriate allotment of quantities and qualities, relative to species, subclass, individual, or organ, each considered externally or internally. Thus a human being, for example, has a temperament most suitable to him in comparison with other species, with degrees between two extremes, one degree being nearest to true balance. Likewise a Turk in comparison with other subclasses and with individuals of his own subclass; Zayd in comparison with other individuals and with his own states; and the heart similarly.
