The range of a species' temperament encompasses the temperaments of its subclasses; a subclass's range, those of its individuals; and an individual's range, those of his states.

The organ is considered separately only because medicine investigates the body and its organs. Otherwise an organ is a species in its own right, like the human being, and should admit the three considerations. An individual's balance is considered according to the counterbalancing of his organs' powers, so that their totality approximates true balance. Clearly there is no blending of parts here and no single quality resulting. It is therefore as though this is merely position and relation, or qualities of all the organs insofar as some affect others by mere proximity without blending.

Departures from this balance are likewise confined to eight, by analogy with true balance. The objection is that departure through two contrary qualities is possible here, since both may exceed or fall below the appropriate amount. Departure may therefore occur through one, two, three, or four qualities, by increase or decrease, together or separately, producing eighty kinds.

<a id="m-iv-p451"></a>

**Source p. 451**

The answer is that receiving the appropriate allotment means that the two active qualities have a proportion suitable to the blend, and likewise the two passive qualities. So long as the proportion is preserved, balance remains, whether the amounts increase or decrease. If, for example, the appropriate amounts are ten parts of heat and five of cold, heat may become twelve or eight and cold six or four. If cold becomes six and heat eleven, the blend is colder than appropriate, not hotter; if heat becomes thirteen, it is hotter, not colder. The objection is nevertheless plausible for an individual's balance considered internally: Zayd's heart may become hotter and drier than in its most balanced state, and his brain colder and moister, or conversely. Here there is no intermediate quality preserved through preservation of a proportion.

They disagree over the most balanced locality according to celestial configurations. Ibn Sīnā says the equator, because its inhabitants' conditions of heat and cold are similar. It is no objection that the sun is always overhead or nearby, since overhead passage quickly ends and has little injurious effect; nor that their winters resemble the summer of a locality whose latitude is twice the maximum declination, when the sun passes overhead and heat is extreme. That heat may result from daylight increasing to nearly twice the night, unlike the equator, where they are always equal, balancing heat and cold. Or equatorial inhabitants, habituated to heat, may have temperaments unaffected by overhead heat and experience the air as cold when the sun reaches a solstice, so that they do not depart from balance.

The answer is that similarity in the sense of the absence of appreciable change does not establish proximity to true balance, which is what is disputed.

<a id="m-iv-p453"></a>

**Source p. 453**

Most say it is the fourth clime, because of the greater perfections observed there, following temperament: the nearer temperament is to true balance and the more akin to the One that is the principle, the more worthy it is of having perfection bestowed upon it. The equator is the opposite. Although this could depend on terrestrial causes, it is an intuitive judgement approaching certainty. Indeed, the habitation of the climes and abundance of generation in them depend on balance. The more central a clime and the more abundant its generation and habitation, the closer it is to true balance and the farther from underdevelopment and burning. There is, however, no dispute that a locality more balanced than either may exist through a coincidence of terrestrial causes.

## The investigations

These concern the divisions of the blended compound, called generated things: minerals, plants, and animals. If a principle of nutrition and growth is realised in a compound, either it accompanies a principle of sensation and motion, producing an animal, or it does not, producing a plant. Otherwise the compound is mineral. The absence of sensation and motion in minerals and plants is not certain; indeed, these are sometimes claimed for plants, with indications offered as evidence.

Since differences in the perfection of forms' ranks follow differences in temperaments' proximity to balance, it is not implausible that, in some, diminished receptivity falls short of complete absence and amounts only to weakness and hiddenness.

<a id="m-iv-p455"></a>

**Source p. 455**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.12](#c1-12).*

“Animal” here includes the animal, its parts, and its products, such as hair, bone, milk, silk, and pearl. “Plant” includes trees, fruits, products derived from them, and coral. “Mineral” includes all other blended compounds, even artificial ones such as verdigris and cinnabar, so that the genera are exhaustively divided. Exhaustively dividing the species, however, is beyond human ability.

### First investigation: Minerals

A mineral is either fusible—with malleability, combustibility, or neither—or nonfusible, owing to excessive moisture or dryness.

The first kind includes the seven metallic bodies: gold, silver, tin, lead, iron—which is melted through an artifice—copper, and khārṣīnī. Indications attest their formation from mercury and sulphur according to the differing attributes and mixtures of these. The absence of mercury and sulphur in their mines may result from their transformation or the extreme smallness of their parts. Many affirm the artificial production of these bodies, especially gold and silver. They maintain that obtaining their specific forms, assuming such forms are established, does not require knowledge of their realities and details. It suffices to know how to combine the materials so that they become fit to receive the emanation of forms through causes unknown to us.

<a id="m-iv-p457"></a>

**Source p. 457**

The second kind includes sulphur and arsenic compounds: things whose dryness and oiliness are weakly blended and solidified by cold. The third includes vitriols and salts, whose blend is weak and whose abundant moisture has been solidified by heat and dryness; hence they dissolve in water. Vitriols contain, alongside saltiness and sulphurousness, the power of certain fusible metallic bodies.

The fourth includes mercury, formed by a strong blending of abundant wateriness and subtle sulphurous earthiness. The fifth includes ruby, spinel, peridot, and similar things, in which dry parts are strongly blended with a little wateriness that cold transforms into earthiness, leaving no oily moisture.

Minerals ultimately derive from vapours and smoky exhalations. The artificial production of some, such as sal ammoniac and salt, is evident. They agree that the forms of compound materials such as mercury and sulphur disappear when gold forms, since those forms follow a temperament that ceases when the parts become extremely small. Hence gold's volume and weight do not lie between those of mercury and sulphur, as is the rule for compounds whose constituents retain their forms.

Conclusion: bodies differ in heaviness because their forms differ. Accordingly they differ in volume, spatial occupation, floating on water, and sinking in it; each body's weight in water differs from its weight in air. All this is determined by measuring the water expelled from a vessel when a determinate amount of each body is placed in it. For example, the water displaced by one hundred mithqāls of gold is five and a quarter mithqāls; by silver, nine and two-thirds. The ratio of gold's displaced water to silver's is the ratio of its volume to silver's volume, and of silver's heaviness to gold's heaviness. Subtracting each body's displaced water from its weight in air leaves its weight in water. If its displaced water weighs less than it does, it sinks; if more, it floats; if equal, it descends so that its upper surface touches the water's surface.

<a id="m-iv-p459"></a>

**Source p. 459**

### Second investigation: The natural powers

Because plants possess a greater degree of balance, they share with animals things functioning like certain organs, and powers by which individuals are preserved, attain their quantitative perfections, and produce their likes, through which the species persists. These are called natural powers.

One is the nutritive power, which transforms food into likeness with the nourished thing. Four powers serve it: (1) the attractive, drawing food; (2) the retentive, holding what has been drawn until digested; (3) the digestive, transforming food into what suits the nourished thing's substance; and (4) the expulsive, removing what is not needed. These motions and rests are not voluntary, since food has no will; nor natural, since they occur against its nature; they are therefore forced. Their constraining agent is neither the animal's will, since they may occur without it, nor something external, which is evident. They must therefore be powers within the animal. All four occur in every organ and may be multiplied in some.

The stages of digestion are not restricted in number, but are counted as four with regard to the organs and the evident changes: (1) in the stomach, beginning in the mouth; (2) in the liver; (3) in the vessels; (4) in the organs. In the stomach food becomes a substance resembling thick gruel, called chyle. Its dense portion is expelled through the intestines, while its subtle portion is drawn to the liver through the mesenteric vessels and becomes the four humours. These are then driven through the vessels, what suits each organ is distinguished, and it seeps onto that organ through the dense vessels' openings. It then becomes like the organ in colour, consistency, temperament, and adhesion.

<a id="m-iv-p461"></a>

**Source p. 461**

The likeness between food and the nourished thing relevant to preserving health is that existing when the food becomes part of the organ, for only then is it actually food. Before this it is potentially food, with varying degrees of proximity and remoteness.

Another is the growth power, which inserts food among the body's parts, increasing its dimensions in a natural proportion. It is sometimes said to be the nutritive power itself: initially this can supply both replacement and increase because of its great strength, the body's smallness, and abundant moisture; later it cannot. Thus, when weakness and diminished moisture render it unable to supply replacement, the appointed term arrives.

Another is the generative power, which obtains from food what can serve as the principle of another individual of the nourished thing's species, divides it into different parts, and bestows the configurations through which it becomes an actual likeness. The majority hold that the generative power obtains and differentiates the matter, while another power, called the formative, produces the configurations.

Some deny the formative power, being certain that actions of this kind—the principal basis of inference to the Maker's power, knowledge, and wisdom—cannot issue from a simple, unconscious power inhering in matter uniform in its parts or blend.

The objection that the soul's powers are its instruments and servants and therefore cannot arise before it or act independently applies only if the soul is made to arise after the body and the formative power is made a power of the offspring's soul, like its nutritive and growth powers.

<a id="m-iv-p463"></a>

**Source p. 463**

Their uncertainty here is shown by their disagreement over what gathers the parts and preserves them. The Imām states that the parents' souls gather the embryo's bodily parts; the temperament then remains under the mother's soul's governance until it is prepared for the arising of a soul that preserves it and gathers the remaining parts. Ibn Sīnā is reported to say that the parents' souls gather them, while their combination is preserved first by the body's formative power and then by its rational soul. In the *Shifāʾ* he explicitly says that what gathers the elements of every animal's body, combines them appropriately, and preserves their proper order is its own soul.

The more plausible account is that the parents' souls initially act through their powers until they separate from the humours what is suitable as material for semen, preparing it for a form preserving its temperament. It then develops in the womb until prepared for a soul from which, alongside preservation of temperament, vegetative acts issue. This attracts nourishment to that matter and prepares it to receive a soul from which, alongside the preceding acts, animal acts issue, and so on until the rational soul.

They remain uncertain: (1) whether these powers are essentially multiple, since the multiplicity of acts, their differences in strength and weakness, and their differences in origination and persistence may depend on differences in recipients and instruments; (2) whether they differ from the vegetative or animal soul; (3) whether the nutritive power differs from its servants; and (4) whether the principle of obtaining food, assimilating it, and attaching it is one or multiple.

<a id="m-iv-p465"></a>

**Source p. 465**

They are perplexed over how these carefully ordered acts issue from weak powers, especially when one considers the marvellous forms and shapes and remarkable patterns and colours observed in animal and plant species. They resort—as sound innate disposition requires—to the mighty Creator's permission and the determination of the All-Powerful, All-Knowing.

Conclusion: just as a plant is not an animal, it is not living, since life is an attribute requiring sensation and voluntary motion. Some make the processing of nourishment life and therefore call the plant living; others go further, assigning plants intellect as well as sensation.

### Third investigation: The psychic powers

Because of its greater balance, the animal possesses powers called psychic, either apprehensive or motive. A further power may be affirmed as their principle, specifically called the vital power, present in a paralysed or withered organ.

The apprehensive powers are the external and internal senses, each numbering five according to what is established by immediate awareness and demonstration. The impossibility of others is not certain, since something possible for a thing may fail to arise through the absence of a condition.

Some posit another power apprehending pleasure, pain, and indeed all immediately experienced states, because when these occur we find a state different from their intellectual or imaginative apprehension. The answer is that these are apprehensions, not objects apprehended.

<a id="m-iv-p467"></a>

**Source p. 467**

### The external senses

One is touch, a power pervading the body through which heat, cold, and similar things are apprehended upon contact. It is indispensable to the animal as the nutritive power is to the plant. Hence, with the nerves' assistance, it pervades all organs except those harmed by it, such as liver, spleen, kidney, lung, and bone. An animal survives the loss of the other senses but not touch. Some affirm touch for celestial bodies, others for the simple elements.

Ibn Sīnā inclines toward a multiplicity of tactile powers corresponding to the different contrarieties among tactile objects. For the contrariety between heat and cold differs in kind from that between moisture and dryness, for example, unlike the contrarieties among tastes.

Another is taste, a power distributed through the nerve spread over the tongue's body. Through it tastes are apprehended, on condition of contact and the mediation of salivary moisture free of a similar or contrary taste, so that it acquires the tasted thing's quality or particles of the thing mix with it and penetrate. The effects of the tongue's powers of taste and touch may be distinguishable, as with sweetness and heat, or indistinguishable, as with pungency.

Another is smell, a power in the two projections at the front of the brain. It apprehends odours when air qualified by them reaches those projections, not when particles detached from the fragrant thing reach them; otherwise frequent smelling would diminish its weight and volume. The detachment of vaporous particles may, however, help the air acquire the quality quickly, and frequent touching may help dissolve the fragrant things' moistures. Hence

<a id="m-iv-p469"></a>

**Source p. 469**

heat stirs odours, and smelling makes an apple wither. Nor does the fragrant thing affect smell without alteration in the air; otherwise someone arriving after the fragrant thing had disappeared would not apprehend its odour. Asking how a fragrant thing acts across parasangs while fire, despite its strong effect, heats only nearby things is merely an appeal to improbability.

Some philosophers maintain that celestial bodies possess smell and contain odours, and that air's reaching the nasal passage is a condition only in the elemental world.

Another is hearing, a power in the nerve inside the auditory canal through which sounds are apprehended. Sound may subsist in every part of the air entering through narrow passages, while hearing is conditioned by the arrival's being first, since a thing is not affected by its like. Reports of hearing celestial sounds therefore do not accord with philosophical principles.

Another is sight, a power at the junction of the two nerves branching toward the eyes. It apprehends colours, lights, and other things through the imprinting of the visible object's likeness in part of the crystalline humour. What is seen is the thing, while what is imprinted is its likeness; their differing in size is not impossible. Alternatively, sight occurs through the emission of a ray shaped as a solid cone, or composed of lines gathered near the vertex and dispersed near the base. Some say the ray extends straight while its extremity moves over the visible object; others that sight occurs through air qualified by the visual ray; others through confrontation alone under its conditions, without imprint or ray. The truth is that it occurs solely through God's creation.

<a id="m-iv-p471"></a>

**Source p. 471**

The well-known philosophical views are imprint and ray. The principal argument for imprint is that the eye's light is visible, and that a thing's likeness necessarily imprints itself in a confronting polished, illuminated surface. Yet this does not establish that vision occurs through it. Arguments are also drawn from analogy with the other senses, to which the sensible object comes; from the sun's image sometimes remaining in the eye after prolonged looking and subsequent turning away; and from a nearby thing's appearing larger, supposedly because imprint occurs along a cone of air whose base is the visible object's surface, so that proximity makes the chord of the angle greater. This is weak.

Proponents of the ray argue that vision differs according to the ray's scarcity or abundance, thickness or thinness, and the object's position along the cone's axis or sides; that light is sometimes seen separating from the eye in darkness; and that, when the eye is narrowed at a lamp, ray-like lines appear.

The answer is that this returns to the eye's light, called the visual spirit, which prepares a corresponding effect in the confronting object, with an imaginary cone traced between it and the visible thing. Perhaps this is what is intended by emission of the ray or ray-body. For it is certainly impossible that something should issue from the eye and spread over half the world's sphere, move in different directions, penetrate the heavens, and remain undisturbed by blowing winds, besides other indications.

Nevertheless, emission of a ray in the sense of its falling from the eye upon the visible thing, as with luminous bodies, is a view chosen by many investigators. They base on it the apparent enlargement of nearby things or things in water, seeing one thing as two, seeing a tree inverted in water, and other details fully treated in optics.

<a id="m-iv-p473"></a>

**Source p. 473**

Conditions sometimes stipulated for vision, besides soundness of the sense, the viewer's intention, and the visible object's presence, are that the object be dense, illuminated, confronting the viewer or equivalent to this, without a veil, excessive proximity, remoteness, or smallness, or a cause of error.

Vision is claimed to follow necessarily when these conditions are present; otherwise towering mountains could be before us [unseen]. The reply is that our denial of this belongs to knowledge grounded in the customary order.

### The internal senses

One is the common sense, the power in which sensible forms gather, conveyed to it through the senses. Evidence for it includes judging some sensible objects of others; sleepers' and sick persons' seeing what is not externally present; and everyone's seeing a falling drop as a line and a revolving flame as a circle. This rests on sensible forms not being imprinted in the soul, although the soul judges and apprehends, and on the necessary claim that only what confronts sight, or is equivalent to it, can be imprinted in sight.

If it is said, “Touch and taste certainly do not occur in the brain,” we reply: yes, in the sense that the brain is not their primary, particular instrument.

Another is the image-retaining faculty [khayāl], which preserves sensible forms after they disappear from the common sense. Two arguments support it.

<a id="m-iv-p475"></a>

**Source p. 475**

First: preservation differs from reception and therefore requires a distinct principle. Their combination in the image-retaining faculty may depend on matter and power. The common sense's diverse apprehensions depend on the many paths of transmission, just as the soul's apprehensions and acts depend on its powers.

Second: a form imprinted in the common sense may disappear, not completely as in forgetting, but with the possibility of being recalled through the slightest attention, which is absent-mindedness. Unless it were stored in another power, absent-mindedness would be forgetting. Both arguments are weak.

Weaker still is the attempted refutation through the impossibility of a large thing's being imprinted in a small thing, and through the crowding together of forms while their distinction remains. These difficulties concern external things, not their forms.

Another is estimation [wahm], through which particular meanings are apprehended, such as Zayd's particular hostility. “Meanings” are what cannot be apprehended by the external senses; “forms” are the opposite. Thus, when we see something yellow and judge it honey and sweet, what is attributed to estimation is the particular judgement, not yellowness or sweetness. All are present to the soul with the assistance of instruments.

Another preserves estimation's judgements, and is called memory with regard to retrieving them. Another manipulates forms and meanings by combining and separating them. It is called the cogitative faculty insofar as intellect employs it, and the imaginative faculty insofar as estimation employs it.

<a id="m-iv-p477"></a>

**Source p. 477**

Conclusion: the front of the brain's first ventricle is the seat of common sense, its rear the seat of image-retention; the middle ventricle is the seat of imagination; the front of the last ventricle, estimation; and its rear, preservation. The principal basis for multiplying these powers and locating their seats is the multiplicity of effects and their impairment when the seats are impaired, together with the certainty that sensation belongs to bodily powers, that their instruments mean only their substrates, and that they are not impaired through some other organ. It would therefore be invalid to make them one and refer the multiplicity and impairment to their instruments. All this is weak.

Ibn Sīnā hesitated over distinguishing estimation and imagination as multiple powers. Physicians confined themselves to image-retention, cogitation, and memory.

### The motive powers

One is appetitive, prompting the attraction of what is conceived as beneficial, and called the concupiscible power; or prompting the repulsion of what is conceived as harmful, and called the irascible power. Another effects the drawing of nerves toward their origin, as in contraction, or in the opposite direction, as in extension. The principle of appetite belongs to the apprehensive powers.

Some of these powers may be absent in certain animal species or individuals, whether through their constitution or an incidental condition.

## Second treatise: Matters concerning immaterial entities

<a id="m-iv-p479"></a>

**Source p. 479**

It comprises two chapters.

### Chapter One: The soul

It comprises several investigations.

#### First investigation

Soul is divided into celestial and human. The term may also apply to what is not immaterial, such as the vegetative soul, the principle of plants' effects, and the animal soul, the principle of animals' effects. Accordingly, soul is explained as the first perfection of a natural body possessing instruments. With respect to nutrition and growth it is vegetative; with respect to sensation and motion, animal; with respect to intellection of universals, human; and with respect to willing circular motion, celestial—if stars, epicycles, and the like are treated as instruments. To restrict the definition to terrestrial souls, “having life potentially” is added.

Their principles would seem to require, in a human being for example, a human soul, another animal soul, and another vegetative soul. Yet they state that this is not so. Rather, the vegetative soul produces what the mineral form produces; the animal soul produces what both produce; and the human soul produces what all produce.

For us, effects depend on the powerful agent, and bodies differ through incidental attributes, since they consist of homogeneous substances. Nevertheless, revealed texts attest that the human being has a spirit beyond this sensible frame, which is constantly changing and dissolving. Necessary awareness almost establishes this with the slightest prompting. This is what is meant by the human soul.

<a id="m-iv-p481"></a>

**Source p. 481**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.13](#c1-13).*

The position relied upon among the theologians is that it is a subtle body pervading the body, neither changing nor dissolving; or the abiding original parts, life being unable to subsist in fewer than these. This appears to be what is meant by the particular frame and sensible constitution—that is, a constitution of a kind capable of being sensed.

The philosophers and many Muslims hold that it is an immaterial substance governing the body, connected first with a cardiac spirit that pervades the body and bestows their powers upon the organs.

We have several arguments. First: we predicate a universal of a particular and must therefore apprehend both. What apprehends the particular in us is only body, as in other animals. Second: everyone is certain that what is indicated by “I” is present here, standing and sitting, and this is only body. Third: if the soul were immaterial, its relation to bodies would be equal, so it could migrate, and Zayd now would not be the person he was. All these arguments are weak. Fourth: the apparent senses of revealed texts, which do not yield certainty.

As for arguing that there is no proof of its immateriality and it must therefore be denied, besides being weak, this is countered by saying that there is no proof of its spatial occupation and that this must therefore be denied.

They argue in several ways. First: through intellection the soul becomes a substrate for what is not material, such as immaterial entities; for what cannot be restricted to position and magnitude, such as universals; for what admits no division, such as existence, unity, the point, and all the simple things in which compounds terminate;

<a id="m-iv-p483"></a>

**Source p. 483**

and for what cannot coexist in a body—such as contraries, or indeed different forms and shapes—though it can coexist in an immaterial entity, where forms do not crowd one another, even forms of contraries or contradictories.

This rests on intellection's occurring through the acquisition of a form; on denying something positioned but indivisible; on a form and the thing whose form it is being alike in immateriality, position and magnitude, divisibility, contrariety, and the absence of these; and on division of the substrate entailing division of the inherent when inherence belongs to the substrate itself rather than to a nature attaching to it, as a point belongs to a line by reason of its termination.

Second: the soul apprehends itself, its instruments, and its apprehensions. Repeated acts and the weakness of organs and instruments bring it strength and perfection rather than weakness and fatigue. No bodily power behaves so. This returns to induction and analogy.

Third: if the intellectual power were in a body, either the body's presence would suffice for its intellection, in which case that intellection would never cease—like the soul's intellection of itself and its necessary attributes unrelated to something else—or it would not suffice, in which case intellection of the body would never occur, since multiple forms of a single thing in a single matter are impossible. This rests on apprehension's occurring through acquisition of a form.

They then build upon universal apprehension's requiring an immaterial intellect, and particular apprehension's requiring instruments, to affirm immaterial souls and bodily powers for the celestial spheres. For their motions are not natural—what is naturally sought is not naturally fled—nor forced, since forced motion opposes nature and is absent when nature is absent, and follows the constraining force and would therefore be uniform. Their motions are instead voluntary. Mere imagination does not suffice, since it does not remain ordered forever; nor does universal intellection, since it cannot be the principle of particular motions, its relation to all being equal. Most of these premises are open to denial.

<a id="m-iv-p485"></a>

**Source p. 485**

#### Second investigation

Souls are alike because their definition is one; alternatively, they differ because their concomitants and effects differ. Both arguments are weak.

For us, their dependence on the freely choosing powerful agent requires their temporal origination, whether they are immaterial or not. The apparent senses of revealed texts differ over whether this origination precedes or follows the body.

Among the philosophers, some say souls are beginningless, because what is originated cannot be everlasting or independent of a substrate. Both claims are denied. Others say they are originated, for several reasons.

First: before attachment to bodies they would be idle, and there is no idleness in existence. This differs from their state after separation, when they enjoy repose and bounty or suffer torment and fires.

Second: when a body acquires its particular temperament, a soul corresponding to its preparedness emanates upon it, because emanation is universal. What is conditioned by something originated is originated. If it is said, “Its cessation must then follow that condition's cessation,” we reply: the condition concerns origination, not existence.

The objections are that what awaits the acquisition of perfection is not idle, and that temperament is a condition of attachment, not origination.

Third—the principal argument: souls are certainly multiple after attachment. Before attachment, if they were one, multiplicity after unity would conflict with immateriality or entail the conclusion;

<a id="m-iv-p487"></a>

**Source p. 487**

if multiple, distinction through quiddity and its concomitants would conflict with their likeness; distinction through what inheres in them, such as awareness of their own identity, would entail circularity; and distinction through material accidents, with bodies succeeding one another beginninglessly, would entail transmigration and beginningless body. After separation, however, distinction remains through the properties each soul has acquired, the least being awareness of its own identity.

The objections are: (1) their likeness is denied, even between two souls; (2) the impossibility of beginningless body and transmigration is denied—especially since the refutation of transmigration has itself been based on the soul's origination. If it is said, “A soul becomes determinate only through a particular body; before that body there is no determination and hence no existence, whether transmigration is false or not,” we reply: one must refute its having become determinate earlier through another particular body, and so on.

It may be answered that the opponent admits the two premises.

The soul is certain that no other governor accompanies it in this body and that it does not govern another body. Soul and body therefore correspond: no body has two souls and no soul two bodies, either simultaneously or successively. Otherwise the soul would have to remember something of the first body's states; the number of generated bodies would have to correspond to that of corrupted bodies; and another soul, newly arising through complete preparedness and universal emanation, would accompany it.

The objection is that, even if these arguments are granted, they exclude only migration to another human body, not to another animal, a plant, an inanimate object—as the transmigrationists variously hold—or a celestial body.

<a id="m-iv-p489"></a>

**Source p. 489**

Their strongest grounds for affirming transmigration generally are that nothing in existence is idle; that souls are constituted to seek perfection, which occurs through attachment; and that souls are beginningless and therefore finite—depending on finite causes and aspects, and because an actually infinite multitude cannot exist—whereas bodies are infinite. All these arguments are weak.

The established transformation of some unbelievers into apes and swine, and the return of souls to resurrected bodies, have nothing to do with the disputed doctrine.

Some relate that perfect souls join the world of intellects; intermediate souls attach to celestial bodies or imaginal forms, which you will learn about; and deficient souls attach to animal bodies corresponding to the character traits they have acquired and the dispositions established in them, progressing until the sufferings and agonies they encounter release them from darkness. The decisive revealed texts concerning the return after death declare this false. They nevertheless turn some verses concerning the inhabitants of the Fire toward this account, fabricating a claim against God.

#### Third investigation

Those who distinguish soul from body agree that it does not perish when the body perishes, since a relation of governance plainly does not require this. For us, however, proof of its persistence is revelation. For the philosophers, it is the impossibility of its perishing, because it depends on what is beginningless, either independently or through a condition required for origination but not persistence. This is weak.

They also argue that, if it could perish, it would be in matter like forms and accidents. For the potentiality and receptivity for perishing—in the sense of dispositional possibility, not intrinsic, conceptually considered possibility—requires a substrate remaining when what is received occurs, in which what belongs among the soul's attributes subsists. The reply denies this requirement when what is received is privative.

<a id="m-iv-p491"></a>

**Source p. 491**

#### Fourth investigation

For us, the soul apprehends particulars: (1) it predicates the universal of the particular and judges two particulars different; (2) its particular acts depend on particular apprehensions, since a universal judgement relates equally to particulars; and (3) everyone is certain that he himself sees and hears.

For the philosophers, the senses apprehend particulars. Otherwise there would be no certainty that seeing belongs to sight and hearing to hearing; impairment of an organ would not require impairment of its act; sensation would not depend on presence, since the soul's state would not differ; things with positions and magnitudes would not be imagined, since they cannot be imprinted in something immaterial; and right and left would not be distinguished when we imagine a square flanked by two equal squares, since these are distinguished only by their locations.

Interpreting their statement to mean that the soul apprehends particulars not directly but through instruments removes the dispute and reconciles the two parties' proofs. It is not undermined by beasts' sensation without a soul: even if this absence is granted, sharing concomitants does not require sharing their ground. Nor by the soul's apprehension of its own identity, since this requires no imprinted form; moreover, the discussion concerns material particulars whose forms cannot be imprinted in it. Nor by the argument that attachment to this body requires conceiving and intending it, since conceiving some body would not suffice, relating equally to all: this attachment is a natural yearning according to affinity, not voluntary so as to depend on conceiving that individual body. Nor by apprehension of instruments when the soul intends to employ them: this may be imaginative, or their particular determinations may arise through relation without reaching full particularity, as when it apprehends a hearing power belonging to us in this sensible body.

<a id="m-iv-p493"></a>

**Source p. 493**

A difficulty nevertheless remains: in apprehending a sensible object, if the form is also imprinted in the soul, the original impossibility returns. If it is not, what state arises in the soul when the form is imprinted in the instrument that we call “apprehension” and “the thing's presence to the soul”? Why would a similar state not suffice for apprehension of the universal without a form in the soul?

Notice: on their account, apprehension of particulars does not remain when the instruments are lost. On ours it remains; indeed, the apparent teaching of Islam affirms newly arising apprehensions as well. Hence benefit is obtained from visiting graves and seeking assistance from the souls of the virtuous.

#### Fifth investigation

The soul's power, insofar as it receives an effect from the principle in order to be perfected, is called theoretical intellect; insofar as it acts upon the body to perfect it, practical intellect.

The theoretical has four ranks. It is either: (1) a weak preparedness, consisting solely in receptivity to intelligibles, called material intellect; (2) an intermediate preparedness for acquired intellectual objects through possession of necessary cognitions, called intellect in habitu; or (3) a strong preparedness, the capacity to summon acquired intellectual objects without a new acquisition because they have been acquired and stored, called intellect in actuality. Or (4) it is the soul's perfection in this respect, the presence of acquired intellectual objects to it in actual contemplation, called acquired intellect. Alternatively, the soul is either empty; adorned only with necessary cognitions; adorned also with acquired cognitions without their present contemplation; or adorned with them together with their presence.

Expressions differ over whether these four name the states, the soul considered according to them, or powers that are their principles; and whether acquired intellect requires mere presence,

<a id="m-iv-p495"></a>

**Source p. 495**

so that in existence it precedes intellect in actuality, although it is the end in nobility and perfection; or the presence of all intellectual objects without any ever disappearing, making its attainment while the soul remains attached to a body impossible or extremely unlikely. The first better fits the exhaustive division of ranks.

The practical intellect—the power of acting upon things, devising crafts, and distinguishing benefits from harms to order affairs in this life and the next—seeks assistance from the theoretical, since its acts arise from particular judgements derived from universal judgements.

Theoretical wisdom, explained as knowing things as they are within human capacity, follows from the theoretical intellect. Practical wisdom, explained as carrying out affairs as they should be within that capacity, follows from the practical intellect. Hence wisdom is said to be the soul's emergence from potentiality into actuality in its possible perfection, and understanding [fiqh] to name knowledge and action together.

Practical wisdom may also mean knowledge of matters connected with our choice, theoretical wisdom being restricted to what is otherwise. If theoretical wisdom concerns what is independent of matter both mentally and externally, it is metaphysics; if independent mentally only, mathematics; if requiring matter in both, natural philosophy. Practical wisdom, if concerned with reforming an individual, is the refinement of character; with those sharing a household, household management; with a city, political governance.

<a id="m-iv-p497"></a>

**Source p. 497**

The roots of virtuous character are the balance of the concupiscible power, which is temperance; of the irascible power, which is courage; and of the rational power, which is wisdom. Their combination is justice. Each has two vicious extremes, excess and deficiency: for temperance, insensibility and licentiousness; for courage, rashness and cowardice; for wisdom, sophistical cleverness and stupidity.

#### Sixth investigation

Extraordinary acts and apprehensions are sometimes observed from souls. For us they occur solely through God's creation. Concerning acts, the philosophers say that a soul may possess power to act upon something beyond its own body, perhaps becoming like a soul of the world or of certain bodies, especially those akin to its own body. It is therefore not implausible for it to produce rain and earthquakes, destroy cities, remove diseases, and the like. It may injure something it admires through a property within itself: the evil eye. Or it may produce evils or extraordinary effects through its wickedness and the practice of particular learned acts: sorcery; through assistance from spiritual beings: adjurations; through celestial bodies: planetary invocations; through combining celestial and terrestrial powers: talismans; through elemental properties: natural magical operations; or through mathematical proportions: mechanical contrivances. Some of these may combine with others.

Concerning apprehensions during sleep, they say that the soul connects with the unseen world because the senses are inactive,

<a id="m-iv-p499"></a>

**Source p. 499**

and receives a particular apprehensive form, either in itself or through the imagination's production. If the form remains unchanged, so that the produced form differs only in universality and particularity, and reaches the common sense, it is a veridical dream. If imagination alters it by substituting forms, then, if it can be restored to its original through a kind of analysis, it is a dream requiring interpretation; otherwise confused dreams.

Confused dreams also include forms reaching the common sense from images imprinted in the image-retaining faculty through sensation, or transferred there from imagination during sleep. These may have existed previously or arise during sleep because imagination's acts change with the temperament of the spirit bearing it, as when predominance of yellow bile leads one to see yellow things.

Concerning waking apprehensions, they say that the soul may become strong enough to withdraw from the sensible world and connect with the unseen world, while imagination frees the common sense from the external senses. The soul then discovers some unseen things. This may occur through hearing a delightful or terrifying voice, through writing, or through speech from a human, angel, jinn, or unseen caller. Some of this may occur when sensory powers are weakened by illness or spiritual discipline, or when sense is stunned and imagination bewildered by rapid running, gazing at a transparent thing that makes sight tremble, overwhelming fear, or the like.

In general, the soul's wonders are too manifest to be hidden and too numerous to count. The discussion concerns only their causes. Certain extraordinary occurrences in other animals, as established in zoology, may attest that they too have immaterial souls. Knowledge rests with God.

<a id="m-iv-p501"></a>

**Source p. 501**

### Chapter Two: Intellect

It comprises several investigations.

#### First investigation: Establishing intellect

There are several arguments.

First: the first created thing must exist alone and bring what follows it into existence. This can only be intellect. Body has multiplicity; prime matter, form, and accident require something besides their cause in order to exist; and soul requires something else in order to produce existence, otherwise it would be intellect.

Second: the cause of the first body must contain multiplicity, lest one cause have multiple effects, and must be independent of corporeality in essence and action, lest a thing precede itself. The cases of body and accident are evident. Soul's action is conditioned by body, while prime matter and form cannot be realised without one another.

Both arguments rest on the impossibility of many proceeding from one and on denying choice and attributes. Moreover, the first effect need not bring what follows it into existence; it may merely be an intermediary, so it may be a soul or one of body's two constituents. Further, the soul's requiring body for its apprehensions does not prevent it from independently bringing body into existence.

Third: the perpetual voluntary motions of the spheres can only seek an enduring but nonstatic resemblance to an intelligible that is actually perfect and whose perfections have no limit. Otherwise motion would cease or seek the impossible. This intelligible is not the Necessary Existent, since then the motions would not differ; it must therefore be intellect. The reply denies most of the premises.

<a id="m-iv-p503"></a>

**Source p. 503**

#### Second investigation: The states of intellects

They maintain that intellects number ten: after the first, according to the number of the spheres. This excludes fewer, not more. The tenth governs the elemental world according to the preparednesses arising from renewed celestial configurations. Because intellects are free of matter both in inherence and attachment, they are beginningless; each species is confined to one individual; they possess all their perfections; and they intellect themselves and the other immaterial entities—indeed all universals, but not particulars.

They are principles of the perfections of celestial and human souls, and indeed of souls and bodies themselves. Thus it is said that the first emanation is an intellect, from which proceeds an intellect in consideration of its existence, a soul in consideration of its necessity through another, and a body in consideration of its contingency, according to the most fitting correspondence; and so on to the last.

The objections are that, if these considerations are positive, the original difficulty returns; if privative, they cannot be constituents of the giver of existence. Even if this is granted, why does the Necessary Existent not suffice through His negations and relations? And how can one intellect suffice for the sphere of the fixed stars? Moreover, if intellects share one quiddity, the series does not terminate; if they do not, a sphere may arise only after several intellects, so how can their number be asserted as ten? Furthermore, individual spheres certainly exceed nine, and comprehensive spheres may do so. Extricating their account from these difficulties requires a prolixity unsuitable for this book.

<a id="m-iv-p505"></a>

**Source p. 505**

#### Third investigation: Angels, jinn, and devils

They maintain that angels are the immaterial intellects and celestial souls; jinn are immaterial spirits acting upon elemental things; and the devil is the human imaginative power. Some hold that, after separation, good human souls are jinn and evil souls devils.

They maintain that every sphere has a spirit from which many spirits branch, connected with its parts and extremities. The governor of the Throne is called the universal soul, whose effect is seen in everything within it; its branches stand to it as powers stand to the human soul. Likewise, every division of elemental things—mountains, deserts, inhabited places, plant and animal species, and others—has a spirit governing it and preserving it from harm, called the perfect nature, and in the language of revealed law the angel of that species.

For us, angels are subtle bodies assuming different forms, characterised by goodness, obedience, knowledge, and power to perform arduous works. Jinn are similar, except that some obey and others disobey. Devils are characterised by evil and misleading others. Fire predominates in them, and air in the first two kinds.

They may sometimes acquire coverings of dense bodies and become visible to human beings; or contain enough dense element to appear to some eyes and in some circumstances. Their temperaments and specific forms may preserve their composition against dissolution and allow them to assume forms. On the doctrine of the freely choosing powerful agent, there is no difficulty.

<a id="m-iv-p507"></a>

**Source p. 507**

Conclusion: some people maintain that between the sensible and intelligible worlds is an intermediary called the imaginal world, whose cities cannot be counted. In it, every existent, immaterial or material—even colours, shapes, tastes, smells, positions, motions, rests, and so forth—has a likeness subsisting through itself, independent of matter, time, and place. Hence these are called suspended likenesses and immaterial apparitions. Upon this they base their accounts of bodily resurrection, dreams, many apprehensions and extraordinary events, jinn, devils, ghouls, and the like.

They argue that the forms seen in mirrors and similar things are not sheer nonexistence; nor do they belong to the sensible world, which is evident; nor to the intelligible world, since they possess magnitudes; nor are they imprinted in a bodily instrument, since a large thing cannot be imprinted in a small one.

This is a feeble specious argument upon which an exalted claim has been built. Investigators among the theologians and philosophers have therefore paid it no attention. God knows best.

---

<a id="division-v"></a>

# Division V: Divine matters

<a id="m-v-p509"></a>

**Source p. 509**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.14](#c1-14).*

It comprises several chapters.

## Chapter One: The essence

It comprises several investigations.

### First investigation: Establishing His existence

There are two routes, those of the philosophers and the theologians. Their content is that contingent existents must have a necessary giver of existence, and temporally originated things a beginningless originator, because circularity and infinite regress are impossible.

It may be imagined that one can dispense with the falsity of predominance without a determinant, saying instead that there must be an existent requiring no other, to avoid circularity and regress. Or that one can dispense with the falsity of circularity and regress, offering the following arguments.

First: if no necessary existent existed, the contingent would exist through itself, which is evidently false.

Second: the totality of contingents, taken so that none falls outside it, must because of its contingency have something independently sufficient in agency. This cannot be the totality itself, nor every part of it, which is evident; nor some of its parts, since that would make a part the cause of itself and its causes, and since it requires another part and is therefore not independent, and since, whatever part is supposed, its cause has a stronger claim. It must therefore be external to the totality: the Necessary Existent, exalted is He.

<a id="m-v-p511"></a>

**Source p. 511**

Third: the totality of contingents must have a cause through which its existence becomes necessary and its nonexistence impossible. No individual in the totality is such, since each requires another and no necessity results by considering it alone.

Fourth: if the independently sufficient principle of originated things were neither necessary nor inclusive of the Necessary, then, if it had an external cause, its independence would be nullified. Otherwise, if it were impossible before the originated thing existed, an alteration of intrinsic status would follow; if possible, predominance without a determinant would follow.

Whether these arguments dispense with refuting infinite regress is open to examination.

### Second investigation

Since what is manifest to everyone's observation is the world of bodies, celestial and elemental, simple and composite, inference from their essences and attributes, through their contingency or origination, to a beginningless, powerful, knowing Maker has become widespread.

God's speech frequently directs people to this because it benefits the generality more and affects souls more deeply, whereas the subtlety of philosophical proofs opens the door to doubts. No weight is given to the possibility that this Maker might be other than the Necessary Existent. This is either because intuition attests that He can only be absolutely independent—which is what “Necessary” means—making these persuasive arguments whose multiplication rarely fails to lead to certainty; or because the mind naturally proceeds to the conclusion that, if He were created, His creator would have a stronger claim to these attributes, and this cannot proceed endlessly; or because the purpose is to refute those who acknowledge for this world no existent possessing creation and command, from whom is the beginning and to whom is the end. Everyone's acknowledgement of Him under necessity is also invoked to indicate that, besides being established by demonstration and persuasion, His existence is among widely accepted truths, in keeping with what suits exalted inquiries.

<a id="m-v-p513"></a>

**Source p. 513**

### Third investigation

The Necessary Existent's essence differs from contingent essences. Otherwise He would be distinguished through a particular feature. Necessity would then belong either to the essence, entailing the necessity of contingents, or to it together with the particular feature, entailing the Necessary Existent's contingency through composition.

It is said instead that His essence resembles theirs and is distinguished by necessity, life, and perfect knowledge and power; or by divinity requiring these four. Arguments resembling those previously given for existence's commonality are offered.

The reply is that these establish only the unity of the concept “essence” predicable of essences, not likeness among the essences themselves. For its application to them is that of a concomitant, not an essential constituent, as previously explained concerning existence.

### Fourth investigation

Since the Necessary Existent is that whose nonexistence is impossible, establishing His existence requires no further proof that He is beginningless and everlasting. The theologians, restricting their demonstration to a Maker of the world, require such proof: beginninglessness follows from the falsity of infinite regress and from the forthcoming proof that everything exists through the Beginningless One's power; everlastingness follows from the earlier account of beginninglessness entailing the impossibility of nonexistence.

## Chapter Two: Declaring Him free of imperfection

It comprises several investigations.

### First investigation: Divine unity

There is no multiplicity in the Necessary Existent: neither of parts, because a composite is contingent, nor of individuals, for several reasons.

<a id="m-v-p515"></a>

**Source p. 515**

First: suppose two necessary existents. Necessity is identical with quiddity; otherwise it would be contingent and caused either by the quiddity—preceding itself, since a cause necessarily precedes through necessity—or by something else, in which case it would not be intrinsic. The two would therefore be distinguished by an individuation, which is positive, making the Necessary Existent composite.

Second: if the Necessary were multiple, the individuation distinguishing the individuals would either be the necessary quiddity itself, or be caused by it or a concomitant of it, in which case there would be no multiplicity; or be caused by something separate, in which case there would be no intrinsic necessity.

Third: if multiple, necessity and individuation either could separate or could not. If they could, necessity without individuation would follow, which is impossible; or individuation without necessity, which is contingency. If they could not, necessity would arise through individuation, producing circularity; or individuation through necessity, or both through the essence, excluding multiplicity; or through something separate, excluding intrinsic necessity.

Fourth: if two gods existed, an object of power intended by both would occur either through them jointly, eliminating independence; through each, making one object of power belong to two independently powerful agents; or through one, producing predominance without a determinant. For their relation to objects of power is equal, since power belongs intrinsically to each and susceptibility to power belongs through contingency.

Fifth: if one wills something, either the other cannot will its contrary, in which case he is incapable, with no impediment except the first one's power's attachment; or he can, in which case supposing this contrary will to occur entails either both contraries, which is impossible; neither, showing both incapable and also impossible in cases such as a body's motion and rest; or only one, producing predominance without a determinant and incapacity in the one whose will is not realised.

<a id="m-v-p517"></a>

**Source p. 517**

Sixth: if they agree on every object of power, independent agencies converge on one effect; otherwise mutual obstruction occurs.

Seventh: if what distinguishes them is a concomitant of divinity, the account is false; otherwise it can disappear, eliminating duality.

Eighth: there is no proof of a second, so it must be denied; otherwise absurd ignorances follow.

Ninth: if there were multiplicity, it would be unlimited, since no number has priority.

Tenth: the revelational proofs, comprising consensus and decisive texts. Some of the preceding arguments are plainly weak.

Conclusion: divine unity is not impaired by affirming beginningless attributes, or by an animal's bringing its acts into existence, although the expression “creation” is objectionable. More objectionable is assigning evils and ugly acts to the devil. As for affirming beginningless intellects that produce souls and bodies, and beginningless spheres governing the elemental world, this is a grave matter. Those unanimously recognised as associating partners with God are the dualists, who posit two principles, light and darkness; the Magians, who assign evils, even impure bodies, to Ahriman, although they make him generated from Yazdān; idol-worshippers, through interpretations they imagine; and those affirming a child for God. “Glory be to God above what they associate” [Qurʾān 52:43].

### Second investigation

God is neither body, substance, nor accident, and is not in place or direction. The philosophers argue that body requires its constituent, accident its substrate, and substance has existence additional to its quiddity; place and direction are peculiar to body. The theologians argue

<a id="m-v-p519"></a>

**Source p. 519**

that “substance” linguistically signifies what is the origin of a thing, and “accident” what cannot persist, even if their technical applications track what subsists through itself and what subsists through another. Body is originated, as previously explained, necessarily occupies space, and possesses particular contraries and shapes through a determiner, so it is dependent. If the Necessary Existent occupied space, something originated—spatial locus—would be beginningless; the Necessary would be contingent and place necessary, since the occupant requires its locus, not conversely. He would either be in every locus, mingling with what is unfitting and entailing interpenetration; or in some through a determiner, making Him dependent; or in some without a determiner, producing predominance without one.

Among opponents, some use “body” to mean existent and “substance” to mean self-subsisting. The correct position is to prohibit these expressions on grounds of revealed law and caution. Others are corporealists, who say He is a body in the form of a beardless youth, a grey-haired old man, or a glittering white ingot; and assimilationists, who say He is in the upper direction above the Throne, touching it or facing it at a finite or infinite distance. They invoke the claims that every existent is bodily or corporeal, spatially occupying or inhering in an occupant, connected to or separated from the world, and inside or outside it; and the apparent senses of texts suggesting corporeality and direction. The answer is evident.

Notice: He is therefore not qualified by any quantities or qualities such as length, breadth, shape, colour, taste, smell, joy, distress, anger, pleasure, or pain. The philosophers' affirmation of intellectual pleasure because He apprehends His perfections and delights in them holds only if apprehending what is agreeable in the unseen case is established as pleasure or as necessarily accompanied by pleasure, as in the observed case.

<a id="m-v-p521"></a>

**Source p. 521**

### Third investigation

He does not unite with another, for the reasons given, and because one thing cannot be necessary and contingent. Nor does He inhere in another, since what inheres in a thing requires it; and because, if He required the substrate, His contingency would follow, whereas otherwise His inherence would be impossible.

It is also argued that inherence is either a perfection, entailing His perfection through another, or not, in which case it must be denied; that the inherence agreed upon by rational people is dependence in spatial occupation; and that, if He could inhere in bodies, His noninherence in the most contemptible bodies would not be certain.

Inherence or union is reported from Christians regarding Jesus, peace be upon him; from some extremists regarding their imams; and from some Sufis regarding their perfected adepts. As for some people's claim that multiplicity disappears upon annihilation in divine unity, or that there is no multiplicity in existence at all, this is another inquiry.

### Fourth investigation

He cannot be qualified by something originated, meaning an existent after nonexistence, contrary to the Karrāmiyya. Qualification by something having newly arising attachments, or by renewed negations, relations, and states, is not disputed here and cannot serve their argument. Their inference that what permits qualification is attribute as such—beginninglessness being irrelevant because privative—is invalid. What permits it may be the reality of the beginningless attribute; beginninglessness may be a condition; or origination an impediment.

<a id="m-v-p523"></a>

**Source p. 523**

We have several arguments. First: consensus that whatever may belong to Him, if a perfection, is never absent from Him; otherwise He is not qualified by it. Second: qualification by something originated is change, which is impossible for Him. Third: if such qualification were possible, it would be possible beginninglessly, since alteration of intrinsic status is impossible. This entails the possibility of an originated thing's existing beginninglessly, since qualification by a thing without that thing is impossible. Fourth: such qualification would entail His never being devoid of something originated, because before that originated thing He would possess its contrary, which is originated because it ceases, and His receptivity to it, which is originated for the reasons given.

The first is judged weak because the originated things might be successive perfections, the beginning of each conditioned by the ending of another; this objection is open to examination. The second is answered by saying that change in the sense of replacement of attributes without being affected by another is the very point disputed. The third: what follows is the beginninglessness of possibility, whereas what is impossible is the possibility of beginninglessness. The fourth: the premises of the implication are denied.

## Chapter Three: Positive attributes

It comprises several investigations.

### First investigation

His attributes are additional to the essence: He is knowing and has knowledge, powerful and has power, living and has life, and so forth, contrary to the philosophers and the Muʿtazila. We have several arguments.

<a id="m-v-p525"></a>

**Source p. 525**

First: a knower is defined as one in whom knowledge subsists, and the cause of being knowing is knowledge. This does not differ between the observed and unseen cases, unlike matters that do not account for a knower's being knowing, such as accidentality, temporal origination, and the like.

Second: nothing is understood by “knower” except one who has knowledge, or by “known” except what knowledge relates to. Necessarily, therefore, if He is knowing and has something known, He has knowledge.

If it is said, “His knowledge is His essence,” we reply: predicating it of the essence would then convey nothing; the attributes would not be distinguished or require proof; and knowledge, for example, would be necessary, worshipped, the world's Maker, and qualified by perfections.

If it is said, “Difference in concept suffices, as with other predicates,” we reply: the discussion concerns knowledge, power, and life, not such predicates as knowing, powerful, and living.

If it is said, “His essence, insofar as it relates to objects known, is knowing, indeed knowledge, and insofar as it relates to objects of power, is powerful, indeed power—like one being half of two, a third of three, and so on, although only one existent is present,” we reply: it is certainly known that the essence is not knowledge and power, but knowing and powerful. The discussion remains over the meaning from which the derivative predicate is formed. Calling it a relation does not help, since it certainly belongs to real attributes, not intellectual considerations.

Third: God's statements, “He sent it down with His knowledge” [4:166], “Know that it was sent down only with God's knowledge” [11:14], “Possessor of power, the Firm” [51:58], and “Power belongs to God” [2:165].

<a id="m-v-p527"></a>

**Source p. 527**

The opponent invokes several arguments.

First: everything depends on Him, especially His attributes, entailing that He is both recipient and agent. The reply denies that this is false.

Second: they are attributes of perfection, so He would be perfected through another. The reply is that they are not other than Him. Even if this is granted, the impossibility of being perfected in the sense of possessing an attribute of perfection is precisely the disputed point.

Third: His being knowing, for example, is necessary, and what is necessary is not caused. The reply, even granting that being knowing differs from knowledge, is that we do not grant the impossibility of what is necessary—in the sense that the essence cannot lack it—being caused by an attribute arising from the essence.

Fourth: affirming multiple beginningless things is unbelief by consensus. The reply is that there is no otherness here and therefore no such multiplicity. Even if there is, not everything beginningless in time is “beginningless” in the relevant sense, but only what subsists through itself. Even if this is granted, what is unbelief by consensus is multiplicity of the beginningless in the sense of not being preceded by another. Even if that is granted, it concerns essences alone, as with what follows for Christians.

As for arguing that possession of attributes entails composition in the divine reality; that beginninglessness is the deity's most distinctive attribute and reveals His reality, so attributes sharing it would be gods; that there is no proof of attributes and they must therefore be denied; or that subsistence means only dependence in spatial occupation, entailing the Creator's spatial occupation—these are extremely weak.

<a id="m-v-p529"></a>

**Source p. 529**

A strong argument against the opponent's own principles is that the persistence of attributes entails one meaning's subsisting in another. Replies that they are not qualified by persistence, persist through the essence's persistence, or have a persistence identical with themselves are weak.

To deny power, they argue that, if He had power, it would not relate to creating bodies. Observed power does not do so, supposedly for a shared reason: being power. Moreover, His power would either resemble observed powers or differ from them only to the degree that observed powers differ among themselves. We reply: perhaps the reason is more specific and the difference greater.

To deny knowledge, they argue that, if He knew through knowledge as in the observed case, the two knowledges would be alike, relating to the known object in one respect, and would therefore share beginninglessness or temporal origination. Being knowing is different: in Him it occurs through the essence's relation, and in us through knowledge's relation. Furthermore, His knowledges would be infinite because He knows infinitely many things, and above Him would be a knower, since God says, “Above every possessor of knowledge is a knower” [12:76].

We reply: sharing a concomitant does not entail essential likeness, nor does likeness entail equality in attributes. The attachments of one thing may be many, even infinite; and universal expressions may be restricted.

### Second investigation: He is powerful

This means that He is enabled to act and refrain, and that either can proceed from Him according to motives. The foundation is that a beginningless Maker together with an originated product is conceivable only in a freely powerful agent, since an effect cannot lag behind a necessitating cause.

<a id="m-v-p531"></a>

**Source p. 531**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.15](#c1-15).*

If the origination of everything, or its proceeding from Him without an intermediary, is established, the matter is evident. Otherwise one must exclude a beginningless succession of events conditioning originated things' proceeding from a beginningless necessitating cause, as already discussed. One must also exclude the Necessary Existent's necessitating a beginningless freely choosing being upon whom events depend; this exclusion is agreed upon.

Let us enumerate several proofs.

First: once originated things are established as ultimately depending on the Necessary, He must be powerful. Otherwise He either necessitates an originated thing directly, entailing its lagging behind its cause, or does not, entailing an infinite regress.

Second: if His producing the world's existence occurs through necessitation, it occurs either without an intermediary or through a beginningless intermediary, entailing the world's beginninglessness; or through an originated intermediary, producing a regress of originated things.

Third: bodies' differing accidents do not arise from corporeality or its concomitants, since these are shared; nor from accidents, essential constituents, or bodies possessing a particular determination, since infinite regress is impossible. The freely choosing agent must therefore be affirmed, since a necessitating cause's relation to all is equal.

Fourth: if the world's giver of existence were necessitating, the world's removal would entail His removal, since the removal of what entails something is a necessary consequence of removing what it entails. But removing the Necessary is impossible.

Fifth: if the stars' and poles' assignment to their positions and the spheres' assignment to their places did not arise through the powerful agent's will, predominance without a determinant would follow, since a necessitating cause's relation to all is equal.

Sixth: if the agent producing animals' organs and shapes were a nature or necessitating principle, they would be separate or joined spheres. The freely choosing powerful agent must therefore be affirmed.

<a id="m-v-p533"></a>

**Source p. 533**

Revelational proofs, such as consensus and others, are also invoked; as are the claims that power and the other attributes are perfections while their opposites are marks of deficiency, and that the world's Maker, given its exact ordering, can necessarily only be knowing and powerful. Despite the room for objection in these arguments, their combination may yield certainty.

The opponent invokes several arguments.

First: if power's attachment requires a determinant, an infinite regress follows; if not, the route to establishing the Maker is closed. The reply denies both implications. The determinant may be will's attachment through itself. Moreover, a powerful agent's preferring one object of power without a determinant, meaning assigning it without a motive, differs from a contingent's prevailing without a determinant, meaning its being realised without an effecting cause.

Second: power's and will's attachment is either beginningless, entailing the world's beginninglessness, or originated, generating a regress of events. The reply denies this: they may attach beginninglessly to bringing the world into existence subsequently, or their attachment may arise through themselves.

Third: if the agent possesses everything indispensable, its effect is necessary, since lagging behind is impossible. It is therefore necessitating, since this is what that means. Otherwise the effect is necessarily impossible. The reply, even granting that a powerful agent's effect cannot lag behind, is that necessity arising from a freely powerful agent does not conflict with choice but establishes it. Necessity from a necessitating agent differs: it cannot truly be said of that agent, “If it wished, it would refrain.”

<a id="m-v-p535"></a>

**Source p. 535**

Fourth: power's relation to existence is like its relation to nonexistence. Nonexistence cannot be an object of power, being beginningless and sheer negation; therefore existence cannot either. The reply is that power over nonexistence means “if He wills, He does not act,” and “if He does not will, He does not act,” not “if He wills, He produces nonexistence.”

Fifth: if acting is preferable for a freely choosing agent to refraining, perfection through another follows; otherwise purposelessness follows. The reply is that excluding purposelessness requires only that the act be preferable in itself or relative to another.

Sixth: if the freely choosing agent's effect is impossible in beginninglessness, alteration of intrinsic status follows; if possible, a beginningless effect's dependence on a freely choosing agent is possible. The reply is that, in beginninglessness, it is intrinsically possible but impossible insofar as it is the effect of a freely choosing agent.

Seventh: He knows beginninglessly either that the effect exists, making it necessary, or that it does not, making it impossible. It therefore cannot be an object of power. The reply is that He knows its existence through His power.

Conclusion: His power is unlimited in the sense that its possible attachment never reaches an endpoint, and universal in the sense that its attachment is not confined to some things. For the essence requires being powerful, contingency makes something susceptible to power, and before existence there is no distinction restricting power to some things. Better is to invoke such texts as “God is powerful over everything” [2:284].

<a id="m-v-p537"></a>

**Source p. 537**

The Magians disagree concerning evils, including harmful bodies; al-Naẓẓām concerning the creation of ignorance, lying, and other ugly acts; and ʿAbbād concerning what God knows will not occur, since its occurrence from Him is impossible. Al-Balkhī disagrees concerning an act like a servant's object of power, since it would be purposeless, foolish, or self-abasing. The reply, even granting this enumeration, is that these are incidental features that do not prevent essential likeness.

Al-Jubbāʾī disagrees concerning the very same object of the servant's power, since an object of power belonging to two powerful agents entails the possibility of one creation belonging to two creators. We deny the implication, because the servant's power does not produce the effect. Abū al-Ḥusayn denies that the consequent is false, as with the motion of a substance attached simultaneously to the hands of someone pulling and someone pushing.

The universality of His power, in the sense that everything is brought into existence by Him immediately or through an intermediary, is not disputed by those affirming a Maker; only its details are disputed. In the sense that there is absolutely no effecting agent besides Him, only some theologians affirm it, invoking the apparent senses of the revealed texts—which is the truth—and the proofs from convergent independent agency and mutual obstruction, both of which are weak.

The philosophers disagree concerning spheres, elements, and the events within them—indeed everything except the first intellect—as already explained. The Ṣābians and astrologers disagree concerning this world's events, attributing them to celestial spheres and stars through their configurations and motions; likewise the naturalists attribute them to temperaments and natures. Their strongest ground is concomitant variation.

<a id="m-v-p539"></a>

**Source p. 539**

The Muʿtazila disagree concerning evils, ugly acts, and animals' voluntary actions, as will be discussed.

### Third investigation: He is knowing

For us, this follows because He makes the world in its ordered precision, and because He is freely powerful, as explained. If what is observed from certain animals were truly their own action, it would indicate their knowledge. Invoking revelation here, however, is circular.

For the philosophers, He knows because He is immaterial and every immaterial being knows; and because He knows Himself, is the principle of everything, and knowledge of a principle entails knowledge of what derives from it.

It is said that He does not know Himself, since knowledge is a relation or an attribute possessing a relation, requiring duality; nor another, since this would produce multiplicity in the essence and make the one thing both recipient and agent. The answer is that difference of consideration suffices, as in our knowledge of ourselves; multiplicity of relations and the combination of receptivity with agency are not impossible.

Conclusion: His knowledge is unlimited, encompassing infinitely many things, such as numbers and shapes, and every existent and nonexistent, universal and particular.
