This follows from the general expressions of revelation, and because the essence requires His being knowing and susceptibility to being known requires its possibility, without a restricting determinant: He is exalted above dependence in His perfection.

Some disagree concerning knowledge of knowledge, since it would entail infinite attributes; others concerning knowledge of infinitely many things, because their existence would be impossible, besides the preceding difficulty.

<a id="m-v-p541"></a>

**Source p. 541**

Others disagree concerning knowledge of the nonexistent, since it is sheer negation without distinction, whereas what is known is distinguished. The weakness of all these arguments is evident.

The philosophers disagree concerning knowledge of particulars as particulars, since this would entail change in the Beginningless. For example, if He knows that Zayd will enter and Zayd then enters, that knowledge either becomes ignorance or disappears into another knowledge.

The reply is that some particulars do not change, such as God's essence; and that a change of relation does not require change in the related thing, as the Beginningless exists before an originated thing, then with it, then after it. For one who makes knowledge a relation, no change of essence follows. For one who makes it an attribute possessing a relation, no change of the attribute follows, much less of the essence.

This is indicated by the statement that the Creator's knowledge that a thing will exist is the same as His knowledge that it has existed. Someone who continues until tomorrow knowing that Zayd will enter the house tomorrow knows tomorrow, through that very knowledge, that he has entered. Knowledge does not change when its object changes, just as it does not multiply when its objects multiply. It is like a mirror through which forms are disclosed, or a person whose companion moves from his right to his left.

Since this plainly fails on the view that knowledge is a relation between knower and known, Abū al-Ḥusayn refuted those Muʿtazilites who asserted it. First: someone who continues knowing that Zayd will enter the town tomorrow, but sits in a dark house without knowing that tomorrow has arrived, does not thereby come to know that Zayd has entered. Second: the two cognitions have different objects and incompatible conditions. Knowing that he has existed is conditioned by his existence, and knowing that he will exist by his nonexistence; otherwise it would be ignorance.

<a id="m-v-p543"></a>

**Source p. 543**

Third: the two cognitions may be separate, as when someone knows Zayd will arrive but, when he arrives, does not know that he has arrived, or conversely. Abū al-Ḥusayn consequently accepted change in God's knowledge of changing particulars, as Hishām held that He beginninglessly knows realities and quiddities but knows individuals and states only after they arise.

### Fourth investigation: He wills

They agree on this, and His being an agent by choice indicates it. For us, He wills through a beginningless attribute subsisting in His essence, analogously to the other attributes. For the determination of one of an object's two alternatives to occur certainly arises through a particular attribute which we find in ourselves and which is neither knowledge, power, nor the like.

Will's attachment occurs through itself, so no regress of wills follows. The intended object's necessity through will does not conflict with choice. Will's beginninglessness neither requires the object's beginninglessness nor conflicts with its attachment's origination. Will's being originated while subsisting in His essence, as the Karrāmiyya hold, entails regress and His being a substrate of originated things. Its being originated while subsisting through itself, as the followers of al-Jubbāʾī hold, is necessarily false. The philosophers' claim that will is knowledge of the most perfect order denies what we call will. So do al-Najjār's claim that it is His being neither compelled nor inattentive, and al-Kaʿbī's claim that, concerning His own act, it is knowledge, and concerning another's act, command.

Many Muʿtazilites hold that will is the motive: some in the unseen case alone, others in both unseen and observed cases. A motive in the observed case means knowledge, belief, or supposition of a preponderant benefit in the act; in the unseen case, knowledge of it. They argue that will is certainly the willing agent's act, and an agent must be aware of his act, whereas we are aware only of an unopposed motive or one outweighing a deterrent.

<a id="m-v-p545"></a>

**Source p. 545**

The reply is that we grant neither that will is voluntary nor that there is no awareness beyond the motive. Awareness of a state following it is immediately evident. A counterargument is that a thirsty person or fugitive inclines toward one of two waters or roads when they are equal.

Conclusion: His will encompasses all actual things, and conversely [everything He wills occurs], contrary to the Muʿtazila on both principles. This will be discussed under acts.

### Fifth investigation: He is living, hearing, and seeing

The divine books attest this, and the prophets—indeed the majority of rational people—agree. Knowledge and power indicate life; life indicates the possibility of hearing and sight, which are therefore affirmed in actuality. Clearly the absence of these attributes from one capable of possessing them is a deficiency, or at least a shortfall in perfection. It is false that the Necessary should be marked by deficiency or be less perfect than a human being. Taken together, these considerations yield certainty, although some individually leave room for dispute.

On our companions' principle, beginningless attributes are affirmed: life, hearing, and sight. This does not require beginningless objects heard and seen, since attachment may arise. The claim that these attributes are identical with balanced temperament and the sense's being affected, or conditioned by these, is denied even in the observed case; how much more in the unseen?

<a id="m-v-p547"></a>

**Source p. 547**

On the report from the Shaykh that sensation is knowledge of the sensible object, although another kind of knowledge, a further attribute need not be affirmed, since the differing kinds may be the attachments themselves.

For the philosophers and some Muʿtazilites, His life is His knowing and being powerful, and His hearing and seeing are His knowledge of audible and visible things.

Conclusion: the doctrine is that He apprehends odours, tastes, and such things as heat and cold. Revealed law, however, has not used these expressions, and reason does not permit His being described as smelling, tasting, or touching, since these are attributes of bodies. Moreover, the expressions do not convey apprehension's reality, since one can correctly say, “I smelled it but did not apprehend its odour.”

### Sixth investigation: He speaks

This has been transmitted recurrently from the prophets, blessings and peace be upon them, whose truthfulness is established by miracles without depending on speech. It is also argued that the contrary of speech in a living being is deficiency or a shortfall in perfection, as previously explained.

For us, His speech is a beginningless attribute incompatible with silence and impairment, indicated through verbal expression and writing, and not of the genus of sounds and letters. All the other groups oppose us, holding that intelligible speech is sensory, not inward. Only the Ḥanbalīs and Ḥashwiyya affirm the beginninglessness of sensory speech, through ignorance or obstinacy, since the succession of its parts and the impossibility of its persistence are evident. The Karrāmiyya maintain that it is originated yet subsists in God's essence; they call it His utterance and make His speech the power to produce utterance. For the Muʿtazila it is originated in some body, and God's speaking it means His creating it there.

<a id="m-v-p549"></a>

**Source p. 549**

Our argument is that “speaker” means one in whom speech subsists. What is composed of letters is originated and cannot subsist in God's essence. The inward meaning must therefore be intended, since there is no third alternative.

If it is said, “Someone is called a speaker although speech does not persist so as to subsist in him; even if it does, it subsists in his tongue rather than in him, or indeed in another's tongue. Moreover, a composition may have simultaneously present parts, as in the memoriser's mind or a seal's impression, so its beginninglessness and subsistence in the essence are not impossible,” we reply: subsistence does not require persistence or possession of all the parts simultaneously. Speaking through another's tongue is a figurative expression for conveying speech to him. The ordered composition's having successive parts and being unable to persist is necessarily evident; it differs from an imprinted or written image.

Further, one who commands, prohibits, or reports finds within himself a meaning other than knowledge and will, indicated through expression, writing, or the like. Calling this speech is widespread among users of the language.

They invoke several arguments. First: it is necessarily known in religion, even to children, that “Qurʾān” names this composed text. Second: the well-known properties of the Qurʾān apply only to originated expression, not beginningless meaning: its being an Arabic reminder sent down to the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace; recited by tongues, heard by ears, written in codices, accompanied by challenge, divided into chapters and verses, admitting abrogation, and occurring after the will to bring something into being.

<a id="m-v-p551"></a>

**Source p. 551**

We reply: “His speech” is applied equivocally or by established metaphor to the particular verbal composition, not merely because it indicates His beginningless speech, but because He produces it through its written marks in the Tablet or its letters in the angel. Its Arabic form is specifically named Qurʾān. This is the usage familiar to the general public and in legal theory, and to it the listed properties return.

The correct view is that what matters is the particular composition, not a particular substrate. What we recite is therefore the Qurʾān itself, not its likeness. Categorically denying that it inheres in tongue or codex serves reverence and removes misconception. Moreover, applying an indicator's attribute to what it indicates is common, as in “I heard, read, or wrote this meaning.” Moses's distinction in being addressed is that he heard without sound or letter, just as God will be seen in the hereafter without quantity or modality; or that he heard through a sound from every direction, or from one direction without acquisition on his part.

Third: reporting in the past tense beginninglessly would be falsehood, which is impossible for God by consensus, the prophets' reports, and its being a deficiency according to rational people. It would also make truthfulness in that report impossible, since the beginningless cannot cease, which is certainly false. We reply: His report becomes past, future, or present only in subsequent existence, since there is no time in beginninglessness.

Fourth: command, prohibition, and report without an addressee or hearer are foolish and purposeless. The answer is that His speech becomes one of these divisions only in subsequent existence. Even if this is granted, an intellectually conceived addressee suffices for inward speech. To this returns the majority's statement that the nonexistent is commanded on the supposition of existence: the beginningless command is a demand addressed to someone who will exist,

<a id="m-v-p553"></a>

**Source p. 553**

like requesting learning from a son yet to be born, or the Prophet's commands to those who will exist. Furthermore, foolishness consists in the absence of wisdom from what is related to wisdom, whereas the beginningless is not such. Even if it is, the existence of wisdom at a later time suffices.

Fifth: if speech were beginningless, it would be everlasting, so obligation would remain in the abode of recompense. Sixth: His addressing Moses would occur forever, not only on the Mount. Seventh: speech's relation to its objects would be equal, so what is commanded would be prohibited and conversely. We reply: attachment arises through choice.

Conclusion: the doctrine is that His beginningless speech is one, becoming multiple according to attachment. This does not mean that it becomes multiple only in subsequent existence, as Ibn Saʿīd maintains; nor that it is report and all other kinds return to report, as the Imām al-Rāzī maintains. Rather, speech has been established only through revelation, which has not affirmed multiplicity; and expressing command, prohibition, report, and other kinds through one speech is not impossible, as with knowledge and power.

### Seventh investigation: Disputed attributes

Attributes are not confined to those mentioned. Arguing that another attribute has no proof and must therefore be denied, or that it would be known if it existed because complete knowledge is obligatory, is weak.

One is persistence. The Shaykh and his followers affirm it, because something persisting without persistence resembles a knower without knowledge. Persistence is not existence itself, since something may exist without persisting. Many disagree, for several reasons.

<a id="m-v-p555"></a>

**Source p. 555**

First: what is understood by persistence is the continuation of existence, meaning existence insofar as it relates to the second time.

Second: what persists through a persistence not identical with its essence is not intrinsically necessary, especially if persistence is explained as an attribute through which existence occurs at the second time. This is not an attribute's dependence on another attribute, like will's dependence on knowledge, but existence's dependence.

Third: either persistence requires the essence, producing circularity; the reverse, making persistence rather than the essence necessary; or neither requires the other and their simultaneous realisation merely coincides, multiplying necessary existents. Moreover, an attribute's independence of the essence is unintelligible.

Fourth: either persistence has a persistence, generating regress and one meaning's subsistence in another; or it does not, resembling a knower without knowledge. If it is said, “Persistence's persistence is itself,” we reply: let the attributes stand likewise to the essence.

It may be answered that this is impossible for the reasons given, unlike persistence's persistence being itself. Yet the difficulty remains of one meaning's subsisting in another in the persistence of attributes. It is not removed by saying, “We do not call attributes persistent; rather, the essence persists through its attributes,” or “Their persistence is themselves,” or “Their persistence is the essence's persistence because there is no otherness.” The first is necessarily false; the second allows the essence's persistence likewise to be itself, so no additional beginningless attribute is established; the third is excluded because a thing's attribute cannot subsist in what is not identical with it, even if not other than it.

Another is bringing-into-being [takwīn]. Some jurists affirm it, invoking the agreed claim that He is Creator. An attribute must therefore subsist in Him, called creating, providing sustenance, giving life, causing death, and the like according to differing objects of attachment, and beginningless like the other attributes.

<a id="m-v-p557"></a>

**Source p. 557**

The reply is that this applies to real attributes. Bringing into existence is only a meaning conceived from the effecting cause's relation to its effect, and this occurs in subsequent existence.

They say: He praises Himself in His beginningless speech as Creator, Originator, and Fashioner. If these belonged to Him only subsequently, He would praise Himself for what He did not possess, and perfection would follow deficiency. We reply: this resembles His praise in “Whatever is in the heavens and earth glorifies Him” [59:24], and “He is God in heaven and God on earth” [43:84]. Its reality is that He is beginninglessly such that these belong to Him subsequently.

They say: you acknowledge that He brings things into being at their times through the beginningless word “Be.” This is what bringing-into-being means. We reply: it then returns to the attribute of speech.

They say: it is a perfection, so its absence is deficiency. We reply: yes, where it is possible; its beginningless possibility is denied.

These arguments are countered by saying that bringing-into-being means only origination and bringing something from nonexistence into existence, as they themselves explain it. It belongs to intellectual relations, not real attributes, as previously discussed. Moreover, if it were beginningless, what is brought into being would necessarily be beginningless, since the two cannot separate.

If it is said, “Rather, it is an attribute through which things come into being at their times and emerge from nonexistence into existence. It is not power: power entails possibility, bringing-into-being entails existence. Moreover, because it persists and the effect follows after a time, separation does not result; it is not like striking without something struck,”

<a id="m-v-p559"></a>

**Source p. 559**

we reply: why say it differs from power conjoined with will? Is power anything but an attribute acting in accordance with will? Hence the Imām al-Rāzī says that this attribute either acts contingently, in which case it is indistinguishable from power, or necessarily, in which case the Necessary Existent is not freely choosing.

As for the report from the Shaykh that bringing-into-being is the thing brought into being, it is said to mean that what is understood by the expression “creation” is the creature; or that, when one thing affects another, what is externally realised is the effect alone.

Other disputed attributes are beginninglessness, mercy, approval, and generosity, according to Ibn Saʿīd. The majority hold that He is beginningless through Himself and that the others return to will.

Others are expressions occurring in revelation's apparent wording whose literal meanings cannot be applied, such as settling upon, hand, face, eye, and the like. The truth is that these are metaphors and figurative representations.

## Chapter Four: His states

It comprises two investigations.

### First investigation: Seeing Him

The people of truth hold that God can be seen, although free of direction and confrontation, and that believers will see Him in Paradise, contrary to the other groups. They do not dispute the possibility of complete intellectual disclosure, nor do we dispute the impossibility of an imprinted form, a ray's contact, or a state requiring either. Rather, when we look at the full moon, we have an apprehensive state called vision, different from our state when we close our eyes, even if the latter contains a clear disclosure. The dispute is whether servants can have that state in relation to God without confrontation.

<a id="m-v-p561"></a>

**Source p. 561**

We have two arguments for its possibility.

The first is God's report of Moses's words, “My Lord, show Yourself to me, that I may look at You” [7:143]. Moses requested vision and was neither frivolous nor ignorant. God made vision conditional upon the mountain's remaining firm, which is intrinsically possible.

Against the first point it is objected that Moses requested only necessary knowledge or sight of a sign; even if vision, he requested it for his people or for increased assurance through the mutual support of reason and revelation; even if this is denied, ignorance of the question of vision does not impair knowledge of God. The reply is that “You shall not see Me” denies vision, not knowledge or seeing a sign. Knowledge was already present and signs abundant; the sign occurring then depended on the mountain's collapse, not its stability. Moreover, seeing conjoined with looking followed by “at” explicitly conveys vision.

The people either believed Moses, in which case his report that vision was impossible would suffice, or did not, in which case his report from God would not help. It does not befit a prophet to delay refuting falsehood, as with the request to make a god; to seek evidence in this manner; or to be ignorant in divine matters of what individual Muʿtazilites know.

Against the second point it is objected that the condition is the mountain's stability after the looking, when it was collapsing and stability was impossible. The reply is that stability is necessarily possible, although it did not occur so as to entail vision. Only the coexistence of stability and collapse is impossible.

<a id="m-v-p563"></a>

**Source p. 563**

The second argument is that we necessarily and by agreement see substances and accidents. Their susceptibility to vision must have a shared cause, either existence or origination. Origination is privative and cannot be such a cause, so existence is designated. Existence is shared by them and the Necessary Existent, so He too must be susceptible to vision. By the cause of susceptibility to vision is meant what can serve as vision's object, as the Imām al-Ḥaramayn explicitly states.

On this account several objections are removed. First: susceptibility to vision is itself privative, so let its cause be likewise. Second: contingency is also shared, so let it be the cause. Contingency too is privative and shared by existent and nonexistent, although the nonexistent cannot be seen.

Third: even if the two susceptibilities are alike, one species of effect may have different causes. The answer is that vision can relate to a thing without apprehending its substantiality or accidentality, much less its further particularity. We see Zayd through one vision relating to his identity, and may then analyse him into substances and accidents, or remain unaware of this even after reflection.

Fourth: despite a shared cause, the judgement may fail because the original case alone possesses a condition or the new case has an impediment. The answer is that susceptibility to vision is necessary once something fit to be its object is realised. Denying that existence is shared is answered by what preceded.

<a id="m-v-p565"></a>

**Source p. 565**

The consequence that every existent can be seen—even sounds, tastes, smells, beliefs, and so forth—is accepted. Denying it is an appeal to improbability. Their not actually being seen is established like other customary facts.

For vision's actual occurrence we invoke the community's consensus before the opponent arose, and revelation. From the Book: “Faces that day will be radiant, looking toward their Lord” [75:22–23]. Looking followed by “toward” either means vision, necessarily accompanies it, or is a metaphor specifically assigned to it, as transmission, usage, and customary language attest.

The objection is that looking may mean waiting, as in the poet's words:

> Faces waiting on the day of Badr,
> toward the All-Merciful, who brings success.

Moreover, *ilā* may be a noun meaning bounty. Looking may have attributes vision lacks, such as intensity and turning aside; and occur without vision, as in “I looked for the crescent but did not see it.” Supplying “toward their Lord's reward” is also an evident, transmitted possibility.

The answer is that waiting neither fits the verse's context nor befits the abode of reward. *Ilā* is evidently a preposition here, and the early authorities did not depart from this. Making “looking toward” mean waiting is strained, as is abandoning the literal or established figurative meaning for an ellipsis without a determining indication.

Other verses are God's reproach and belittlement of unbelievers, “No indeed: that day they will be veiled from their Lord” [83:15], and “For those who do good is the finest reward and an increase” [10:26], the increase meaning vision, as the prophetic report indicates and the early authorities attest.

<a id="m-v-p567"></a>

**Source p. 567**

From the Sunna: “You will see your Lord as you see this moon on the night of the full moon, without being crowded in seeing Him”; “He lifts the veil, and they look upon the face of God, mighty and majestic”; and “The most honoured of them before God is the one who looks upon His face.”

The opponent invokes several arguments. First: if He were visible, He would necessarily confront the viewer, and therefore be in a direction, a substance or accident. Second: vision occurs through a ray or imprint, both plainly impossible. We reply: even if the implication is granted, it concerns the observed case alone.

Third: if He could be seen, vision would be continuous in Paradise, indeed in this life and the next, since the conceivable conditions—soundness of sense and His susceptibility to vision—are fulfilled. Otherwise towering mountains and vast seas might be before us unseen, because God had not created vision or because some special condition was absent. We reply: denying their presence is not based on that argument, but is necessary knowledge, like other customary facts. Even if vision necessarily occurs in the observed case, it may not in the unseen case because of a difference in quiddity or a requirement for additional strength that may be absent.

<a id="m-v-p569"></a>

**Source p. 569**

Fourth: “Eyes do not apprehend Him” [6:103]. Visual apprehension is vision or its necessary concomitant, and it is universally denied. What suits the context and is usual in such expressions is universal negation, applying the negation to all, rather than merely negating universality by denying attribution to all. Moreover, the context praises Him for this, so its contradictory would be deficiency and impossible.

We reply: even granting universality across persons and times, visual apprehension is vision encompassing the visible object's sides, or the imprinting of its likeness in the eye. The word conveys attaining and reaching, as in “I apprehended so-and-so,” meaning I caught up with him. Its denial therefore entails neither denial of vision nor vision's being an imperfection and thus impossible. It may instead entail vision's possibility, so that denying visual apprehension constitutes praise, as with one inaccessible behind the veil of majesty, unlike the nonexistent or sounds, smells, and tastes.

Fifth: “You shall not see Me” [7:143]. *Lan* signifies perpetual denial or emphatic future denial; and if Moses does not see Him, no one else does, by agreement. We reply: perpetuity is not established from reliable linguistic authorities, and emphasis does not entail all times.

Sixth: “It is not for a human being that God should speak to him except by revelation, or from behind a veil, or by sending a messenger who reveals, by His permission, what He wills” [42:51]. The verse is said to deny face-to-face speech and to have been revealed when people said to the Prophet, “Will you not speak to God and look at Him, like Moses?” It therefore affirms speech and denies vision.

<a id="m-v-p571"></a>

**Source p. 571**

We reply: this is denied. Rather, the verse explains kinds of divine address. If revelation itself denied vision, “from behind a veil” would be superfluous, since it means only the absence of vision.

Seventh: God never mentions the request for vision without treating it as grave and reprehensible, even calling it wrongdoing and insolence: “Those who do not expect to meet Us said…” [25:21]; “When you said, O Moses, we will not believe you…” [2:55]; and “The People of the Book ask you to bring down for them a book from heaven…” [4:153]. We reply: this was because of their obstinacy and defiance. Hence requests for angels or a book to descend were also treated as grave, although these are possible.

Conclusion: the proof from existence entails that the attributes can be seen, like other existents. Yet this has not occurred in the customary course, and no proof establishes its occurrence. The same applies to the other kinds of sensory apprehension, especially on al-Ashʿarī's view. The discussion does not concern smelling, tasting, or touching themselves, which are certainly impossible, but the apprehension arising upon them.

### Second investigation: Knowledge of His reality

Many investigators hold that human beings do not attain it, since what is known of Him is existence, attributes, negations, and relations; and since His essence excludes sharing,

<a id="m-v-p573"></a>

**Source p. 573**

whereas what is known of Him does not exclude sharing, as shown by our needing to demonstrate divine unity. What is known nevertheless suffices for correctly making judgements about Him.

As for the possibility of knowing His reality, the philosophers deny it, because knowledge occurs through an imprinted form, inconceivable in the case of the Necessary and entailing His predicability of many; and because knowledge would arise either immediately, whereas there is no immediate cognition here; through an essential definition, whereas He has no composition; or through a descriptive definition, which does not yield conception of the reality. The first argument is denied; moreover, what is impossible is predication of multiple individuals, not multiple forms. The second, even granting its exhaustive division, is answered by saying that a descriptive definition may lead to such conception.

## Chapter Five: His acts

It comprises several investigations.

### First investigation

The servant's act occurs through God's power, while the servant has only acquisition [kasb]. For the Muʿtazila it occurs through the servant's power contingently; for the philosophers, necessarily. For the Ustādh it occurs through both powers, both relating to the act together. For the Qāḍī, God's power relates to the act's basis and the servant's power to its qualification, such as being obedience or disobedience. Compulsion in the sense that the servant's power has no role whatsoever, whether in bringing into existence or in acquisition, is necessarily false.

<a id="m-v-p575"></a>

**Source p. 575**

Acquisition is said to be the qualification through which the servant's power relates to the act; or the act created by God's power insofar as He creates for the servant a power relating to it; or that through which an object of power occurs without the powerful agent's being independently sufficient, and what occurs in the power's substrate. The truth is that acquisition is evident, while its verbal expression is obscure. More clearly, it is a relational matter necessarily proceeding from the servant, which does not necessitate the object's existence but the agent's qualification by it: for example, determining and preferring one alternative, and directing power toward it.

We have rational and revelational proofs. The rational proofs are several.

First: if the servant's act occurred through his power, two effecting causes would converge, given the universality of God's power established earlier.

Second: he would know its details. The falsity of the consequent is evident in the sleeper, walker, speaker, and writer.

Third: if the servant's act occurred through his power and choice, he would be able both to perform and to omit it. This is false, because acting must prevail over refraining through a determinant not originating from him, upon which the act becomes necessary. For predominance without a determinant, an infinite regress of determinants, and an effect's existence without necessity are impossible.

The objection is that this also applies to God's act, and that necessity through choice does not conflict with equality of the alternatives with respect to power. The answer is that in God's case the determinant is beginningless: the beginningless will, attached beginninglessly to the act's existence at its time. Here the determinant is originated and requires another determinant, nullifying the servant's independence and ability to refrain.

<a id="m-v-p577"></a>

**Source p. 577**

Fourth: what God knows of the servant's act is either its occurrence, making it necessary, or its nonoccurrence, making it impossible. It therefore no longer lies within the servant's capacity, although intrinsically possible. If it is said, “What is known is its occurrence through the servant's power and choice,” we reply: that becomes necessary, and the difficulty returns. God's own act is offered as a counterinstance.

As for arguing that God wills either occurrence or nonoccurrence, the reply allows that He wills neither, and that something contrary to His will occurs.

Fifth: if the act occurred through the servant's power, then, when he willed to move a body and God willed its rest, the two intended objects would either agree in occurring or in not occurring, which is impossible; or differ, producing predominance without a determinant, since both powers are assumed independently sufficient. The answer is that equality in independence does not exclude inequality in strength: God's intended object occurs because His power is stronger.

It is also argued that, if the servant had power over his act, he would have power to repeat it, produce its like, and create body, since nothing permits susceptibility to power except origination or contingency; that his act, such as creating faith, would be better than the Creator's act, such as creating the devil; and that asking for faith or giving thanks for it would be inappropriate.

Revelational proofs are exceedingly numerous. They include verses praising Him as the sole Creator: “Creator of everything” [6:102], “He created everything” [6:101], and “We have created everything according to a measure” [54:49].

<a id="m-v-p579"></a>

**Source p. 579**

They include “God created you and what you do” [37:96]. If *mā* makes a verbal noun, requiring no omitted expression, the implication is evident. If it is relative, it encompasses the acts the servant acquires—motions, rests, positions, and configurations—as in “They performed righteous deeds” [2:25] and “They perform evil deeds” [4:18]. These are what is disputed, not the act of effecting itself.

They include “He is God, the Creator” [59:24]; “Conceal your speech or proclaim it: He knows what is within breasts. Does He who created not know?” [67:13–14]; “Is there a creator other than God?” [35:3]; “Those they invoke besides God create nothing” [16:20]; and “What have those besides Him created?” [31:11].

They include reported prayers: “Our Lord, make us submit to You” [2:128]; “My Lord, make me one who establishes prayer” [14:40]; and “My Lord, make him pleasing” [19:6].

They include “Doer of what He wills” [11:107]; “God does what He wills” [14:27]; and “He judges as He wills” [5:1].

They include “Say: all is from God” [4:78]; “Whatever blessing you possess is from God” [16:53]; “Our saying to a thing, when We intend it, is only to say ‘Be,’ and it is” [16:40]; “He inscribed faith in their hearts” [58:22]; “He it is who makes laugh and weep” [53:43]; “He it is who enables you to travel on land and sea” [10:22]; and “None holds them except God” [16:79], among others.

<a id="m-v-p581"></a>

**Source p. 581**

They also include reports whose meaning is recurrently transmitted, indicating that everything that occurs does so through God's determination and will.

As for the Muʿtazila, some claim necessary knowledge. Everyone distinguishes the motion of falling from that of ascending, finds his activities following his motives and purposes, judges it good to praise benefactors and blame wrongdoers, and recognises the appropriateness of requesting walking from a healthy person but not a paralysed one, and moving a clod but not a mountain. What someone requests, prohibits, wishes for, or wonders at is unquestionably the agent's act. All this occurs without investigation or reflection.

The answer is that these establish only that some acts relate to the servant's power and will and occur according to his intention and motive. The dispute is whether he creates and brings them into existence, which most rational people deny. Claiming this as necessary knowledge is a mark of shamelessness. When it comes from someone of exceptional acuteness, it can only be concealment and deception of his followers, lest they discover his return to the truth. For he holds that the powerful agent's preferring action to omission necessarily depends on a motive, and that the act's occurrence following the motive is necessary. The servant's independent agency is then eliminated and the Muʿtazilī position entirely nullified: with the motive created by God, the commanded person is no longer able both to act and refrain, just as if the act itself were created by God.

It is said that necessity means we know he will certainly act while refraining remains possible, like the prophets' reward in Paradise and unbelievers' punishment in the Fire.

<a id="m-v-p583"></a>

**Source p. 583**

We reply: if an unopposed motive necessitates preferring action so that he cannot refrain, the conclusion is established. Otherwise “necessity” is merely a label, and belief in occurrence is conjecture about the unseen. It becomes knowledge only when necessary issuance is believed. Hence the absence of action is used to infer the absence of power and will.

Others argue rationally and textually. Their rational arguments are several.

First: without the servant's independent agency, praise and blame, command and prohibition, reward and punishment, the benefits of promise and threat, sending messengers and revealing books would be nullified; so would the differences between unbelief and faith, wrongdoing and beneficence, the prophet's and devil's acts, words of glorification and delirium, and what occurs through the servant's organs according to his own will or another's, although the distinction is immediately experienced.

Second: some acts are ugly, making their creation ugly for the Wise, such as wrongdoing, associating partners with God, attributing a child to Him, and the like.

Third: the servant's act necessarily occurs according to his will. If God brought it into existence, this would not be so, since He might omit it when the servant wills it and produce it when the servant dislikes it.

Fourth: if God created creatures' acts, He could be qualified by them. An unbeliever means only one who performs unbelief; He would therefore be an unbeliever, wrongdoer, sinner, eater, drinker, stander, sitter, and innumerable other things.

The answer to the first is that no difficulty arises for one who makes the servant's act relate to his power and will and occur through his acquisition and after his resolve. If the difficulty does follow, it follows for the Muʿtazila too, since the act is necessary or impossible on the basis of the necessitating determinant and beginningless knowledge, whether of existence or nonexistence.

<a id="m-v-p585"></a>

**Source p. 585**

To the second: even granting rational ugliness, what is ugly is performing the ugly act, not creating it. To the third: even if its necessary occurrence is granted, this accords with God's will, which customarily agrees with the servant's will. To the fourth: it is folly or shamelessness.

Their revelational proofs are exceedingly numerous and have been arranged in kinds.

First: acts are attributed to servants in more passages than can be counted, but this is not disputed.

Second: verses containing command, prohibition, praise, blame, promise, threat, and accounts of past peoples for warning and instruction. Their answer has preceded.

Third: expressions instituted for bringing into existence are attributed to servants: “Whoever performs a righteous deed” [16:97]; “Whatever good you do” [2:197]; “God knows what you make” [29:45]; “Every soul is paid in full what it has earned” [3:25]; “They put their fingers in their ears” [2:19]; “Blessed is God, the best of creators” [23:14]; “Until I produce for you a mention of it” [18:70]; and “Monasticism which they invented” [57:27].

We reply: there is metaphor in the predicate or its attribution, to reconcile the proofs; or the effecting cause is the combination of power and will created by God, in which case neither difficulty nor independent agency remains.

Fourth: verses indicating no impediment to faith and obedience and no coercion toward unbelief and disobedience: “What prevented people from believing?” [17:94]; “Why do they not believe?” [84:20]; “Why do you clothe truth in falsehood?” [3:71]; “How can you disbelieve in God?” [2:28]; and “Why do you bar people from God's path?” [3:99], and similar passages.

<a id="m-v-p587"></a>

**Source p. 587**

The reply is that these mean evident impediments acknowledged by everyone, or impediments to resolve, directing power, and matters belonging to the servants.

Fifth: servants' acts are made dependent on their will rather than His: “Whoever wills, let him believe; whoever wills, let him disbelieve” [18:29]; and “Do what you will” [41:40]. We reply: yes, but their will is through His will: “You do not will unless God wills” [76:30].

Conclusion: the impossibility of predominance without a determinant and ignorance of the details of acts lead toward compulsion; the goodness of praise, blame, command, and prohibition, and acts' following the servant's intention and motive, toward independent human agency. The servant's being the source of deficiency suits compulsion, while the abundance of folly, purposelessness, and ugliness in acts suits independent human agency. Verses and reports abound on both sides. The truth is therefore neither compulsion nor delegation, but a matter between the two: the proximate principles involve choice, the remote principles necessity. The human being is compelled in the form of one who chooses.

His acts occur through God's decree and determination, meaning His creation and measuring, immediately or through a necessitating intermediary. Contentment is obligatory only with the decree, not the thing decreed. For the Muʿtazila, decree and determination can mean only notification and inscription, or binding imposition specifically in obligatory matters. The philosophers say decree is the existence of things collectively in the intelligible world, while determination is their detailed existence in external matters. Evil enters decree consequentially.

<a id="m-v-p589"></a>

**Source p. 589**

There is no disagreement over condemning the Qadariyya. The Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, even said, “The Qadariyya were cursed upon the tongues of seventy prophets.” They were named thus because of their excessive denial of divine determination. Their claim that those affirming determination deserve the name more is answered by his sayings, “The Qadariyya are the Magians of this community,” and “When the Resurrection comes, a caller will proclaim among those gathered, ‘Where are God's adversaries?’ and the Qadariyya will rise”; and by saying that one who attributes determination to himself deserves this name more than one who attributes it to his Lord.

Corollary: once everything's dependence on God is established, the Muʿtazilī doctrine of generated effects [tawlīd] and its consequences are invalidated. Most regard the generated effect as the servant's act, invoking arguments like those already discussed concerning the creation of acts. Al-Naẓẓām says the servant acts only where his power resides. Muʿammar says only willing is his act; another view restricts it to thinking. Everything else follows the substrate's nature, since it may not accord with the motive, and its omission may be impossible, as with a released arrow. The reply is that this results from an impediment.

Our argument that attributing the effect to the servant entails either two effecting causes or predominance without a determinant, in the motion of a body pulled by one agent and pushed by another, is answered by denying that either power independently suffices for that motion.

<a id="m-v-p591"></a>

**Source p. 591**

### Second investigation: The universality of His will

The truth is that every actual thing is willed by Him, and conversely. The early authorities agree that whatever God wills occurs and whatever He does not will does not occur. Since He creates everything, He wills it; and since He knows the nonoccurrence of what does not occur, He does not will it. Will is an attribute whose function is to prefer and determine one of two alternatives equal in relation to power. Directing a motive toward the impossible, even what is impossible through another, is wishing rather than willing. The texts supporting this are innumerable.

The Muʿtazila do not merely exclude ugly acts from His will; they affirm that He wills their opposites. They thus make most of what occurs in His dominion contrary to His will. Their arguments are that willing an ugly act is ugly; punishing what was willed is injustice; commanding what is not willed and prohibiting what is willed is foolishness; and will entails command, approval, and love. All are unsound. Such verses as “God does not will injustice for His servants” [40:31], “God does not command indecency” [7:28], “He does not approve unbelief for His servants” [39:7], and “God does not love corruption” [2:205] therefore do not establish their position.

The rebuke of those who said, “Had God willed, neither we nor our fathers would have associated partners with Him” [6:148], concerns their intention to mock. Hence God says, “Thus those before them denied, until they tasted Our might” [6:148], describing them as deniers and punished for it. He then expressly states that, had He willed, He would have guided them all.

<a id="m-v-p593"></a>

**Source p. 593**

They also invoke “I created jinn and human beings only that they might worship Me” [51:56], and “The evil of all that is hateful before your Lord” [17:38]. The first, even granting universality, means that I might command them to worship, or that they might submit, or be My servants. The second, even granting that it refers to what actually occurs, means hateful among people and within customary evaluations.

### Third investigation

Reason does not judge goodness and ugliness in the sense of deserving praise and blame before God, contrary to the Muʿtazila. There is no dispute concerning goodness and ugliness as perfection and deficiency, or suitability and unsuitability to a purpose or nature. For us, goodness arises through command and ugliness through prohibition—indeed, they are identical with these. For them, command follows goodness and prohibition ugliness. Even when reason apprehends neither immediately nor through investigation, revelation discloses rather than establishes them.

Our revelational proof is “We do not punish until We send a messenger” [17:15]. Our rational proofs include the following. First: if an act were intrinsically good or ugly, its goodness and ugliness would not vary, as killing does when judicial punishment or injustice, striking when discipline or torment, and lying or truthfulness when saving or destroying life.

<a id="m-v-p595"></a>

**Source p. 595**

Second: if goodness and ugliness were intrinsic, they would not coexist, as in the report of one who says, “I shall certainly lie tomorrow,” or “This statement I am uttering is false.” Third: the servant is not independently responsible for producing his act, as explained, whereas on their account God's praise and blame apply only to what the servant independently produces.

The following arguments, however, are weak: if goodness and ugliness were real—and positive, as contradictories of the privative not-good and not-ugly—the act's possessing them would entail one meaning's subsisting in another, indeed an existent's subsisting in a nonexistent, because as motive and deterrent they precede the act; and, if acts differed in goodness and ugliness intrinsically or by consideration, the Creator's choice in legislating judgements and determining lawful and unlawful would be nullified.

They invoke several arguments. First: no rational person doubts the goodness of beneficence and ugliness of aggression, even without religion. We reply: not in the disputed sense. Second: someone for whose purpose truth and falsehood, or rescuing and destroying a drowning person, are equal prefers truth and rescue, solely because reason recognises their goodness. We reply: rather, because they are more beneficial, better suit the public's purposes, and accord with fellow-feeling. Moreover, this certainty concerns a supposed equality, not an actual equality, which is impossible. Third: if goodness and ugliness depended on revelation, neither would be established, since the impossibility of the Creator's lying, commanding ugliness, or prohibiting goodness would likewise depend on revelation, producing circularity.

<a id="m-v-p597"></a>

**Source p. 597**

We reply: the impossibility of His lying has already been explained without circularity. Moreover, we make goodness not something caused by command but the command itself, so no circularity follows.

Fourth: unless lying and displaying a miracle at a liar's hands were ugly for Him, prophecy could not be established. We reply: something may be possible yet certainly not occur, as with other customary facts. Fifth: someone who knows His essence, attributes, and blessings, yet associates partners with Him, attributes to Him an unfitting spouse, child, or other marks of origination and deficiency, and persists in ingratitude and idol-worship, is certainly exposed to blame and punishment. We reply: this is known because revealed laws have established it and customary judgements continuously affirm it. Sixth: unless investigation were rationally obligatory, prophets could be silenced; this has already been discussed. Because of the last two arguments' strength, some of us affirm rational goodness and ugliness for certain acts.

### Fourth investigation

Nothing ugly proceeds from God, although He creates everything, and nothing is obligatory upon Him, although revelation judges His acts good. The Muʿtazila, affirming obligations upon Him and rationally established ugly acts, hold that He certainly performs what is obligatory and omits what is ugly. Both sides therefore agree that He performs no ugly act and omits no obligation. They disagree confusedly over what obligation upon Him means, ultimately being driven to say that He certainly performs it although omission is possible. Besides conjecturing about the unseen, this is merely assigning a name.

<a id="m-v-p599"></a>

**Source p. 599**

### Fifth investigation

Imposing what exceeds capacity is not impossible, and His acts are not caused by purposes, contrary to the Muʿtazila. Their principal argument is that imposing the impossible is foolish and an act without purpose futile, neither befitting the Wise. You already know both arguments' weakness.

The first dispute concerns what is intrinsically possible but never an object of the servant's power, such as creating bodies, or not customarily so, such as ascending to heaven. It does not concern the intrinsically impossible, such as combining contradictories: the majority exclude imposing this, because obligation requires conceiving its object as realised, whereas the impossible is conceived only through comparison or negation. Nor does it concern what is impossible because of God's prior knowledge or report of its nonoccurrence, since imposing this actually occurs by agreement.

The dispute concerns permissibility; actual occurrence is denied by revelation and induction. It concerns obligation as demanding performance and making omission deserving of punishment; a demand intended to demonstrate incapacity occurs by agreement. Hence opponents' appeal to “God burdens no soul beyond its capacity” [2:286] does not address the disputed point. Neither do proponents' appeals to “Produce a chapter like it” [2:23]; to the servant's act not occurring through his power; to obligation preceding the act while power accompanies it; or to someone whom God knows will not believe being obligated to believe, although belief from him is impossible because ignorance is impossible for God.

<a id="m-v-p601"></a>

**Source p. 601**

Many investigators nevertheless say that imposing the intrinsically impossible, such as combining contradictories, is permissible and actually occurs. Someone like Abū Lahab must affirm the Prophet's truthfulness in everything he brings, including the report that Abū Lahab will never affirm him. He is thus required to affirm him in saying that he will not affirm him: a combination of contradictories. Replies that the obligation concerns only acquiring faith, intrinsically possible but impossible through prior knowledge and report, or that he must affirm everything except this report, are weak.

Concerning purpose, some arguments establish its necessary exclusion: if God acted for a purpose, He would be intrinsically deficient and perfected through another; and everything has been established as immediately depending on Him, without some things being ends and others subordinate to them. Other arguments merely deny that a purpose is necessary: the series must terminate in something not acting for a purpose; and no benefit to anyone is conceivable in such things as unbelievers' everlasting punishment. The latter position is closer to the truth, since texts attest purposes for some acts, especially legislation; consensus nearly establishes this, and legal analogy rests upon it.

Conclusion: the Muʿtazila hold that obligation's purpose is to offer the opportunity for reward, since reward is not good without the desert acquired through hardships. Their arguments include: first, such verses as “Whoever obeys God and His Messenger, He will admit him to gardens” [4:13].

<a id="m-v-p603"></a>

**Source p. 603**

Second: by consensus there is no other purpose, since you affirm none and we deny any other; this purpose is therefore established. Third: imposing hardships causes harm, which without desert or benefit is injustice; offering an opportunity for benefit is therefore what makes it good.

The reply is that what follows may be God's bounty, not an effect of that upon which it follows. How could everlasting bliss be deserved by a mere utterance and assent in someone who believes and then dies? Nor do we grant consensus that there is no other purpose. Some say testing, others gratitude, preserving order, or something inaccessible to reason. Even if the exclusion of other purposes is granted, this establishes that it is the purpose only after the necessity of a purpose is established, which it has not been.

## Chapter Six: Further questions concerning acts

### First investigation

Guidance may mean being rightly guided, opposed to straying; or pointing out the path leading to the destination, opposed to leading astray. Guiding may also mean calling to truth, rewarding, or directing toward Paradise's path in the hereafter; leading astray may mean wasting or destroying. Both may be figuratively attributed to causes.

The dispute concerns texts describing the Creator as guiding, leading astray, impressing and sealing unbelievers' hearts, and extending them in their rebellion. For us, these mean creating guidance and error, since He alone has been established as Creator.

<a id="m-v-p605"></a>

**Source p. 605**

For the Muʿtazila, guidance means direction bringing one to the goal, clarification through establishing proofs, or granting gracious assistance. Leading astray means destruction and punishment, naming someone astray, or withholding gracious assistance; alternatively, the attribution is figurative. Besides resting on their unsound principle, this conflicts with the apparent senses of many verses.

### Second investigation

The Imām says gracious assistance [luṭf], enabling grace [tawfīq], and protection from sin [ʿiṣma] are the creation of power for obedience; abandonment [khidhlān] is the creation of power for disobedience. One granted enabling grace therefore does not disobey, and conversely. Protection is also said to mean that God does not create sin in the servant, or a special property through which sin cannot issue from him. The philosophers call it a settled disposition preventing depravity while preserving power to commit it.

The Muʿtazila call gracious assistance that upon which an accountable person chooses obedience, by omission or performance, or approaches either, while retaining the capacity for both alternatives. These are called accomplishing and approximating assistance. Assistance accomplishing an obligation is specifically called enabling grace; that accomplishing avoidance of ugliness, protection. It is also said that enabling grace is God's creating an assistance upon which He knows the servant will obey; abandonment is withholding assistance; protection is an assistance leaving no motive to omit obedience or commit disobedience, while preserving power for both. They say assistance differs among accountable persons and God knows no assistance effective for everyone. Hence they interpret will in such verses as “Had We willed, We would have given every soul its guidance” [32:13] as a will to compel and constrain.

<a id="m-v-p607"></a>

**Source p. 607**

### Third investigation

An appointed term [ajal] is a time, commonly the time at which God knows an animal's life will cease. It is one. Death is God's act, sometimes following the servant's act through the customary course. A slain person dies at his appointed term. Had he not been slain, neither his death nor his survival could be asserted certainly. Abū al-Hudhayl says he would certainly die at that time; many Muʿtazilites say he would certainly live until another duration, his appointed term.

Our proofs include “When their appointed term comes, they cannot delay or advance it by an hour” [7:34], and that, without knowing the appointed term, neither death nor survival is known. “No long-lived person's life is prolonged, nor is any of his lifespan diminished” [35:11] means from the lifespan of some long-lived person, not that same person. The increase of lifespan through righteousness—besides being reported in a solitary report—may mean abundant goodness and blessing. Allowing delayed death does not alter God's knowledge but preserves it, since absence of killing is conceivable only on the supposition that God knows it. The killer deserves recompense for the act he acquired and the prohibition he violated, not for the death existing in its subject.

### Fourth investigation

Provision is what God conveys to an animal and it benefits from. Everyone therefore receives his provision in full, and no one consumes another's provision. Another definition says “so that it may benefit”; sometimes provision is restricted to food. The Muʿtazila add that no one has the right to prevent it, excluding the unlawful according to their principle concerning ugliness. Thus someone who ate only unlawful food throughout life would have received no provision. Our proofs are the texts indicating God's guarantee of provisions.

<a id="m-v-p609"></a>

**Source p. 609**

They ask: why then is he prevented from it, blamed and punished for it, and forbidden to seek it? We reply: because he violates a prohibition and acquires an ugly act.

### Fifth investigation

Price is the determination of what a thing is sold for. Dearness and cheapness arise through causes from God, even when some belong to servants' acquisitions. God alone therefore determines prices, contrary to the Muʿtazila.

### Sixth investigation

The Muʿtazila hold several things obligatory upon God.

First: gracious assistance, an act bringing one nearer to obedience or accomplishing it, and distancing one from disobedience without reaching compulsion. They argue that, if what brings one nearer the commanded act could be withheld, the act would not be willed, a contradiction; that withholding assistance defeats the purpose and brings about or approaches disobedience, making it ugly; and that an obligation is completed only through what accomplishes or approaches it, which must therefore be obligatory. All are weak. They are countered by their own principle that the utmost assistance is obligatory, which would leave no unbeliever or sinner; and by the consequences that God would not report some people's blessedness and others' wretchedness, since this induces despair or tempts to sin, and that no age would lack prophets, saints, and caliphs.

Second: compensation, a benefit without accompanying honour, in exchange for pain or the like inflicted on a servant. It is obligatory because omitting it is injustice: harm neither deserved,

<a id="m-v-p611"></a>

**Source p. 611**

nor containing benefit or averting harm, nor customary. They say pain inflicted as recompense for wrongdoing is punishment. Otherwise, if it comes from God, from an accountable person without good deeds, from a nonrational creature driven by a cause from God, or occurs through His command, permission, or enabling, compensation is incumbent upon Him.

They disagree over whether compensation must be everlasting; whether its recipient must know it is his due; whether God may graciously discharge an oppressor's compensation to the oppressed; whether compensation must occur in the hereafter; whether sins cancel it; and whether equivalent benefits may be graciously given without preceding pain. They are divided over whether unbelievers', sinners', and nonrational creatures' compensation—including children's and animals'—occurs here or hereafter, whether animals enter Paradise, and whether knowledge is created in them.

Third: recompense, to be discussed later. Fourth: taking life prematurely when God knows that a protected or repentant person would disbelieve or sin if allowed to remain, since omission would frustrate the purpose. Most deny its obligation, because the frustration arises through the servant's act.

Fifth: what is best for servants in religion, according to the Basrans, and in worldly affairs too, according to the Baghdadians. They agree that granting capacity and opportunity is obligatory, as is the utmost possible benefit for everyone. Nothing within divine power would, if performed for unbelievers, bring them all to belief;

<a id="m-v-p613"></a>

**Source p. 613**

otherwise omitting it would be miserliness and foolishness, like a wise ruler commanding obedience while withholding, despite ability and absence of harm, what leads to it; or a generous host inviting a guest but replacing a cheerful reception with harshness. They also invoke the certainty that action is necessary when an unopposed motive and power are present.

Even conceding their premises, we reply: if what is best were obligatory, God would not create a poor unbeliever afflicted throughout life by suffering and calamity. Their analogies would oblige everyone to do what is best for his servants. Everlasting punishment would have to be best for unbelievers; everything God does for servants would discharge an obligation and deserve no gratitude; His possible gracious assistances would be finite; and the deaths of prophets, saints, benefactors, and generous people, together with the survival of oppressors, misleaders, Iblīs and his offspring, and those known to become apostates, would be best for servants. Prayer to avert affliction would not be good; His favour toward unbelievers and prophets would be equal; and He would have no scope for bounty or choice in beneficence.

## Chapter Seven: His names

### First investigation

A name is an instituted expression; the named is the meaning for which it is instituted; naming is its institution or utterance. Their difference is therefore necessary. The well-known statement that the name is the named, while naming differs from both, intends by “name” the signified, as in “Zayd is a writer,” unlike “Zayd is written.”

<a id="m-v-p615"></a>

**Source p. 615**

The Shaykh's distinction—that a name may be identical with the named, as “God”; other than it, as “Creator”; or neither identical nor other, as “Knowing”—rests on taking the signified broadly enough to include what is signified by inclusion, and taking the named as the essence and reality itself.

Both parties' appeals to such verses as “Glorify the name of your Lord Most High” [87:1] and “To God belong the most beautiful names” [7:180] are weak, besides suggesting that the dispute concerns the letters constituting “name,” which it does not. A name may itself be sanctified, and honouring it may express honouring the essence. For the Shaykh it may mean naming; moreover, multiple concepts do not conflict with one essence.

If it is said that expression and meaning plainly differ, while signified and named do not, leaving no intelligible subject of dispute or confusion, we reply: when a name is mentioned, the judgement may concern the signified, as “Zayd wrote,” or the signifier, as “I wrote ‘Zayd.’” Every expression thus seems to possess a proper-name use for itself, as “‘struck’ is a past-tense verb” and “‘from’ is a preposition.” Some names are instances of what they name, such as “word” and “name”; some significations concern the named's essence, as “human,” others an accident, as “laughing.” “Named” may mean the concept or the individuals of which it is predicated. Such usages may therefore generate confusion over whether the name is identical with or other than the named.

### Second investigation

God's names depend on revealed authorisation, contrary to the Muʿtazila and the Qāḍī without qualification, and al-Ghazālī concerning attributes. The Imām al-Ḥaramayn suspends judgement. The dispute concerns an expression whose meaning belongs to the Creator,

<a id="m-v-p617"></a>

**Source p. 617**

for which neither permission nor prohibition has been revealed, nor for its synonym, and which conveys reverence without suggesting imperfection. Our argument is that such unauthorised naming is impermissible for the Prophet; indeed, ordinary people would not accept it. They say it is common in other languages. We reply: that is outside the disputed case.

The Imām says permissibility and prohibition are revealed judgements requiring revealed evidence, while analogy has no standing in names and attributes. We reply: naming belongs to practical acts.

Al-Ghazālī says applying attributes reports the presence of their meanings and is therefore permitted by the evidence allowing, indeed recommending, truthfulness unless an impediment intervenes. Naming differs: it is an exercise of authority over the named and belongs only to one possessing that authority. Such names as “recogniser” and “discerning” are forbidden because they suggest imperfection; “tiller” and “sower” because they lack reverence.

### Third investigation

A name may signify the essence itself, or be taken with reference to a part or some incidental feature—attributes, acts, negations, or relations. Through these considerations God's names multiply. Reference to a part is plainly impossible. They disagree over a name signifying the essence itself, claiming this follows their disagreement over knowledge of the essence. This is unsound: God Himself may institute the name, or knowledge of the essence under some aspect may suffice. Hence investigators hold that “Allah” is a proper name of the essence.

<a id="m-v-p619"></a>

**Source p. 619**

If it is said: expressions truly applicable to the Creator are exceedingly numerous, and more than one hundred and fifty occur in the Book and Sunna; why then the restriction to ninety-nine? We reply, even granting that a stated number excludes additions: the Prophet's words “whoever enumerates them enters Paradise” may function as a restrictive description [of those particular ninety-nine]. The greatest name may be among them, left unidentified and known only to the elect; or outside them, their superior distinction being relative to the other names apart from it. Moreover, many hadith scholars judge weak the report that enumerates the ninety-nine individually.

---

<a id="division-vi"></a>

# Division VI: Matters known through revelation

<a id="m-vi-p621"></a>

**Source p. 621**

## Chapter One: Prophecy

### First investigation

A prophet is a human being whom God sends to convey what is revealed to him. “Messenger” has the same meaning, although sometimes restricted to one distinguished by a revealed law and book. Sending prophets, encompassing innumerable benefits, is God's gracious assistance and mercy, with which He singles out whom He wills among His servants. It is neither obligatory upon Him, contrary to the Muʿtazila, nor a necessary consequence from Him, contrary to the philosophers and some theologians. The latter hold that wisdom's requirement must occur because foolishness is impossible, just as what is known to occur must occur because ignorance is impossible.

The deniers have several objections. First: a mission depends on the envoy's knowing that God is the sender, and there is no route to this. The reply allows establishing proofs or creating necessary knowledge. Second: the mission is futile, since what reason judges good is performed, what it judges ugly omitted, and what it judges neither is performed according to benefit. The reply is that revelation reinforces reason where reason suffices, assists where it does not, removes uncertainty from what it supposes, and supplies access to what it cannot apprehend. Moreover, entrusting matters to unequal intellects threatens the disruption of order.

<a id="m-vi-p623"></a>

**Source p. 623**

Third: prophecy rests on imposing obligations benefiting neither the servant, whom they harm, nor the worshipped Lord, who is above need, while distracting the inner consciousness from complete orientation toward Him. The reply is that its benefit greatly predominates. Fourth: revealed laws contain things suggesting they are not from God, such as the acts of prayer, pilgrimage, ablution, full washing, and other matters outside reason's rule. The reply is that, for those attending to outward practice, these are trials reinforcing the disposition of compliance; for realised investigators, they embody subtle wisdoms and mysteries made manifest. Fifth: objections to miracles, to be discussed, God willing.

### Second investigation

A miracle is something breaking the customary order, conjoined with a challenge and the absence of successful opposition. Alternatively, it is something intended to manifest the truthfulness of a claimant to messengership. Some add agreement with the claim; others, occurrence during the period of obligation, since extraordinary things appear at its ending without intending to confirm a claimant.

Its possibility is necessarily evident, as is the possibility of reporting it to absent people. Its indication consists in its being equivalent to explicit confirmation. Suppose someone claims to be a king's messenger and, when asked for proof, says the king will depart from his custom and rise from his throne three times; the king then does so. This illustrates by example, rather than proving the unseen through analogy with the observed.

<a id="m-vi-p625"></a>

**Source p. 625**

If it is said that several possibilities prevent the conclusion, they are these. First: the event may depend on the claimant through a property of his soul, a bodily temperament, or knowledge of particular properties or celestial configurations; or upon an angel, jinn, or something else. Second: it may begin a new customary sequence or repeat something occurring only after ages. Third: it may admit opposition that was withheld for a purpose, or opposed without the opposition being reported because of an impediment. Fourth: it may not intend confirmation, either because there is no purpose or because another purpose exists—assistance to an accountable person, answering prayer, a miracle for another prophet, testing or misleading servants. Even if equivalent to explicitly saying “You are truthful,” this proves the claim only if falsehood in God's report is impossible, which is established only by revelation.

The general answer is that rational possibilities do not exclude certain knowledge, as in other customary matters. In detail: first, we have established that none produces effects, especially extraordinary ones, except God; moreover, His merely enabling the occurrence suffices. Second, the discussion concerns what is certainly known to break custom and what the challenged parties failed to match despite utmost concern and effort. Hence each prophet's miracle belonged to what predominated among his contemporaries: magic in Moses's time,

<a id="m-vi-p627"></a>

**Source p. 627**

medicine in Jesus's, music in David's, and eloquence in Muḥammad's, blessings and peace upon them. Third, ends plainly follow God's acts even if not motivating purposes. We claim only that the miracle indicates a confirmation subsisting in Him, whether or not it is a purpose. Fourth, although a miracle's appearing at a liar's hands is rationally possible, its nonoccurrence is certainly known. Some of us call it impossible because it would make demonstrating a true prophetic claim impossible; because truthfulness necessarily accompanies the miracle as knowledge accompanies skilful action; or because equating truthful and lying claimants is foolish. Fifth, the miracle yields knowledge of truthfulness without requiring consideration of a divine report, being equivalent to “I have appointed you a messenger and established messengership in you.”

Conclusion: the means of establishing prophecy generally against its deniers is the miracle alone. This does not exclude God's creating necessary knowledge of it, or its establishment through another prophet's report or a revealed book.

### Third investigation

The philosophers say that human subsistence requires association with fellow humans and cooperation, possible only through transactions and exchanges. These require an agreed law, appropriately instituted by someone distinguished from others through a distinction from the Creator of all and signs requiring acknowledgement and submission.

<a id="m-vi-p629"></a>

**Source p. 629**

In respect of human intellectual power, these signs consist in knowledge of unseen things through the soul's connection with the unseen world. In respect of animal power, concerning movements, they consist in acts whose like others cannot perform: winds, earthquakes, rending, inundation, the destruction of unjust people or corrupt cities, and similar events, through the soul's particular power over bodies beyond its own. Concerning rests, they consist in abstaining from nourishment for an unusual duration because the soul is drawn to the holy world and draws after it the nutritive power and its servants.

Hence immaterial intellects and celestial souls—especially the active intellect, particularly connected with the elemental world—may appear to his perfected imagination as shaped apparitions addressing him. They produce in his hearing ordered speech preserved and recited. This is revelation and the descent of angel and book.

They explain how this comes from God for worldly order, salvation hereafter, and servants' welfare, while denying intention and purpose in His acts and knowledge of particulars as particulars, as follows. Divine providence—His prior knowledge encompassing the appropriate order of existents—requires that order's emanation in its detailed sequence, including law and lawgiver, so that what exists conforms to what is known. Clearly this does not suffice for what is necessarily established in religion.

### Fourth investigation

Muḥammad is God's Messenger, God bless him and grant him peace. He claimed messengership, which is evident, and displayed miracles: he brought the inimitable Qurʾān, reported unseen things, and manifested extraordinary states.

<a id="m-vi-p631"></a>

**Source p. 631**

For the first kind, its inimitability is established because he challenged the leading eloquent speakers with its shortest chapter, although they were numerous and renowned for partisan pride. They turned from matching it to fighting, demonstrating their inability.

Most locate inimitability in its highest degree of eloquence. Many locate it in diversion [ṣarfa]: God diverted minds from opposition despite their ability. The objections are that eloquent Arabs marvelled at the Qurʾān itself, not at the absence of easy opposition; that relinquishing perfect eloquence would more effectively demonstrate inimitability by diversion; and the verse “If humans and jinn gathered together…” [17:88].

Other accounts locate it in an unfamiliar style unlike their speech, freedom from inconsistency and contradiction, subtle sciences and wisdoms and benefits, or reports of unseen things. The replies are that Musaylima's and others' fabrications employ that style; many eloquent speakers' and sages' words lack contradiction and contain sciences and truths; and many chapters contain no unseen reports.

The general answer to attacks is that Arab leaders, despite skill and hostility, acknowledged it and yielded, without making these attacks, instead attributing it to magic because of its consummate beauty.

<a id="m-vi-p633"></a>

**Source p. 633**

The detailed answer concerns objections raised by obstinate enemies of religion: it contains non-Arabic words, such as *istabraq* and *sijjīl*, so how is it clear Arabic? It contains grammatical error, as “These two are certainly magicians” [20:63]. It contains eleven verses of human speech, beginning “My Lord, expand my breast” [20:25], so how can its challenge concern a chapter when the shortest has three verses? It contains passages invoked by misguided people, such as “The All-Merciful settled upon the Throne” [20:5]. It has faulty repetition, such as Pharaoh's story, “Which of your Lord's favours will you two deny?” [55:16], and “Woe that day to the deniers” [77:15]. Its readings differ greatly, despite “Had it been from other than God, they would have found much inconsistency in it” [4:82]. It contains contradiction, as “That day neither human nor jinn will be questioned about his sin” [55:39] alongside “By your Lord, We shall question them all” [15:92]; outright falsehood, as “We created you, then fashioned you, then said to the angels, ‘Prostrate to Adam’” [7:11]; and poetry in every metre, as “Whoever wills, let him believe; whoever wills, let him disbelieve” [18:29], and others.

The answers are that two languages may share words, or the whole is called Arabic by predominance; the grammatical error lies in alleging error, as grammar explains; reported speech need not reproduce the original speaker's expression; ambiguous passages have benefits, such as the reward of investigation or suspending judgement;

<a id="m-vi-p635"></a>

**Source p. 635**

repetition may be an excellence; the denied inconsistency is variation of composition falling short of inimitability; and supposed contradiction, falsehood, and poetry arise from ignorance of exegesis and poetry's meaning.

The second kind includes reports of past prophets and others, and future events in revelation: “God has promised you abundant spoils” [48:20]; “Alif Lām Mīm. The Romans have been defeated…” through “God does not fail His promise” [30:1–6]; “The host will be defeated and turn in flight” [54:45]; “You will certainly enter the Sacred Mosque” [48:27]; and similar passages.
