Prophetic reports include his saying to ʿAlī, “After me you will fight the oath-breakers, the unjust, and those who depart”; to ʿAmmār, “The rebellious faction will kill you”; and “My community's dominion will reach what was folded together for me.” He also foretold the disappearance of the dominions of Chosroes and Caesar, Turkish ascendancy, and other events.

The third kind includes the light transmitted through his ancestors, his birth circumcised and with the umbilical cord already severed, the seal of prophecy, seeing behind himself, and his consummate truthfulness, trustworthiness, chastity, courage, eloquence, generosity, renunciation, humility, compassion, patience, knowledge, noble character, and beneficial conduct; answered prayers; idols falling and the battlements of Persian royal palaces collapsing on his birth-night;

<a id="m-vi-p637"></a>

**Source p. 637**

clouds shading him, the moon splitting, trees uprooting themselves, stones greeting him, water flowing between his fingers, the palm-trunk lamenting, camels complaining, the poisoned sheep testifying, pebbles glorifying God, and almost innumerable other events.

Other arguments attest his prophecy to an impartial person. First: his combined intellectual, practical, psychic, bodily, and external perfections. Second: his law's provisions concerning beliefs, worship, transactions, governance, and other matters. Third: his religion's ascendancy over religions despite few supporters and abundant misguided aggressors. Fourth: his appearance after an interval without messengers, amid disordered religions, widespread error and falsehood, and need for someone to renew religion, repel unbelieving subverters, and raise the banner of the God-fearing; none else possessed this qualification. Fifth: texts of heavenly books. In the Torah: “God came from Mount Sinai, shone from Seir, and manifested Himself from the mountains of Paran.” In the Gospel:

<a id="m-vi-p639"></a>

**Source p. 639**

“I ask my Father and your Father to grant you a Paraclete to remain with you forever.” In the Psalms: “Gird on the sword, mighty one; your law and ordinances accompany the majesty of your right hand; your arrows are sharpened, and nations fall beneath you.”

Most deniers are ignorant or obstinate. The strongest argument of the others attacks abrogation generally and the abrogation of Moses's religion particularly. Against abrogation generally they offer two arguments. First: it is either without benefit, hence futile; for a benefit previously unknown, hence ignorance; or for one known but previously neglected and subsequently observed, hence a change of mind. We reply: it concerns a newly arising benefit. Second: the ruling is either time-limited, so its cessation afterward is not abrogation; perpetual, so abrogation contradicts it; or unrestricted, in which case God knows it either to continue forever, so it cannot cease, or to end at a limit, beyond which there is neither removal nor abrogation.

<a id="m-vi-p641"></a>

**Source p. 641**

We reply: the ruling is unrestricted concerning, for example, the obligation's duration or perpetuity, while what is known is the obligation's continuation until abrogation. There is no contradiction, even if the commanded performance is perpetual, as in “Fasting forever is obligatory.”

Against abrogation of Moses's religion they offer two arguments. First: perpetuity is recurrently transmitted, as “Keep the Sabbath forever.” We reply: this is fabricated; even if granted, it expresses a long duration. Second: if he expressly affirmed his law's perpetuity, the point is established; if its termination, this would be recurrently transmitted because motives to report it were abundant, but it is not; if silent on both, the law would neither recur nor remain established until abrogated, whereas it did. We reply: he declared termination, but it was not recurrently transmitted because motives and transmitters were few at each level; or he remained silent and the law continued through the presumption of continuity or recurrent causes.

### Fifth investigation

Texts and consensus establish that he is sent to all people, indeed both humans and jinn, not Arabs alone; that he is the Seal of Prophets, with no prophet after him and no abrogation of his law; and that he is the best prophet and his community the best community. Opinions differ about the next best: Adam, Abraham, Moses, or Jesus.

<a id="m-vi-p643"></a>

**Source p. 643**

Christians prefer Jesus to all: he is a holy spirit from God and a word conveyed to the lady of the world's women, purified of defilement; raised among prophets; speaking in the cradle; never without divine unity and revealed laws; indifferent to worldly adornments and pleasures; never seeking anyone's destruction; not dying but ascending to heaven; and distinguished by miracles such as reviving the dead.

We reply: better is one consummate in divine unity and knowledge, exemplary in goodness and perfections, despite birth among polytheist men and women and upbringing among them; one continually contemplating the holy presence while outwardly engaged in ordering servants' affairs here and hereafter and establishing truth and demolishing falsehood through struggle; and one distinguished by a miracle enduring through time and a manifest resting-place visited by pilgrims, through which blessings descend.

Conclusion: the Prophet's journey to the Furthest Mosque is established by the Book; its occurring bodily and while awake by the second generation's consensus; the ascent to heaven by widely transmitted reports; and the ascent to Paradise, the Throne, or the world's extremity by solitary report. Reports that ʿĀʾisha said his body was not absent, and Muʿāwiya called it a true dream, do not outweigh this. Had he claimed only a spiritual ascent or dream, unbelievers would not have opposed it so vehemently.

<a id="m-vi-p645"></a>

**Source p. 645**

### Sixth investigation

Prophets are protected from what contradicts the miracle's implication, such as lying in transmission, although the Qāḍī allows this inadvertently; from unbelief, which the Azāriqa allow insofar as they allow sin while regarding every sin as unbelief; from deliberate major sins—by revelation for us and reason for the Muʿtazila, although the Ḥashwiyya allow them; from repellent minor sins, and from deliberately committing nonrepellent minor sins, contrary to the Imām al-Ḥaramayn and Abū Hāshim. The preferred view also excludes inadvertent major sins.

Our argument is that, were they to sin, following them would become forbidden, their testimony rejected, rebuking them obligatory, and punishment and blame deserved. They would not attain the covenant of prophecy, be sincere or hasten toward good deeds, or number among the chosen and virtuous. These consequences are false. Whether some arguments establish protection from minor sins and inadvertent major sins requires examination.

Opponents invoke reports attributing disobedience and sin to prophets, and their repentance and requests for forgiveness. The general answer rejects solitary reports and interprets recurrently transmitted or scriptural reports as inadvertence, abandoning the preferable, events before commission, or similar matters. Details belong to the commentaries on scripture.

<a id="m-vi-p647"></a>

**Source p. 647**

Conclusion: prophecy requires maleness, complete reason, strong judgement, and freedom from repellent things: lowly fathers, unchaste mothers, harshness, such conditions as leucoderma and leprosy, lowly trades, and anything impairing dignified conduct or the mission's wisdom. A report numbers prophets at 124,000 and messengers at 313. Better, however, not to specify a number, lest prophecy be affirmed where absent or denied where present, and lest this conflict with “Among them are those We have related to you and those We have not related to you” [40:78].

### Seventh investigation

Angels are honoured servants, constant in obedience, appearing in different forms and capable of arduous acts. Although living bodies, they are described as neither male nor female. The community disagrees over their protection from sin and superiority to prophets. Those affirming protection invoke “They are not arrogant; they fear their Lord above them and do what they are commanded” [16:49–50], and “They glorify night and day without flagging” [21:20]. Opponents argue that Iblīs, although an angel, “refused, was arrogant, and became one of the unbelievers” [2:34]; that “Will You place therein one who will spread corruption?” [2:30] disparages the vicegerent, finds God's act improbable, and expresses self-admiration; and that Hārūt and Mārūt are punished for practising magic.

<a id="m-vi-p649"></a>

**Source p. 649**

The answers are that Iblīs belongs to the jinn and was included among angels by predominance; that disparagement and self-admiration require intending another's deficiency and one's own excellence, whereas the angels intended wonder and inquiry into the wisdom of appointing an apparently unsuitable vicegerent when suitable beings existed; and that Hārūt and Mārūt neither practised magic nor believed it effective. Magic was disclosed to them as a trial for people, and they taught while admonishing, “We are only a trial” [2:102]. Their punishment was reproach, as prophets are reproached for a lapse.

Those preferring prophets—the majority of our companions and the Shīʿa—argue: first, angels were commanded to prostrate to Adam as inferiors honouring a superior, not merely greeting a visitor. Iblīs's arrogance and claim to superiority through fire over clay show this. Second, Adam was commanded to teach them the names to manifest his superiority. Third, “God chose Adam, Noah, Abraham's family, and ʿImrān's family above all worlds” [3:33], including angels. Fourth, constant obedience despite distractions and acquiring perfection despite obstacles more strongly merit reward.

Opponents—the Muʿtazila, and among us the Qāḍī and al-Ḥalīmī—argue: first, verses affirm angels' nobility, proximity, honour, constant obedience, and freedom from arrogance.

<a id="m-vi-p651"></a>

**Source p. 651**

The answer is that these do not establish superiority. Second: “I do not tell you that God's treasuries are with me, nor do I know the unseen, nor do I tell you I am an angel” [6:50]. The answer is: I am not an angel with Gabriel's power to bring punishment by God's permission, or knowledge directly conveyed by God. Third: “Your Lord forbade you this tree only lest you become angels” [7:20]. Besides being Satan's deceptive suggestion, this establishes at most superiority to Adam before his commission. Fourth: “One mighty in power taught him” [53:5], meaning Gabriel, and the teacher is superior. The answer is that Gabriel conveys; teaching comes from God.

Fifth: “The Messiah will not disdain to be God's servant, nor the angels brought near” [4:172]. One says, “The prince does not disdain this, nor one above him,” not “nor one below him.” The answer is that this establishes superiority only in what is alleged to warrant disdain: Jesus's fatherless birth and healing the blind and leprous. Angels surpass him in these respects, having neither father nor mother and possessing powers he lacks. Sixth: angels are consistently mentioned before prophets.

<a id="m-vi-p653"></a>

**Source p. 653**

The answer is their earlier existence, or the greater strength involved in believing in them because their nature is hidden. Seventh: angels are intrinsically immaterial, connected to celestial forms, free of matter's darkness and of evil and ugliness, possessing actual intellectual and practical perfections, capable of marvellous acts, knowing unseen mysteries, and foremost in good deeds. The answer is that some claims rest on philosophical principles, some are shared, and some face counterarguments. Eighth: their acts are more numerous, enduring, and upright, and their knowledge more complete and extensive. The answer is that acts involving overcoming opposition and enduring hardship more strongly merit reward.

### Eighth investigation

A saint [walī] knows God and turns his aspiration away from everything else. A saintly wonder [karāma] is an extraordinary event appearing through him without a claim to prophecy. It is possible, even when intended by the saint and belonging to the same kind as prophetic miracles, because God's power is universal. It actually occurs, as with Mary, Āṣaf, the Companions of the Cave, and the recurrently attested general class of reports from Companions, Successors, and many righteous people.

The Muʿtazila disagree: wonders would confuse prophets with others, since miracles distinguish them; eliminate some customary regularities because saints are numerous; close the route to establishing prophecy,

<a id="m-vi-p655"></a>

**Source p. 655**

since an apparent miracle might honour rather than confirm; and diminish prophets' greatness through saints' sharing their distinction. The answers are that saintly wonders do not accompany prophetic claims; their abundance perpetuates the breaking of custom; conjunction with a prophetic claim customarily yields certainty of truthfulness; and saintly wonders increase prophets' greatness, since their followers attain them through the blessing of following them.

A strong objection to reporting unseen things is “Knower of the unseen: He discloses His unseen to none except a messenger He approves” [72:26–27]. The answer is that, even granting “unseen” a universal sense, context may restrict it to the Resurrection; the intention may merely deny universal disclosure; or disclosure may be restricted to revelation's particular mode.

Conclusion: no saint reaches a prophet's rank; perfect sainthood does not remove obligations; and a nonprophet's sainthood does not surpass prophecy. The discussion concerns a prophet's own sainthood. Some prefer it because of proximity and special intimacy; others prefer his prophecy because it mediates between God and creatures, serves both abodes' welfare, and includes the honour of beholding the angel.

### Ninth investigation

Magic manifests an extraordinary event through particular practices that can be taught and learned, assisted by the soul's wickedness and admitting successful opposition.

<a id="m-vi-p657"></a>

**Source p. 657**

It is rationally possible, like saintly wonders and prophetic miracles, and established by revelation: “They teach people magic” [2:102] and the related verses; and by established reports that the Prophet, ʿĀʾisha, and Ibn ʿUmar were bewitched. Unbelievers' false accusation that the Prophet was bewitched meant that magic had removed his reason. The protection promised in “God will protect you from people” [5:67] means protection from their destroying him or impairing his prophecy. A magician cannot inflict whatever harm he wishes on prophets, remove caliphs' dominion, or the like. “It appeared to him through their magic…” [20:66] does not establish that all magic is illusion and deception like sleight of hand, as the Muʿtazila hold.

The evil eye nearly has the status of an observed fact; concerning it was revealed “The unbelievers almost strike you down with their eyes” [68:51]. Those affirming magic and the evil eye disagree over employing protective recitations and formulas, hanging amulets, blowing, and wiping. This is a practical legal question.

## Chapter Two: The return after death

### First investigation

Restoring what has ceased to exist is possible, contrary to the philosophers universally, some Muʿtazilites concerning accidents, and others concerning nonpersistent accidents such as sounds.

<a id="m-vi-p659"></a>

**Source p. 659**

Our persuasive argument is that possibility is presumed until necessity or impossibility is proved. Our argument against the opponent's principles is that the restored is like the initially existent—indeed identical with it—so it cannot be possible at one time and impossible at another. It is even claimed that initial existence increases preparedness for receiving existence, as “It is easier for Him” [30:27] suggests; this requires examination. One cannot answer that some necessary concomitant makes restoration impossible, since it would then make initial existence impossible too.

Some deniers obstinately claim necessary knowledge; others argue as follows. First: restoration would interpose nonexistence between a thing and itself, necessarily false. The reply denies this: nonexistence intervenes between two times of the very same thing's existence, just as existence intervenes between two nonexistences of that thing. Second: if restoration with every individuating feature were possible, its first time could also be restored. It would then be initial precisely insofar as restored, combining opposites, denying its restoration—existence at a second time—and removing the distinction, since it would be restored only insofar as initial.

<a id="m-vi-p661"></a>

**Source p. 661**

The reply is that time is not an individuating feature. Even if it were, existence at the first time entails initial existence only if that time is not itself restored, or the existent is not preceded by another origination. Thus initial means occurring first, not occurring in a first time; restored means occurring second, not occurring in a second time. Third: restoration's possibility would allow an initially created thing alike in quiddity and every individuating feature, leaving two things indistinguishable. The reply is that indistinguishability in reality does not follow, and indistinguishability to the intellect is not impossible. Fourth: the nonexistent cannot be indicated and therefore cannot be judged. The reply, even granting its lack of establishedness, is that intellectual distinction and establishedness suffice, as in “The contingent nonexistent can exist.”

### Second investigation

People disagree over the return. Naturalists deny it, identifying the human being with this sensible frame, which perishes in its form and accidents and is not restored. Galen suspends judgement, uncertain whether soul is temperament or an abiding substance. Philosophers and adherents of revealed religions affirm the return: philosophers spiritually alone; most Muslims bodily alone, regarding spirit as a subtle body. Muslim investigators such as al-Ghazālī, al-Ḥalīmī, al-Rāghib, and the Qāḍī Abū Zayd affirm both spiritual and bodily return, holding the soul immaterial. Most Sufis, Shīʿa, and Karrāmiyya share this view.

<a id="m-vi-p663"></a>

**Source p. 663**

This is not transmigration, which is a worldly return to some body. Here it is a return in the hereafter to a body composed of the first body's original parts. Saying it is not numerically the first body does not impair the account, and may be supported by “Whenever their skins are roasted, We replace them with other skins” [4:56], “Is He who created the heavens and earth not able to create their like? Indeed” [36:81], and reports that Paradise's inhabitants are hairless and beardless and a person in Hell has a molar like Mount Uḥud.

Our proof is that a truthful informant reports something possible. Our Prophet's affirmation is recurrently transmitted, and revelation contains passages admitting no figurative reinterpretation: “He who first produced them will revive them” [36:79]; “They hasten from their graves to their Lord” [36:51]; “Does the human being think We shall not gather his bones?” [75:3]; “The day the earth splits from them as they hurry forth: that gathering is easy for Us” [50:44]; and others. Treating these as representations of spiritual return to encourage and frighten the public and complete social order attributes to prophets false transmission and intentional misguidance.

Deniers argue: first, bodily return depends on restoring the nonexistent, since temperament, life, composition, and configurations certainly perish, and restoration's impossibility has been shown. The reply denies both premises.

<a id="m-vi-p665"></a>

**Source p. 665**

Second: if one human eats another, the consumed parts are restored either in the eater, so the eaten person is not numerically restored, or conversely. Neither has priority, and the parts cannot belong to both. If an unbeliever eats a believer, disobedient parts would receive bliss and obedient parts punishment. The reply is that the original parts are restored, avoiding the difficulty. God may preserve them from becoming original parts of another body; the Muʿtazila indeed make this obligatory so that recompense reaches its rightful recipient.

If verses such as “Who will revive bones when decayed?” [36:78] and “When we have died and become dust…” [56:47] suggest that restoring all parts is disputed, we reply that they remove the incredulity concerning reviving decayed matter and dust. Many passages establish restoration itself: “He originates creation, then restores it” [30:27], and “They will say, ‘Who will restore us?’ Say: ‘He who first created you’” [17:51].

Third: restoration without purpose is futile; for a purpose benefiting God, deficient; benefiting the servant, it brings either pain, which is foolish, or pleasure. But no pleasure exists, especially in the sensible world, except escape from pain, and inflicting pain to provide subsequent escape does not befit wisdom.

<a id="m-vi-p667"></a>

**Source p. 667**

The reply denies the necessity of purpose; its restriction to these alternatives, since delivering recompense to its rightful recipient may be a purpose; and pleasure's being merely relief from pain, especially otherworldly pleasure.

Notice: once the soul's immateriality and persistence after bodily dissolution are established, spiritual return needs no further proof. It means either returning to its former condition—pure immateriality or freedom from attachment's darkness, enjoying or suffering what it acquired—or attaching to the resurrected body. Neither reason nor revelation supports another soul's attaching to that body while its own remains idle or attaches elsewhere.

### Third investigation

Those allowing bodily annihilation disagree whether it occurs through an annihilator's action, an arising contrary, or a condition's absence. For the first, the Qāḍī and some Muʿtazilites say God directly annihilates; Abū al-Hudhayl says through “Perish,” as existence occurs through “Be.” For the second, Ibn al-Ikhshīd says God creates annihilation in a particular direction, annihilating all substances. Ibn Shabīb says an annihilation is created in each substance, requiring its disappearance at the second time. Abū ʿAlī says annihilations numbering the substances are created without a substrate; Abū Hāshim says a single annihilation.

<a id="m-vi-p669"></a>

**Source p. 669**

For the third account, Bishr identifies the condition as persistence created without a substrate. Most of our companions identify it as persistence subsisting in body, created anew at every moment. The Imām al-Ḥaramayn identifies it as the accidents necessarily belonging to body; the Qāḍī, in one position, as spatial states continually created in it; al-Naẓẓām as body's creation itself, since body does not persist but is continually recreated.

### Fourth investigation

They disagree whether resurrection is existence after annihilation or gathering after dispersal. The truth is to suspend judgement. The former argue: first, consensus before opponents appeared; this is denied. Second, “He is the First and the Last” [57:3], conceivable only if creatures cease to exist. This does not occur after the Resurrection, by agreement, so must precede it. The answers are that He is origin and end; the sole deity; the one remaining after living beings die; or first in creating and last in providing. Third, “Everything is perishing except His face” [28:88] [28:88]. This cannot mean loss of usefulness, since indicating the Maker remains useful after dispersal. The answer is that contingency is itself perishability; so is losing the use for which a thing was created, although another use remains. Not every substance was created for inference.

<a id="m-vi-p671"></a>

**Source p. 671**

Fourth: “He originates creation, then restores it” [30:27], and “As We began the first creation, We shall restore it” [21:104]. Beginning is from nonexistence, so return is likewise. The answer is that creation's beginning need not be from nonexistence: “He began the creation of the human being from clay” [32:7]. Fifth: “Everyone upon it perishes” [55:26]. The answer is that perishing may mean losing intended usefulness, as provisions or food perish and war destroys people.

The others argue: first, what is restored after nonexistence is not numerically the initial existent, so recompense would not reach its rightful recipient. You know this argument's weakness. Second, the Muʿtazila argue that annihilation has no conceivable purpose, benefiting no one and not constituting recompense. The answer allows gracious assistance for the accountable, manifestation of majesty and independence, and exclusive permanence and persistence. Third, verses suggest resurrection through revival after death and gathering after dispersal: “Show me how You revive the dead” [2:260], “How will God revive this after its death?” [2:259], “Thus is resurrection” [35:9], and “Thus will you be brought forth” [30:19]. The answer is that these at most fail to indicate annihilation, since they explain revival and gathering; moreover, the preceding verses suggesting annihilation counter them.

<a id="m-vi-p673"></a>

**Source p. 673**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.16](#c1-16).*

### Fifth investigation

Paradise and Hell are already created, contrary to some Muʿtazilites. Our proofs are Adam and Eve's story and texts such as “Prepared for the God-fearing” [3:133], “Prepared for unbelievers” [2:24], and “Paradise was brought near to the God-fearing, and Hell displayed to the erring” [26:90–91]. Figurative interpretation departs from the apparent sense without evidence.

Deniers argue: first, creating them before recompense is futile, plainly weak. Second, if created they would perish, according to “Everything is perishing except His face,” contrary to text and consensus. We reply: they are excepted from the general expression; perishing means something other than annihilation; or they perish momentarily, which does not contradict permanence in customary usage.

Third: if existent, they are either in this world or another. They cannot be in its spheres, since rending, ascent, and descent are impossible, nor its elements, since these cannot contain a Paradise as broad as heaven and the soul's return to an elemental body would be transmigration. Another world is impossible: requiring determinate directions, it would be spherical, leaving a void between worlds; and its elements would have natural loci, giving one element two natural loci and requiring inclination toward and away from one.

<a id="m-vi-p675"></a>

**Source p. 675**

We reply: most premises are philosophical. Moreover, both worlds could lie within something encompassing them, like two epicycles in a sphere; their elements could have different natures; or their spatial occupation in one world could be nonnatural. Transmigration is the soul's attachment to another body in this world.

Conclusion: Paradise's and Hell's locations are not certain. Most place Paradise above the seven heavens and below the Throne, invoking “At the Lote Tree of the Boundary, near it is the Garden of Refuge” [53:14–15], and “Paradise's roof is the Throne of the All-Merciful”; and Hell beneath the earths. The truth is to suspend judgement.

### Sixth investigation

Questioning and punishment in the grave are real. Proofs include “The Fire, to which they are exposed morning and evening” [40:46]; “They were drowned and admitted into a fire” [71:25]; “Our Lord, You caused us to die twice and revived us twice” [40:11] [40:11], the second revival being in the grave; “Provided for, rejoicing in what God has granted them” [3:169–170]; and “The grave is a garden of Paradise or a pit of Hell.” Reports in this matter are recurrently transmitted in meaning.

Deniers invoke revelation and reason. Their scriptural proofs include “They taste no death therein except the first death” [44:56]. Life in the grave, necessarily followed by death, would entail two deaths before Paradise;

<a id="m-vi-p677"></a>

**Source p. 677**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.17](#c1-17).*

“You were dead, and He revived you, then causes you to die, then revives you” [2:28]; and “You caused us to die twice and revived us twice.” Revival in the grave would make three revivals: worldly, in the grave, and at resurrection. The answer is that affirming one or two does not exclude a second or third. “Then revives you” apparently refers to the hereafter, omitting the grave because its hidden reality and limited manifestation do not suit an appeal encouraging belief and expressing wonder at unbelief. The two revivals in their reported words concern this world and the grave, omitting the hereafter because witnessed; or the grave and resurrection, meaning revivals followed by necessary knowledge of God and acknowledgement of sins.

Rationally, pleasure, pain, conversation, and similar things depend on life, which depends on constitution and temperament. A corpse may remain observed without movement or speech, be buried too narrowly to sit, or be burned and its ashes scattered. Allowing its life and punishment is no less remote than allowing the bier's life, speech, and punishment. The answer is that improbability has no weight against a truthful report. Even if life requires constitution, sufficient parts may remain; questioning and punishment may concern the spirit or original parts, unseen by an observer; and the freely choosing powerful agent may widen the grave enough for sitting.

<a id="m-vi-p679"></a>

**Source p. 679**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.17](#c1-17).*

Conclusion: religion necessarily establishes that the dead person has a kind of life in the grave sufficient for pain and pleasure. Whether the spirit is restored remains uncertain; life's impossibility without spirit is denied.

### Seventh investigation

Everything else reported in Book and Sunna concerning reckoning and its terrors, the Bridge, Balance, Pool, and details of the gathering and Fire consists of possible things reported by the truthful informant and must be affirmed.

Some Muʿtazilites deny the Bridge and Balance as described: crossing something finer than hair and sharper than a sword, if possible, is torment; and acts are accidents, whose weighing is unintelligible. They interpret the Bridge as the path to Paradise or Hell, clear proofs, acts of worship, or revealed law; the Balance as justice established in everything, or apprehension, as senses apprehend sensibles and knowledge intelligibles. The answer is that God may ease the crossing, some passing like lightning and others eventually falling face-down. Acts' records are weighed, or good deeds are made luminous bodies and evil deeds dark bodies.

### Eighth investigation

Investigators among the philosophers hold that revelation's detailed descriptions of Paradise, Hell, reward, and punishment represent souls' ranks and states in blessedness and misery, pleasure and pain. Souls do not perish, but remain enjoying their perfections—their reward and gardens—or suffering their deficiencies—their punishment and fires.

<a id="m-vi-p681"></a>

**Source p. 681**

Their worldly failure to notice this results from attachments and obstacles removed by separation. Their misery need not be everlasting: they may progress from depths of misery to degrees of happiness. Everlasting misery is firmly established compound ignorance and wickedness opposed to virtuous disposition.

In detail, the soul lacks perfection through a privative factor, such as deficient natural capacity, or through a positive factor, entrenched or otherwise; each concerns theoretical or practical power. Deficient natural capacity incurs no punishment. An entrenched contrary in theoretical power, such as compound ignorance, incurs lasting punishment. The remaining three cases disappear after punishment varying qualitatively and quantitatively with the contrary dispositions' severity and speed of removal. A soul lacking perfection, longing for it, and its contrary lies within God's spacious mercy.

Some disallow its remaining without apprehension and require attachment to another body, either as its governing soul—transmigration—or to employ it for imagination, imagining and enjoying forms previously possessed. This body would not have a temperament requiring another soul's emanation, but would be celestial, aerial, or similar. Some do not find bodily resurrection implausible: glad tidings and warnings clearly benefit social order, and fulfilling them by rewarding obedience and punishing disobedience increases benefit for most, although harming the punished.

<a id="m-vi-p683"></a>

**Source p. 683**

### Ninth investigation

Reward is bounty and punishment justice. Neither is obligatory upon God except in the sense that He promises and threatens and does not fail, with disagreement concerning threats. Servants deserve them only in the sense that they follow acts and omissions and are appropriately attributed to them in rational judgement. Basran Muʿtazilites and many Baghdadians agree with us.

Our arguments are: first, nothing is obligatory upon Him, as explained. Second, even abundant obedience cannot repay gratitude for some blessings, so it merits no compensation. If it is said that requiring gratitude for beneficence is rationally ugly, gratitude is possible without hardship, and hardships therefore require compensation lest they be futile, we reply—even granting rational goodness and ugliness and necessary purpose—that what is ugly is beneficence performed for gratitude, not requiring gratitude for beneficence. Even if compensation must be the purpose, graciously given compensation suffices.

Third: if deserved as an obligation, reward or punishment would not fall away from someone obedient throughout life who then disbelieves, or disobedient throughout life who then believes. If dying obedient or disobedient conditions desert, desert would never arise, because cause and condition do not coexist.

<a id="m-vi-p685"></a>

**Source p. 685**

Opponents argue: first, imposing hardships without corresponding benefit—reward—is unjust; without harm for omitting them—punishment—it entails that supererogatory acts are obligatory, since performing them brings benefit. The reply, even granting necessary purpose, allows gratitude for blessings or pleasure in praise for fulfilling duty as the purpose. An act may be obligatory because it has a ground of obligation with hardship, or be made arduous for another purpose. Second, denying obligatory reward and punishment encourages negligence and wrongdoing. The reply is that mere possibility of omission does not impair the aim, given comprehensive promises and threats and abundant texts affirming actual recompense. Third, their nonobligation entails broken promises and falsehood in truthful reports. The reply is that actual occurrence does not entail obligation and desert.

Conclusion: Muʿtazilites disagree whether omitting an ugly act merits reward and omitting an obligation punishment. Earlier scholars deny it, since nonexistence cannot cause anything, and every moment includes omission of innumerable ugly acts.

<a id="m-vi-p687"></a>

**Source p. 687**

Later scholars affirm it, invoking “He did not believe in God the Magnificent” [69:33] and “We were not among those who prayed, nor did we feed the needy” [74:43–44]. They also require reward to include honour, punishment humiliation, both everlasting and unmixed with their opposites. Deserving honour and humiliation is necessarily known; benefits may be graciously given initially, so imposing hardships for them would be futile, unlike honour, which is not good without desert; everlastingness is gracious assistance and therefore obligatory; and unmixed recompense more effectively encourages and warns.

They disagree when desert arises: at obedience or disobedience; in the hereafter; at death; or at the act, conditioned on maintaining one's state until death without its being cancelled.

### Tenth investigation

There is no disagreement that those entering Paradise remain forever, nor that an unbeliever through obstinacy or conviction remains forever in Hell, even after strenuous inquiry, since the general texts include him. The dissent of al-Jāḥiẓ and al-ʿAnbarī carries no weight. The same applies to one classed as an unbeliever juridically, such as polytheists' children, contrary to the Muʿtazila, who call their punishment unjust and make them servants of Paradise's inhabitants. Another view places in Paradise those whom God knows would believe and obey upon reaching maturity, and in Hell those whom He knows would disbelieve and disobey.

<a id="m-vi-p689"></a>

**Source p. 689**

For a believer committing a major sin and dying unrepentant, our doctrine affirms neither pardon nor punishment certainly: God may pardon or punish as He wills, but the person does not remain in Hell forever. The Muʿtazila affirm everlasting punishment. No weight belongs to Muqātil's and some Murjiʾites' view that sinful believers are never punished and the Fire is solely for unbelievers.

Our proofs are: first, texts affirming believers' admission to Paradise, which by agreement is not before admission to Hell, but afterward or without it. Second, texts affirming their emergence from Hell. Third, if everlasting punishment for someone obedient a hundred years who drinks one mouthful of wine is not injustice on your account, nothing is. Fourth, sin is finite in duration and measure, so just recompense is likewise. Fifth, the entitlement to reward, by promise or reason, is not removed by a major sin, as will follow, and can be realised only by leaving Hell.

The Muʿtazila invoke general threats of everlasting punishment: “Whoever disobeys God and His Messenger has Hell's fire, remaining therein” [72:23]; “Whoever deliberately kills a believer, his recompense is Hell, remaining therein” [4:93]; “Those who transgress have the Fire as refuge; whenever they desire to leave, they are returned” [32:20]; “The wicked are in Hell, entering it on Judgement Day and never absent from it” [82:14–16]; “Whoever disobeys God and His Messenger and transgresses His limits, He admits him to a Fire, remaining therein” [4:14]; and “Whoever earns evil and is encompassed by his offence: those are the Fire's inhabitants, remaining therein” [2:81].

<a id="m-vi-p691"></a>

**Source p. 691**

The reply, even granting the expressions' generality, is that repentant people and minor sinners are excepted from the first, making it no longer certain by agreement; major sinners may likewise be excepted. Moreover, the entitlement there is bounded by seeing punishment: “Until they see what they are promised” [72:24]. Even granting entitlement to everlasting punishment, actual occurrence does not follow. “Deliberately” may mean deeming the killing lawful, as Ibn ʿAbbās explains; or permanence may mean a long stay, reconciling the proofs. “Those who transgress” means unbelievers denying resurrection, indicated by “Taste the Fire's punishment which you denied” [34:42]. The remaining texts are restricted to unbelievers to reconcile the evidence; or absence is not universally denied, or prolonged residence is emphasised. Permanence is similarly understood; or the transgression concerns Islam's boundaries and the offence encompasses the person so that no faith remains.

Second: a sinner entering Paradise must deserve it, but desert has been cancelled or counterbalanced, as will follow. Both premises are denied. Third: if a sinner's punishment ends, an unbeliever's must too, since both sins are finite. The reply denies finitude's causal role, unbelief's finitude in measure, and analogy's validity against text or in matters of belief. Fourth: threatening permanent punishment is more deterrent gracious assistance and therefore obligatory and irrevocable. Even granting obligatory assistance, finite punishment also assists: let it concern believers and everlasting punishment unbelievers, since maximal assistance is not obligatory for everyone.

<a id="m-vi-p693"></a>

**Source p. 693**

### Eleventh investigation

A believer mixing good and evil deeds reaches Paradise, for us, even after Hell. For the Muʿtazila he remains in Hell forever, because evil deeds cancel good deeds. Most hold that a single major sin cancels all obedience. This is false scripturally, since texts affirm that God does not waste benefactors' reward; and rationally, since cancelling a hundred years' obedience for a mouthful of wine is certainly ugly, the ground of desert—an act's being good and compliant—remains on their account, and their position makes a major sin incompatible with valid obedience, like apostasy.

They say reward is lasting, unmixed benefit with honour, punishment lasting, unmixed harm with humiliation, so entitlement to both cannot coexist. We reply: even granting purity and permanence, competing entitlements are not excluded. Even if they were, cancelling goodness through evil has no priority over the reverse, especially since “Good deeds remove evil deeds” [11:114].

The two Jubbāʾīs hold that whichever predominates in measure of reward or guilt, not number, cancels the other. Abū ʿAlī says the lesser disappears without diminishing the greater: pure cancellation. Abū Hāshim says it disappears while subtracting its equivalent from the greater: counterbalancing. They disagree whether this concerns the acts, the deserved reward and punishment, or the entitlements themselves.

<a id="m-vi-p695"></a>

**Source p. 695**

They support cancellation generally with “Lest your deeds be nullified” [49:2], “Their deeds are nullified” [9:17], and “Do not invalidate your charities through reproach and injury” [2:264]. These do not establish the disputed cancellation of a complete good deed by an earlier or later evil, much less the detailed comparison.

The Imām refutes Abū ʿAlī's account because prior obedience becomes ineffective, unjust by their principles and excluded by “Whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it” [99:7], besides involving predominance without a determinant. Against Abū Hāshim: the new event requires the previous one's disappearance, so causing that disappearance produces circularity; no parts of the greater quantity have priority, so all must disappear; and mutual simultaneous removal requires both to exist while nonexistent, since the cause exists when its effect arises, whereas successive removal produces an effect without its cause, the second disappearing without an annihilator.

The objection is that entitlement is a revealed legal consideration, without real causal action or passion; reward and punishment exist only hereafter; and one act's annihilation by another is inconceivable. Cancellation means God does not reward a sinner's obedience; counterbalancing means He withholds its reward and omits an equivalent punishment. The Imām al-Ḥaramayn says no major sin's guilt exceeds the reward of knowing God, so their principles require that knowledge to avert all major sins.

<a id="m-vi-p697"></a>

**Source p. 697**

### Twelfth investigation

The community agrees on pardon for minor sins without qualification and major sins after repentance, and on no pardon for unbelief, with disagreement over rational possibility. Concerning major sins without repentance, our companions allow and affirm pardon; the Muʿtazila deny it scripturally, though most allow it rationally.

For possibility, punishment is His right and He may relinquish it. For occurrence, texts state “He pardons evil deeds” [42:25], “He pardons much” [42:30], “God forgives all sins” [39:53], and “God does not forgive association with Him, but forgives what is below that for whom He wills” [4:48], besides many reports. Restricting these to minor sins or repentance, or interpreting them as delayed punishment, absence of prescribed penalties for most sins, or withholding burdens and worldly disgrace, departs from the apparent sense without evidence, contradicts exegetes and explicit sound reports, and fails for some passages. Forgiveness through repentance is not restricted below association, does not fit qualification by will, and the other meanings do not fit exclusion of association.

Those denying rational possibility argue that pardon encourages ugliness. Even granting their principle, encouragement is denied: possible punishment deters, especially when probable and supported by texts.

<a id="m-vi-p699"></a>

**Source p. 699**

Scripturally they invoke threats against sinners, since failure and falsehood are unanimously deficiencies. The reply is that sinners also fall within general promises. Breaking a promise is unanimously false, whereas failing to execute a threat is generosity, which some allow.

The difficulty of falsehood and alteration of speech nevertheless remains. Better to except these people from the expression's general scope: this is specification, not abrogation of a report. Claiming that falsehood cannot concern the future is extremely weak. So is saying that, for us, His speech's truthfulness is beginningless and unchanging, while for opponents falsehood is excluded only by ugliness and is not ugly here because pardon depends upon it, like someone announcing he will kill Zayd tomorrow and refraining. Beginningless truthfulness would instead require withholding pardon; permitting falsehood in divine reports produces innumerable corrupt consequences.

Conclusion: the Muʿtazila are known for affirming sinners' everlasting punishment and calling suspension of certainty about punishment *irjāʾ*. This should concern only some of them. Most prefer the view that major sins cancel obedience only when their punishment exceeds its reward, which God alone knows. They disagree over equality, and explicitly allow pardon rationally and scripturally among Basrans and some Baghdadians, and rationally among those other than al-Kaʿbī.

<a id="m-vi-p701"></a>

**Source p. 701**

### Thirteenth investigation

For us, intercession may remove major sinners' sins, as shown by the preceding proofs of pardon and recurrently transmitted reports that intercession is reserved for major sinners. One may also invoke “Seek forgiveness for your sin and for the believers” [47:19], meaning their sins; and that intercession is established by text and consensus. It does not properly mean requesting benefits, as the Muʿtazila hold, or we would intercede for the Prophet when asking God to increase his honour. Rather, it removes harms. On their account repentance leaves no punishment and avoidance of major sins leaves no minor sins, so intercession must remove major sins.

They invoke: first, general denials, “No intercession is accepted from it” [2:48], “Intercessors' intercession does not benefit them” [74:48], “Before a day without trading, friendship, or intercession” [2:254], “Wrongdoers have neither intimate friend nor heeded intercessor” [40:18], and “Wrongdoers have no helpers” [3:192]. Even granting universal times and states, these are restricted to unbelievers to reconcile proofs; unqualified wrongdoing means unbelief, and denying helpers does not deny intercessors. Second, verses excluding intercession for major sinners: “They intercede only for one He approves” [21:28], and “Forgive those who repent” [40:7]. The answer is that a sinner is approved in respect of faith; repentance here is from association, since requesting forgiveness for someone repentant and righteous would, on their principles, be futile or merely request omission of injustice.

<a id="m-vi-p703"></a>

**Source p. 703**

Third: verses of sinners' everlasting punishment, already answered. Fourth: consensus allows the prayer “God, make us among those receiving Muḥammad's intercession.” The answer is that qualification for his intercession in the event of sin derives from obedience and faith.

Conclusion: a major sin is disobedience indicating little concern for religion; alternatively, one individually threatened by the Lawgiver; or association, murder, slanderous accusation of unchastity, fornication, flight from battle, magic, consuming orphans' property, disobedience to parents, and sacrilege in the Sanctuary. Usury, theft, and wine-drinking are sometimes added.

### Fourteenth investigation

Repentance is remorse for disobedience because it is disobedience. Whether remorse from fear of Hell, desire for Paradise, ugliness combined with another purpose, or during dangerous illness qualifies is disputed. Some add resolve to refrain in future, qualified by remembering and possessing capacity; thus resolve is not required if capacity is removed. Apparently this explains rather than restricts the definition. Remorse means sorrow, grief, and wishing one had not acted, marked by prolonged regret and weeping.

The Muʿtazila require only believing one did wrong and would undo it if possible, since Paradise's inhabitants regret shortcomings without grief, and sinners must repent continually although they may be unable to produce grief.

<a id="m-vi-p705"></a>

**Source p. 705**

For us repentance is scripturally obligatory: “Repent to God, all of you” [24:31]. For the Muʿtazila it is rationally obligatory because it averts harm. It is immediately obligatory, so omission brings successive sins.

Its acceptance is established for us by probable evidence; for the Muʿtazila it is obligatory, since punishment after repentance is injustice. Thorough apology supposedly removes blame, and continuing obligation offers reward, conceivable only if punishment is removed, for which repentance alone provides a route. The argument's weakness is evident. Most Muʿtazilites make repentance itself remove punishment; some, its abundant reward; we, sheer generosity. Valid repentance is worship whose reward is not cancelled by returning to the sin; renewed repentance is another worship.

It need not be renewed whenever the sin is remembered, contrary to the Qāḍī and al-Jubbāʾī, nor encompass every sin to be valid. Consensus accepts conversion despite persistence in some sins, and repentance's reality—return, remorse, and resolve—is present. Abū Hāshim says remorse must concern ugliness, common to all sins. The reply is that ugliness generally is common, not this sin's ugliness. General repentance suffices even when sins are individually known, contrary to some Muʿtazilites.

<a id="m-vi-p707"></a>

**Source p. 707**

They say that, concerning God's rights, remorse may suffice, as for fleeing battle or omitting enjoining good; sometimes something additional is needed, as for drinking or neglecting prayer and alms. Concerning servants' rights, one must restore the right or its equivalent for injustice such as usurpation or killing, guide someone one misled, and apologise for injury such as backbiting. Precisely, the additional act is a separate obligation, although remorse may be invalid without it, as with restoring usurped property.

### Fifteenth investigation

Book, Sunna, and consensus agree that enjoining right and forbidding wrong are obligatory. “Right” here means obligatory and “wrong” prohibited; encouraging recommended acts and discouraging disapproved acts is recommended, not obligatory. “Attend to yourselves; those astray cannot harm you when you are guided” [5:105] means rectify yourselves through duties, avoiding sins, and enjoining right and forbidding wrong: after admonition, others' obstinacy does not harm you. “No compulsion in religion” [2:256] is abrogated by the fighting verses. The Prophet's permission to omit admonition concerns absent conditions: knowing the act's moral status, allowing the possibility of effect, and absence of harmful consequences.

The duty is not restricted to rulers unless it reaches fighting, to independent jurists unless its basis requires independent judgement, or to those not committing the same sin, since the two obligations differ. Some performing it discharge the others, since it is collective; appointment, as of a public inspector, makes it individual. Whether a jurist may admonish another in a disputed matter is disputed.

## Chapter Three: Designations and judgements

<a id="m-vi-p709"></a>

**Source p. 709**

### First investigation

Faith [īmān] linguistically means assent [taṣdīq], a fourth-form derivation from security, conveying becoming secure or making secure. It takes the prepositions *bi-* and *li-* with acknowledgement and submission in view. Since it ultimately means taking something as true, and truth qualifies speaker, speech, and judgement, faith relates to things under different considerations: God, angels, the Book, messenger, Last Day, and determination.

In revealed usage it is assigned either to the heart's act alone, the tongue's alone, both alone, or both together with the other limbs. On the first, most identify it as assenting to the Prophet concerning what is necessarily known to have come through him; the Shīʿa, Jahm, and al-Ṣāliḥī identify it as knowledge. On the second, it is confession, conditioned by knowledge for al-Raqāshī, assent for al-Qaṭṭān, and unconditioned for the Karrāmiyya. On the third, it combines assent and confession, the position of most investigators, although their expressions often substitute knowledge, cognition, or belief for assent. On the fourth, it is confession by tongue, assent by heart, and action by limbs. Those omitting action are either excluded from faith and included in unbelief, as the Khawārij hold; or excluded without becoming unbelievers, as the Muʿtazila hold, differing over whether acts mean duties and avoidance of prohibitions or obedient acts generally.

<a id="m-vi-p711"></a>

**Source p. 711**

Alternatively, omission does not exclude one from faith, as most early authorities hold and is reported from Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī. “Faith” may name either salvation's foundation or complete faith indisputably bringing salvation. Otherwise a thing's disappearance when a constituent disappears is necessary.

We establish several points. First, faith is the heart's act: “He inscribed faith in their hearts” [58:22], “His heart remains assured in faith” [16:106], “Their hearts have not believed” [5:41], and “Faith has not yet entered your hearts” [49:14]; and the reports “God, establish my heart upon Your religion” and “Whoever has in his heart even a mustard-seed's weight of faith…” Opponents invoke “God rewarded them for what they said” [5:85] and the customary acceptance of the two testimonies alone. We reply: reward concerns the meaning uttered or the utterance indicating it. Acceptance concerns worldly judgement, protecting life and property. Hence the report beginning “I have been commanded to fight the people…”

Second, revealed usage has not transferred faith from assent to another meaning. Such transfer conflicts with the presumption, and Arabs complied without inquiry or hesitation except concerning what must be believed, explained in “Faith is to believe in God…”

<a id="m-vi-p713"></a>

**Source p. 713**

At most, faith is restricted to assenting to particular matters. Its meaning is expressed in Persian as *giravīdan*, believing, and *rāst-gūy dāshtan*, holding someone truthful. Its opposite is denial, and hesitation conflicts with it. It differs from knowledge and recognition, since some unbelievers knew without assenting: “They recognise him as they recognise their sons” [2:146], “Those given the Book know it is truth” [2:144], and “They denied them although their souls were certain” [27:14]. Assent's opposite is rejection and denial; recognition's, nonrecognition and ignorance. Hence assent is explained as submission and conversely. Assent is the heart's commitment to what it knows of an informant's report, acquired and voluntary, hence commanded and rewarded; recognition may occur without acquisition.

Some go further: faith requires voluntary assent, voluntarily attributing truth to the speaker, unlike the assent opposed to conception in logic, which may be involuntary and therefore neither linguistic assent nor religious faith. Commanded assent must be a voluntary act of establishing the relation, whereas knowledge is a psychic quality or a reception. This account vacillates between making religious assent a species of logical assent qualified by choice and excluding it from knowledge altogether because it is voluntary action whereas knowledge is quality or reception. The latter is affirmed by those saying the submission explaining assent lies beyond knowledge, belonging to inward speech, as the Imām al-Ḥaramayn and others state, expressed by *giravīdan* and *gardan dādan*, yielding submission.

<a id="m-vi-p715"></a>

**Source p. 715**

We doubt neither that mere knowledge and recognition without assent expressed by *giravīdan* are insufficient, nor that acquisition and choice are required. But consider these points. First, requiring a commanded thing to be voluntary does not necessarily place it in the category of action. Rather, power must relate to it and it must be voluntarily acquired, whether intrinsically a position, such as standing or sitting; a quality, such as knowledge or investigation—“Know that there is no god but God” [47:19]; a reception, such as becoming hot or cold; or something else, such as fasting or prayer.

Second, authoritative logicians explicitly identify the assent into which knowledge divides alongside conception with linguistic assent expressed by *giravīdan*, opposed to denial, and occurring voluntarily or involuntarily. One cannot categorically say faith requires linguistic rather than logical assent. At most its occurrence must involve choice and undertaking the causes, as with commanded matters generally.

If it is said that certainty without submission, as with sophists and some unbelievers, would then be conception or an intermediate rather than assent, we reply: yes—if certainty without submission is established, and some unbelievers are certain of everything the Prophet brought without assenting, and their unbelief results from absent assent rather than assent's being disregarded because signs of rejection appear, such as refusing confession or acceptance of rulings. Compare someone who assents yet throws the codex into filth or prostrates to an idol.

<a id="m-vi-p717"></a>

**Source p. 717**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.18](#c1-18).*

Third: taking choice to be part of the concept of assent in ordinary language, and making assent an act and an influence exerted by the soul rather than a quality that sometimes arises necessarily and sometimes through acquisition and choice, is manifestly unsound. Moreover, it would entail that one could truly be described as assenting only while actually performing and acquiring the act, as the meaning of the category [of acting] requires.

Fourth: the eminent Companions and many scholars commonly express the assent required for faith by the words knowledge, recognition, belief, and submission. Their treating faith as obtained sometimes through proof and sometimes through imitation is the clearest evidence that it belongs to the genus of recognition and belief. This is compatible with requiring its acquisition by choice, without denial or arrogance. Thus, saying that it is not recognition means recognition devoid of yielding assent and voluntary acquisition. Saying that it belongs to the genus of inward speech, which differs from knowledge, means that it need not be knowledge: everything that occurs in the soul, considered insofar as it is expressed by utterance, writing, or gesture, is inward speech, whether it is in itself knowledge, will, a request, a report, or something else. Inward speech is not a separate genus or species that would remove the question of which kind of accident faith and assent belong to.

Fifth: the account given entails that the angels’ assent to what is imparted to them, the prophets’ assent to what is revealed to them, and the utterly truthful saints’ assent to what they hear from the Prophet or what occurs in their hearts when they witness a miracle must have been voluntarily acquired in order to constitute faith. Otherwise, they would still be obliged to acquire that assent by choice.

<a id="m-vi-p719"></a>

**Source p. 719**

The third point: works do not enter into the reality of faith. It has been established that faith is a name for assent and that the name has not been transferred [to a different meaning]. Faith is of no benefit when punishment is directly witnessed, even though there is then no work. A believer can be commanded and prohibited, as in: “You who believe, fasting has been prescribed for you” (Q 2:183), and: “You who believe, do not put yourselves forward before God and His Messenger” (Q 49:1). Texts also indicate that faith and works are two things that can accompany one another, as in: “Those who believe and perform righteous works” (Q 13:29), and can be separated, as in: “If two groups of believers fight one another” (Q 49:9). There is also consensus that faith is a condition of worship, and that someone who assents and professes, then dies before performing any work, is a believer.

The Muʿtazilites say:

We do not deny that faith is applied to assent to the specified matters. We maintain, however, that its meaning has been transferred to works, for several reasons.

First: works are religion, because God says: “That is the upright religion” (Q 98:5), referring to the preceding mention of maintaining prayer and the other acts. The religion that counts is Islam, because He says: “Religion with God is Islam” (Q 3:19). Islam is faith, as will be explained.

The reply is that “that” may refer to sincerity, or to the adoption of religion and submission; and the meaning may be that the religion accepted by God is the religion of Islam. We shall discuss the identification of Islam with faith.

<a id="m-vi-p721"></a>

**Source p. 721**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.19](#c1-19).*

Second: “The believers are only those whose hearts tremble when God is mentioned” (Q 8:2).

We say: complete believers are meant.

Third: “God would not let your faith go to waste” (Q 2:143), meaning your prayer toward Jerusalem.

We say: this is figurative usage, or what is meant is your assent to its obligatory character.

Fourth: the highway robber is disgraced because he enters the Fire, in accordance with God’s saying: “In the hereafter they shall have the punishment of the Fire” (Q 59:3). Everyone who enters the Fire is disgraced, because God reports the words: “Our Lord, whomsoever You admit to the Fire, You have disgraced” (Q 3:192). A believer is not disgraced, because God says: “On the day when God will not disgrace the Prophet and those who believed with him” (Q 66:8).

The reply denies the major premise [that a believer is not disgraced], for “those who believed with him” are the Companions.

Fifth: the Prophet’s saying, God bless him and grant him peace: “The adulterer does not commit adultery while a believer; the thief does not steal while a believer.”

We say: this is an emphatic expression of the offence’s gravity.

Sixth: if faith were mere assent, no act or utterance would make someone an unbeliever.

We say: the Lawgiver may designate certain sins as signs of denial, such as prostrating oneself before an idol.

Seventh: assent can be affirmed while faith in its revealed sense is denied: “Most of them do not believe in God without associating others with Him” (Q 12:106); and: “Among people are those who say, ‘We believe in God and the Last Day,’ although they are not believers” (Q 2:8).

<a id="m-vi-p723"></a>

**Source p. 723**

We say: the first is assent to God alone, and the second is only with the tongue.

Eighth: faith conveys entitlement to the highest praise, as is suggested by God’s saying: “He was among Our believing servants” (Q 37:81). It is therefore incompatible with entitlement to blame, as in the case of a major sin.

We say: the sinner deserves each in a different respect. The highest praise belongs only to one whose faith is complete.

Conclusion: for us, a perpetrator of a major sin is a believer; for the Muʿtazilites, neither a believer nor an unbeliever; for the Khārijites, an unbeliever; and for al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī, a hypocrite.

One of the Muʿtazilites’ specious arguments is that their position adopts what is agreed upon—sinfulness—and abandons what is disputed—faith and unbelief. Its invalidity is plain.

Another is that such a person has some of the believer’s rulings, such as protection of life and property, and some of the unbeliever’s rulings, such as blame and disqualification from the imamate, judgeship, and testimony. He therefore has a position between the two positions and a name between the two names.

We say: those latter rulings do not belong exclusively to unbelief. To say, “He is not a believer in the sense of deserving the highest praise and honour,” is to withdraw from their doctrine.

The Khārijites appeal to texts declaring sinners unbelievers; restricting punishment to unbelievers, although the sinful person is punished; describing the sinful person as denying the resurrection and God’s signs; and making the counterpart of the God-fearing person an unbeliever. Examples are God’s sayings: “Those who do not judge according to what God has sent down—they are the unbelievers” (Q 5:44); “Whoever disbelieves—God has no need of the worlds” (Q 3:97); and: “Punishment falls upon those who deny and turn away” (Q 20:48);

<a id="m-vi-p725"></a>

**Source p. 725**

“None shall enter it but the most wretched, who denied and turned away” (Q 92:15–16); “As for those who acted sinfully, their refuge is the Fire,” down to: “which you used to deny” (Q 32:20); “Those who disbelieve in Our signs are the companions of the left” (Q 90:19); and: “Those who disbelieved shall be driven to Hell in groups,” down to: “Those who were God-fearing shall be driven” (Q 39:71–73).

The reply rebuts the inference by restricting the texts’ scope, taking their wording as an emphatic expression of gravity, understanding an unqualified expression as referring to completeness, and similar considerations.

Those who call the major sinner a hypocrite argue that his disobedience proves that his claim to assent is false, and that the Prophet, God Most High bless him and grant him peace, made lying, betrayal, and breaking promises signs of hypocrisy.

The reply denies the first claim and understands the second as emphasising the enormity of those sins.

<a id="m-vi-p727"></a>

**Source p. 727**

### Investigation two: faith and Islam

The majority hold that faith and Islam are one, in the sense that both resolve into acceptance and yielding assent, and that every believer is a Muslim and conversely, with respect to designation, ruling, and [final] abode. They appeal to consensus on this and to the testimony of the texts. These include: “Whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will not be accepted from him” (Q 3:85), although faith is admittedly accepted; God’s saying: “We brought out whatever believers were in it, but found there only a single household of Muslims” (Q 51:35–36); and: “Say: ‘Do not count your Islam as a favour to me; rather, God favours you by guiding you to faith’” (Q 49:17).

The opponent argues from their separation, as in: “Say: ‘You have not believed; rather, say, “We have submitted”’” (Q 49:14); their conjunction, as in: “The Muslim men and Muslim women, the believing men and believing women” (Q 33:35), and: “It increased them only in faith and submission” (Q 33:22); and their different explanations when clarification is requested, as in the Prophet’s sayings, God bless him and grant him peace: “Faith is that you believe in God …,” and: “Islam is that you testify that there is no god but God …,” to the end of each report.

We say: there is no dispute that Islam can be applied to outward surrender and submission. A difference in concept suffices to make conjunction valid. The report explains the objects of faith and the prescribed practices of Islam; a similar account has also been transmitted concerning faith.

<a id="m-vi-p729"></a>

**Source p. 729**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.20](#c1-20).*

### Investigation three: increase and decrease of faith

The apparent meaning of the Book and the Sunna is that faith increases and decreases. The majority deny this because faith names assent that reaches the degree of certainty, and such assent does not admit of degrees. It admits of degrees only if the name is applied to acts of obedience. Hence it is said that the disagreement rests on the disagreement over the interpretation of faith. This is correct, however, only if leaving works undone is not regarded as departure from faith. In that case, the difference of degree concerns the perfection of faith, not its root.

The reply, after conceding that assent is certainty and that certainty is required of everyone, denies that certainty does **not** admit of degrees: consider necessary and inferential certainty after hesitation and obscurity have disappeared. [For the reading adopted here, see the editorial note on p. 729.]

Those who affirm differences of degree appeal to the fact that the faith of individual members of the community certainly does not equal the prophets’ faith, and to texts explicitly stating this: “When His signs are recited to them, they increase them in faith” (Q 8:2); “That they may increase in faith alongside their faith” (Q 48:4); and: “That those who believe may increase in faith” (Q 74:31). A report says: “Faith increases until it brings its possessor into Paradise.”

The reply is that increase is intended with respect to persistence, firmness, and number; or with respect to an increase in the matters that must be believed when their details are considered; or that what increases is faith’s fruits and lights.

### Investigation four: qualifying the profession of faith

The position of the school is that qualification in the profession of faith is valid, so that “I am a believer, if God wills” may even be preferred to “I am truly a believer.” Most deny its validity because it indicates doubt, or at least gives the impression of doubt.

<a id="m-vi-p731"></a>

**Source p. 731**

We have several arguments.

First: the qualification seeks blessing and expresses proper reverence, rather than doubt and hesitation.

Second: saving faith is a hidden matter. Even someone certain of possessing it cannot be sure that something incompatible with it has not entered it without his knowledge. He therefore entrusts the matter to the divine will.

Third—and this is the principal consideration—the doubt concerns that which is the sign of salvation, namely faith at the end of life, rather than present faith. Their statement, “What counts is faith at the end of life,” does not mean that present faith is not truly faith; it means that present faith does not [by itself] save. The same applies to unbelief, felicity, and misery. One whose final lot is felicity does not change to a final lot of misery; change occurs only in the present condition.

### Investigation five: the faith of the imitator

The majority uphold the validity of the imitator’s faith, because assent depends on decisiveness of belief, not on its stability [through proof]. The argument that it is of no benefit, by analogy with faith at the point of despair, on the common ground that both lack the hardship of reflection and inference through which reward is earned, is unsound. Even supposing that such a matter could be established by analogy, the cause in the original case is that this is faith aimed at averting punishment, not genuine faith, and that the servant no longer possesses the ability to dispose of himself and enjoy his life.

As for those who deny its validity: the Shaykh does not require the ability to establish a proof and repel an objection in every question of the fundamentals. He requires that belief concerning each question rest upon a proof, such that without it one is not a believer. To interpret this as denying complete faith because an obligation has been neglected is to interpret it in a way about which disagreement is inconceivable.

<a id="m-vi-p733"></a>

**Source p. 733**

The Muʿtazilites do require that ability, so that without it faith is absent. This is plainly false, unless a collective obligation is intended, in which case the issue becomes that of the perpetrator of a major sin.

Some hold that the obligation to reflect applies only to certain people. Those incapable of it, such as ordinary people and some slaves and women, are required only to imitate someone who holds the truth and to form a sound opinion. It is also said that they are obliged to hear the elementary proofs that readily reach the understanding. If they understand, they belong to those who hold the doctrines in general; otherwise, they are not subject to obligation.

Later scholars hold that the disagreement does not concern applying the rulings of Islam, but whether the imitator is punished with the punishment of an unbeliever. Some say yes, because he is ignorant of God and His Messenger. Others say no: his punishment is diminished by the assent he possesses.

The disagreement concerns someone who grew up on a mountain peak, did not reflect, and then, when told what he must believe, assented. Someone who grew up in the abode of Islam—even in the deserts—and received recurrent reports establishing the Prophet’s circumstances, God bless him and grant him peace, belongs to those who engage in reflection.

### Investigation six: unbelief

Unbelief is the absence of faith from something capable of it. It is more general than denial, since it includes an unbeliever who has neither assent nor denial. The Qāḍī says that it is disavowal of God, which has been interpreted as ignorance. The objection is that an unbeliever may know God and assent to Him, whereas a believer may not know some of His rulings. The reply is that what is meant is disavowing Him in some matter certainly known to belong to His rulings, or ignorance of that matter both generally and in detail.

<a id="m-vi-p735"></a>

**Source p. 735**

The Muʿtazilites say that unbelief is an ugly act or the neglect of an obligation for which the greatest punishment is deserved. There is obvious obscurity in this.

If it is said: someone subject to obligation may become an unbeliever through an act while his assent remains as it was,

we say: even if that is conceded, some prohibited acts, as opposed to others, may be signs of denial. Determining this belongs to the Lawgiver. The same applies to certain interpretations concerning the fundamentals.

Conclusion: an unbeliever who displays faith is specifically called a hypocrite; one who disbelieves after Islam, an apostate; one who asserts a plurality of gods, an associator; one who adheres to one of the [scriptural] religions, a person of the Book; one who attributes occurrences to time and believes time to be beginningless, a temporalist; one who denies the Maker, a denier; and one who inwardly conceals beliefs unanimously judged to be unbelief, a *zindīq*.

### Investigation seven: doctrinal error among the people of the qibla

A person among the people of the qibla who departs from the truth is not an unbeliever unless he contradicts something necessarily known to belong to religion, such as the temporal origination of the world or the resurrection of bodies. Some say that he is an unbeliever. The Ustādh says: we declare unbelievers those who declare us unbelievers, but not those who do not. The early Muʿtazilites said: we declare unbelievers the determinists and those who affirm beginningless attributes and the creation of acts. Their ignorant adherents say: we declare unbelievers those who affirm attributes additional [to the essence], the possibility of the vision [of God], release from the Fire, and the creation and willing by God of evils and ugly acts.

Our argument is that the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, and those after him did not investigate people’s doctrines and instruct them concerning what was true [in all these questions].

<a id="m-vi-p737"></a>

**Source p. 737**

If it is said: the same is true of the agreed fundamentals,

we say: that was because they were well known and their proofs were evident in a manner appropriate to those who held the doctrines in general. It may also be said that detailed explanation was omitted because general assent sufficed: detail is obligatory only when the details are considered. Otherwise, how many believers do not know what beginningless and temporally originated mean! The various groups’ declarations of unbelief against one another are well known.

### Investigation eight: associated rulings

The rulings concerning the believer, unbeliever, and sinful person are as previously stated. Sinfulness is departure from obedience to God through committing a major sin or persisting in a minor one. It is sometimes qualified by the absence of an interpretive justification, to exclude the rebel. To deem lawful what is certainly a sin, or to treat it with contempt, is unbelief.

An innovator is someone who departs in doctrine from the path of the people of truth. His position is like that of the sinful person. As for this world, the rulings concerning the believer, unbeliever, and sinful person are set out in jurisprudence. The ruling concerning the hypocrite and the *zindīq* is to apply the [outward] rulings; that concerning the innovator is hatred, humiliation, criticism, and cursing. Some adherents of falsehood classify disagreement in jurisprudence as innovation; others go further and include everything that did not exist in the Companions’ time.

## Chapter four: the imamate

The imamate is general leadership in matters of religion and this world, in succession to the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace. Its rulings belong to jurisprudence. However, because corrupt beliefs harmful to many principles became widespread among the people of innovation, its discussions were included in kalām. It contains the following investigations.

<a id="m-vi-p739"></a>

**Source p. 739**

### Investigation one: appointing an imam

Appointing an imam is obligatory upon the community, according to us and the generality of the Muʿtazilites on the basis of revelation, and according to some of them on rational grounds. The Shīʿa make it obligatory upon God. The Najadāt deny the obligation altogether; al-Aṣamm denies it when justice prevails, and al-Fuwaṭī when injustice prevails.

We have several arguments.

First: consensus, to the extent that they gave appointing an imam priority over burying the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace.

Second: obligations such as enforcing the prescribed penalties, defending the frontiers, and other matters relating to the preservation of order cannot be fulfilled without an imam.

Third: appointing an imam brings benefits and averts harms beyond counting, and doing so is obligatory by consensus.

If it is said: it also entails harms,

we say: these are not taken into account because they are few.

If it is said: then the community after the rightly guided imams has been in error,

we say: [its situation arose from] necessity, so there is no sin, and hence no error.
