Fourth: the Book and the Sunna impose the obligation to obey and know the imam. This entails that there must be an imam, which comes about through appointing one.

<a id="m-vi-p741"></a>

**Source p. 741**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.21](#c1-21).*

Those who hold the appointment obligatory on rational grounds argue that it averts harm and is therefore obligatory.

We say: not in the sense that someone who omits it deserves blame and punishment.

For its being obligatory upon God, the argument is that it is a gracious aid that brings about knowledge, draws people toward obedience, and distances them from disobedience.

The reply denies both premises of the syllogism. How could they be granted, when the appointment involves evils arising from differences of opinion and people’s inclination to refuse obedience to their equals? Moreover, performing acts of obedience and avoiding disobedience without an imam is harder and nearer to sincerity. Nor is appointing an imam uniquely determined as the gracious aid: creating people immune from sin would be more gracious. The assertion that it is an unmixed benefit and a gracious aid unobtainable by any other means is denied. Furthermore, the gracious aid lies in his appearing, which you do not make obligatory.

If it is said: his mere existence is a deterrent gracious aid, because people fear his appearance; his manifest exercise of authority is another gracious aid, which the servants have forfeited through their bad choice, frightening him and withholding their support,

we say: then the possibility of his existence, and the judgement that he will exist, even after an interval, would suffice. Fear of an anticipated existence is equivalent to fear of an anticipated appearance. He ought also to appear to the devoted followers who have spent their lives loving and awaiting him, and have sacrificed their lives and property. We decisively judge such an appearance absent according to the ordinary course of events; they judge it absent in actuality.

<a id="m-vi-p743"></a>

**Source p. 743**

The Khārijites argue that appointing an imam provokes strife, because inclinations differ and may not agree upon one person.

The reply is that weighing the grounds for preference, and the prohibition of opposition after some have pledged allegiance, avert strife. Even if the objection is conceded, the strife caused by having no imam is greater.

### Investigation two: the imam’s qualifications

The imam must be subject to legal obligation, free, male, and upright. These conditions are evident. The majority add courage, so that he can enforce the prescribed penalties and resist opponents; the capacity for independent legal reasoning, so that he can attend to religious interests; sound judgement, so that he can set affairs in order; and descent from Quraysh, because of the Prophet’s sayings, God bless him and grant him peace: “The imams are from Quraysh”; “The rulers are from Quraysh”; and: “Give Quraysh precedence, and do not put yourselves before them.”

The Khārijites and most Muʿtazilites disagree, citing his saying, God bless him and grant him peace: “Obey, even if a mutilated Abyssinian slave is appointed over you,” and arguing that lineage has no bearing on the interests of government and religion.

The reply understands the report as referring to someone other than the imam, thereby reconciling the proofs. Nobility of lineage also influences agreement and willing obedience, and none is nobler than Quraysh, especially since the best of the prophets arose among them.

Nevertheless, when the conditions cannot be observed, the rulings dependent upon the imam may be based upon any person possessing effective power who has been appointed or has seized authority.

The Shīʿa require, first, that the imam be a Hāshimite—indeed, an ʿAlid—and know every matter, even hidden things. This is an assertion without proof, contrary to consensus.

<a id="m-vi-p745"></a>

**Source p. 745**

Second, they require that he be the best person of his age, because giving precedence to someone less excellent is rationally ugly. This condition has also been reported from al-Ashʿarī, as fulfilling the purpose of appointing an imam and by analogy with prophecy.

The reply challenges the principle of rational ugliness. Moreover, appointing someone less excellent may sometimes be more conducive to welfare. Prophetic commissioning comes from the All-Wise, All-Knowing, who chooses the best; indeed, superiority itself results from the commissioning. Arguments sometimes given for appointing someone less excellent are consensus after the [rightly guided] caliphs, the institution of consultation, and the fact that superiority is generally hidden from people.

Third, they require immunity from error and sin, on several grounds.

First: analogy with prophecy, the common feature being the establishment of revealed law and protection of the community.

The reply is that appointing the imam belongs to the servants, who have no way of knowing his immunity, unlike the case of a prophet. A prophet must be followed without hesitation or referral to anyone else. His lack of immunity in matters concerning revealed law could therefore lead people astray and deter them from following him. The imam’s case differs.

Second: obedience to him is obligatory by text and consensus. If his immunity were not necessary, he could lie in explaining acts of obedience and disobedience, entailing an obligation to avoid obedience and commit disobedience.

The reply is that he is obeyed only in what does not contradict revealed law. Knowledge, uprightness, and Islam suffice for trusting him. When he contravenes the law, nothing prevents opposing him and referring the matter to the scholars.

<a id="m-vi-p747"></a>

**Source p. 747**

Third: someone not immune is a wrongdoer, because disobedience wrongs oneself or another. Such a person cannot receive the covenant of the imamate, according to text and consensus.

The reply is that absence of immunity does not entail disobedience, still less wrongdoing in the narrower sense. Moreover, the covenant intended in the verse is the covenant of prophecy. In your view, consensus is not authoritative unless it includes the statement of someone immune; establishing immunity by that consensus is therefore circular.

Fourth: we need an imam only because we can err. If he could also err, he would need another imam, and an infinite regress would follow.

The reply is that appointing an imam is obligatory through revelation, on the basis of consensus, not through reason on the basis of the possibility of error. Even if the latter is conceded, it is for innumerable interests. Even if the argument is conceded, knowledge, uprightness, and reference to the Book, the Sunna, and the community’s scholars make immunity unnecessary.

Fifth: he is instituted as a guardian [of revealed law]. If he could err, he would become its undoer.

The reply is that he guards it through proofs and independent legal reasoning, not intrinsically. When he errs or disobeys, he is corrected and restrained. Revealed law is neither undone nor diminished.

Sixth: if he undertakes disobedience, either censuring him is obligatory, contradicting the obligation to obey him, or it is not, contrary to the established proofs.

The reply is that obedience to him is obligatory only in what does not contradict revealed law.

Seventh: there is no way to transmit revealed law through successive ages except through the imam’s immunity, since there may not be enough transmitters to establish recurrent transmission for every ruling.

The reply is that probable judgement suffices in some matters, so isolated reports suffice for them. Certainty is entrusted to recurrent transmission or consensus.

<a id="m-vi-p749"></a>

**Source p. 749**

As for requiring a miracle and knowledge of hidden things, languages, crafts, trades, the natures of foods and medicines, and the wonders of land, sea, heaven, and earth—these are fanciful tales.

### Investigation three: establishing the imamate

For most groups, the imamate is established through the choice of those empowered to bind and loose, even if they are few. They appeal to consensus on Abū Bakr’s imamate without an explicit designation and without waiting for everyone’s agreement; and to the Companions’ undertaking pledges of allegiance and selection after the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, and after ʿUthmān, God Most High be pleased with him, without objection.

The Shīʿa disagree, on several grounds.

First: some conditions are unknowable to those giving allegiance, such as immunity, superiority, and knowledge of the entirety of religion.

We say: even if these conditions are conceded, probable judgement suffices.

Second: appointing someone to offices such as judgeship and supervision of public conduct does not belong to them; a fortiori, neither does this appointment.

We say: if that is conceded, it is because an imam already exists.

Third: selection provokes strife, as in the time of ʿAlī, God Most High be pleased with him, and Muʿāwiya.

We say: the discussion concerns the case in which they submit to the truth and weigh the grounds for preference. Even if the objection is conceded, the strife caused by having no imam is many times greater. We are supposing that no explicit designation exists; otherwise, no choice can override it.

Fourth: the person selected by those giving allegiance would be their successor, not the successor appointed by God and His Messenger.

We say: the proof of revealed law establishes that the person they choose is a successor of God and His Messenger.

<a id="m-vi-p751"></a>

**Source p. 751**

Fifth: if two qualified bodies appoint two qualified persons, and it is unknown which appointment came first, the age must be without an imam. There is no way to validate both appointments, invalidate both, determine which one is valid, or appoint a third person.

We say: one is preferred, or a third person is appointed; there is no absurdity.

Sixth: the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, never omitted appointing a deputy during even the shortest absence, nor explaining even the least matter requiring explanation. How could he have done so for the absence of death and the foundation of important affairs?

Seventh: the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, was more compassionate toward his community than a father toward his child. How could he have omitted entrusting them to one person?

Eighth: God says: “Today I have perfected your religion for you” (Q 5:3). The imamate is among the greatest matters of religion; how could it be neglected?

We say: entrusting the matter to their choice and independent judgement is itself a kind of appointment of a successor, testamentary direction, and completion.

Conclusion: the compact of the imamate is dissolved by something that frustrates its purpose, such as apostasy, insanity, or certain illnesses; by the imam’s abdication for a reason; and by another’s overcoming him, if he became imam through conquest. There is disagreement about his abdication without a reason, and about his removal through sinfulness.

### Investigation four: designation of a successor

The majority hold that the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, did not designate an imam. Some say that he designated Abū Bakr, God Most High be pleased with him, implicitly; others, explicitly. The Shīʿa say that he designated ʿAlī, God ennoble his countenance, implicitly; the Imāmiyya among them also assert explicit designation. This is rebutted in two ways.

<a id="m-vi-p753"></a>

**Source p. 753**

First: had there been an explicit designation concerning such an exalted matter, it would have become famous and evident to the eminent Companions who enjoyed special closeness and intimacy with the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace. They would not have delayed their submission, hesitated when they assembled for this purpose, disagreed over the person to appoint, or doubted the certain truth. To say that they concealed it out of hatred, envy, obstinacy, or contentiousness—or because they believed it abrogated when those in the right did not act upon it and the rightful claimant did not invoke it to establish his right—is fabrication and audacity. It impugns the greatest of the Prophet’s beloved companions, indeed the best of the prophets himself, and indeed the Book that speaks in their praise.

A rational and fair-minded person does not think this of a group whom God Most High described as the best of communities; whom the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, appointed as trustees of his law and guides to his way, knowing their condition and their ultimate fate; and whose uprightness, guidance, abandonment of personal desires, expenditure of wealth and lives in love of him, and killing of relatives and clansmen in his support and in following his law were renowned. One does not suppose that they opposed him before burying him, turned away from the truth and abandoned it, advanced and supported falsehood, withheld and concealed the rightful claimant’s right; nor that he failed to manifest and proclaim it despite his high standing and numerous helpers, although he did assert it without dissimulation when authority passed to him, supporting it with argument and proof, sword and spear. The crisis was then graver, the adversary more obstinate, and the opponents beyond all bounds and numbering.

Second: there are indications which, taken together, may yield certainty that no designation occurred. These include al-ʿAbbās’s saying to ʿAlī, and ʿUmar’s to Abū ʿUbayda, “Extend your hand so that I may pledge allegiance to you”; Abū Bakr’s saying, “Pledge allegiance to ʿUmar or Abū ʿUbayda,” and, “I wish I had asked the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, to whom this authority belongs”; ʿAlī’s acceptance of consultation; his saying to Ṭalḥa, “If you wish, I will pledge allegiance to you”; his appealing against Muʿāwiya to the allegiance pledged to him rather than to a designation of him; his assisting Abū Bakr and ʿUmar in affairs,

<a id="m-vi-p755"></a>

**Source p. 755**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.22](#c1-22).*

and advising them on what was more beneficial; his silence concerning any designation of him in his sermons, letters, assertions of merit, and disputes; Zayd ibn ʿAlī’s denial of it despite his exalted rank; and the same denial by many eminent members of the Prophet’s household.

The opponent argues that, according to the ordinary course of events, the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, could not have neglected such a matter when he did not neglect lesser matters.

The answer is that omitting to designate a particular person is not neglect.

### Investigation five: the first imam after the Messenger

The imam after the Messenger of God, God bless him and grant him peace, was Abū Bakr, God be pleased with him. The Shīʿa say that it was ʿAlī. Our proof is the consensus of those empowered to bind and loose, even though some assented only after hesitation. It is established that ʿAlī obeyed Abū Bakr’s commands and prohibitions, attended Friday and festival prayers with him, called him caliph, praised him in life and after death, and excused his delay in pledging allegiance.

Moreover, they agreed that the imam was Abū Bakr, ʿAlī, or al-ʿAbbās. The latter two did not contest his position, so he was determined as imam. To invoke dissimulation is to impute error to the imams. Had dissimulation applied, it would have applied in Muʿāwiya’s time.

An appeal is sometimes made to God’s saying: “Say to the desert Arabs who stayed behind …” (Q 48:16). According to the exegetes, the summoner whom obedience was obligatory to follow was Abū Bakr; according to some, ʿUmar, which also establishes the desired conclusion.

<a id="m-vi-p757"></a>

**Source p. 757**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.22](#c1-22).*

An appeal is also made to the Prophet’s sayings, God bless him and grant him peace: “Follow the two after me, Abū Bakr and ʿUmar”; “The caliphate after me will last thirty years”; and his saying during his illness: “Bring me writing materials and paper, so that I may write a document over which no two people will disagree,” followed by: “God and the Muslims will accept none but Abū Bakr.”

Other arguments are that the Emigrants, whom God described as truthful, used to address him as “Successor of the Messenger of God”; that the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, appointed him to lead the prayer and did not remove him, for which reason ʿAlī, God be pleased with him, said, “The Messenger of God approved you for our religion, so we approve you for our worldly affairs”; and that, had his imamate not been rightful, a group who approved it and remained silent about it would not have been the best community brought forth for humanity, commanding right and forbidding wrong.

These are probable considerations which may, in combination, yield certainty. Moreover, the question belongs to jurisprudence, where probable judgement suffices.

The Shīʿa argue in several ways.

First: there is no dispute that after the Messenger of God, God bless him and grant him peace, there was an imam. It can have been none other than ʿAlī, God ennoble his countenance, because the conditions of immunity, designation, and superiority were absent from others.

The answer denies that these are conditions, and then denies their absence in Abū Bakr’s case, God be pleased with him.

Second: God’s saying, “Your patron is only God and His Messenger …” (Q 5:55), was revealed concerning ʿAlī when he gave a beggar his ring while bowing in prayer. What is meant by patron is the person who exercises authority over affairs, since supportive patronage belongs to everyone. The person who exercises authority over the community’s affairs is the imam.

<a id="m-vi-p759"></a>

**Source p. 759**

We say: the preceding verses are a true witness that the reference is to the patronage of love and support, not authority and the imamate. The description of the believers may be for praise rather than restriction, or to indicate their greater honour and entitlement. “While they are bowing” may be a conjunction, meaning that they bow in their prayers, unlike Jewish prayer, or that they submit humbly. Moreover, support ascribed to some people concerns people other than themselves, since a person does not support himself. The restriction excludes a contested alternative, which was not the imamate. The apparent sense affirms patronage actually and presently, whereas the authority of the imamate did not belong to him then. Referring it to the future cannot work in the case of God and His Messenger. Understanding a plural expression as a single person requires proof. It is exceedingly unlikely that the argument from this verse escaped the Companions generally and ʿAlī particularly.

Third: the recurrently transmitted reports of Ghadīr and of the corresponding rank. In the first, *mawlā* means the person entrusted with affairs and possessing prior authority to administer them, as in God’s saying, “Your refuge is the Fire; it is your *mawlā*” (Q 57:15), and the Prophet’s saying, God bless him and grant him peace, “Any woman who contracts her own marriage without her *mawlā*’s permission …” It does not mean an emancipator, emancipated person, ally, neighbour, or cousin, as is evident; nor does it mean a supporter, since that relationship was already evident. Hārūn’s rank in relation to Mūsā, peace be upon them both, is general, equivalent to a term made definite by the article. When prophecy is excluded, succession and authority over the community’s affairs remain, had he survived him. This is the meaning of the imamate.

<a id="m-vi-p761"></a>

**Source p. 761**

The answer denies recurrent transmission. Indeed, the authenticity of the Ghadīr report, and its indication that the imamate is restricted to ʿAlī, God be pleased with him, are themselves disputed. Furthermore, isolated reports carry no weight against consensus. The eminent Companions’ failure to invoke the two reports is an indication that they do not convey the proposed meaning. Attributing this failure to obstinacy is the utmost error.

Even if the expression of rank is granted to be general through its annexation to a proper name, it does not include succession and the exercise of authority by deputation, since Hārūn shared in prophecy. Nor does it indicate that deputation continues after the death of the person appointing the deputy. Its cessation is not dismissal or diminution, but a return to completeness, namely independence. Had Hārūn survived, his exercise of authority would have been by virtue of his prophecy. Prophecy is excluded in ʿAlī’s case, God be pleased with him, and so is what depends upon it.

Fourth: explicit designating texts, such as: “Greet him with the title Commander of the Believers”; “You are the successor after me”; “He is the imam of the God-fearing”; “This is my successor over you”; and: “You are my brother, my legatee, my successor after me, and the adjudicator of my religion”—with a *kasra* on the *dāl* [in *dīnī*].

The answer is that these are isolated reports set against consensus. Had they been authentic and conveyed the claimed meaning, they would not have escaped the Companions and those after them, especially the pure household.

Fifth: challenging the imamate of the others. In general, the argument calls them wrongdoers because they had previously been unbelievers, citing God’s saying: “The unbelievers are the wrongdoers” (Q 2:254). The covenant of the imamate does not reach a wrongdoer, because God says: “My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers” (Q 2:124). The invalidity of this is evident.

In detail, the argument is that Abū Bakr, God Most High be pleased with him, contradicted God’s Book by denying inheritance from the Prophet, God Most High bless him and grant him peace, on the basis of a report that he himself transmitted.

<a id="m-vi-p763"></a>

**Source p. 763**

We say: a general expression in the Book may be restricted by an isolated report whose indication is decisive, especially one heard from the Messenger of God’s own mouth, God Most High bless him and grant him peace, since for its hearer this has the standing of recurrent transmission.

[They argue that] he withheld Fadak from Fāṭima al-Zahrāʾ, God Most High be pleased with her, although she claimed it as a gift and ʿAlī and Umm Ayman testified; whereas he accepted the wives’ claims to their chambers without a witness.

We say: even if this is conceded, a judge may rule on the basis of his own knowledge without ruling on the statement of someone claimed to be immune.

[They argue that] he opposed the Messenger of God, God Most High bless him and grant him peace, by appointing ʿUmar as successor, although the Prophet, God Most High bless him and grant him peace, had removed him from responsibility for alms.

We say: according to you, he appointed ʿAlī as successor. Moreover, the termination of an appointment when its task ends is not dismissal; and simply doing something the Prophet, God Most High bless him and grant him peace, did not do is not a ground for censure.

[They argue that] he did not know the rulings, since he cut off a thief’s left hand, and hesitated concerning a grandmother’s inheritance and the meaning of *kalāla* [a particular case of collateral inheritance].

We say: even if this is conceded, how many similar instances occur among those exercising independent legal judgement!

[They argue that] he doubted his entitlement, saying at his death: “I wish I had asked the Messenger of God, God Most High bless him and grant him peace, to whom this authority belongs, so that we would not have disputed it with those entitled to it.”

We say: if authentic, this does not indicate doubt. It indicates the absence of an explicit designation and his utmost diligence in seeking the truth.

[They argue that] ʿUmar, God Most High be pleased with him, ordered the stoning of a pregnant woman and of an insane woman, and prohibited excessive marriage gifts.

We say: even if conceded, these matters do not invalidate his imamate.

<a id="m-vi-p765"></a>

**Source p. 765**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.23](#c1-23).*

[They argue that] he doubted the Prophet’s death, God Most High bless him and grant him peace, although the Book declares it.

We say: this was because of extreme distress and grief, or because he interpreted the verse to mean that the Prophet would die after his mission was completed.

[They argue that] he disposed of the treasury and spoils without right, withheld the household’s fifth, and prohibited temporary marriage and the combined enjoyment of pilgrimage and lesser pilgrimage.

We say: these are matters of independent legal judgement that do not impugn the imamate, even if error becomes evident.

[They argue that] he made the caliphate a matter of consultation among six, although there is consensus that two simultaneous imams are inadmissible.

We say: that concerns independent possession of the office, not consultation to determine one of them.

[They argue that] ʿUthmān appointed people whose sinfulness and corruption became manifest; directed treasury funds to his relatives; reserved pasture for himself; harmed Ibn Masʿūd, ʿAmmār, and Abū Dharr; recalled a man banished by the Messenger of God, God bless him and grant him peace; and waived retaliation against Ibn ʿUmar and the prescribed penalty against al-Walīd ibn ʿUtba. The Companions abandoned him until he was killed, and he was not buried until three days later.

We say: some of this does not impugn him personally, such as his governors’ corruption; some is fabrication; and some is independent legal judgement. The recall of the banished man rested on something he had heard, which was insufficient for them but sufficient for him. If the failure to assist or bury him without excuse were authentic, it would impugn them, not him.

Conclusion: Abū Bakr, God be pleased with him, preferred ʿUmar and entrusted authority to him. The community agreed upon him. He subdued the insolent and developed the lands. When he was mortally wounded, he made the matter one of consultation among six who were the best of the servants. Agreement fell upon ʿUthmān, who collected the Qurʾān and suppressed aggression.

<a id="m-vi-p767"></a>

**Source p. 767**

Then the people of rebellion rose against him. He submitted until what happened happened. Those empowered to bind and loose agreed to pledge allegiance to ʿAlī and follow him. The eruption of strife did not arise from disagreement about his caliphate. Authority then passed to al-Ḥasan, God Most High be pleased with him. Six months after allegiance was pledged to him, he handed it to Muʿāwiya to spare blood, preserve lives, and extinguish the fire raging among the masses, as the best of the prophets had foretold. Kingship passed to Muʿāwiya and the imamate came to an end; and so the succession continues until the Resurrection.

The Shīʿa maintain that the imam after the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, was ʿAlī, God ennoble his countenance; then al-Ḥasan; then al-Ḥusayn; then ʿAlī Zayn al-ʿĀbidīn; then Muḥammad al-Bāqir; then Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq; then Mūsā al-Kāẓim; then ʿAlī al-Riḍā; then Muḥammad al-Jawād; then ʿAlī al-Zakī; then al-Ḥasan al-ʿAskarī; then Muḥammad, the awaited Mahdī. They maintain that each one’s designation of his successor is recurrently transmitted, and that the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, said concerning al-Ḥusayn: “This son of mine is an imam, son of an imam, brother of an imam, and father of nine imams, the ninth of whom is their one who shall arise.”

We add nothing beyond expressing astonishment.

### Investigation six: relative excellence

For us, excellence follows the order of the caliphate, with some hesitation between ʿUthmān and ʿAlī, God be pleased with them both. For the Shīʿa and the majority of the Muʿtazilites, ʿAlī is the most excellent.

Our general argument is that the agreement of most scholars on this entails that they possessed a proof. In detail, God says: “The most God-fearing shall be kept far from it, who gives his wealth to purify himself” (Q 92:17–18).

<a id="m-vi-p769"></a>

**Source p. 769**

This was revealed concerning Abū Bakr. The most God-fearing is the most honoured, and therefore the most excellent. The Prophet also said, God bless him and grant him peace: “Follow the two after me, Abū Bakr and ʿUmar”; thus ʿAlī was commanded to follow them. He said: “They are the two chiefs of the mature men of Paradise, apart from the prophets and messengers”; “The best of my community is Abū Bakr, then ʿUmar”; and: “After the prophets and messengers, the sun has neither risen nor set upon anyone better than Abū Bakr.” Such reports are numerous. He said, peace be upon him: “If there were a prophet after me, it would be ʿUmar.” He said: “ʿUthmān is my brother and companion in Paradise,” and: “Should I not feel modest before someone before whom the angels of heaven feel modest?” This ranking is also established as a statement of ʿAlī, Ibn ʿUmar, and Ibn al-Ḥanafiyya.

It is indicated by recurrently transmitted accounts of their deeds, histories, and efforts for Islam: uniting hearts, successive conquests, overcoming the apostates, and breaking the power of Persia and Rome; conquering the east, subduing the Persian state, ordering affairs, spreading justice, and strengthening the weak; conquering lands, exalting God’s word, uniting people upon one codex, equipping armies, spending wealth to support religion, and similar achievements.

The Shīʿa appeal to God’s saying: “Say: ‘Come, let us summon our sons and your sons, our women and your women, ourselves and yourselves’” (Q 3:61), taking ʿAlī to be intended; and His saying: “Say: ‘I ask you for no recompense for it except affection for near kin’” (Q 42:23), ʿAlī, God be pleased with him, being among them;

<a id="m-vi-p771"></a>

**Source p. 771**

and His saying, “Jibrīl and the righteous one among the believers” (Q 66:4), taking that righteous one to be ʿAlī. They also invoke the Prophet’s sayings, God bless him and grant him peace: “Whoever wishes to behold Ādam in his knowledge, Nūḥ in his piety, Ibrāhīm in his forbearance, Mūsā in his awe-inspiring dignity, and ʿĪsā in his worship, let him behold ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib”; “The best judge among you is ʿAlī”; “O God, bring me the one most beloved to You among Your creatures, to eat this bird with me,” whereupon ʿAlī came; and: “You stand to me as Hārūn stood to Mūsā”; together with other reports.

They argue that he was more learned, so that the leading figures of the sciences traced their learning to him, and he himself declared this in the report of the cushion; more courageous, as his campaigns testify, so that the Prophet, God bless him and grant him peace, said, “One blow struck by ʿAlī is better than the worship of humankind and jinn”; more ascetic, so that he repudiated the world altogether; more devoted to worship and more generous; nobler in character and more cheerful; more eloquent; and earlier in embracing Islam.

The answer is that the discussion concerns superiority in the sense of honour before God and abundance of reward. The generality of Muslims have testified concerning this, and ʿAlī, God be pleased with him, acknowledged it. What you have cited is opposed by what we have cited. Moreover, your evidence contains evident points for examination, especially the claim concerning precedence in Islam and effort to raise its banners.

After them, it is established that Fāṭima al-Zahrāʾ is the foremost woman of the worlds; that al-Ḥasan and al-Ḥusayn are the two chiefs of the young men of Paradise; and that the ten, including the four imams, were given glad tidings of Paradise. Thereafter, excellence belongs to knowledge and piety. Consideration of lineage in marital suitability concerns a worldly matter. The excellence of the pure household lies in their being signs of guidance and supporters of the prophetic mission, as is indicated by their being joined to God’s Book in the statement that holding fast to both saves from error.

<a id="m-vi-p773"></a>

**Source p. 773**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.24](#c1-24).*

### Investigation seven: the Companions

The people of truth agree that the Companions must be honoured and that attacks upon them must be avoided, especially the Emigrants and the Helpers, because the Book and the Sunna praise them and warn against failing to revere them: “Fear God, fear God concerning my Companions; do not make them targets after me”; “Do not revile my Companions”; and: “The best generation is my generation.” Had they become corrupt after him, he would not have said this, but would have warned of it. Much of what is related about them is fabricated. What is authentic admits of explanations and interpretations.

ʿAlī’s hesitation in pledging allegiance to Abū Bakr, God Most High be pleased with him, arose from grief and sorrow and from having no leisure for reflection and independent judgement. His hesitation in assisting ʿUthmān arose from the latter’s unwillingness [to accept such assistance], not from ʿAlī’s approval [of his killing]. Hence he said: “By God, I neither killed ʿUthmān nor conspired against him.” He hesitated to accept allegiance because of the enormity of the event and his rejection of it. He delayed retaliation against the killers because of their effective power, or because he regarded them as rebels, who, according to some, are not held liable for the bloodshed and destruction of property they cause.

Saʿd, Saʿīd, and others refrained from going out with him to the wars because of their own independent judgement and because he did not compel them, not because they disputed his imamate or refused obedience to him.

In the Battle of the Camel, the Battle of Ṣiffīn, and the war against the Khārijites, ʿAlī was in the right, because his imamate was established and the difference between the sides was manifest. It was not that both parties were right, as those who declare every independent judgement correct maintain; nor that one was right without specifying which, as some Muʿtazilites maintain.

<a id="m-vi-p775"></a>

**Source p. 775**

His opponents were rebels, because they rose against the rightful imam on the basis of a specious interpretive ground. They were not sinful persons or unbelievers, as the Shīʿa maintain in ignorance of the difference between opposition and warfare with an interpretive justification and without one. Hence ʿAlī prohibited cursing the Syrians and said: “Our brothers have rebelled against us.” The withdrawal of the people of the Camel [from their position] is authentically reported. Moreover, some among us say that the battle did not arise from a deliberate resolve to fight, and that ʿĀʾisha’s purpose, God be pleased with her, was solely to reconcile the parties.

In the war against the Khārijites, the matter is still clearer. Arbitration does not provide even a specious ground for withdrawing obedience: how could it, when it is a form of reconciliation? God Most High has said, “Reconcile them” (Q 49:9). The command to fight does not require immediate execution.

After them, the calamity grew grave, the events momentous, and the tear too wide for the patcher. Nevertheless, the early generations took the greatest care to avoid the path of error, fearing the outcome and considering the final consequence.

Conclusion: authentic reports have come concerning the appearance of an imam from the descendants of Fāṭima al-Zahrāʾ, God be pleased with her, who will fill the world with equity and justice as it had been filled with injustice and oppression. The Imāmiyya’s assertion that he has already been born and has remained hidden for more than four hundred years from fear of enemies is an assertion without proof of an imam without a purpose. Moreover, after the Banū al-ʿAbbās, people seek him even from heaven: what reason has he to remain hidden?

<a id="m-vi-p777"></a>

**Source p. 777**

*Critical apparatus: [C1.25](#c1-25).*

[Authentic reports have also come] concerning the descent of ʿĪsā, the emergence of the Dajjāl, and other signs: the beast of the earth; Yaʾjūj and Maʾjūj; the sun’s rising from the west; the three sinkings of the earth; the scarcity of knowledge and trustworthiness; the abundance of sinfulness and betrayal; the leadership of the sinful and ignoble; the vast excess of women over men in number; Islam’s nearing disappearance; and the world’s order approaching dissolution.

This is the evil in relation to which the superiority of earlier generations becomes certain, also in respect of abundance of reward. It occurs when the Hour is extremely near and the time of repentance and obedience is ending. It therefore does not contradict the possibility that the latter part of the community may be better, in accordance with the Messenger of God’s saying, God bless him and grant him peace: “My community is like rain: it is not known whether its beginning or its end is better.” This rests on the possibility that, despite the long lapse of time and corruption of the age, the reward for recognition, certainty, obedience, and faith may be greater.

May God make our hearts steadfast in religion and grant us success in what pleases Him on the Day of Judgement. He is the best giver of success and help. May God bless the Prophet Muḥammad, his pure family, and all his Companions.

Praise belongs to God, Lord of the worlds, for granting success in setting down the aims of kalām concerning the doctrines of Islam. This was completed after dawn on Monday, the fourteenth of Dhū al-Qaʿda in the year seven hundred and eighty-four.

---

<a id="critical-apparatus"></a>

# Selective critical apparatus: revision C1

References without a volume number are to Y’s Arabic matn pages. The evidence labels are defined in the critical introduction. Arabic lemmata are locally transcribed or quoted readings, not a continuous reconstructed text. Orthographic vocalisation is not claimed as a manuscript feature unless expressly stated.

<a id="c1-01"></a>

## C1.01 — p. 125: the judgement of self-evidence [C/T]

**Lemma:** «أن الحكم كسبي». The claim concerns the judgement **that conceiving existence is self-evident**. It does not mean that every judgement is acquired, nor that the conception of existence itself has just been declared acquired. The English now supplies the referent in brackets. In N I:56 (PDF 72), the commentary explicitly explains that the Imām treats assent to the self-evidence of the conception of existence as acquired and adduces proofs. No Arabic emendation is made.

<a id="c1-02"></a>

## C1.02 — p. 135: identifying “the Shaykh” [C]

**Lemma:** «وذهب الشيخ». The second investigation’s opening in the *Sharḥ* expressly attributes identity of existence and essence to Abū al-Ḥasan al-Ashʿarī; the subsequent lemma resumes that discussion. The English retains the title and the note identifies its referent. Ibn Sīnā is not supplied merely because other philosophical texts call him “the Shaykh.” See JK, «قال البحث الثاني المنقول عن الشيخ أبي الحسن الأشعري» and the later «قال وذهب الشيخ».

<a id="c1-03"></a>

## C1.03 — p. 185: extrinsic modalities [D/C/T]

**Lemma:** «والغيريان يتشاركان في اسم الضرورة». The first draft made the shared name “necessity” appear conditional on opposing the related terms. The revised translation separates two claims: both extrinsic necessity and extrinsic impossibility are necessities; when their related terms are opposed, they coincide in application. Thus necessary existence corresponds to impossible nonexistence, whereas necessary existence and impossible existence exclude one another. N I:117 (PDF 133) directly confirms this explanation in the central commentary. These examples explain the sentence; they are not inserted as extra sentences of the matn.

<a id="c1-04"></a>

## C1.04 — p. 191: a negative attribute and denied predication [D/C/T]

**Lemmata:** «إمكانه لا» / «لا إمكان له». The revised English contrasts “its contingency is nonexistent” with “it has no contingency.” The former is explained as the attribute’s being negative or lacking independent external actuality; the latter denies that the attribute is predicable of its subject. The objection identifies them; the reply rejects that identification. N I:122–123 (PDF 138–139) preserves the matn contrast and explains it. The revision must not be read as asserting that the contingent thing lacks contingency. Nor is a different formulation from another work silently substituted here.

<a id="c1-05"></a>

## C1.05 — p. 225: necessity, actuality, and the complete cause [D/C/T]

**Lemma:** «فوجوبه معها». “Necessity” is retained. In N I:153 (PDF 169), the commentary expressly distinguishes an account formulated in terms of existence from one formulated in terms of necessity. The latter prevents the mere coexistence of matter and an actual composite from defining matter as formal cause. This is an interpretive distinction in the commentary, not a newly collated manuscript variant.

**Lemma:** «كالبسيط للبسيط إيجابا أو اختيارا». The two examples correspond in order: a simple agent necessarily producing a simple effect illustrates the agent alone as complete cause; production by choice illustrates the agent with the end. “Respectively” has been added to the English to preserve that correspondence. N I:154 (PDF 170) states the pairing explicitly.

The same commentary distinguishes the agent that produces an effect from negative conditions accompanying its action. Calling removal of an obstacle a condition does not make a nonexistent entity an independently producing agent. Accordingly, the preceding English “incidentally” is retained for the originated thing’s antecedent nonexistence being counted among its principles.

<a id="c1-06"></a>

## C1.06 — p. 247: “agent or part of an agent” [R/C/U]

**Reading retained:** «(فاعل أو) جزء فاعل». Y’s page image and note 1 were checked. The note reports obliteration of «فاعل أو» in J; this particular manuscript folio has not been inspected. The commentary’s explanation is unequivocally about form contributing to agency, with matter’s need for form not making one particular form its independently sufficient cause. See JK, I:170–171, «قال المبحث السابع المادة للصورة».

The parenthesis is retained to expose the textual issue. Treating *aw* as explanatory is possible, but is not promoted to an established manuscript reading. Deleting the phrase throughout all witnesses, or translating it as proof of independent formal agency, would go beyond the available evidence.

<a id="c1-07"></a>

## C1.07 — p. 281: a lost negative in extraction [D]

**Verified printed reading:** «أو لا قسمة». The English “or nondivision” follows the visible page, not the defective extraction «أو القسمة». The distinction excludes unity and the point in the definition of quality. This is correction of the reading aid; no witness has been emended.

<a id="c1-08"></a>

## C1.08 — pp. 283, 291: kinds of sensible quality [C]

The *Sharḥ* distinguishes stable sensible qualities (*infiʿāliyyāt*) from transient affections (*infiʿālāt*), and discusses the status of heat and cold as qualities rather than treating cold as mere absence. See JK, I:200–201, «قال القسم الأول الكيفيات المحسوسة». This distinction belongs in the explanatory apparatus; the shorter matn is not expanded by importing the whole commentary.

At p. 291, “particular whitenesses” preserves the theory under discussion: whiteness without qualification is not being treated as one lowest species whose members simply differ in degree. The author then scrutinises the premise that quiddity and its constituents cannot admit degrees. His criticism is retained; the passage is not recast as an unqualified endorsement of that premise.

<a id="c1-09"></a>

## C1.09 — p. 413: two tests of simple and composite [C]

The paragraph applies two definitions under two considerations: composition from bodies of different natures, and sameness of a quantitative part with the whole in name and definition; each can be considered in reality or in sensory appearance. “Simple” is therefore not silently replaced by “chemically elemental.” Gold can be simple to sense but composite under the proposed underlying account. The distinction is explicit in JK, «قال والمأخوذ في كل», following «قال الفصل الثاني فيما يتعلق بالأجسام على التفصيل». The digital sequence in this region contains displaced material; its page order is not used as evidence of the matn’s order.

<a id="c1-10"></a>

## C1.10 — p. 415: the recipient of above and below [D/R/C]

**Y and N matn:** «والقابل ليس إلا واحدا». **Reading adopted in English:** «والقابل ليس واحدا» — “the recipient is not one.” Y reports omission of «إلا» in J and S. Those specific manuscript locations remain uninspected.

N I:336 (PDF 352) has now been checked directly. Its surrounding matn retains the restrictive particle, but its central commentary distinguishes the encompassing sphere as bearer of above from the centre as bearer of below and expressly denies that their recipient is one. This supports the earlier disclosed decision to follow the reported omission. It also shows why agreement between a printed matn and its own commentary cannot be assumed. The decision is strongly supported locally, while the manuscript report remains R rather than D.

<a id="c1-11"></a>

## C1.11 — pp. 421–423: astronomical quantities [D/U]

The lunar figure 354 days plus one sixth and one fifth is preserved as printed; it is not silently converted into a lunar-month period. The scope of the compressed sentence needs fuller astronomical collation. At p. 423, the checked page reads twenty-four degrees and twenty-three minutes for the lunar deferent. No daily numerical entry for Jupiter is invented where this list supplies none. Retaining a transmitted historical quantity is not a modern scientific endorsement.

<a id="c1-12"></a>

## C1.12 — p. 455: *khārṣīnī* [D/C/U]

**Lemma:** «الخارصيني». N I:373 (PDF 393) describes a copper-like substance used for mirrors, then distinguishes it from a later composite mirror metal called Chinese iron. This passage has been inspected in the scan. The translation therefore retains *khārṣīnī*; identifying it categorically as zinc, brass, or a particular modern alloy would add certainty the evidence does not supply.

<a id="c1-13"></a>

## C1.13 — p. 481: intellection rather than attachment [R/C]

The English “through intellection” preserves the argument that the soul receives intelligibles. Y’s apparatus reports «بتعقلها» in J’s margin; JK’s explanation has «فلأنها تتعقلها». The erroneous extraction with the sense “attachment” is rejected. The relevant J folio is not among the reproduced leaves inspected here. This remains a commentary-supported reported reading, not a claim of direct collation of J at this point.

<a id="c1-14"></a>

## C1.14 — p. 509: independence and necessary existence [C]

The disputed inference is not settled by calling an existent independent of another. As the *Sharḥ* explains, without excluding an alternative’s prevailing without a determinant, such independence would not itself exclude accidental existence and nonexistence. See JK, II:56–57, «مجرد الاستغناء عن الغير لا يقتضي الوجوب». The text’s objection is therefore preserved. No proof rejected in the next sentence is presented as Taftāzānī’s final demonstration.

<a id="c1-15"></a>

## C1.15 — p. 531: what is agreed [C]

**Lemma:** «وهو وفاق». The agreement concerns excluding the hypothesised beginningless, freely choosing intermediary. It does not affirm the intermediary’s existence. The commentary’s «المخالف معترف بأنه لا يمكن» resolves the reference. See JK, II:79–80. The bracketed contextual clarification is interpretive, not a variant.

<a id="c1-16"></a>

## C1.16 — pp. 673, 677: verse locators [T]

The English quotations “Everything is perishing except His face” and “You caused us to die twice and revived us twice” now carry the missing locators Q 28:88 and Q 40:11 respectively. This repairs reference presentation. It is not an assertion that every Qurʾānic quotation, hadith, biblical allusion, and historical report in the work has received a complete source audit.

<a id="c1-17"></a>

## C1.17 — pp. 677–679: the Carullah reproduced leaf [D]

J f. 159a, reproduced at Y p. 781, contains the end of the grave-punishment discussion and the transition to the seventh investigation concerning spiritual resurrection. Its opening considers the alternatives that a residual bodily constitution remains or that questioning and punishment concern the spirit or original parts. This verifies the local continuity of the passage. The attribution of the first three lines to the author’s hand is reported by the edition; inspecting the reproduction does not independently authenticate the hand.

<a id="c1-18"></a>

## C1.18 — p. 717: assent and voluntary action [C]

The *Taʿrīfāt* formulation defining assent through voluntarily attributing truth to an informant is comparative evidence, not a rule imposed on this text. Taftāzānī here challenges the inclusion of voluntary choice in the ordinary concept of assent. The existing lexical note is retained so that dictionary comparison records a disagreement instead of erasing it.

<a id="c1-19"></a>

## C1.19 — p. 721: the highway-robbery citation [U]

The printed text couples its discussion of the highway robber with wording assigned to Q 59:3. The English retains the citation and identifies the problem in the notes. Replacing it silently with Q 5:33 would conflate verification of a scriptural source with establishment of Taftāzānī’s transmitted wording. A final decision requires collation at this exact locus; no correction is represented as settled here.

<a id="c1-20"></a>

## C1.20 — p. 729: degrees of certainty [D/R/C]

**Y main text:** «بمنع قبوله التفاوت». **Reading adopted:** «بمنع عدم قبوله التفاوت». Y reports «عدم» as a marginal correction in J. The English therefore says that the reply denies certainty’s **non-admission** of degrees.

N II:262 (PDF 660) has now been checked: the central commentary explicitly says «فلا نسلم أنه لا يقبل التفاوت بل لليقين مراتب». This independently verifies the argument in an image rather than only in a searchable transcription. It does not turn the reported J correction into a directly inspected manuscript reading. Without the added negative, the English would make the reply repeat the position it is opposing.

<a id="c1-21"></a>

## C1.21 — p. 741: absence of an imam and obligation of appearance [R/C]

**Reading retained:** «مع عدم الإمام أشق». Y’s note 6 reports S’s «مع الإمام أسبق» instead. The commentary expressly links greater difficulty and proximity to sincerity to acting without an imam; see JK, II:275, «أداء الواجب وترك القبيح مع عدم الإمام». The base reading is retained. No new direct inspection of the S folio is claimed.

**Reading retained:** «وأنتم لا توجبونه». Y’s note 9 supports this wording from J and L against divergent forms elsewhere; the page image has been checked. The argument addresses opponents who do not make the imam’s appearance obligatory. The English “which you do not make obligatory” is retained. It is not converted into a claim that the opponents do not love him.

<a id="c1-22"></a>

## C1.22 — pp. 755–757: where the Serez copy stops [D]

S f. 190b, reproduced at Y p. 783, ends during the argument for Abū Bakr’s imamate, with the Prophet’s appointing him to lead prayer and not removing him. A continuation catchword is visible below the text. This is not the end of the *Maqāṣid*. The absence of the subsequent conclusion and dated colophon in this incomplete witness cannot be counted as an intentional omission of those passages.

<a id="c1-23"></a>

## C1.23 — p. 765: al-Walīd’s name [R/U]

The reading text’s Ibn ʿUtba is retained, with the printed editor’s identification of Ibn ʿUqba disclosed in the existing note. A historically better identification and the words transmitted in this work are separate questions. The hadith comparison cited there is not promoted into a manuscript variant. The name requires direct collation before a new Arabic lemma is established.

<a id="c1-24"></a>

## C1.24 — p. 773: resolving two pronouns [D/C]

**Lemma:** «لعدم رضاه لا لرضاه». N II:304 (PDF 702), now inspected, explains that ʿUthmān did not approve armed defence on his behalf; ʿAlī’s failure to fight was not approval of the killing. The existing bracketed referents in the English are retained. This is syntactic clarification from the commentary, not textual emendation.

<a id="c1-25"></a>

## C1.25 — p. 777: the ending and two different dates [D/R]

The English retains the matn’s authorial completion notice: after dawn, Monday, 14 Dhū al-Qaʿda 784 AH. The reproduced J f. 184b at Y p. 779 contains the closing authorial text followed by a distinct scribal completion notice dated 789 AH. The second is not translated as part of Taftāzānī’s composition. The reproduction also contains other annotations; they are not indiscriminately incorporated into the text.

In the reproduced M II:226 at Y p. 791, the marginal matn ends with praise of God without the full 784 AH completion notice. N II:308 (PDF 706) likewise ends its marginal matn with «والحمد لله رب العالمين» and the completion marker «متن», while the commentary continues. These are direct observations of the printed terminal passages, not inferences from a failed text search. They establish differing treatment of the ending in those prints; they do not by themselves establish the cause or date of that difference.

The authorial date is not confused with the copyist’s date, and a missing end in S is not counted as testimony against the authorial notice. A Gregorian or Julian conversion is not added without a separate calendrical check.
