<a id="editorial-notes"></a>

# Editorial notes

## Introduction and Division I

- **M-P01, p. 89:** Imkān, wujūb, ḥudūth and qidam already carry the modal and temporal distinctions developed later. The imagery is the author’s, not a substitute for those distinctions.
- **M-P02, p. 89:** The published apparatus reports إزاحة for إزالة in J and S. Both express removal here; the English follows the printed reading without treating that variation as a doctrinal difference.
- **M-P03, p. 89:** The force of the imperative followed by its result is preserved. Al-amam al-mītāʾ is rendered provisionally as the direct, well-trodden way; it is not a technical name for a separate doctrine.
- **M-P04, p. 89–91:** The Arabic plays on titles of learned works, including muḥaṣṣal, lawāmiʿ al-asrār, maṭāliʿ, ṭawāliʿ al-anwār, ṣaḥāʾif, mawāqif and miṣbāḥ. Individual bibliographic identifications require a separate allusion register; they are not silently substituted for the literal imagery.; Two identical unvowelled forms تقفون have different senses: taqfūna, ‘follow’, and taqifūna, ‘stand’. The printed vocalisation and context distinguish them.; The 1277 print’s wording reported by YEK uses لقحته ... ومخضته, an impregnation/gestation image, against the printed نقحته ... ولخصته, refinement/distillation. This is a material rhetorical variant, not merely spelling.
- **M-I-01, p. 93:** ‘Certain’ is epistemic: yaqīnī. ‘Theoretical’ means requiring rational investigation in this context, not hypothetical or merely speculative.; The edition reads تحلية الإيمان بالإتقان. The short formulation distinguishes the discipline’s end, ghāya, from its benefit, manfaʿa.
- **M-I-02, p. 93:** ‘According to the governing principles of Islam’ describes a disciplinary constraint. It does not reduce qānūn to modern legislation. The author's own commentary explains this qualification and the inclusion of doctrinal opponents.
- **M-I-03, p. 93:** Ḥāl here names the disputed ontological ‘state’, not an ordinary changing condition. ‘Principles and supplementary discussions’ is the author’s terse classification of the examples.
- **M-I-05, p. 95:** The printed text reads المبدئية. The author’s commentary explains that some questions depend upon these preliminary discussions, which are therefore established so that the intended demonstration does not rest on something unexplained (OpenITI JK001157, lines 397–405; digital print marker 1:12). An initial transcription as المسلّمة was rejected after checking the page image. This is an editorial correction to our transcription, not a manuscript variant.
- **M-I-06, p. 95:** The last comparison concerns linguistic principles used in uṣūl al-fiqh, not a claim that all of uṣūl is established by grammar.
- **M-I-08, p. 97:** The claim concerns the conception of knowledge, not whether every item of knowledge is necessary. The necessity at issue is epistemic, distinct from necessary existence.
- **M-I-10, p. 97:** ‘Non-original mode of existence’ renders وجود غير متأصل: the thing is represented in cognition rather than instantiated as that very quality. The printed example illustrates the distinction; it is not an inference from someone's outward identity to their inner state.
- **M-I-11, p. 97–99:** The printed page reads ويفضي, “and leads to”. An initial transcription containing an extra nūn was corrected against the page image; it is not a witness reading.
- **M-I-12, p. 99:** ‘Admitting no contradictory alternative’ qualifies the cognitive discrimination, not the metaphysical necessity of every object known. The sentence about customary events explicitly preserves this distinction.; Mūjib is translated ‘adequate ground’; madhkūr, ‘what is referred to’. These choices avoid reducing the definition either to subjective confidence or to objects that exist externally.
- **M-I-15, p. 101:** The Imām here is Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī. ‘Acquisition’ means acquiring a conception through an account that defines or describes it; the dispute is not about whether conceptions occur in time.
- **M-I-16, p. 101:** Kāsib denotes the account through which the conception is acquired, not the person acquiring it. The terse argument presupposes the distinction between a quiddity’s constituents and what lies outside it.
- **M-I-17, p. 103:** The distinction is between an aggregate of conceptions and a conception of an aggregate. Replacing both with ‘a concept of the parts’ would erase the reply.
- **M-I-18, p. 103:** The six classes are classes of necessary assent in this discussion. Fiṭriyyāt does not mean an unargued disposition or a modern nativist theory. ‘Intuition’ here is the technical ḥads, whose definition must be followed in the later section on the soul.
- **M-I-19, p. 105:** ‘Open to question’ renders لا تخلو عن نظر here. The occurrence of naẓar in that expression does not itself classify the disputed cognitions as inferential.
- **M-I-20, p. 105:** Three levels remain distinct: hearing words; establishing their attribution; and assenting to their normative content. This paragraph is an epistemological example, not a complete source-critical authentication of the quoted report.
- **M-I-22, p. 105:** Waḍʿ and ḥaml concern logical subjecthood and predication here. Translating waḍʿ as linguistic convention in this sentence would confuse the argument.
- **M-I-23, p. 107:** The final sentence is the author’s violent polemic. It is retained as historical text and is not an editorial endorsement. Its wording must not be softened into an innocuous metaphor.
- **M-I-24, p. 107:** Defining investigation does not yet establish its truth-producing character. The last clause includes defective inquiry; the next investigation distinguishes sound and unsound forms.
- **M-I-25, p. 109:** The qualification ‘through God’s creation’ applies to all three positions grouped under ‘our account’, including rational entailment. The text should not be reduced to one undifferentiated occasionalist formula.; Matter and form refer to the components of the reasoning and their inferential arrangement. Kasb and tawlīd are retained as distinct doctrinal explanations, not merged as ordinary human learning.
- **M-I-28, p. 109–111:** The distinction between the singular case and an unquantified or universal proposition is essential to the reply to circularity. ‘The first figure’ is a logical figure, not a diagram in this edition.
- **M-I-31, p. 111:** The terse last reply is not expanded into a claim to comprehend the divine essence. The distinction between conception under an aspect and exhaustive comprehension must remain open for the commentary.
- **M-I-32, p. 113:** The repeated pronouns are resolved from the argument and the author’s commentary. The reply distinguishes entailment from a cause on which its effect’s existence depends.
- **M-I-34, p. 113:** Jahl means false belief here, not mere absence of knowledge. The point concerns the truth of the resulting belief: valid inference from false premises can end in a true conclusion.
- **M-I-36, p. 115:** Dalīl, madlūl, dalāla and jihat al-dalāla remain four distinct terms. ‘Ground of indication’ is a contextual translation of the last, not another name for the proof itself.
- **M-I-37, p. 115:** The final answer is reported before its assessment; it must not be omitted to make the argument appear one-sided. The exception concerning revelation is part of the matn.
- **M-I-38, p. 115:** ‘Heretics’ renders the author's polemical designation al-malāḥida. Identifying the intended historical group requires the separate commentary and historical note; it must not be used as a neutral modern label.
- **M-I-39, p. 117:** ‘Without qualification’ will be distinguished from obligation conditional on an already obtained prerequisite. The doctrinal contrast concerns the ground of obligation, not whether reason can investigate.
- **M-I-41, p. 117–119:** The paired examples distinguish a prerequisite whose acquisition is included in an obligation from a condition upon which that obligation depends. The compact wording should not be read as making residence and the means of pilgrimage obligatory prerequisites to acquire.; The analogy involving killing is retained as the author’s abstract illustration of an end and its means; it supplies no present instruction or ruling.
- **M-I-42, p. 119:** Ilzām is binding someone by argument here. The concluding distinction between actual obligation and knowledge of it is the reply’s central point.
- **M-I-43, p. 119–121:** “The second” preserves the matn’s reference to investigation or the intention to investigate. In the commentary Taftāzānī identifies the intention as temporally prior but explains why the matn leaves both alternatives grouped together (OpenITI JK001157, lines 1591–1597; digital print marker 1:47).; Compound ignorance is firm belief that fails to correspond to reality. It is not merely not knowing that one does not know.
- **M-I-44, p. 121:** Qiyās is syllogism in this logical classification, while tamthīl is analogy. Translating both as ‘analogy’ would obscure the threefold contrast with induction.; ‘Of itself’ is بالذات; the relation between an inferential method and what it obtains is being specified, without deciding all the causal questions discussed earlier.
- **M-I-46, p. 123:** The denial of ‘purely transmitted’ proof concerns ultimate epistemic dependence. It does not erase the preceding practical classification by a proof’s immediate premises.
- **M-I-47, p. 123:** The first naql in this paragraph’s argument is transmitted evidence; naql in the list of linguistic possibilities is transfer of meaning. They must not be translated identically without explanation.; The pronouns in فتكذيبه تكذيبه are resolved as undermining reason and thereby undermining transmission, following the direction of dependence stated immediately before them.; Qurʾānic examples: 112:1 and the phrase embedded in 47:19. The second is a phrase from the verse, not its full wording.

## Division II

- **P. 135 — The Shaykh:** Here the Shaykh is Abū al-Ḥasan al-Ashʿarī. The commentary’s opening to the second investigation expressly names him in connection with the thesis that each thing’s existence is identical with its essence (JK001157, passage beginning قال البحث الثاني المنقول عن الشيخ أبي الحسن الأشعري). The later قال وذهب الشيخ resumes that attribution. The title is preserved in the translation; it does not refer here to Ibn Sīnā.
- **P. 137–139 — Mental and external distinction:** The passage distinguishes additional in the intellect from intellectually or in intellection for those denying mental existence. The translation preserves that restriction.
- **P. 147–157 — Thubūt and existence:** Establishedness translates thubūt. Statements that make it coextensive with existence are the author’s position, followed by competing positions; do not impose that equation on the reported Muʿtazilī account.
- **P. 153,171 — Negative judgement and negative predicate:** Salb and ʿudūl distinguish negating a proposition from affirming a negative predicate. The distinction is essential to the replies about blackness and humanity.
- **P. 163–167 — Three considerations of quiddity:** On condition of something, on condition of nothing, and without condition of anything remain distinct. The author’s treatment of the natural universal must not be silently harmonised with a different exposition.
- **P. 181–191 — Modality:** Contingency renders imkān khāṣṣ; broader and dispositional senses are translated as possibility. Predicate and copular uses require a further logical review.
- **P. 193–199 — Determination and choice:** Distinguish an alternative prevailing without a cause from an agent preferring equal alternatives. Necessity through a complete cause is not silently equated with compulsion.
- **P. 239–245 — Arguments against regress:** Preserve the author’s objections to the proofs, including his criticism of correspondence arguments. Do not present every reported proof as his accepted demonstration.
- **P. 247 — Editorial parentheses:** The printed parenthesis around “agent or” is retained. YEK p. 247, note 1, reports that فاعل أو has been obliterated in J but survives in the other matn witnesses accessible to the editor. This is a reported cancellation, not a folio directly inspected here. The author’s commentary describes form as جزء فاعل and excludes its independent agency: matter receives existence with form’s assistance. See JK001157, I:170–171, قال المبحث السابع المادة للصورة. Retaining the parenthesis records the textual uncertainty; the phrase must not be taken to establish form as an independently sufficient efficient cause.

## Continuation: local textual decisions

These are translator's notes, not part of Taftāzānī's text. Page references are to the 2019 YEK Arabic–Turkish edition. They record local decisions and do not constitute complete collation.

- **III, p. 281 — definition of quality.** The page image reads «أو لا قسمة» (“or nondivision”), confirming the English. The extracted text lost the negative particle. This is a correction of extraction, not a textual emendation.
- **III, p. 295 — shade.** The page image confirms «الظل» and «من الهواء». The translation therefore retains “shade” and “air,” despite the historical account's unfamiliar terminology. The passage is not silently rewritten into modern optics.
- **IV, p. 415 — the recipient of direction.** The printed main text reads «والقابل ليس إلا واحدا», literally “and the recipient is only one,” while the following explanation distinguishes the heavenly bearer of above from the earthly bearer of below. The printed apparatus records omission of «إلا» in J and S; these particular locations have not been independently inspected. The author's commentary explicitly says that, if the determiner means the recipient, the determiner of above and below cannot be one, because the centre does not subsist in the encompassing sphere (OpenITI JK001157, passage beginning «قال ثم معنى تحديده», around lines 11347–11356 in the acquired file). The translation adopts the reading without «إلا»: “the recipient is not one.” Revision C1 additionally verifies the disagreement between marginal matn and central commentary directly in the Istanbul 1305 scan, I:336 (PDF 352); see C1.10.
- **IV, p. 421 — lunar period.** The Arabic gives 354 days plus one sixth and one fifth of a day under the general statement about traversing the signs. This is a lunar-year figure, not the period of a single lunar circuit. The translation preserves the stated number and wording rather than silently substituting a modern orbital period. The scope and possible compression of the sentence require further collation with the commentary and witnesses.
- **IV, p. 423 — lunar deferent.** The printed image confirms twenty-four degrees and twenty-three minutes. The spelling «ثلث» here represents “three” in the compound twenty-three, not a separate one-third of a degree. Jupiter has no separate daily numerical entry in this printed list; none has been supplied by the translator.
- **IV, p. 459 — digestion.** «ماء الكشك الثخين» is rendered “thick gruel.” An earlier draft specified barley-water; the Arabic does not warrant fixing the preparation to barley. “Chyle,” “humours,” “temperament,” and the account of digestion belong to the physiology reported in the text and should not be read as modern anatomical equivalents without qualification.
- **IV, p. 481 — intellection and immateriality.** “Through intellection” follows the argument about the soul's receiving intelligible forms. The page image and its note 8 have been inspected: the apparatus reports a reading with *taʿaqqul* and a marginal «بتعقلها» in J. The commentary explicitly explains the inference with «فلأنها تتعقلها» (“because it intellects them”), in the paragraph beginning «قال احتجوا أي القائلون بتجرد النفس». The mechanically extracted «بتعلقها» must therefore not be allowed to turn the argument into one about attachment to body. The reported J reading is not a manuscript independently inspected here.
- **V, p. 531 — the intermediary.** The compressed «وهو وفاق» concerns agreement that the hypothesised beginningless, freely choosing intermediary must be excluded. It does not report agreement that such an intermediary exists. The author's expansion explicitly has «المخالف معترف بأنه لا يمكن»; compare the acquired JK *Sharḥ*, II:79–80. This is clarification from the commentary, not a manuscript variant.
- **VI, pp. 637–639 and elsewhere — biblical and prophetic quotations.** Quotations are translated as they occur in Taftāzānī's Arabic. Biblical passages have not been silently replaced with a modern Bible translation. The attribution, exact wording, transmission, and authenticity of each prophetic or historical report require a separate reference apparatus. The author's descriptions “authentic” or “recurrently transmitted,” and his denials of those descriptions, are his judgements, not new certifications by the translator.
- **VI, p. 707 — abrogation.** The statement concerning Q 2:256 is the position advanced in this historical argument. The translation does not independently adjudicate the exegetical dispute or imply that the claim is agreed by all interpreters.
- **VI, p. 717 — voluntary assent.** Al-Jurjānī's *Taʿrīfāt*, entry 374, defines assent by voluntarily attributing truth to an informant: «أن تنسب باختيارك الصدق إلى المخبر». Taftāzānī here objects to building choice into the ordinary-language concept of assent and to treating it solely as an act. The dictionary is therefore evidence of a contested formulation, not authority for overriding the argument. His allowance of voluntarily acquired assent must be distinguished from the claim that all assent is intrinsically a voluntary action.
- **VI, p. 721 — scriptural citation and argument.** The printed text introduces the highway robber but cites the wording «ولهم في الآخرة عذاب النار» with the reference Q 59:3. The citation and reference have been retained rather than silently replaced with the highway-robbery passage in Q 5:33. Their fit with the argument requires further textual checking. “The major premise” is clarified in brackets as the proposed exemption of every believer from disgrace: the author's reply restricts “those who believed with him” to the Companions.
- **VI, p. 729 — a negative that changes the argument.** The printed main text has «بمنع قبوله التفاوت», literally “by denying its admission of degrees.” Its apparatus reports that J adds «عدم» as a marginal correction, giving «بمنع عدم قبوله التفاوت»: denying its **non-admission** of degrees. The translation adopts that reported addition. The author's *Sharḥ* independently confirms the intended argument: «فلا نسلم أنه لا يقبل التفاوت بل لليقين مراتب»—he does not grant that certainty cannot vary, since certainty has degrees. See JK *Sharḥ*, II:261–262, in the discussion beginning «قال المبحث الثالث ظاهر الكتاب والسنة». The printed page and apparatus have been visually inspected; the J manuscript itself has not. This is an explicitly disclosed editorial choice, not a silent correction or a newly inspected manuscript reading.
- **VI, p. 729 — “number.”** The compressed explanation of increase according to “persistence, firmness, and number” concerns, in the *Sharḥ*, the succession and number of occurrences of assent, under the theory that an accident does not persist. It should not simply be taken as counting religious propositions; an increase in the objects of belief is the next, distinct explanation (JK *Sharḥ*, II:262).
- **VI, pp. 731–733 — imitation and certainty.** “Imitation” renders *taqlīd*: accepting another's statement without one's own examination of its proof, not merely copying behaviour. Compare al-Jurjānī, *Taʿrīfāt*, entry 410, which distinguishes this from inquiry into evidence. The passage's contrast between decisiveness and stability of belief is preserved: firm assent and possession of a proof are not being treated as identical.
- **VI, pp. 735–737 — confessional designations.** Terms such as *kāfir*, *fāsiq*, *mubtadiʿ*, and *zindīq* belong to the author's doctrinal and juridical classification. “Unbeliever,” “sinful person,” and “innovator” preserve that classification; *zindīq* is retained where the specified concealment of beliefs is decisive. These labels should not be flattened into the modern, interchangeable word “heretic.” “The Shīʿa” and “the Muʿtazilites” retain the author's broad attributions, which are not a claim that every member or branch held the reported position.
- **VI, pp. 743–749 — immunity.** *ʿIṣma* in this argument concerns protection against error and sin; its scope is itself disputed. “Immunity” is therefore explained by context rather than replaced everywhere by either “infallibility” or “sinlessness.” Al-Jurjānī's *Taʿrīfāt*, entry 973, distinguishes the disposition to avoid sins while able to commit them from juridical senses of protected status. These senses must not be conflated.
- **VI, p. 759 — patronage and authority.** *Walī*, *mawlā*, and *wilāya* carry the contested meanings in the argument. “Patron” and “patronage” keep the polysemy visible; *mawlā* is retained where the author explicitly lists alternative senses. Neither “friend” nor “political successor” can replace every occurrence without deciding in advance the very dispute being translated.
- **VI, p. 761 — “my religion.”** The text expressly specifies a *kasra* on the *dāl* in «ديني». The translation consequently gives “my religion,” not “my debt,” while retaining the author's vocalisation notice. This wording is not offered as an authenticated independent version of the cited report.
- **VI, p. 765 — personal names and historical reports.** “Al-Walīd ibn ʿUtba” and the non-specific “Ibn ʿUmar” reproduce the reading text. The printed page has been visually inspected; its modern note 4 identifies the former as al-Walīd ibn **ʿUqba** ibn Abī Muʿayṭ. The acquired JK *Sharḥ*, II:294–295, likewise has *ʿUtba* and expands the latter name as ʿAbd Allāh. Agreement between these reading layers is not sufficient historical verification. In addition, [Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1707a](https://sunnah.com/muslim:1707a) recounts a punishment of al-Walīd under ʿUthmān, involving ʿAlī and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar. This is a reference for comparison, not a substitute reading of Taftāzānī's text. Names, chronology, and the polemical allegation remain matters for fuller collation rather than silent normalisation.
- **VI, p. 767 — the end of the imamate.** In this concluding historical sequence, the transition from *imāma* to *mulk* is translated as a transition from the imamate to kingship. It should not silently be harmonised with the broader earlier discussion of effective rulers and the legal functions of an imam. The author uses the term in a more restricted evaluative sense here.
- **VI, p. 773 — identifying the pronouns.** The contrast «لعدم رضاه لا لرضاه» is clarified from the author's commentary: ʿUthmān did not authorise fighting in his defence; ʿAlī's inaction did not express approval of the killing. Compare JK *Sharḥ*, II:303, beginning «وأما توقفه عن نصرة عثمان». Square brackets make the supplied referents visible.
- **VI, p. 775 — the four-hundred-year interval.** The statement about the duration of concealment belongs to the author's historical present. It has not been updated to the present day. Likewise, “after the Banū al-ʿAbbās” remains the author's temporal reference.
- **VI, p. 777 — closing date.** The author's colophon gives Monday, 14 Dhū al-Qaʿda 784 AH, after dawn. The Islamic date and weekday are translated as supplied; no unverified conversion to a Julian or Gregorian date is added.

<a id="technical-glossary"></a>

# Working technical glossary

The first table preserves the working decisions for the earlier divisions. The contextual supplement extends the glossary across Divisions III–VI and records the additional lexical comparisons actually made. These are working equivalents, not a claim that every occurrence has received independent review.

## Earlier divisions

| Arabic | Transliteration | English | Context and decision |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| كلام | kalām | kalām; dialectical theology | Retain the disciplinary name; ordinary speech or discourse remains contextual. (M-I-01) |
| علم | ʿilm | knowledge; cognition; science | The author distinguishes senses. Do not force every occurrence into one English noun. (M-I-01,12–14) |
| عقائد دينية | ʿaqāʾid dīniyya | religious doctrines | The objects of the discipline, not all religious practices. (M-I-01) |
| موضوع | mawḍūʿ | subject matter | A science’s subject; logical subject in the relevant propositions. (M-I-01–07) |
| معلوم | maʿlūm | the known | Broader than what exists externally. (M-I-01) |
| مبادئ | mabādiʾ | principles | Prerequisite cognitions or starting points; determine function locally. (M-I-01,06,24) |
| مبدئية | mabdaʾiyya | serving as a preliminary principle | Reading verified against the image and the author’s commentary; not an assumed premise. (M-I-05) |
| تحقيق | taḥqīq | reasoned verification; establishing a point | Jūrjānī: establishing the question through its proof. (M-P04; D-J04) |
| تقليد | taqlīd | uncritical following | Acceptance without examining the proof, not every legitimate use of teaching. (M-I-12,40,43; D-J05) |
| تصور | taṣawwur | conception | Apprehension without judgement; distinguish occurrence of knowledge from conceiving knowledge. (M-I-08–18; D-J02) |
| تصديق | taṣdīq | assent | The matn’s logical definition controls; the brief Taʿrīfāt reporter entry is narrower. (M-I-12–18) |
| حكم | ḥukm | judgement; ruling | Logical judgement and normative ruling must be distinguished. (M-I-12–13,39) |
| ضروري | ḍarūrī | necessary | Epistemic necessity here, not modal necessary existence. Some uses are relative to a creature’s power. (M-I-08–23; D-J-277) |
| نظري | naẓarī | theoretical; requiring investigation | Not hypothetical or speculative in the modern loose sense. (M-I-13–14,26–28) |
| نظر | naẓar | rational investigation | Two movements in the full account; لا يخلو عن نظر means open to question in M-I-19. (M-I-24–47) |
| فكر | fikr | thought | The matn’s movement among meanings is broader than a sound proof. (M-I-24; D-J-1101) |
| بديهيات / أوليات | badīhiyyāt / awwaliyyāt | self-evident / primary judgements | Narrow sixfold classification distinguished from broader usage. (M-I-18; D-J-277) |
| مشاهدات | mushāhadāt | observations | Includes external sensation and internal awareness. (M-I-18) |
| وجدانيات | wijdāniyyāt | inward awarenesses | Not restricted to emotions. (M-I-18) |
| فطريات | fiṭriyyāt | innate judgements | Mediated by an ever-present middle; not merely an unreasoned disposition. (M-I-18) |
| مجربات | mujarrabāt | experiential judgements | Repeated observation; certainty and necessity are discussed separately. (M-I-18–19) |
| متواترات | mutawātirāt | multiply transmitted judgements | Distinguish hearing the words, establishing attribution and accepting content. (M-I-18–20) |
| حدسيات | ḥadsiyyāt | intuitive judgements | Technical ḥads; verify against the later account of the soul. (M-I-18–19) |
| ظن | ẓann | probabilistic judgement | Preponderance with a contrary possibility, not a numerical measure. (M-I-24,43,47; D-J-934) |
| وهم | wahm | weaker supposition; imagining | In the contrast with ẓann it is the weaker alternative; later faculty psychology has another sense. (M-I-43) |
| يقين | yaqīn | certainty | Do not conflate certainty of cognition with intrinsic necessity of the object. (M-I-12,47; D-J07) |
| جهل مركب | jahl murakkab | compound ignorance | Firm false belief, not simply ignorance of one’s ignorance. (M-I-43; D-J-519) |
| ماهية | māhiyya | quiddity | Preserve parts, conceptions of parts and conception of their aggregate. (M-I-16–17) |
| دور | dawr | circularity | Distinguish dependence of existence, conception and knowledge. (M-I-08–09,26–28; D-J-695) |
| تسلسل | tasalsul | infinite regress | Current use concerns chains of epistemic or explanatory dependence. (M-I-29,37; D-J-364) |
| دليل | dalīl | proof | Can name a complex inference or the thing from which one infers. (M-I-32,36,44–47; D-J06) |
| مدلول | madlūl | what is indicated | Not always a proposition written as a conclusion. (M-I-32,36) |
| دلالة | dalāla | indication | The proof’s capacity to lead to knowledge; distinct from its ground. (M-I-36) |
| جهة الدلالة | jihat al-dalāla | ground of indication | The aspect through which inference proceeds. (M-I-36,43) |
| شبهة | shubha | specious objection; specious argument | Context distinguishes an objection from apparent evidence. (M-P02; M-I-36) |
| كسب | kasb | acquisition | Acquiring a conception and the theological account of agency are distinct uses. (M-I-15–17,25) |
| توليد | tawlīd | generation | A specific Muʿtazilī causal account; retain Arabic in doctrinal notes. (M-I-25) |
| حد / رسم | ḥadd / rasm | essential definition / description | Each can be complete or incomplete in the author’s classification. (M-I-44) |
| قياس | qiyās | syllogism | Logical usage here. Legal qiyās corresponds to analogy in the separate disciplinary usage. (M-I-44; D-J-1160) |
| استثنائي / اقتراني | istithnāʾī / iqtirānī | exceptive / conjunctive | Conjunctive is a historical technical name, not simply a conjunction connective. (M-I-44; D-J-1160) |
| استقراء | istiqrāʾ | induction | Complete and incomplete forms must remain distinguished. (M-I-44; D-J-108) |
| تمثيل | tamthīl | analogy | Matn and commentary control where the digital dictionary wording is corrupt. (M-I-44; D-J-423) |
| نقل | naql | transmission; transfer of meaning | The linguistic list uses semantic transfer; the proof classification uses transmitted evidence. (M-I-46–47) |
| وضع | waḍʿ | subjecthood; linguistic convention | Logical subjecthood in 22; linguistic designation in 47. (M-I-22,47) |
| حدوث / قدم | ḥudūth / qidam | temporal origination / beginninglessness | Avoid treating qidam as mere antiquity. (M-P01; M-I-34,36) |
| إمكان / وجوب | imkān / wujūb | contingency / necessity | Modal necessity differs from epistemic necessity and normative obligation. (M-P01; M-I-36,39) |

## Contextual supplement for Divisions III–VI

These entries explain working equivalents in this translation. “Local” means the decision follows the author's argument in the indicated division; it does not mean that every occurrence or every dictionary has been collated. The lexical comparisons listed after the table are the entries actually consulted during the final continuation review. Where one Arabic word has different technical senses, context governs the English.

| Arabic | Transliteration | Working English | Context and distinction |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| جوهر | jawhar | substance | III–IV: contrast with accident; its definition differs between theologians and philosophers. Not “jewel.” |
| جوهر فرد / جزء لا يتجزأ | jawhar fard / juzʾ lā yatajazzaʾ | indivisible substance / indivisible part; atom | IV: the atom of kalām, not a modern chemical atom. |
| عرض | ʿaraḍ | accident | III: an ontological category, not an unplanned event; distinct from modal contingency. |
| محل | maḥall | locus; substrate | III–IV: that in which something inheres; not automatically the narrower philosophical “subject.” |
| موضوع | mawḍūʿ | subject; subject matter | I: the subject matter of a science; III–IV: an independently constituted substrate; in logic, the subject of a proposition. These are not interchangeable. |
| حلول | ḥulūl | inherence | III–V: interpreted through the dependence relation under discussion; not merely occupying a place. |
| مقولة | maqūla | category | III: highest categorial divisions; in VI p. 717, the category of acting is relevant to whether assent is an act or a quality. |
| كم / كيف | kamm / kayf | quantity / quality | III: categorial terms, not simply “how many” and “how.” |
| إضافة | iḍāfa | relation | III: categorial relation; elsewhere it may be grammatical annexation, as in the imamate argument on p. 761. |
| أين / متى | ayna / matā | where / when | III: categories of location and temporal placement. |
| وضع | waḍʿ | position; disposition | III–IV: spatial arrangement of parts; elsewhere linguistic institution or convention. |
| ملك / جدة | milk / jida | possession | III: the relevant category; distinct from *mulk*, kingship, in VI. |
| أن يفعل / أن ينفعل | an yafʿala / an yanfaʿila | acting / being acted upon | III: categorial action and affection, rather than ethical activity and passivity. |
| كون / أكوان | kawn / akwān | mode(s) of spatial being | III: the discussion of motion, rest, conjunction, and separation; context can give *kawn* a different sense elsewhere. |
| حركة / سكون | ḥaraka / sukūn | motion / rest | III–IV: motion may include change outside locomotion; the theory under discussion determines its scope. |
| حيز | ḥayyiz | spatial region | III–IV: the region occupied or assigned, with disputed accounts of place and void. |
| خلاء | khalāʾ | void | IV: the disputed possibility of empty extension, not a presupposition of modern vacuum physics. |
| زمان | zamān | time | III: its reality and relation to motion are argued over, not assumed in the translation. |
| هيئة | hayʾa | configuration; disposition | III–IV: arrangement or state; not automatically a substantial form. |
| هيولى | hayūlā | prime matter | IV: the philosophical recipient of form, distinguished from body and from ordinary material. |
| صورة | ṣūra | form | IV: substantial form, sensible form, or cognitive representation according to context. |
| طبيعة | ṭabīʿa | nature | IV: a principle of natural motion and rest in the theories reported. |
| مزاج | mizāj | temperament | IV: the quality resulting from the interaction of elemental qualities; not a modern personality trait. |
| عنصر / أسطقس | ʿunṣur / usṭuqus | element | IV: the traditional elemental scheme, not the periodic table. |
| خلط / أخلاط | khilṭ / akhlāṭ | humour(s) | IV: the historical medical constituents, not an informal name for every body fluid. |
| روح | rūḥ | spirit | IV: may denote a subtle physiological vehicle; context must distinguish this from the immaterial soul. |
| نفس | nafs | soul; self | IV: vegetative, animal, human, or celestial soul; reflexive and theological contexts can require “self.” |
| عقل | ʿaql | intellect | IV: the faculty or immaterial entity under discussion; elsewhere reason as a source of judgement. |
| تجرد | tajarrud | immateriality | IV: separation from matter in essence and, where specified, operation; not mere abstraction in a modern psychological sense. |
| قوة | quwwa | power; faculty; potentiality | IV–V: capacity, psychic faculty, or contrast with actuality; one English word cannot cover all uses. |
| إدراك | idrāk | apprehension | III–IV: broad cognitive reception; not limited to sensation. |
| حس مشترك | ḥiss mushtarak | common sense | IV: the internal faculty unifying sensible forms, not ordinary practical good judgement. |
| خيال | khayāl | imagination | IV: the preservation or representation of sensible forms in the psychological theory being reported. |
| وهم | wahm | estimation | IV: the internal faculty apprehending particular nonsensible meanings; elsewhere the weaker side of a doubtful judgement or a mistaken supposition. |
| حافظة / ذاكرة | ḥāfiẓa / dhākira | retentive faculty / memory | IV: preservation and recovery of the meanings apprehended by estimation. |
| متخيلة / مفكرة | mutakhayyila / mufakkira | imaginative / cogitative faculty | IV: combining and separating forms and meanings, named according to its employment by estimation or intellect. |
| خارصيني | khārṣīnī | khārṣīnī | IV: a historical material name retained where a secure modern identification has not been established. |
| صانع | ṣāniʿ | Maker | V: the divine cause in the proofs; “artisan” would import the wrong physical model. |
| موجب / مختار | mūjib / mukhtār | necessitating agent / freely choosing agent | V: the contrast concerns the mode of causation, not simply whether an agent possesses a will in ordinary language. |
| مرجح | murajjiḥ | determinant; ground of preference | II, V: what accounts for one alternative's predominance; its causal role is itself disputed. |
| تعلق | taʿalluq | relation; attachment | V: an attribute's relation to its object; IV: the soul's attachment to body. The word does not always denote spatial contact. |
| قدرة | qudra | power | V: ability related to a possible act; compare the disputed roles of divine and human power. |
| إرادة | irāda | will | V: a specifying attribute or an agent's willing, according to the position reported. |
| كسب | kasb | acquisition | V: the servant's relation to an act created by divine power. Taftāzānī records competing formulations; the term must not be reduced to earning a reward. |
| خلق / إيجاد | khalq / ījād | creation / bringing into existence | V: distinguished from acquisition; the author also considers their predication of human agents. |
| حسن / قبح | ḥusn / qubḥ | goodness / ugliness | V: normative praise and blame, benefit and harm, or perfection and defect according to the explicitly distinguished sense. |
| لطف | luṭf | gracious aid | V–VI: especially the disputed aid drawing a servant toward obedience; the term carries an argument about obligation, not merely gentleness. |
| تكليف | taklīf | imposition of obligation | V–VI: being subject to divine commands and prohibitions; *mukallaf* is a person subject to such obligation. |
| نبوة / رسالة | nubuwwa / risāla | prophecy / messengership | VI: retain distinctions when the author makes them; do not assume that every occurrence is interchangeable. |
| معجزة | muʿjiza | miracle | VI: an extraordinary sign associated with a claim to prophecy and a challenge; distinct from a saintly wonder. |
| كرامة | karāma | saintly wonder | VI: an extraordinary occurrence associated with a saint, without a prophetic claim. Elsewhere the same noun can mean honour. |
| خرق العادة | kharq al-ʿāda | breaking the customary course | VI: deviation from the ordinary course; avoids importing an unargued modern concept of inviolable natural law. |
| تحدي | taḥaddī | challenge | VI: a demand that opponents match the sign offered in support of the prophetic claim. |
| إرهاص | irhāṣ | anticipatory sign | VI: an extraordinary sign preceding the prophetic commission, where distinguished from a confirming miracle. |
| عصمة | ʿiṣma | immunity from error and sin; protection | VI: the scope and grounds are disputed; juridical protected status is a separate sense. |
| معاد | maʿād | return; the hereafter | VI: the doctrine of ultimate return, including the disputed account of bodily resurrection. |
| حشر | ḥashr | gathering; resurrection | VI: the gathering of bodies and persons for judgement; context determines whether the process or doctrine is foregrounded. |
| إعادة المعدوم | iʿādat al-maʿdūm | restoration of the nonexistent | II, VI: the problem of numerical identity after nonexistence, not simply making a similar replacement. |
| سمعيات | samʿiyyāt | matters known through revelation | VI: “hearing” concerns transmitted revelation as an epistemic source, not auditory sensation. |
| إيمان / إسلام | īmān / islām | faith / Islam; submission | VI: their identity or difference is expressly debated. Distinct English terms preserve the debate. |
| تصديق / إذعان | taṣdīq / idhʿān | assent / yielding assent | I, VI: the cognitive and religious analyses must be distinguished; see the note on p. 717. |
| تقليد | taqlīd | imitation; uncritical following | VI: acceptance of another's doctrine without one's own investigation of its proof. |
| موافاة | muwāfāt | final state; condition at the end of life | VI: *īmān al-muwāfāt* is faith at life's end, distinguished from present faith. |
| كفر / فسق | kufr / fisq | unbelief / sinfulness | VI: distinct classifications; the status of the major sinner is the subject of explicit disagreement. |
| نفاق / زندقة | nifāq / zandaqa | hypocrisy / concealed unbelief (*zandaqa*) | VI: technical confessional uses; the author's definitions control, not a general moral accusation. |
| إمامة / خلافة / ملك | imāma / khilāfa / mulk | imamate / caliphate or succession / kingship | VI: political-theological offices and evaluations whose scope varies with the argument. |
| أهل الحل والعقد | ahl al-ḥall wa-l-ʿaqd | those empowered to bind and loose | VI: the body whose selection and allegiance are debated; not equated without argument with a modern electorate. |
| نص | naṣṣ | text; explicit designation | VI: designation of a successor in the imamate debate; not every occurrence means a scriptural verse. |
| بيعة | bayʿa | pledge of allegiance | VI: a constitutive political act in the discussion, not a commercial sale despite the shared root. |
| ولي / مولى / ولاية | walī / mawlā / wilāya | patron / *mawlā* / patronage or authority | VI: the competing senses are part of the argument; see p. 759. |
| تقية | taqiyya | dissimulation | VI: concealment under danger in the arguments reported, not an unqualified charge of habitual deceit. |
| بغاة | bughāt | rebels | VI: the juridical category discussed in relation to power and an interpretive justification; not interchangeable with unbelievers. |
| تأويل | taʾwīl | interpretation; interpretive justification | VI: can name the interpretive ground invoked for action, not only nonliteral scriptural interpretation. |

### Lexical evidence consulted for this supplement

- **Al-Jurjānī, *al-Taʿrīfāt*, entry 374, *taṣdīq*.** The voluntary attribution of truth to an informant is a formulation Taftāzānī disputes in VI, p. 717. This is a useful counterexample to mechanically letting a dictionary determine the translation.
- **Al-Jurjānī, entry 410, *taqlīd*.** Acceptance without inquiry into a proof supports the technical sense of imitation in VI, pp. 731–733.
- **Al-Jurjānī, entry 973, *ʿiṣma*.** The entry distinguishes avoidance of sins while capable of them from juridical protection. “Immunity” in the prophecy and imamate debates therefore needs an explicit contextual scope.
- **Al-Tahānawī, *Kashshāf*, entry *imāma*, I:259–260.** The entry explains succession to the Messenger in maintaining religion and protecting the community, and compares definitions and contested conditions. It is later comparative evidence, not an independent witness to Taftāzānī's wording.
- **Al-Tahānawī, entry *karāma*, at the opening of the discussion reaching II:1360.** The entry associates the extraordinary occurrence with saints and distinguishes it from other extraordinary phenomena. This supports “saintly wonder” as a useful English distinction from *muʿjiza*.
- **Al-Tahānawī, entry *kasb*, II:1362 and following.** Its discussion distinguishes the relation of a servant's power and will to an act from divine bringing into existence and records multiple formulations. The present translation nevertheless follows Taftāzānī's own formulation in V.
- **Al-Tahānawī, entry *muʿjiza*, II:1575 and surrounding text.** A prophetic claim, an extraordinary sign, the challenge, and failure of opposition are distinguished from anticipatory signs and saintly wonders. These distinctions govern the English, rather than the undifferentiated use of “miracle” for every extraordinary event.

Locators refer to the entry numbers and page markers in the acquired OpenITI transcriptions: *Taʿrīfāt* JK000768 and *Kashshāf* Shamela0002573. Those markers identify the digital reading sources; they do not certify a scan or manuscript collation. Full entries, including embedded quotations and modern editorial matter, are not reproduced here.

# Remaining editorial work

The translation is complete in coverage. The agreed basis is the published YEK 2019 critical Arabic text. Complete manuscript acquisition and a new reconstruction of the Arabic are not required. Remaining checks concern the English translation against every source page, technical consistency, quotations and names, mathematical and astronomical expressions, and independent bilingual review. The readings adopted at pp. 415 and 729 remain explicitly disclosed departures supported by the published apparatus and the author’s commentary. Reported manuscript readings must continue to be distinguished from directly inspected images.
